<p align="center"><img src="public/crest.svg" width="260" alt="SolarTournament: the seal of the Ministry of War of the United Solar States"></p>

# SolarTournament

A browser space shooter in an asteroid field, built with [three.js](https://threejs.org/). A physically correct world: no drag, so a ship keeps every bit of speed it burns, no sound in space, no shield that stops a laser, and a careless burn ends in a rock.

What flies, collides, burns and breaks behaves the way it would out there, as long as it stays fun.

## Run

```
npm install
npm run dev        # http://127.0.0.1:5173/
npm test           # unit tests (Vitest)
npm run e2e        # browser tests (Playwright; first once: npm run e2e:install)
npm run bench      # benchmark in the test browser (see Performance); PROFILE=pixel8 as the reference phone
npm run build      # static site in dist/ (the voices and the types first; the workflow CI runs the tests before it goes live)
```

## Performance

The game draws the same world on every device, but not with the same effort: the picture's quality moves along a ladder of looks (`src/view/quality.ts`), from the picture as designed (rung 0) to the cheapest (rung 13). Each rung changes one or two knobs of the one above it, the largest saving for the least loss first. Only looks change: rocks, colliders, the round and everything that goes over the network are the same on every rung.

**Rocks and ice draw few triangles; their relief is a map, not geometry.** A near rock has 2 000 triangles, one seen from afar 500 (`src/world/rocks.ts`; `NEAR_DETAIL`, `FAR_DETAIL`), a sliver of the 22 000-triangle shape its collisions are still measured from (see world/surface: the measured surface never changes, so every browser collides the same whatever a rock draws). The fine relief and the surface's shade instead come from a relief map baked once per shape (16 of them): a cube map of 6 faces of 128 × 128 texels (`RELIEF_RES`, about 0.7° a texel, 1.5 to 4 m on a rock of the field), each texel the surface's normal in the rock's own frame and its shade, indexed by the direction from the rock's own center (a rock is star-shaped around it: every direction meets its surface once, so the map needs no UV unwrap). WebGL filters a cube map across its faces' edges and builds its mipmaps, so it shows no seam, and a rock far away calms into its mean instead of shimmering. All 16 take 6.3 MB, 8.4 MB with their mipmaps; ice has 16 of its own of the same size (`src/world/ice.ts`, see Rings of Saturn), its alpha the kind of ice instead of the rock's shade; all 32 bake in up to four background workers while the game loads (`relief.worker.ts`, about 60 ms a map on a desktop core, ice as rock), side by side with the collision shapes and the billboards, and an arena only puts on the GPU the maps of what it draws. A shader samples the map directly from the mesh's own local position and turns the normal into world space with the object's normal matrix, exactly as it would a vertex normal; the near and far meshes share the same map, so the relief looks identical at any of them. Up close, the near meshes lay a shared detail map over it (256 × 256 texels, one tile repeating every 11 m of the template, 28 to 77 m on a rock of the field): the smallest craters, pebbles and the regolith's grain, projected along the rock's three axes and blended by the mesh's facing, three more texture reads a pixel, whole within 100 m and gone beyond 450 m; it keeps the rock's mean tone and tilt, so its fading is not seen; ice lays a tile of its own the same way (frost grain, small fractures, tiny pits). The far meshes never take it, so on the rungs that draw every rock with its far mesh (`rocks: 0`) a rock up close shows the relief map alone. The far switch (below) only trades the coarser silhouette's few hundred triangles for fewer yet, the surface shading staying as it was.

**Farther yet, a rock or an ice piece draws as a billboard** (`src/view/impostor.ts`): a camera-facing quad, at a fixed distance regardless of the ladder's rung (80 of the shape's mean radii, and never nearer than 7 km, so that even a fragment of a fragment or a small chunk of ice switches farther out than the arena is across, 6.75 km, and the switch is never seen; within it a small piece draws its far mesh), showing 8 pre-rendered views (from the corners of a cube around the shape) picked by how the shape is actually turned now: the nearest one, and the next up to three as far as they come within about 12° of it, blended, so a turning rock fades from one view to the next instead of jumping; each view is turned about the line of sight as the shape is. A view holds no colors: each of its 48 × 48 cells (`IMPOSTOR_RES`) holds where its ray meets the shape (marched through the shape's own bump, the one its meshes' vertices take) and how far inside or outside the silhouette it lies, so filtering finds the silhouette between the cells, sharp at any size, and the views are mipmapped (the views of all 16 shapes take 1.2 MB, 1.6 MB with their mipmaps, shared by rock and ice, baked in about half a second on a desktop core while the workers bake the relief maps). The billboard reads the shape's relief map there, the very map its meshes read, through its mipmaps, and paints it with the meshes' own code, color, roughness and metalness (an ice piece's from its own relief map, with the ice's cold glow and the sunlight coming through its side away from the sun); it is lit at that moment by the scene's own sun and sky as three.js's standard material is (Lambert's diffuse light and the sun's highlight), so it shows the far mesh's colors and brightness and tracks a solar flare. The same billboards draw almost all of the belt beyond the arena (`src/world/belt.ts`): only rocks within 1.4 × the belt's inner edge, close enough that a ship coasting past the arena's limit could still reach them, keep the real low-poly geometry; the rest of the ring, the great majority of its 480 rocks, is cheap enough as billboards (two triangles each, one draw call per shape) that it needs no culling by direction.

**A broken rock's pieces are cut in the background, before it breaks** (`src/world/fracture.ts`, see Asteroids). Cutting a piece takes 100 to 200 ms in Node on a desktop (most of it the 22,000-triangle shape its collisions are measured from, cut and measured; the drawn meshes are cut from the light shapes), too long for a frame, so a worker (`src/world/fracture.worker.ts`) cuts them ahead: the pieces of the first break of every shape in the field as soon as the field is laid out, and the pieces of a piece as soon as it is born; only a piece not cut yet (a browser without workers, a rock broken in the first seconds, a newcomer rebuilding a field full of pieces) is cut on the spot, with a hitch. A piece's near mesh has 2,000 to 2,800 triangles, its fracture faces faceted (each triangle its own vertices, about 5,000 vertices, some 150 KB), its far mesh about 500, smooth; both are kept once cut, shared by every piece of that shape, rock and ice alike. A piece shades its outer surface with its whole shape's relief map, read by its position in the shape's frame, and its fracture faces with one break tile every piece shares (256 × 256, about 350 KB of graphics memory with its mipmaps; three more lookups a pixel, mipmapped, so a far face calms into its mean color instead of shimmering). A piece draws no billboard: 80 of its mean radii and never nearer than 7 km lie beyond the arena, so its far mesh is all it needs.

**A landmark seen only in its own arena sheds detail cheaply, since it never shares a frame with the others.** Jupiter's cap, its cloud deck (`src/world/landmarks.ts`, `Landmarks.setDetail`), is a sphere built once at three segment counts; a cheaper rung only swaps which one its mesh uses, so the saving costs nothing to apply. Its bands and storms are its map and noise in the fragment shader, not geometry, so the coarser sphere still shows them, only with a rounder-looking limb up close. The two sheets of cloud under the deck (`src/world/jupiter.ts`) are see-through spheres, each a full-screen pass at most: the first rungs draw both, from rung 2 the upper one, from rung 9 neither. Their gaps are noise that evens out where its cells shrink to a few pixels, so they never shimmer in the distance. The deck's cloud tops (see The arenas, Jupiter) are lookups of one 256 × 256 noise texture (about 350 KB of graphics memory with its mipmaps) and some arithmetic per pixel, besides the map's two (sharp, and blurred for the bands): nine on the full picture (the large and the small eddies, the billows, their grain, the threads, the billows once more towards the sun for their relief, the pop-up clouds, their shadows and the gaps), seven from rung 2 (without the small eddies and the relief), five from rung 9 (without the grain and the pop-ups' shadows); the vortices and the great storm's spiral are arithmetic only, two hashes for each of the two grids. The texture is mipmapped, so a pattern finer than a pixel fades to its mean by itself, and whatever is shaped beyond the texture (a cloud's sharp edge, a thread, a vortex, a white oval, a pop-up cloud) fades out where it is smaller than a few pixels, so the tops never shimmer. A sheet under the deck reads the texture twice besides its blurred map (the large eddies and the gaps). The cost of the tops is not measured yet. Saturn and Jupiter draw from real maps (see The arenas): the page loads them at 1024 × 512 (about 50 and 120 KB), and only once a rung with at least half the landmark's detail is reached does the browser fetch the sharp 2048 × 1024 maps (about 170 and 370 KB) and swap them in, so a phone on the cheaper rungs never downloads them and keeps about 6 MB of graphics memory per map rather than about 11. Earth's night map below the Satellite Junkyard covers only the region a pilot sees, so its 1024 × 832 (about 190 KB) and sharp 2048 × 1664 (about 560 KB) are about as sharp there as a whole-Earth map five times as wide; its day map covers only the part of that region the sun reaches, at 512 × 896 (about 130 KB) and sharp 1024 × 1792 (about 420 KB), swapped by the same rule. The junk beyond that arena's field, about 5,000 pieces, is one draw call of point sprites: each moves, turns and lights itself in the vertex shader, and the fragment shader draws its wing or its stage at its size on screen, never thinner than a pixel and never more than 64 pixels wide, so nothing in it crawls or shimmers. Saturn's ring and the specks of ice in it read the sun's shadow map only on a rung with shadows; on the others the shaders leave it out. The Corona's sun (`src/world/corona.ts`) is five draw calls: its face, the glow along its limb (a quad around it, square to the camera's line of sight), all its prominences as one mesh, each a ribbon that rises and falls in the vertex shader, and two quads over the whole picture, placed in clip space with no work on the processor: the sky's scattered light, drawn at the far plane so the depth test leaves it only the pixels nothing else covers, and the lens's veil over everything, which is not drawn at all while the sun stands behind the camera; each a handful of operations a pixel, smooth by the angle from the sun, with nothing in them to shimmer; the face's finest grain, the penumbrae's filaments and the prominences' strands fade out where they would be smaller than a few pixels, and a knot of coronal rain is drawn at least three pixels long, so none of it crawls on a phone. Nothing in it distorts the picture behind it: no heat shimmer. From rung 2 it shows the twelve tallest prominences, from rung 9 the eight tallest and its face without its finest grain. The black hole's lens (`src/view/lensing.ts`) marches a fixed number of steps outward along each pixel's bent ray looking for what it should show; a cheaper rung marches fewer, so the bend reads a little coarser at its edge, never unbent. The black hole's disk draws its streaks as three octaves of concentric rings (24, 60 and 150 across the disk), 27 hashes a pixel; each ring turns as a whole at its own speed, dark at its edges where the next one slides past, so the streaks never shear into stripes finer than the picture however long the round lasts. An octave evens out to its mean where its rings would be narrower than a pixel, so the disk never shimmers in the distance, and from rung 2 on the finest octave is left out (18 hashes a pixel).

**The Belt's mining is seven draw calls for all of it.** Everything the Rockborn put on the Belt's rocks (`src/world/mining.ts`) is merged into one geometry per material, built once from the field's rocks: steel (rigs, buoys: one worn surface of riveted plating tiled across every face, 6 m a tile, twice that along a beam, its hazard stripes drawn in the shader and smoothed over the pixel they fall in, fading to their mean where a pixel spans a stripe, so they never crawl; the dust towards the rock and the lit panes in the vertices' colors), stone (the cut faces), paint and light on the rocks (graffiti, the lamps' pools, the glowing cuts and tunnels), the lamps as points, the cutting lasers, the sparks and drill dust as points, and the dust clouds as one instanced quad. Each vertex carries the slot of its rock, and the vertex shaders place it by that rock's matrix from a uniform array of twelve, copied once a frame; a rock that breaks gets a zero matrix and its dressing is out of sight. The blinking, the laser's sweep, the sparks and the dust are all worked out in the shaders from the field time, so the CPU only copies the matrices and moves the six drill holes the hull listens to (see The Belt). A lamp, a beam or a mote is drawn at least 2.5 pixels wide and dimmer instead of smaller, so nothing small crawls from pixel to pixel. The ladder's share of particles thins the sparks, the drill dust and the dust clouds alike (the clouds cost fill rate, fading out within twice their radius of the camera, so no wall of dust ever fills the screen).

**Jupiter's haze is a few operations per pixel, not a volume.** The height fog of its air (`src/view/haze.ts`) is computed in the shader of everything drawn in the world, as three.js's fog chunks: the haze summed along the view ray in closed form from the altitudes of the ray's two ends (two exponentials and a division, no marching), so it costs the same at any depth on every rung. Only while the arena has air does the scene carry a fog, so the shaders of the other arenas are compiled without it and pay nothing; entering Jupiter compiles the fogged ones once. What the flight computer draws (the HUD, the racing line, the reference plane's grid) and the arena's edge stay clear of it. Under the deck the haze's light carries its clouds (see The arenas, Jupiter): a ray reads them at one or two points, each four lookups of one 256 × 256 noise texture (a lookup reads two layers of a 3D noise; one of them shoves the clouds about in swirls), two points on the full picture and one from rung 2 on; their features are tens of meters wide where the ray reads them, thousands of times a pixel's width, so they never shimmer. They change only the haze's light, never its sum. Under the deck a sphere around the eye takes the place of the haze's floor, so every view that would end in nothing ends in the haze: one opaque full-screen pass of it. The wisps are one draw call of 96 quads (56 from rung 2, 32 from rung 9), laid around the eye in the vertex shader from fixed seeds, the CPU setting two uniforms a frame; they fade out within a quarter of their length of the eye and at the edge of the box they wrap in, so none pops in or out. The cost of the haze, its clouds and the sheets of cloud is not measured yet.

**An arena's mood costs no pass of its own.** Its color grade (`src/view/grade.ts`) is a few operations per pixel inside the output pass that tone-maps the picture and encodes it for the screen anyway, the same on every rung. Its nebulae are at most four sprites with one of three cloud textures of 256 × 256 pixels, made once at load and shared by all arenas; the clouds are soft and coarse (their finest detail some 30 pixels or more on a screen), so they never shimmer. Space's color, the sky's light, the stars and the dust only change values the shaders already read. The cost of the larger nebulae (more of the screen covered by sprites drawn additively) is not measured yet.

**The real sky costs two draw calls, a small download and a bake sized by the rung.** Its 33 096 stars (the catalogue's 9,096 and 24,000 fainter ones generated) are one draw call of points (`src/world/starmap.ts`, 28 bytes each on the GPU, 0.9 MB), so they stay sharp on any screen without a texture large enough to hold them; the Milky Way behind them is one sphere drawn additively under everything else, each of its pixels reading a cube map once. The page downloads only the catalogue's stars (`stars.bin`, 73 KB). The Milky Way is generated in code (`src/world/milkyway.ts`) by background workers (up to three, one per spare core, the faces dealt out round them; a worker that cannot start, fails or stays silent for 30 s leaves its share to the main thread) while the game loads: at 512 texels a face (8.4 MB on the GPU with its mipmaps); at 1024 a face (34 MB on the GPU) only once a rung up to 8 asks for it, like the planets' sharp maps (see rung 9 below), kept beside the small one, so a phone that starts on the cheaper rungs holds 9.3 MB of sky, a desktop on the sharper rungs 43 MB. On a desktop (an M-series Mac, under load) a worker bakes its two faces in about 0.1 to 0.2 s at 512 and 0.3 to 0.8 s at 1024; the faint stars take about 0.2 s in the first of those workers. The Event Horizon's 6,000 stars generated in code stay one draw call of their own. The cost per frame is not measured yet.

**A hot hull costs two draw calls, only while it glows.** The plasma around a ship in Jupiter's air (`src/view/reentry.ts`, see Jupiter's air) is a sphere of about 1 200 triangles stretched into the shock layer and its tail in the vertex shader, and the hull's glow is the hull's own geometry drawn once more with an additive shader; both are built when a ship first heats and hidden while it is cool. The plasma trail, the flakes, the sparks and a burning ship's fireball are particles of the shared systems (one draw call per texture for all of them), so a cheaper rung thins them out with the rest. The licks at the edges of the view are one tube around the camera, drawn only above 45 % strength, its pixels at the view's edges. Its glow sways and streams slowly and never flickers frame to frame; with reduced motion it stands still. The cost is not measured yet.

| Rung | Change | Tier |
|---|---|---|
| 0 | Everything: up to 2 device pixels per CSS pixel, bloom at full resolution, the sun's shadows in a 4096 px map, lacquered hulls, all particles, dust and grains of the ring, the Belt's sparks, drill dust and dust clouds, the whole belt (480 rocks), the field's rocks keep their near mesh out to 12 of their radii, a comet's nucleus its near mesh (8 800 triangles) within 2 km, the title screen's pad with its shadow | **high** |
| 1 | The rocks keep their near mesh (2 000 triangles, drawn twice: for the picture and for the sun's shadows) only out to 6 of their radii; farther, the coarser far mesh (500 triangles), its relief unchanged (see above); a comet's nucleus its near mesh within 1 km, farther its far mesh (2 000 triangles) | |
| 2 | The near mesh only out to 3 radii, a comet's nucleus's within 500 m; Jupiter's cap (seen only in its own arena) at 128 × 48 segments instead of 256 × 96, and only the upper of its two sheets of cloud under the deck, its cloud tops without their small eddies and the low sun's relief, the haze's clouds read at one point of a ray instead of two, 56 wisps of 96, two layers of the auroras of three; the black hole's disk without its finest rings; the Corona's twelve tallest prominences of sixteen | |
| 3 | 1.75 device pixels per CSS pixel | |
| 4 | Bloom at half resolution (a quarter of its pixels); the black hole's lens (seen only in its own arena) marches 4 steps along each pixel's ray instead of 6 | |
| 5 | Shadow map 2048 px | |
| 6 | 1.5 device pixels per CSS pixel, the pad as well; the sun's shadow map (still on through rung 9) is refreshed only every other frame, not every one, and at once when it does not exist yet or changed size (`Game.updateShadowMap`): what casts it barely moves between two | **medium** |
| 7 | Shadow map 1024 px | |
| 8 | 1.25 device pixels per CSS pixel; the pad at 1, without its shadow | |
| 9 | Half the particles, dust and grains of the ring, the Belt's sparks, drill dust and dust clouds, half the belt's rocks beyond the arena; Jupiter's cap at 64 × 24 segments, without its sheets of cloud, its cloud tops without their grain and the pop-up clouds' shadows, 32 wisps, one layer of the auroras; Saturn's, Jupiter's and Earth's maps at 1024 instead of 2048 wide, the real sky's Milky Way at 512 instead of 1024 texels a face; the Corona's eight tallest prominences, its sun's face without its finest grain | |
| 10 | No shadows | |
| 11 | 1 device pixel per CSS pixel, every rock of the field and a comet's nucleus keep only their far mesh, a quarter of the belt, the pad drawn every second frame, the reference plane's grid only its rings; the lens down to 2 steps | **low** |
| 12 | No bloom | |
| 13 | 0.75 device pixels per CSS pixel, a third of the particles and grains of the ring and of the Belt's sparks, drill dust and dust clouds, no lacquer on the hulls (the second specular layer and the battleship's film of colors) | |

The HUD's attitude display (horizon, pitch ladder, bank scale, heading tape) is a canvas over the whole screen: it follows the picture's resolution down the ladder (never below 1 pixel per CSS pixel, never above 2) and is drawn anew only when the camera turns or its lens changes. The HUD, the radar and the attitude are written once a frame, after the frame's steps of the simulation, however many a slow frame runs.

**Tiers and the governor.** The menu's row under the languages offers Auto, Low, Medium and High. A tier pins its rung. Auto starts where the device was last found smooth, or with a guess: a software renderer starts on the Low tier's own rung (11); a phone or tablet starts lower yet, on the ladder's very last rung (13), since a fight must never stutter and a touch screen's GPU is the one most likely to struggle; a small machine (four cores or fewer, 4 GB or less) or an integrated Intel GPU starts on Medium, everything else on High. Then the governor watches the frame rate in the menu and in a running round, each on a rung of its own: the title screen's pad costs what a fight does not, so the menu never decides the round's rung, nor the rungs barred in it. Below 50 frames a second over 2 seconds (1 second on a touch device) it takes a rung down; at 58 or more for 8 seconds (14 seconds touched), with the frame's own work under 60 % of the frame, it takes one up — a touch device steps down faster and climbs back up more cautiously than that. After every change it waits 3 seconds. A rung left for being slow is barred for 30 seconds, then twice as long after each further time, so the picture never seesaws. A screen that refreshes slower than that (a phone's battery saver holds it at 30 Hz) is slow on every rung: after two steps down to a lower resolution (rungs that only thin rocks gain nothing on a GPU held back by its fill rate, so they are no evidence) that raised the frame rate by less than 5 % while the frame's own work stayed under 60 % of the frame, the governor takes the cap for the screen's, goes back to the rung before them and holds it, until the frame rate reaches 50 again (the cap is lifted) or falls below 90 % of the cap (the device itself is slow again). A rung of a running round held for 20 seconds, never under a cap, is kept in the browser as the start next time (`solartournament.quality`, the same entry as the pinned tier). Shadows on or off and the lacquer compile shaders anew; the governor changes them only where the hitch goes unseen (in the menu, on the results, while the own clone is dead), the other knobs at once.

**The benchmark.** `?bench` on the game's address (`?bench=<seconds>` per measurement, default 3; the test stage works too, https://test.solartournament.games/?bench) plays a fixed scene: a solo deathmatch of the default rivals in the belt, the Host's clone watching, the camera on a fixed arc around the field. It measures the frame time with everything on, then with each feature off on its own (the occasional effects, glitch and datamosh, on), against the mean of everything on just before and after: post-processing as a whole, bloom, resolution, shadows, lacquer, rock detail, the field, the belt, the landmark, the sky, the dust, particles, ships, wreckage, the DOM HUD, the radar, the attitude display, the motion streaks, sound. Then every rung of the ladder, every arena with and without its landmark (the black hole with and without its lens), the race with and without the racing line, Capture the Flag and the President's Fleet, and the title screen with and without its pad. It takes about ten minutes. The table shows on screen at the end, with buttons to copy or download it as JSON, and goes to the console; the frame time is the time between two frames shown (never below the screen's refresh), next to it the frame's own work and, where the browser can time the GPU (`EXT_disjoint_timer_query_webgl2`), the GPU's time, draw calls and triangles. `?fps` shows the frame rate, the frame's own work and the rung at the bottom of the screen, to watch the governor at work.

On a phone: open the address with `?bench` (the test stage, or the dev server over the LAN), lay the phone sideways, wait until the table shows, and press "Copy JSON".

`npm run bench` runs the same in the test browser (`e2e/bench.spec.ts`); `PROFILE=pixel8` pretends to be the reference phone, a Pixel 8 held sideways with its CPU slowed to the phone's browser speed (see `e2e/README.md`); `BENCH_ONLY=quality` (or `menu`, `features`, `arenas`, `modes`, comma-separated; `&only=` on the address) runs only those sections. A feature's cost is counted in GPU time where the browser times the GPU, since the frame time snaps to the screen's refresh; the title screen's pad draws in a context of its own and is counted in frame time.

**Measured triangles.** `BENCH_ONLY=quality` under the Pixel 8 profile, in the belt arena (`npm run bench`), triangles submitted per frame and GPU time at each rung (quiet machine, an Apple M4):

| Rung | Triangles | GPU ms |
|---|---|---|
| 0 high | 547 089 | 200.1 |
| 1 | 502 459 | 163.9 |
| 2 | 484 180 | 158.0 |
| 3 | 484 219 | 139.8 |
| 4 | 483 947 | 116.8 |
| 5 | 483 941 | 107.1 |
| 6 medium | 470 804 | 87.2 |
| 7 | 470 722 | 72.9 |
| 8 | 470 621 | 62.4 |
| 9 | 276 232 | 51.6 |
| 10 | 264 175 | 47.5 |
| 11 low | 153 595 | 33.1 |
| 12 | 153 572 | 30.4 |
| 13 | 153 591 | 25.7 |

**Measured costs of the other features.** One run each in the test browser on an Apple M4, the machine otherwise quiet, 5 seconds per measurement. The test browser draws without a GPU (swiftshader), so the milliseconds are its own and only the costs next to each other mean something: what a feature costs here is no promise of what it costs on a phone's GPU. "Desktop" is 1280 × 720 at 1 device pixel per CSS pixel, "Pixel 8" the phone's screen held sideways (915 × 412 at 2.625, 2.6 million pixels) with its CPU speed. Cost per frame of each feature switched off (glitch and datamosh switched on), in GPU time, as a share of the frame with everything on:

| Feature | Desktop | Pixel 8 |
|---|---|---|
| Shadows | 17 % | 18 % |
| Half the resolution | 39 % | 41 % |
| Post-processing as a whole | 37 % | 34 % |
| Bloom | 32 % | 32 % |
| Datamosh (while on) | 22 % | 14 % |
| Glitch (while on) | 7 % | 2 % |
| DOM HUD, radar, attitude, sound, particles, dust, sky, ships, wreckage, lacquer, landmark of the belt | each within ±3 % | each within ±8 % |
| The title screen's pad (frame time, in the menu) | 44 % | 38 % |
| Landmark of Jupiter (its map's cap; its sheets of cloud and haze not measured) / the rings / the black hole | 47–61 % / 10–18 % / 12–17 % | 42–58 % / 4–14 % / 20–26 % |
| The black hole's lens | 17–37 % | 20–28 % |

The landmarks' and the lens's rows come from two runs of the arenas section alone (`BENCH_ONLY=arenas`, 3 seconds per measurement), as the range between them: single measurements of one scene scatter that far in the test browser. A bench round runs 12 seconds before its first measurement, since the frame's GPU time takes some ten seconds after an arena is loaded to come down to where it stays. The lens is a full-screen pass whose cost goes with the picture's resolution: measured on, off and on again at every rung (twice each), in the test browser at 1 device pixel per CSS pixel it costs some 25 ms (about 30 % of the frame) on every rung from 0 to 12 alike, whatever steps it marches, and some 15 ms on rung 13 at three quarters of that resolution. The disk's gas it adds in front of a rock inside the disk's slab, a faint veil, is summed coarser and without the gas's clumps, whose noise would not show through it and would cost the most of the lens there; the gas across the shadow takes only the coarser clumps (see `src/view/lensing.ts`).

On the desktop the ladder's own resolution rungs (3, 6, 8, 11, 13) change little: at 1 device pixel per CSS pixel, the picture is already drawn at its lowest; the "Half the resolution" row above forces a cut below that floor to measure the full-screen passes' cost per pixel on their own.

## Search engines and link previews

Every page a search can land on (the game, `about.html`, the handbook, the radio) has one address per language, its texts in place in the file itself: `/`, `/about`, `/handbook`, `/radio` in English, `/de`, `/about-de`, `/handbook-de`, `/radio-de` in German, the same with `es` and `fr`. Opening one of them chooses its language; the English addresses still follow a browser's own language, so links already out there go on working as they did. The build writes all of it (`tools/seo/`): canonical address, siblings, link preview, the home page's structured data, the sitemap. Only production is found: the test stage and the raw Pages addresses answer `X-Robots-Tag: noindex`, and the German legal pages carry `noindex`.

## Deploy

The game is a static site on [Cloudflare Pages](https://pages.cloudflare.com/), project `solartournament` (`wrangler.toml`). Two stages, one branch each:

| Branch | Stage | Address |
|---|---|---|
| `stage_prod` | production | https://solartournament.games |
| `stage_test` | test | https://test.solartournament.games |

Development happens on `main`, and nothing is pushed to the two stages by hand: every push to `main` runs the workflow CI (`.github/workflows/ci.yml`), which renders the voice sprites, checks types and duplicates, runs the unit tests and the site's build, and only then moves the commit on to `stage_test` and, as long as the game has no players, to `stage_prod`; once it has, `stage_prod` moves only when Johannes asks. Cloudflare builds only those two branches (Git integration: build command `npm run build`, output directory `dist`, Node from `.node-version`), and its build takes the sprites the workflow rendered for the commit from the release `voice-sprites` (`tools/voice/prebuilt.ts`, each file checked against the SHA-256 the commit's pointer names) instead of rendering them: rendering every language anew, after the spoken lines changed, takes longer than a Cloudflare build may run. Its build is the voices, the types and Vite; no Cloudflare token lives in GitHub. `test.solartournament.games` points to the branch alias `stage-test.solartournament.pages.dev`; `www.solartournament.games` and `solar-tournament.games` (with `www.`) redirect permanently to `solartournament.games`, path and query included.

The project is a classic Pages project, not a Worker: the game is a static site, and its only server code is a Pages Function of the same project, `functions/turn.ts` (`GET /turn`), which hands out short-lived TURN credentials (see below), never to a request the browser marks as coming from another site (`Sec-Fetch-Site` other than `same-origin`; older browsers that send no such mark are answered, and a zone rate limit keeps scripts from collecting credentials in bulk).

Every page goes out with a Content Security Policy (`dist/_headers`, written by the build from `vite.config.ts`): scripts and styles of the site itself only (its one inline script, the redirect from the Pages addresses, by its hash; `blob:` for the audio worklets of the radio), connections to the site and to the Nostr relays by name (the checked list and Trystero's built-in ones; the build fails when the header line grows past the 2,000 characters Cloudflare Pages takes), no frames around the game, no plugins; together with `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff` and `Referrer-Policy: no-referrer`.

## The title screen

Under the callsign and the tint, the menu stands on two legs. **The story** holds the solo campaign, **Lot 7731**: the road of one nobody from a scrapyard in the Belt to the President's Games, flown alone (its design in `docs/campaign/`). It is shown as in the making and takes no click until its first chapter is built; on the address with `?campaign` it can be flown as far as it is built (`src/campaign/`): the prologue and chapter 1, on the flight school's drills. Nicole, a salvaged flight computer short of memory, speaks whole sentences and now and then crashes mid-sentence (NAVIGATION UNIT · MEMORY FAULT · REBOOTING: the picture torn, her voice cut off, a falling tone), boots with her greeting and has forgotten what came before. The yardmaster Rask calls in over the comm, in a voice of his own through a narrow radio band, his bust a hologram in amber at the left of the screen while his line stands; so do Pim, the yard's welder (pink, choppy hair and goggles, a girl's quick voice), and Hedda Kroll, the captain of the hauler Patience (teal, high collar and a scar, a low, worn voice). Cold Boot (Nicole boots, crashes and boots again; Rask explains the debt; out of rack 7731, down the lane to the depot, braking below 10 m/s 400 m short of the hall's door, in and down onto bay 3's pad: within its 15 m, slower than 2 m/s for a second, and the clamps take the hull; Pim, the yard's welder, a girl of fifteen, comes on and welds the thrusters up to 70 %, free); every mission after it begins on that pad, briefed by Rask, and runs on into the next without the menu; Lamplighter (six dead beacons on masts at the ends of the rows, each held by within 40 m for 3 s until its lamp flickers into life and lights, before the shift ends after 6 minutes; ☉ 150); Live Target (tourists' training lasers from three gunners around the yard, fired at where the ship was 0.4 s before, 90 seconds lived through inside 1.5 km of the yard's middle, 3 s given to fly back in after leaving it; ☉ 300, less ☉ 10 for every hit taken, before the yard's cut); chapter 1's jobs are flown with the autopilot, which holds the ship against Psyche's faint pull; Canister Sweep (four canisters drifting between the rows, each flown through to take it in tow, a drum of depot plating hanging 15 m behind and 4 m below the stern on a cable and swinging out in the turns, and brought to the crusher's hopper behind the hall; ☉ 200); The Cutter (the yard fits a mining cutter and puts its ☉ 1,200 on the debt: a laser of 400 m reach putting 35 % heat a shot into the radiators); First Cut (Rask hands over eight of the Rockborn's mining charges, the cutter only scorching rock: three rocks of the yard's spoil broken with them (see Mining charges), each shedding seven glints of ore, gold specks drifting away at 3 to 8 m/s and coming to rest on top of the ground they meet, and twenty glints flown through, each within 25 m; the marker leads to the nearest glint, else to the nearest rock; ☉ 400; after it Pim paints the hull's name on its fin: the Magpie, for everything on it is nicked); Docking Lane (the hauler Patience moored 260 m over the yard, 700 m from bow to stern: ore bins on a spine, one of them gone, a ring habitat and the bridge at the bow, the workshop with the clamp under the spine, the drive and three bells at the stern, in the yards' patched plating, her name on her flank; her hull solid as a battleship's; six loose hulls drifting at her at 40 m/s, each shot before it strikes her, three strikes failing it; ☉ 600), after which the clone climbs out of the cockpit for the first scene in person: the lift up to Rask's office in the glazed cab on the tower (his desk by the glass over the yard, the lamp over it, a cigar burning in the ashtray, the crane's console, his neon sign), Rask behind the desk and Captain Kroll beside it in the flesh, a few meters off, both facing the clone; the camera is the clone's eyes, the stick turns its head (about 100° to either side, 50° up and down), nothing of the HUD but the panel of what is said shows, and their lines come from the room instead of the comm. Rask hands the Host on and shakes the clone's hand, wet, as Nicole says, the touch jolting through the link (what the clone feels reaches the Host), and Captain Kroll takes the Host's clone aboard. The people in person are figures made with MakeHuman (the same as their holograms' busts), posed and textured. The menu's button names the next mission, the wallet, the debt and the rank, and opens the campaign's job board: the place, the wallet, the debt and the rank, the next job with what it pays (Enter takes it, from the drill the Host left it at), the jobs done, each to be flown again from its start for nothing and without moving the story on (afterwards the board again), each job that pays with its medal (★): flown clean, from its first drill to its last without a clone lost, the first time or flown again, it pays a bonus of a quarter of its pay once, less the yard's cut while the debt is unpaid (a trial's round has none); the courier runs (one at each of 17 places once its first job is done: six coils in order from the place's start, laid clear of its ground, the limit by the course's length at 140 m/s, ☉ 800 and 60 ratings the first time) are side trips too, and so are the mining runs at the six of them where loose rock or ice drifts near the start (rock broken with twelve mining charges sheds its ore, twelve glints flown through within 90 s of the first break win it, ☉ 900 and 60 ratings the first time) and the ambushes at the same places (three bandits of skill 0.45 round the courier course's first beacon, all three down wins, ☉ 1,000 and 80 ratings the first time); under Rask's counter the bounties (three wanted faces at each of those places, one after the other, their people's standing opening the second and third: the face and one to three escorts round the courier course's third beacon, the face of skill 0.45, 0.55 and 0.65, the last with the hook and the nanobots, the face down wins, ☉ 1,500, 2,500 and 4,000) and the smuggling runs (once the place's people count the Host as Known: cargo to the courier course's last beacon past two of the Ministry's patrol cutters circling 400 m round points 1.6 km beside the lane, seen within 2 km while the ship runs hot, so it coasts past them silent, and seen, it starts again from the lane's start at rest; held within 300 m of the drop slower than 10 m/s for 3 s within 4 minutes, ☉ 1,400); every side job pays a fifth of its pay on every flight again, less the yard's cut, and earns standing with the people of its place (3 the first time, 1 again, a bounty 4; Known from 6, Trusted from 15, Kin from 30: the peoples' own parts at Rask's counter); a time trial has a gold time besides (★), between what a middling pilot and a sharp one fly, paying the same bonus once when its last coil is flown within it; each job that pays has a master's run besides (★), clean, without one hit on the hull and, with a gold time, within it, paying the same bonus once; Rask's counter sells three parts from the racks at a time, turning after every job flown (a gain bought with a loss each, ☉ 700 to 1,800, one to a slot: a coil, the drive, the thrusters, the plating, the radiators, a torpedo rack; paid from the wallet, never on the debt); every clone the campaign loses but the Pit's is printed for 40 ☉ from the wallet (what it lacks on the debt while it is unpaid and not held, else on the house), or for 10 ☉ in PrintMe's back room, whose clones boot with their link glitching for 3 s and their hands trembling for 8 s; and every clone lost counts towards a scar of what killed it, deepening at 3, 10 and 25 deaths of a kind, from which the campaign's clones fly better: Stone Memory (rock, wreckage, rams: the impact warning 0.5 s earlier a tier), Flinch (beams: every hit 3 % softer), Burn Scar (air, flare, plumes: the radiators 4 % cooler), Blast Nerves (torpedoes, blasts, novas, comets: the thrusters 3 % harder), Undertow (a well: the drive 3 % harder); the board lists the scars and the standing, and what the hull is fitted with and which of Nicole's modules are in; once her own voice is back (Voice II, chapter 10) a button chooses it clean or through the old voice board's crackle, a look only; while it is open, the title screen's pad is Pim's, Lot 7731 standing on it as far as it is fitted. A mission done says what it paid and what the yard took. All of it is flown in the Boneyard on Psyche, the campaign's first place (never in a tournament's order of arenas): on the flat floor of a crater on Psyche, a metal world 226 km across, whose gravity (0.144 m/s², about 0.015 g) holds the yard down and lets a ship that does nothing sink slowly towards the floor, the crater's wall 400 m high some 5 km off, the sun low over it (about 8°), so every boulder and rim casts a long shadow and the floor stays dim, as the mood of the place: eight rows of racks of dead fighters standing on the ground, 240 m long and 45 m high each, three tiers of fighters on their cradles, floodlight masts 80 m high along the lane open through the middle, and at the lane's far end the depot (built as a station is in space, for the pull is next to nothing: round modules moored to the ground by cables and anchors; the hangar a vault, half a cylinder 70 m in radius, with bay 3 inside, 80 m deep, 90 m wide and 45 m high, its door towards the lane and the round landing pad on its floor; two habitation cylinders on their saddles with the couches where the Hosts in debt lie; Rask's office tower, a column 120 m high with the round, glazed cab of his office on top and a crane's boom over the yard; the printer hall a dome; the crusher with its hopper, three tanks, a dish; all of it solid, the ground too, and all of it patched, rusted and left half broken); the yard's spoil of boulders lies at its edge; the clone is printed in the lane beside the last rack. Chapter 2 is flown at the campaign's second place, Rheasilvia on Vesta, its six missions: Rheasilvia (Patience takes the launch ring to Vesta, and the clone in the hull's cockpit gets the gel like every body aboard: the Host has 20 seconds to choose, the expired gel, 6 seconds of agony over the link, or the self-destruct's button held for a second and a half, the clone dead and the next printed by Patience's printer after the boost, ☉ 2,000 more on the debt; left alone it is the gel; Patience then holds 2 km over the claims; the clone climbs from the hull in her workshop up to her galley, the second scene in person: a cramped room of patched plating with its long table and benches, the counter with a hot plate and dented pots, lockers, pipes under the ceiling, a lamp over the table and a porthole on Vesta below, Pim at the counter and Captain Kroll at the head of the table in the flesh ("Don't count them. I stopped at eleven hundred."), then climbs back into the cockpit; Captain Kroll names the work, Pim fits Nicole a second-hand voice board, and from then on Nicole speaks every sentence to its end, without crashing) and Claim Survey (the buoys of the five claims filed first, flown past in the order they were filed, within 100 m of each, before the survey window shuts after 8 minutes; ☉ 400 and 20 ratings) and Cut and Haul (the claim without a rig nearest to a working rig cracked with 24 mining charges, each break of it shedding eleven glints of ore wherever its pieces drift (its smallest pieces crumble before long), and forty of them flown through, at ☉ 35 a load, ☉ 1,400 and 25 ratings; every beam that ends on a rig or a buoy instead of on rock takes ☉ 50 off the pay; it cannot fail) and Saw Brothers (three claim-jumpers of skill 0.15 sawing at the rig nearest the place's middle: each holds 400 m off it and fires at it until a ship comes within 800 m of it, then fights; their beams wear the rig down, never its rock, and at 30 of their hits it falls and the attempt starts over; two of them shot down and the third runs; ☉ 1,200 and 80 ratings; the brothers call in together, in one giggling voice, their bust a claim-jumper in a leather jacket and a fedora cocked, in toxic lime) and The Peak Run (the Rockborn kids' race through six sections of an old ore line's pipe, 140 m across inside and 160 m long, rusted, flanged and ribbed, never lit, standing on two legs on the flank, solid; they hang on an ellipse 3.2 by 2.4 km round the claims, 300 m over the flank, flown through their bore either way (a pipe throws nobody on: only a ring's coil does), two laps against three rivals of skill 0.2, Nix, Bean and Spark, who race the racing line through them; home in the first two wins ☉ 800, 60 ratings and Pim's thruster parts, which bring the thrusters to a stock ship's and with them the dodge; home later, and the race is flown again) and Practice Log (something on an old frequency from the face of a cut rock: the ship held still before the mouth of its lit tunnel, within 40 m and slower than 2 m/s for 3 seconds, and Nicole hears the first echo, a navigator's log in a voice like her own; 20 ratings). The place: the Rockborn's claims high on the flank of the central peak of the basin Rheasilvia, a basin some 500 km across on a world of 525, the peak 22 km over its floor and some 100 km in radius at its foot (a raised cosine); the claims lie 21 km from the summit and some 20 km over the floor, where the flank leans about 13° (it rises by 0.235 for every unit across), so over the field's width it climbs some 1.2 km towards the summit; twenty-two boulders some 130 to 350 m across lie on it, each bedded in the flank's dust where it fell, sunk a share of its height into it (12 to 30 %, each its own: `FieldLayout.rest`), the Rockborn's rigs, buoys and slogans on them as in the Belt; the flank under them is solid, a box leaning with it 12 km across; beyond it the flank falls away to the basin's floor, which curves down far less than Vesta itself (as a sphere of 900 km would), to its rim 250 km from the summit and 6 km high, and Vesta's own curve beyond (263 km in radius); hills up to a kilometer high over all of it; Vesta pulls with 0.25 m/s², about a fortieth of a g, too weak for a ship to feel; the sun, 2.4 AU off, stands low over the flank; the ground near the claims is the crater floor's photograph tinted to Vesta's pale basalt (Vesta is among the brightest bodies of the Belt), the land beyond in broad patches of basalt and darker eucrite with a pale streak of fresh dust down the flank, and the HUD marks the summit. From near the ground the flank's own convex crest hides the basin, as on any mountain; climbing a kilometer or two brings it and the rim into view. Chapter 3 is flown at Occator on Ceres, as far as it is built: Occator (Patience moors over the crater and Captain Kroll names the place; down to the market terrace and before the mouth of the market's gallery, held within 60 m of its dock, 80 m before the mouth, slower than 2 m/s for 2 s) and Surplus (Pim fits a Navy surplus laser, the Games' own, for ☉ 6,000: what the wallet holds pays for it and the rest goes on the debt; Nicole switches on her targeting module with it, the crosshair turning orange over a hull and the ships marked and named; Rask calls to say he has sold the debt to the Tallymen) and Ore Barge (a Rockborn ore barge, 240 m long, flies its lane out of the crater, 3.3 km at 20 m/s from before the market over the terrace and up; 25 s after it sets out three scrappers of skill 0.2 come, and once they are down three of skill 0.3, each holding 500 m off the barge and firing at its hull until a ship comes within 800 m of it, then fighting it; the barge at its lane's end with both waves down wins ☉ 2,000 and 120 ratings, 300 hits on its hull fail it; the barge is solid, and stops beams, torpedoes, wreckage and probes as a hull does) and Repo Men (Ma Tally of the Tallymen, who hold the debt now, calls in, her bust in a bank sign's violet; their tug lies 1.5 km off over the crater, and 4 s in its harpoon bites the hull and its winch reels it in at 12 m/s, the cable as long as the way was and shortening, the hull held on it whatever its drive does; four guards of skill 0.3 fight round the tug; twelve beams on the winch snap the cable, or three guards shot down make them give up, ☉ 1,500 and 150 ratings; reeled within 100 m of the winch, the hull is theirs and the attempt starts over) and Wall Run (Hedda's barge of forty Rockborn refugees follows the hull's way 400 m behind it at up to 25 m/s over the Solar Wall, the finished stretch of the President's wall on the lane from the Belt to Mars: a fence of girders hung with mesh across the lane, 20 m thick, its top edge 200 m over the field's middle, reaching on beyond the arena below and along it, so the only way across is over it; fifteen searchlights on its top edge, 400 m apart, each a cone 14° wide on either side reaching 3 km, sweeping across the lane by up to 76° over 24 s and along the wall at a pace of its own, looking up and out, never down at their own feet; a light that holds the barge, bow, middle or stern, for 3 s sees it, the time adding up while it is lit and fading at half that pace while it is not; seen twice fails it, 1 km beyond the wall it is through, ☉ 1,000 and 200 ratings; a barge led within some 50 m over the edge mostly stays under the lights, one led high over it is seen) and Scout Two (in Patience's workshop Pim grinds the depot's grey off the hull, and the scout's old livery shows on both flanks, an off-white band chipped to the metal over a dark blue stripe, the five stars of the united Earth's emblem, PAAA 01 and S2 in letters built from bars; Nicole knows the name: they are Scout Two; then, held before the wall's second step under the neon NO WALL IN SPACE, she hears the second echo of her past; 20 ratings; the hull wears the livery from then on) and Overhaul (Pim replaces the drive's bell, cracked since the Boneyard, with one of Rockborn make for ☉ 1,600, paid as the laser is: the main drive pushes as a stock ship's). From Occator on, the job board offers **the Gullet** besides the jobs, as often as the Host likes and for nothing: Kesh's bar in the market's gallery, a scene in person, where Kesh stands behind her counter, greets a stranger once ("Welcome to the Gullet, Host. Rask's lot, are you? Sit. The first drink's on the house. Nothing after it is.") and a regular from then on, and sees the clone off when the Host leaves by the bar's plate; on the plate, a bet in her book on a job done with a gold time (☉ 100, 250, 500 or 1,000, one at a time, paid back twice when the job is flown again within its gold time, kept by the book when it is not or another job is taken), a rumor for a drink (☉ 60, eight of them in order, each opening a smuggling run or bounty before its people would trust the Host with it), and the Pit downstairs: waves over Occator's crater floor, wave n of n fighters (at most five) of skill 0.3 + 0.06 n (at most 0.85), all against the Host, until the one clone of the night dies, ☉ 150 cash a wave cleared without the yard's cut, its record kept. Chapter 4 is flown at Stickney on Phobos, as far as it is built: the Stickney Grand (Nicole installs her racing line module; eight rings of the Games on an ellipse 4.4 by 3.2 km that dives from over the crater's rim down into it and out again, each 300 m over the ground beneath it, three laps against Perihelion, skill 0.65, who has never died, and four others of skill 0.3 to 0.35, lined up before the first ring; home among the first three wins ☉ 2,500 and 250 ratings, later the race is flown again) and Debt Day (Ma Tally reads out the statement of account, and six of her collectors of skill 0.35 come for the hull over the grooves beyond the rim, fighting every ship they see; four shot down, and the Navy cutter Restraint hails, its officer's bust in the pale steel blue of the Navy: the debt is bought by a party who wishes to remain anonymous, the Tallymen leave, ☉ 2,000 and 200 ratings; from then on the benefactor holds the debt: nobody takes a cut, Debt Day's own pay included, and the debt stands as it is, the job board saying so) and Groove Shoot (a camera drone of Lanes Watch films the hull low in a groove of the land beyond the rim: under 60 m over the ground and faster than 20 m/s for 30 s without a break, ☉ 1,200 and 150 ratings) and Armor Panels (Pim welds Navy surplus panels over the bare left flank for ☉ 1,500, paid as the laser is: a beam from that side counts as from any other). The place: the field hangs over the inner wall of Stickney, Phobos's great crater, 9 km across and 1.2 km deep, its raised rim 150 m over the land beyond, which runs on in long parallel grooves 25 m deep and 300 m apart; Phobos is small (11 km in radius), so its ground curves away fast, some 4.5 km down at 10 km out; the ground collides as the relief it is, hills, hollows and grooves (a box piece whose top is a ground, see the field; its sides solid up to the ground at its edge, for a ship flying low past it); Phobos pulls with 0.0057 m/s², nothing a ship feels; Mars stands still in its sky some 35° up, 42° across, from the USGS Viking color mosaic; the HUD marks Stickney's floor and Mars. Chapter 5 is flown at Valhalla on Callisto, as far as it is built: Pilot Episode (Captain Kroll names the place and Lanes Watch's first show, on air: its moments pay twice their ratings from style, which every job pays, 5 for each step of a moment's rank, with its ratings the first time it is done; Nicole installs her radar module, and the radar sphere shows from then on; the Tallyman Red Ledger and his two wingmen, Carbon and Copy, all of skill 0.4, fight every ship they see over the craters; Red Ledger shot down ends it, ☉ 3,000 and 300 ratings) and Ice Run (three ice tugs for Ganymede, each a short spine with two hoppers heaped with Callisto's ice, solid, fly 400 m apart in a line across the field at 30 m/s, 150 m over the highest ground under their lane; 15 s in, four of the Loose of skill 0.45 come for the frontmost tug still free, holding 250 m off it; they never fire beams at a tug, only harpoons, one each, and they fight a ship that comes near, though never into the Magpie's drive (a raider holds his fire while it faces him); a harpooned tug hangs on the cable and is dragged off its lane at 15 m/s, towards its raider's side, while that raider lives, and glides back once it is shot down; 800 m off its lane it is lost; the convoy at its lane's end with at most one tug lost wins ☉ 2,500 and 200 ratings, a second lost starts it over) and Valhalla Sprint (six coils of the Ministry, leased by Lanes Watch for the show, in a straight line over the ridges, 1 km apart and level 100 m over the highest ground beneath them; from rest 1 km before the first, the clock starts at the first coil and the last within 26 s wins, each core throwing the ship on as a race's coils do; a coil missed or the clock run out starts it over; its gold time 22 s; ☉ 1,500 and 150 ratings) and Callisto Echo (the old frequency from the Navy's listening post among the craters, dead for longer than anyone remembers: a bunker of olive plating dug into the ground under a curved roof, a lattice tower 65 m high with a dish 40 m across turned up at the sky, two aerial masts, all of it solid; the hull held still some 65 m over the dish, as at the other echoes, and Nicole hears the third echo; 30 ratings) and Radiators I (Pim fits radiators of the first grade from a tug yard for ☉ 1,000, paid as the laser is: they shed heat 15 % of a stock ship's faster, as the hangar's first step does, the missing fin's quarter still short). The place: the field lies in the ring zone of Valhalla, the great basin on Callisto, Jupiter's outermost large moon and the most cratered ground in the system; the basin's middle lies far off, so its rings cross the field as long ridges one after the other, 150 m high and 1.5 km apart, each rising gently away from the basin and dropping off in a scarp (the real rings lie farther apart: here they are packed closer, so that a sprint over the field crosses several); some 260 craters from 40 to 700 m in radius lie over the field, their bowls about a third of their radius deep and their rims raised, four in ten of them old and flattened to about a third of that; Callisto's radius of 2,410 km curves its ground away by some 20 m at 10 km; the ground collides as the relief it is; Callisto pulls with 1.24 m/s², an eighth of a g, which a ship feels and its drive easily beats; Jupiter stands still in its sky some 34° up and 4.4° across, eight times as wide as the full moon from Earth, from the same Cassini map as its arena's; the HUD marks the way to Valhalla's middle and Jupiter. Chapter 6 is flown at the Lineae on Europa, as far as it is built: Dose (Captain Kroll names the place and the job; Nicole installs her threat warnings, and ON YOUR TAIL, TRACKED, LOCKED and the torpedo's countdown show from then on; an ice tanker climbs along the Crack 500 m below the plain at 25 m/s, out of it and away over the ice into Jupiter's radiation belt; the clone takes a dose wherever it flies over the ice, none in the Crack, whose ice shields it, more the higher it flies, the full rate from 300 m over the plain on, where 150 s fill the dose; at three quarters Nicole warns, and a full dose is more than a clone can take: the attempt starts over; once the tanker is out of the Crack three of the Loose come for it with their harpoons, as in the Ice Run, and the tanker dragged 800 m off its lane is lost; the tanker at its lane's end wins ☉ 3,500 and 250 ratings) and Under the Lineae (Captain Kroll names the place; down into the Crack to the science station on its floor, the hull held still over its dish as at the other echoes, and Nicole hears the fourth echo, her own name: Nicole Aster, navigator of the PAAA 01; ☉ 1,000 and 100 ratings) and The Quiet Man (Lanes Watch's bounty on a raider of the Loose of skill 0.5 who never finishes a clone: he fires only into a drive, holding his fire while the armor is on his nose, and a hull he has worn down to 30 of its 100 only scorches under his beam; 40 s into the fight, or once he has worn the hull down that far, he turns away and flies off to the arena's far side, and the Host chooses: shot down, he pays ☉ 2,500 and 250 ratings; let go, he is gone within 300 m of where he flies, for ☉ 2,500 and 100 ratings, and the campaign remembers that he was let go) and Plating I (Pim trades the science outfit a weld job for plating of the first grade and pays ☉ 1,500 on top, as the laser is paid: every hit hurts 7 % of it less, as the hangar's first step does). The place: a stretch of Europa's ice shell over its ocean; through the field runs the Crack, a fissure 200 m wide and some 800 m deep with nearly sheer walls, meandering 300 m to either side over 4.5 km, its lips raised into a ridge 80 m high on either side as Europa's double ridges are, rubble up to 20 m on its floor; older double ridges, pairs 50 m high and 600 m apart, cross the plain at angles of their own; the ground is drawn on a grid of 25 m, fine enough for the Crack's walls, and collides as the relief it is; the science station stands on the Crack's floor, built as Callisto's listening post in an outfit's white, solid; Europa's radius of 1,561 km curves its ground away by some 30 m at 10 km; it pulls with 1.31 m/s², an eighth of a g; Jupiter stands still in its sky some 30° up and 12° across, from its Cassini map; the HUD marks the Crack and Jupiter. Chapter 7 is flown at Flux on Io, as far as it is built: the Flux Tube Race (Captain Kroll names the place; the sulfur miners' race, eight pairs of their beacon buoys (the opening between them a ring's) on an ellipse 4.4 by 3.2 km over the plain, each 300 m over the ground beneath it, one side of it running past the flux tube, two laps against Perihelion, skill 0.65, and four others of skill 0.3 to 0.4; near the tube the link crackles, the picture breaking up as on a hit, the stronger the nearer; home among the first two wins ☉ 4,000 and 350 ratings, later the race is flown again) and Tube Fitting (Pim buys a torpedo tube off the sulfur miners for ☉ 2,000, paid as the laser is: one torpedo each time a clone wakes, where a stock ship carries two) and Main Drive I (the miners tune the drive's bell for ☉ 1,000, paid as the laser is: the main drive pushes 5 % of a stock ship's harder, as the hangar's first step does) and Blackout (a sulfur freighter of the miners, some 260 m long with three hoppers heaped with sulfur, solid, flies a straight lane through the flux tube at 20 m/s, the hull escorting it, the link crackling the nearer the tube; where the lane comes nearest the tube's axis the link cuts: the picture white with LINK LOST on it, the voice gone mid-word, none of a death's rot, for 5 s; then a new clone wakes on Patience's printer, the freighter made it out alone, and Patience's log tells the Magpie recovered adrift, canopy open, cockpit empty, Nicole's logs blank for two hours; it cannot fail; ☉ 2,000 and 150 ratings). The place: a stretch of Io's sulfur plains, swelling 20 m over 1.5 km; on one side a patera, a volcano's pit 1 km in radius and 150 m deep with a level floor, a lava lake on it, a crust of dark plates glowing orange through its cracks; on the other a mesa 300 m high, its top 700 m in radius; between them the flux tube, the current between Io and Jupiter, a column of faint violet light 300 m in radius up into the sky, the link crackling from 1.5 km off its axis and full at its glow; the plains beyond in patches of sulfur yellow, orange and white with black tongues of fresh lava; Io's radius of 1,822 km curves its ground away by some 27 m at 10 km; it pulls with 1.80 m/s², a fifth of a g; Jupiter stands still in its sky some 45° up and 19° across, from its Cassini map; the HUD marks the flux tube and Jupiter. Chapter 8 is flown at the Tug Yards over Ganymede: Hollow Jack (Captain Kroll names the place and Lanes Watch's bounty on the chief of the Loose; Jack calls in, a stray with no Host at the other end, his bust in the rust of an old hull, a voice of his own; Jack, skill 0.7, with a Games bot's gear (the hook, which he fires at the ship he hunts, and the nanobots), and three of his raiders of skill 0.45 fight every ship they see among the ice, the announcer calling the bounty ("lanes watch. tonight a nobody hunts a bandit. place your bets"); Jack shot down ends it, his last words cut off, Nicole knowing he had no link and is gone for good; ☉ 6,000 and 600 ratings, which lift the Host to the rank Sponsored) and The Harpoon (the piece of Jack's wreck that holds his harpoon, a torn plate with the harpoon sticking out of it, drifts out of the yard at 15 m/s; flown through within 50 m before it is past 95 % of the arena's reach, it is the Magpie's: Pim puts it on a tug's winch for ☉ 1,500, paid as the laser is, and the hull carries the hook from then on) and Swing Shift (the tug crews' drill, the flight school's hook drill three times among the yard's blocks of ice: hook a block, swing round it, let go faster than 65 m/s; ☉ 1,000 and 100 ratings) and Sponsor Night (Bellamy Crisp, Director of Excitement at Victory Cola, a voice of his own, comes aboard Patience: the clone climbs up to the galley, where he stands in the flesh at the counter, suited head to toe in his cola's red, Hedda at the table, and signs the Host). The place: the yard's blocks of ice, 28 of them, hauled in by the tugs and parked in a layer 400 m thick about the field's middle, float free over Ganymede's ground 600 m below; the ground is two kinds, bright terrain in bands of long parallel grooves 80 m deep and 500 m apart, and dark terrain, old and smooth, swelling 30 m over 2 km; Ganymede's radius of 2,634 km curves its ground away by some 19 m at 10 km; it pulls with 1.43 m/s², a seventh of a g; Jupiter stands still in its sky some 40° up and 7.6° across, from its Cassini map; the HUD marks Jupiter. Chapter 9 is flown at Kraken Mare on Titan, as far as it is built: the Qualifying Heat (Captain Kroll names the place and Victory Cola's Circuit, the announcer the broadcast: "tonight from titan. the air is thick. the clones are thin"; eight Ministry coils on an ellipse 4.4 by 3.2 km whose one side runs 150 m over the haze's deck and whose other dips 300 m into it, three laps against Perihelion, skill 0.65, and four of the Circuit of skill 0.35 to 0.45; home among the first three wins ☉ 4,000 and 400 ratings, later the race is flown again), the Pit Fight (five of the Circuit of skill 0.4 and the Host 600 m under the deck, three minutes, every ship for itself, the Host placed by kills among theirs, a tie counting for the Host; among the first three wins ☉ 4,000 and 500 ratings, else it is fought again), the Haze Duel (Perihelion alone, a sharp shot of quick reflexes (aim and reflexes 0.65) who never takes a risk his clone could die of (daring 0.4), 1.25 km under the deck, where the eye sees some 600 m and the radar twice that; once his hull is down to 35 % he breaks off before his clone can die, as he always does, and the duel is won: ☉ 3,000 and 400 ratings), the Circuit Final (the Qualifying Heat's loop and field, won only by the first home: ☉ 6,000 and 800 ratings), Methane Echo (the seventh echo, 80 m over the sea under the haze, which hides it from the eye and not from the radar; held still there, it is heard: 40 ratings), the Second Tube (the Circuit's pit crew fits a second torpedo tube for ☉ 2,000, paid as the laser is: two torpedoes each time a clone wakes, a stock ship's) and Thrusters I (Circuit-grade nozzles for ☉ 1,000, paid as the laser is: the thrusters push 8 % of a stock ship's harder, as the hangar's first step does). The place: the top of Titan's orange haze runs through the field's middle as the deck of its air, a sheet of dim orange cloud tops curving away as Titan does, the sea of liquid methane 2 km under it, dark, glassy and still, a ship that touches it crashing as into rock; Titan's air works in the game as Jupiter's does (the same haze, drag and heat by depth under the deck, the deck a veil the senses see through only so far), squeezed as Jupiter's is, where the real one is thicker still and some two hundred kilometers deep: 1.25 km under the deck the eye sees some 600 m and the radar twice that, over the sea under 300 m; under the deck every view ends in the haze's orange; Titan pulls with 1.35 m/s², a seventh of a g; over the haze Saturn stands still in the sky some 30° up and 5.7° across, from its real map, its ring seen almost from its edge, a thin bright line, since Titan's orbit lies in the ring's plane; the HUD marks Saturn. Chapter 10 is flown at the Plumes on Enceladus, as far as it is built: the Plume Yacht (Captain Kroll names the job, Bellamy Crisp of Victory Cola calls the show: his yacht, some 120 m long in the brand's red lacquer, new and polished as only what the rich own is, flies its lane across the tiger stripes 400 m over the plain at 15 m/s; for 30 s three of his actors play bandits whose shots fly some 4° wide and hurt nothing; then three real raiders of skill 0.65 shoot the actors down and go for the yacht, and all three shot down wins ☉ 5,000 and 500 ratings, while 400 of their hits on the yacht start it over) Plating II (Crisp's people deliver plate of the second grade for ☉ 3,000, paid as the laser is: every hit hurts two of the hangar's steps, 14 % of a stock ship's, less), Voice II (Pim fits Nicole a voice board out of Crisp's yacht: in the campaign she sounds thin until then, through a band from 420 to 3,600 Hz, and her own voice from then on), Hello, Host (a grey interceptor with SPARE sprayed on its flank, fighting as the Host fought until Io (counted in every fight against a mission's raiders until Blackout is done: his aim 2.25 times the Host's share of beams that hit them, his daring 1.2 times its share of fight with the nearest within 600 m, each between 0.35 and 0.85, his reflexes 0.6; skill 0.6 throughout while fewer than 30 shots or 60 s of fight were counted), breaks off at half its hull and hails: Spare, the clone lost in Io's flux tube; 300 ratings) and Tiger Stripes (a time trial through eight coils of the Ministry, two over each stripe at its vents, 160 m over the plain, down one stripe and back up the next; the clock starts at the first coil, and all eight within the ad's 75 s win ☉ 2,500 and 300 ratings; its gold time 60 s). The place: the south pole of Enceladus, 252 km across and the brightest body in the solar system; its plain lies 600 m under the field's middle, and across it run the four tiger stripes, Alexandria, Cairo, Baghdad and Damascus, 1.2 km apart (the real ones some 130 km long and 35 km apart), each a trough 120 m deep and 120 m wide with a ridge 40 m high along each lip, all of it solid; from five vents on each stripe's floor a plume of ice grains and vapor rises 3 km, widening from 30 m to 400 m, the grains pattering on a hull that flies through; Enceladus pulls with 0.11 m/s², too weak for a ship to feel; Saturn fills the sky some 22° up and 28° across, from its real map, its ring seen almost from its edge, since Enceladus orbits in its plane; the HUD marks Saturn. Chapter 11 is flown at Two Faces on Iapetus: The Ridge (Captain Kroll names the place and two of Spare's strays heading for the old frequency; the eighth echo comes from a cache 50 m over the ridge's crest on its dark side, and held still there it is heard; 8 s in, two strays of skill 0.45 set out for it from the ridge's far end, and one of them there first starts it over; ☉ 2,000 and 300 ratings), Office Hours (the clone climbs up to Patience's galley, where Commissioner Dolores Quill of the Ministry's Office of Admissions offers the fast track to the trials, in person, Hedda at the head of the table), Drive II (the main drive's and the radiators' second steps, ☉ 2,500 each, paid as the laser is, with money Quill's office sent ahead: the drive pushes 10 % of a stock ship's harder, the radiators shed two steps more) and Last of the Tallymen (Ma Tally, skill 0.55, and three escorts of skill 0.5 round her tug over the dark face; all four shot down wins ☉ 5,000 and 500 ratings). The place: Iapetus, 1,470 km across, one half of it as dark as soot and the other as bright as snow; its plain lies 1.5 km under the field's middle, and across it along the equator runs the ridge, 1.1 km high and 3.2 km wide at its foot, steep-flanked, its crest of peaks and saddles winding some 400 m under the field's middle (the real one rises up to some 20 km), all of it solid; north of it lies the dark face, south of it the bright one, the border wandering across it in tongues; Iapetus pulls with 0.22 m/s², too weak for a ship to feel; Saturn hangs 25° up, some 2° across, from its real map, its ring opened some 15°, since Iapetus's orbit is tilted to the ring's plane; the HUD marks Saturn. Chapter 12 is the Ring Trials, four broadcast qualifiers of the Games in the Rings of Saturn: Commissioner Quill announces them, and each trial is a whole round of the Games after its briefing, flown in the campaign's hull against the Games' default rivals; Trial I, a race, three laps, home among the first three (☉ 5,000 and 1,000 ratings); Trial II, a deathmatch, among the first two (☉ 5,000 and 1,200); Trial III, Capture the Flag, the Host's side leading when the clock runs out (☉ 5,000 and 1,200); Trial IV, the President's Fleet, his battleship sunk (☉ 8,000 and 1,600). A trial is judged when its round has run to its end; failed or left, it waits on the job board, and its round pays nothing into the tournament's hangar. Then Torpedo Tubes I (a third tube, ☉ 1,500, paid as the laser is: a torpedo more each time a clone wakes, as the hangar's first step gives) and The Invitation (the ratings lift the Host to Contender, and Quill invites the Host to the Final Trial at the Ninth Planet). Chapter 13 is flown at Verona on Miranda: the Verona Drop (Captain Kroll names the place; down the face through six buoys, each 150 m off it, from near its top edge to the hideout's mouth, under 150 m/s, a slow pace beside a ship's 250 m/s, and once at the first buoy never more than 300 m off the face, or the Navy's scopes see the hull and it starts over; 300 ratings), Among the Loose (Spare's people, clones whose Hosts died, quit or lost the link, over the comm; their overdrive fitted for ☉ 3,000, paid as the laser is), The Sweep (six Navy fighters of skill 0.5 come for the hideout's mouth, Spare on the Host's wing with the Host's own habits (as at Enceladus), on the Host's side and with no clone after his death; one of them within 300 m of the mouth for 8 s in a row opens the hideout and starts it over, the mouth kept clear for four minutes, or all six down, wins ☉ 6,000 and 500 ratings) and Thrusters II (the thrusters' second step, ☉ 2,500, paid as the laser is: they push 16 % of a stock ship's harder). The place: Verona Rupes on Miranda, 470 km across, the tallest cliff in the solar system; it runs across the field, the upland north of it 700 m over the field's middle and its foot south of it 1.3 km under it, the face 2 km high and leaning back some 75° (the real one drops some 10 to 20 km), all of it solid; the Loose's hideout opens in the face 150 m over its foot, a dark door in a frame of amber lamps; Miranda pulls with 0.079 m/s², too weak for a ship to feel; Uranus fills a fifth of the sky, 25° across and 35° up, a pale cyan ball lying on its side, its faint dark rings seen almost from their edge, standing upright; the HUD marks the hideout and Uranus. Chapter 14 is flown at Memorial on Triton and at the Derelict: the Geyser Field (Captain Kroll names the place; a time trial through the Navy's coils past Triton's geysers to the Memorial Station, each coil beside a geyser on the way, its rim passing 120 m outside the column, 150 m over the plain, the clock a minute from the first coil; its gold time 42 s; ☉ 3,000 and 300 ratings), The Old Admiral (Fleet Admiral Corwin Vey over the comm, drifting between centuries: he broke the world ship on the President's first order, "nobody leaves", the habitat ring first for the cameras), The Memorial (the bow's bridge tower, torn off and drifting before the Derelict's torn bow: a shaft of the world ship's plating 140 m wide, four decks of 80 m, open at the bottom where it tore, each deck's floor with a hatch 60 m a side to the next on alternate sides, framed in amber emergency lights, solid as boxes; in from below and up through every hatch to the bridge, where the ninth echo is the log, held still there, a Navy guard of four of skill 0.5 fighting outside; ☉ 4,000 and 400 ratings), Grave Shift (the guard round the tower and on the hull's tail, out to the arena's edge, within 92 % of the arena's limit; ☉ 3,000 and 300 ratings) and Thrusters III (the thrusters' third step, ☉ 5,000, paid as the laser is: they push 24 % of a stock ship's harder). The place: Triton, 2,700 km across, its plain of pinkish nitrogen ice 600 m under the field's middle, dimpled and ridged as the skin of a cantaloupe; ten geysers rise from it, 1 km apart at least, each a column 80 m wide and 2 km high (the real ones some 8 km) of nitrogen and dark dust bending with the thin wind at its top, which pushes a ship in it straight up with 6 g on its axis at its foot (less towards its edge and its top), more than a ship's thrusters push, so a flight computer cannot hold a ship that flies in: it is thrown up; the same for every ship, bots too; the Navy's Memorial Station, its oldest base, stands on the plain, solid; Triton pulls with 0.78 m/s², about a 13th of a g; Neptune hangs 30° up, some 8° across, a little bluer than Uranus; the HUD marks the station and Neptune. Chapter 15 is flown at Last Light on Pluto: the Heart Run (Captain Kroll names the place and the Feeders, who push comets out to the hole for the Ministry and take on haulers; their pilots' race, two laps of eight rings on an ellipse 3.8 by 3 km over the heart, 300 m over the plain, against five Feeders of skill 0.4 to 0.6; only first home wins ☉ 5,000 and 500 ratings, and the Feeders take Patience on), The File (Spare aboard Patience, unarmed, in the galley in person: Rask sold the hull and Nicole to the Ministry the day the Host signed, Operation Lullaby, and Quill cleared the Host's road so Nicole would come to trust a pilot enough to remember where she went), Pluto Echo (the tenth echo over the mountains' edge by the heart; 40 ratings), Last Light (Patience moored where the Feeder convoy forms up, six Ministry pickets of skill 0.55 firing at her; all six down wins ☉ 6,000 and 400 ratings, 500 of their hits on Patience start it over), Wells (Nicole's escape gauge and orbit readout installed) and Plating III (Feeder plate, ☉ 6,000, paid as the laser is: every hit hurts three of the hangar's steps, 21 % of a stock ship's, less). The place: the edge of Pluto's heart, Sputnik Planitia, a plain of nitrogen ice 600 m under the field's middle broken into cells some 1.5 km across (the real ones 20 to 30 km), their rims 15 m low where the ice sinks back; north-west of it the mountains of water ice that frame it, blocky and steep, up to 1 km over the plain (the real ones 3 to 5 km), all of it solid; the sun in 2584 a bright star some 2,400 times dimmer than at Earth, noon as dim as dusk there; Pluto pulls with 0.62 m/s², about a 16th of a g; Charon hangs fixed in the sky 40° up, some 3.5° across; the HUD marks Charon. Chapter 16 is flown on the Long Ladder, the Ministry's chain of fuel depots out past Pluto, at two of its rungs in open space: Haumea's Ring (the depot crews' race between beacons, two laps of eight rings on an ellipse 4.6 by 3.6 km through the ring's blocks of water ice, a layer 200 m thick, against five crews of skill 0.45 to 0.6; only first home wins ☉ 6,000 and 600 ratings; Haumea below the ring, stretched to an egg of 2,322 by 1,704 by 1,026 km, white with a dark red spot, spinning once in four hours), Rung Two (a fuel barge flown from the far side of the field to the depot over Eris, two waves of three scavengers of skill 0.5 and 0.55 on it, as the ore barge's run is; ☉ 6,000 and 400 ratings; Eris below, one of the brightest bodies known, its moon Dysnomia beside it, loose ice round both), The Hunter (Perihelion, now the Ministry's hunter, skill 0.7, and two Navy fighters of 0.5; at 35 % of his hull he breaks off again, swearing; ☉ 5,000 and 600 ratings) and Drive III (the main drive's and the radiators' third steps, ☉ 5,000 each, paid as the laser is: the drive pushes 15 % of a stock ship's harder). The HUD marks Haumea, Eris and the depot.
