Chapter 17 is flown at the game's own planets X and XI, Lantern and Granary, in open space: Kiln (the settlers over Lantern, waiting for passage to the Ninth with their brochures; their letters carried by calling at six beacons in turn within four minutes; 200 ratings; Lantern below, a world as big as Mars, its thick hydrogen air holding in its core's heat over a warm, dark ocean, from space a black disk lit by lightning deep in its air and by the flares of the settlers' heat wells), Granary (into the Feeders' store of comets parked in a ring round a small ice world, to the comet bound for the Ninth at its far side, out of the sensor cones the Feeders' three tugs sweep across the ring, each 2.5 km long and some 25° wide, once round every 22 s; a cone holding the hull 0.4 s sees it once, and seen twice fails it; 400 ratings), Granary Echo (the eleventh echo among the parked comets; 40 ratings), Ride the Comet (the comet hooked and held on to for 30 s while Patience pushes it out, shedding its shower at the hull on the hook: every 1.6 s a piece of 3 m off its face at 80 m/s, aimed where the hull will be give or take 30 m, striking as hard as a piece of the Games' shower, bursting on rock; 500 ratings) and Torpedo Tubes II and III (the Feeders' tubes, ☉ 3,000 and 6,000, paid as the laser is: four torpedoes each time a clone wakes, every tube a ship can carry). The HUD marks Kiln, Lantern, the comet and Granary. Chapter 18 is flown at the Ninth, which is no planet but the primordial black hole of the Event Horizon: The Drain (Captain Kroll says the course ends here; among the ice the Feeders parked lies Scout One, the Magpie's twin, held where she lies, and the crosshair held on her for 2 s within 1.5 km scans her; 500 ratings), Scout One (Nicole sees herself asleep in the pod: "Hello, me."; Spare calls the company coming, and Perihelion hails), The Feeders' Comet (begun, and every clone printed, 600 m off Scout One, facing her; three Navy fighters of skill 0.5 firing at Scout One; all three down, then the comet the Ministry threw at her, its head 45 s out at 100 m/s: hooked, Scout One comes free of her moorings and is dragged on the cable, and must lie 75 m off the head's route when it passes, its shower running ahead of it from 10 s to 32 s into the tow, a piece of 3 m every 1.5 s at 160 m/s down the route within 45 m of it, striking her or the hull as hard as the Games' shower; Scout One destroyed or still in its way starts it over; 800 ratings), First Death (Perihelion, skill 0.75, and four Navy fighters of 0.5, Spare on the Host's wing with its habits; Perihelion shot down ends it, with no breaking off this time: his first death, and the announcer: "the undying is dead. folks, i think the printer just fainted"; 1,500 ratings), Orbit (Patience crippled and her printer gone: the Magpie flown into a stable orbit about the hole inside the disk's glare, as the flight school's orbit drill asks, with Scout One on the hook: she drifts 150 m ahead on the hull's flight, a light scout of 12 t, a fifth of an interceptor, the orbit counts only while she hangs on the hook, and the hole taking her starts it over; a death goes black for 4 s, NO CLONE, without an ad or a new clone's link booting, and the attempt starts over; 2,000 ratings) and The Deal (Commissioner Quill on the cruiser's channel: admission to the Games on three conditions, the debt settled). Chapter 19 is flown at the Ballroom on the Moon: The Parade (ten of the Ministry's rings in a line over the Ballroom's dome, 300 m over its crown; the Magpie's slot moves along them at 100 m/s, the other new Hosts' clones (Kestrel, Halcyon, Vireo, Jackdaw) flying in a diamond 120 m round it, and the hull out of it, 60 m, for 3 s in a row breaks formation and starts it over; 500 ratings), Certification (the clone climbs down from the hull onto the Ballroom's floor and stands before the President in person, on his dais of marble and gold under the dome, in his current body, young, sprayed orange, his hair dyed gold, Commissioner Quill a step below him: "What a ship. Very old. I like old things. I am one thousand years young. Never been better."; back in the cockpit, Nicole's own voice taken for the Ministry's firmware: "Hi, I am Nicole. Your personal flight computer.") and Your First Round (the announcer: "a new host is admitted. the old one did not survive the paperwork"; a whole deathmatch of the Games in the Rings of Saturn, as the Ring Trials are flown, passed once it has run to its end). The place: the President's ballroom on the Moon, beautiful and new as only what he owns is, a dome of gold-tinted glass 600 m across and 150 m high on ribs of polished gold, a ring of lamps round its foot, solid as a box, on the Moon's grey plain 800 m under the field's middle, pocked with craters; the Moon pulls with 1.62 m/s², a sixth of a g; Earth hangs 55° up, some 1.9° across; the HUD marks Earth. The campaign ends there, as far as it is built. Beside the story, the job board offers **side trips** once Act II has begun (Pilot Episode done), flown whenever the Host likes, always from their start, and leaving the drill reached in the story's next job as it was: the **Terminator Run** at Caloris on Mercury (two laps of eight rings on an ellipse round the sun miners' camp, 300 m over the ground, against five sun miners of skill 0.4 to 0.55; first or second home is paid, ☉ 4,000 and 300 ratings), then **Mercury Echo** (Nicole's old frequency held still over the wrinkle ridge north of the camp; 40 ratings). The place: the floor of the Caloris basin, a warm grey plain 800 m under the field's middle with craters 50 to 300 m across and two wrinkle ridges, broad arches 120 m high with a crest 50 m higher; the sun miners' camp at the middle, solid as a box; their fields of solar panels, 60 by 20 m each in rows turned to the sun, on the plain beyond; Mercury pulls with 3.7 m/s², some 0.38 g; the sun, 0.39 AU off, shows 2.6 times as wide as at Earth, its glare slowing the laser's radiators to 0.7 of the Belt's, and solar storms blind the sensors now and then; the HUD marks the camp. Once Victory Cola has signed the Host (Sponsor Night done), the **Cloud Line** at Highball over Venus (two laps of eight rings on an ellipse round the city, the eastern four 150 m under the acid clouds' deck, the western four 800 m over it, against five of Victory Cola's pilots of skill 0.4 to 0.6; only first home is paid, ☉ 5,000 and 400 ratings), then **Venus Echo** (held still over biodome four; 40 ratings). The place: Victory Cola's floating city some 54 km over Venus, a square platform 1.1 km a side and 60 m thick, white over red and gold, six glass halls of gardens on it (each 160 by 90 m and 50 m high), and at its middle a tower shaped as a Victory Cola bottle, 400 m high, all solid as boxes; the acid clouds' deck 1 km under the field's middle, Venus's air working as Jupiter's and Titan's (haze, drag and heat by depth); Venus pulls with 8.87 m/s², some 0.9 g; the sky a pale yellow without stars, the sun 1.4 times as wide as at Earth, its glare slowing the laser's radiators to 0.8 of the Belt's; the HUD marks biodome four. The place: the Rockborn's capital built into the wall of the crater Occator, 92 km across, on Ceres; the field flies over the market terrace, a shelf of the wall 2.8 km deep and 6 km wide, 60 m below the field's middle, the wall climbing behind it in four steps of 300 m to the rim's crest, its steps crowded with habitat domes and their faces cut with the mouths of old mine galleries, lit windows and the capital's neon, all of it solid (the terrace, each step and each dome as a box); before the terrace its edge drops 1.5 km to the crater's dark floor, which runs out some 40 km to the bright salt of Cerealia Facula, and the Ministry's launch ring, 6 km across, hangs 25 km over the crater; the crater lies towards the low sun, which lights the wall's faces; Ceres pulls with 0.28 m/s² (about a 35th of a g), too weak for a ship to feel; the HUD marks Cerealia Facula and the market. The campaign's ship is its own, never the tournament hangar's: the hull in rack 7731 as the yard sold it, the human interceptor's paint worn to the metal and rusted, the yard's stencils on it, without its gun, its tournament stripes and its left strut and fin and the left flank's armor panels (a beam into that bare flank costs the hull twice what one into the other does, until Armor Panels closes it; a quarter less radiator, and no course to hold in air, see Fins under Jupiter's air), its drive at 60 % of the thrust (a cracked bell), its thrusters at 50 % (70 % once Pim has welded them, and then a crooked band of other steel welded around both ends of each thruster housing, its beads still tinted by the heat), without a laser, torpedoes, overdrive or dodge, and the HUD shows no readout for what it lacks. Its flight computer is a salvaged one too: Nicole has the markers, the course marker, where the flight crosses a ring's plane, the autopilot, the brake and the impact warning, but none of her modules yet (`Module` in `src/campaign/campaign.ts`; each comes with a chapter of the story), so the HUD marks and names no ship and the crosshair never turns orange over a hull (targeting), no racing line shows (racing line), no radar (radar), no TRACKED, LOCKED, ON YOUR TAIL or TORPEDO INBOUND (threat), and no escape gauge or orbit readout, though a well's alarm still sounds (wells). A mission's pay goes to the campaign's own wallet, less the yard's 40 % while the debt of 25,000 coins is unpaid; the cut pays the debt off. A mission done the first time also earns ratings, which the yard takes no cut of and which are never spent (Lamplighter 10, Live Target 15, Canister Sweep 10, First Cut 20, Docking Lane 40); they rank the Host with the feeds: Unrated, Background from 50, Local Feed from 250, Regional Feed from 1,200, Sponsored from 3,500, Prime Time from 7,000, Contender from 12,000 (chapter 1 ends in the Background). A mission that raises the rank says so. Beside the story, every place the campaign's jobs fly in (25 of them, the Rings, the Event Horizon and the Derelict among them) hides three black boxes of the dead and one data chip, laid on the place's field from its seed, in the lee of a rack, rock or wreck or low over the ground, at least 10 m clear of both: within 1.5 km the HUD marks one faintly and Nicole names it once ("A black box, Host. Someone’s last words."), and flown within 60 m it is taken, in any job at that place. A black box shows its pilot's name and last words on screen (never spoken: 75 lines in four languages would grow every Host's voice download by about a tenth); a place's third brings the families' reward over Rask's comm, ☉ 300 less the yard's cut while the debt is unpaid, once. A chip holds a track of Radio 2584, played next in the campaign and offered on the radio page under "Found on data chips". Once a place's first job is done, the job board offers a free flight there (no clock, nothing to fail, left through the pause menu), with what it still hides ("Black boxes 1/3 · chip found") and the count of all ("Black boxes 7/75 · data chips 2/25"), and from Rheasilvia on a visit to Patience's galley, whose shelf keeps a memento of ten jobs once each is done and a tag on its rail for every black box found. **The Games** hold the ways into the tournament: the flight school, **bot training** (a solo round against the bots, in the mode, arena and rivals the Host picks) and the online tournament. The ship to fly and the hangar sit under the pad that shows it (see The hangar and the Solar Coins). Enter starts the flight school until its basic training is done, then bot training, or the tournament a link invited the Host to.

## Languages

The game speaks English, German, Spanish and French. It starts in the language the Host chose in the menu (the buttons at its foot, each language named in itself; the page starts anew in it), else in the first of the browser's languages it speaks, else in English. Every text on screen is translated, and so are the flight computer and the announcer, whose voices are rendered in each language: Nicole speaks the other languages in the sounds espeak-ng spells them in, with her accent. The regime's voice on Radio 2584 keeps its own English and German, and the titles of its tracks are their seeds, the same for every Host. The legal pages are German. English is the source: every text is written in English where it is used and translated through catalogs (`src/i18n/de.ts`, `es.ts`, `fr.ts`, keyed by the English text); `npm run i18n -- de` lists what a catalog still lacks, and the unit tests fail while one lacks anything.

## Controls

| Keyboard and mouse | Gamepad | Action |
|---|---|---|
| Mouse pointer (pushed the way the nose should turn; the farther from the center, the faster) / arrow keys | Left stick (pull back = nose up) | Turn the nose (yaw, pitch); with the autopilot the ship flies where it points |
| W | RT (analog) | Main drive |
| S | B | Brake, a switch like the hook: pressed, the flight computer remembers the ship's whole attitude, turns it about flat and burns against the flight with the full drive, the maneuvering thrusters pushing against the flight too while the ship swings round, so the ship stops on its line and is not bent off it; pressed again (when the speed suits the Host), or at rest, it turns the ship back into that attitude, nose and roll alike, a dotted ring ("Target") marking where the nose returns to. Steering meanwhile (to shoot, say) turns the nose, the drive burning only while it points within 30° of against the flight; let go, and the brake turns it back against the flight, the aim assist pulling the nose onto a rival close to where it points (a ship on the tail). W breaks the maneuver off at once, and so does steering hard during the turn back. The flip (X) is the other maneuver: it turns about and stays there |
| Shift (hold) | L3, the left stick pressed in | Fine aim: steers at 30 % for precise aiming |
| A / D | Right stick left / right | Thrusters: sideways |
| | X | Thrusters: backwards |
| R / F | Right stick up / down | Thrusters: up / down |
| G | D-pad up | Torpedo: a limited load per ship (four, more with the hangar's tubes; each new clone's ship comes loaded), homing on the ship closest to the nose (see Combat rules). On touch screens the TORPEDO button |
| V / right click | D-pad down | Hook: pressed, a harpoon on a cable bites the rock or ship ahead, and while it holds the winch reels in and the ship swings around what it holds; pressed again, it lets go and the ship flies off along its swing (see Combat rules). A toggle, not a button to hold, so every other control stays free on the cable. On touch screens the HOOK button, tapped the same way |
| B (hold) | D-pad left (hold) | Self-destruct, gear from the hangar: held for a second, the ship blows itself up and takes every rival within 60 m along (see Combat rules). Not in a race nor in the flight school. On touch screens the SELF-DESTRUCT button, held |
| K (hold) | D-pad left (hold) | A mining charge, only in the campaign's mining jobs (where the self-destruct never is): held for 0.6 s within 60 m of a rock, it sets a charge that goes off 6 s later (see Mining charges). On touch screens the CHARGE button, held. |
| A, D, R or F tapped twice | LB (the way the right stick points) | Dodge: a 60 m/s kick of all thrusters that way (enough to throw the ship back onto the line after a missed ring), ready again after 1.5 s. On touch screens two quick flicks of the push stick |
| X (hold) | Y (hold) | Flip: while held, the flight computer turns the ship about flat until the drive points against the flight and keeps it there. W can be held at the same time: the drive only fires once the nose points back, so X and W together brake by hand. Letting go hands the ship back |
| T | R3, the right stick pressed in | Autopilot / free drift, switched at once. Every round starts in free drift: steering turns the nose and nothing else, the ship keeps any drift, and the drive, pushing along the nose, bends the flight; slides and shots sideways come with it. With the autopilot on the ship flies where it points, the flight computer taking out every drift across the nose with thrusters and drive, and W pushes it exactly that way (see Flight): an aid for a head busy with something else. On touch screens the AUTO button |
| E | RB | Overdrive, the last resort: one shove of 200 m/s past the speed limit that puts the ship onto the crosshair's line, on a ram course at whatever lies there. It leaves the clone at the edge of blacking out and the main drive overheated for 4 seconds (no thrust; turning, sidestepping and firing go on), and recharges for 20 seconds (the HUD counts down). Aim it across the attacker's line of fire or ahead of the own drift: straight away from a ship on the own tail it only makes a farther target. On touch screens the OVERDRIVE button |
| Click / Space | LT / A | Fire laser |
| C | View | Chase / cockpit camera |
| M | D-pad down (paused) | Radio on / off (also with the ♪ button in the top right corner; a click on the song title opens the radio page). In fullscreen the mouse flies the ship, so the radio's keys are the way to it there; the gamepad's d-pad flies the ship too (hook, decoy, self-destruct) and works the radio only off the flight |
| , / . | D-pad left / right (paused) | The game master as DJ: previous / next song of the programme (the radio box's ⏮ ⏭); for another Host online the HUD says that only the game master picks the music |
| Esc / P | Menu | Pause |
| Q | | On the pause screen: quit the round |
| F1 | | Controls sheet over any screen, the running round included (F1 again closes it; the pause menu's Controls opens it too, on every device). It opens at the controls of whatever the Host steered with last (a key or the mouse, a gamepad's button or stick, a finger on the screen), with tabs for the other two; the HUD's key labels and the flight school's key hints follow the same device. What the ship lacks (gear not bought in the hangar, or what the campaign's hull is not fitted with yet: the laser, the dodge, the overdrive, torpedoes, the hook, the self-destruct) stays on the sheet, grey and struck through, its key doing nothing |
| Tab (hold) | | Standings over the running round: place, kills, deaths, score, in a race the lap or the finish time. While watching a full tournament it also switches to the next pilot |
| Enter | A | Confirm (start, resume, boot the next clone) |

**The essentials** are W, A, S, D, Shift, Space, X and the arrow keys or the mouse; both work at the same time, so keyboard pilots and mouse pilots share one layout. The up and down thrusters and the camera are extras. The pilot does not roll: the flight computer keeps the ship upright (see Flight).

**Mouse flight.** The pointer stays visible and nothing captures the mouse. Its offset from the screen center is the stick: at 25 % of the shorter screen side the nose turns at full rate, within 8 % of that around the center it does not turn, and a pointer that leaves the window lets go of the stick. The crosshair is the nose: where the laser hits and, with the autopilot, where the ship flies; the chase camera looks along it. Centered, the nose stops and the ship flies straight on. Arrow keys, gamepad and touch stick turn the nose the same way.

Gamepads with the standard layout (Xbox, PlayStation, most others in current browsers) work without setup. A pad whose layout the browser does not know is left alone: its axes may rest anywhere (a trigger or a throttle at full) and would fly the ship by themselves.

On phones and tablets touch controls appear: the fixed stick on the left turns the ship (drag up = nose up; nothing within 8 % of its middle, then by the power 2.2 of the way out, so a small push aims finely and only its rim turns at full rate), a second stick appears under the right thumb and fires the maneuvering thrusters. Hold **Drive** for the main drive and **Fire** for the laser; **Brake** (tap) brakes, tapped again it turns back on target, **Flip** (hold) turns the ship against the flight, **Hook** (tap) fires the hook and reels in, tapped again it lets go; **Self-destruct** (hold for a second) blows the ship up. Hook and Self-destruct show only on a ship that carries them. Pressing a key on a hardware keyboard hides the touch controls (the phone's volume keys and a headset's media keys do not), touching the screen brings them back; a hold button stays held while any finger is on it. On a phone (a touch screen less than 500 px high or 600 px wide) the HUD shrinks to the edges so the middle of the picture stays free: smaller buttons, the gauges as a slim row of labels over bars, the round's panel narrow beside the radar, messages and transmissions smaller and out of the middle, and the radio only its switch and the title in the top right corner (the phone's own keys set its volume). No piece of the HUD covers another: the round's panel, the alarms with the message below the crosshair, the transmissions, the radar, the gauges, the radio, the kill feed, the touch buttons, the height and climb and the fixed references each keep their own place, on a big screen and a small one, with keyboard or touch; on a low screen with a keyboard the transmissions run on the left beside the alarms' column, the radar shrinks and the gauges close up. A round is flown with the phone held sideways: held upright, a plate over the round asks to turn it ("Turn your phone sideways to fly."), with a phone turning on it; a solo round stands still meanwhile and goes on as soon as the phone turns, an online tournament does not wait. The menu works either way.

**Fullscreen.** On phones and tablets a round goes fullscreen by itself and, where the browser allows it, holds landscape; the menu and the pause menu have a Fullscreen button for every device. Browsers that do not let a page go fullscreen (the iPhone's) get it by adding the game to the home screen: the web app manifest (`public/manifest.webmanifest`, icons rendered from the seal) starts it without the browser's bars.

## Online tournament

**Online tournament** in the menu opens the list of tournaments. On top, the public tournaments running right now, each with its name, how many Hosts fly, whether bots fill it and the time left in the round, and a button to join (or to watch, when it is full). Below, a form starts one:

- **A name** of the Host's choice ("Kessler's Revenge"), up to 32 characters.
- **Public or private.** A public tournament is announced in the lobby for everyone; a private one is only found through its link.
- **With or without bots.** With bots, the lead browser fills all eight places and every Host who joins takes a bot's place; without, only Hosts fly.
- **Race, deathmatch, Last Clone Standing, Capture the Flag, the President's Fleet, or all in turn** (see Modes).

The room's code, name and rules live in the fragment of its link (`#t=kessler-s-revenge-k7m2pq&n=Kessler%27s+Revenge&pub=1`, `&bots=0` without bots, `&m=race` or `&m=mixed` for the mode), so they never reach the web server. The code is the name plus a random part: 6 characters for a public tournament, so two of the same name never meet, 20 for a private one (about 99 bits), far beyond guessing from the public relays. Starting a tournament copies its link at once, to be sent to friends; the share button (see Sharing) and the pause menu's **Invite friends** share it any time. Whoever follows the link joins; when nobody is in the room any more, the first to come starts it again with the link's rules, so a tournament can be played forever. Every build names the version of its network protocol in the app id it announces itself with, so a tab of an older build left open across a deploy meets only tabs of its own protocol (rooms and a lobby of their own) and never misreads a newer one's messages. There is no game server: the browsers find each other through public Nostr relays ([Trystero](https://github.com/dmotz/trystero)) and then talk directly over WebRTC. Leaving the tournament and the list closes the connections to the relays.

- **The relays.** Every browser connects to the same 12 public relays, a list in the game (`src/net/relays.json`) that a check renews every week (`npm run relays`, run by the workflow Relays every Monday): it takes the relays already listed, Trystero's built-in ones and those that independent relay monitors report healthy (NIP-66), and keeps the ones that, three times in a row, answer a subscription, accept an event of the kind Trystero announces with and hand it back; those already listed first, so builds a week apart still meet, then the fastest, one per operator, and of those only the monitors name, at most 2 new ones a week. Two Hosts meet as long as one relay works for both. When none of the list opens a connection within 10 seconds, the browser falls back to Trystero's own choice (5 of its built-in relays). The game never asks a third party for relays while it runs.
- **A notification when a public tournament opens.** In the list of tournaments a Host can ask to be told when a public tournament opens. The lead browser of a public tournament tells the site so (`/push`) when it starts announcing it and every two minutes after; a tournament not heard of for 10 minutes counts as new again. Each Host chooses from how many open public tournaments on to hear (one, two or three). A new tournament raises the number open, and the notification goes to every subscriber whose number it reaches, in their language and saying how many are open, at most one every 15 minutes for all tournaments together (a new one heard within those 15 minutes waits, and the first word of any open tournament after them announces it, so none goes unannounced and none waits longer than 15 minutes and a heartbeat; the list says so under its button), naming no tournament (a name a Host typed never reaches a lock screen); a tap opens the list. Nobody can prove a tournament open, since it lives between the browsers: each word is kept with a keyed hash of the sender's network that changes every day, the tournaments of one network count as one, and a network whose other tournament was heard within the hour opens no new one, so a script sends no more notifications than a Host would. The notification goes out 25 subscriptions a call (a free function makes at most 50 requests a call), the lead browser asking for the next until all have it. Its two buttons, where the browser shows them (Chrome, Edge, Android), and two in the list pause it: quiet for an hour, or until 8 o'clock tomorrow morning on the device's clock (today's 8 o'clock while it is still ahead); the list then says until when, with a button to hear again at once; a pause lasts at most a day and a half. The site keeps the subscriptions in a database and writes at most 50,000 rows a day, half of what its free plan allows; beyond, the notifications rest until the next day and the game plays on.
- **The lobby** is one shared room on the same relays. The lead browser of a public tournament announces it every five seconds, and at once to everyone who opens the list; a tournament not heard of for 16 seconds leaves the list. Announcements are checked like every message from another browser. A tournament's listing only follows the browser that announced it, until that one has been quiet for 10 seconds (as when the lead moves on); a browser leads one tournament at a time, so its newest announcement replaces its earlier ones. The list keeps at most 50 tournaments and lets the one heard of longest ago go first. Until the lobby has listened for six seconds, the list says it is still looking.
- **Eight places per tournament.** A ninth Host does not fly but watches the broadcast, and the own clone is printed as soon as a place comes free; the HUD shows the watched pilot's gauges. A director cuts the broadcast: it follows the pilot in the thick of it (another ship close, a nose on an enemy, an enemy on its tail, a battered hull, in a race the leaders), holds a pilot at least 6 seconds and cuts only to one clearly more in the thick of it, at once when the followed one dies; every other cut is a wide shot from the side instead of the chase camera. Tab picks the next pilot by hand; 10 seconds after the last Tab the director takes over again. Hosts who were in the room first keep their places. In its first six seconds a newcomer cannot know yet who came first and counts every browser it is connected to: with eight or more, it watches.
- **Every Host has their own pilot.** Each browser simulates its own ship and sends its state 15 times per second; the other browsers predict the motion in between (a ship without thrust flies straight on) and blend corrections in smoothly: the shown ship moves on as predicted and the blend only takes out the correction, so it is drawn where the prediction says. The ships of a browser that has sent nothing for 2.5 seconds (a hidden tab) leave every screen until it sends again, and its Host does not count as flying.
- **The shooter decides about hits.** A beam that goes through a ship on the shooter's screen is reported to the browser flying that ship, which applies the damage and announces a destruction. A nova core goes off on every browser with the field time it went off at on its holder's, so the rock it breaks breaks there at that time into the same fragments everywhere (see Blasts), and a destroyed ship's wreckage is seeded with its death count, so it breaks into the same pieces everywhere, flying from where the ship's own browser saw it break up. A piece of wreckage shot or rammed bursts on every screen, and a Host who joins gets the pieces in flight together with the field.
- **One field for everyone.** Where a rock stands and how it is turned follows from the seed and the field time. The lead browser shares its field time with the round clock 15 times per second; the others glide towards it and jump when they are more than half a second off. A round's start (from which race times and the probes count) is known from its first clock: the lead's clock smooths it by up to a quarter second, never more, so a round the lead ends early (a race all finished, a side that won, the battleship gone) ends sooner everywhere but keeps its start; the sides, cores and battleship in the lead's message are taken before its clock. A newcomer gets the field time, every rock's remaining strength against the campaign's charges and when each rock broke, the motion of every piece a comet, the President's battleship or a contact with another piece or a ring shoved and of the pieces broken off them (see Comets, Modes and Asteroids), the wreckage, the probes in flight and the ones taken, and learns from the first round clock which round runs; its clone is then printed again in the round's arena (on the grid in a race), since it was printed before the arena was known; a browser that starts over (see below) gets the same and rebuilds its field from it. The snapshot names its arena, so a newcomer builds the field in the right one. Every new round brings a fresh field in the next arena (see Arenas); rocks circling the center follow from the field time as well.
- **The same numbers on every machine.** Every browser computes the shared world itself, so every one has to get the very same bits. JavaScript's own `Math.sin`, `Math.exp`, `Math.pow` and their kin do not give them: their last bits differ between an x64 and an ARM processor, and `Math.log` and `Math.pow` between macOS and Linux (measured on all three with two versions of Node), enough to bow a racing line differently and, over a round, to set two browsers' fields apart. The game computes every such function with its own (`src/detmath.ts`: sin, cos, tan, asin, acos, atan, atan2, exp, expm1, log, log1p, log2, log10, pow, sinh, cosh, tanh, cbrt, hypot), built only from what IEEE 754 rounds alike everywhere (`+ - * /` and the square root), within 1 ulp of the true value; three.js's rotations, interpolations and its centripetal curve, which call `Math`'s, have their own counterparts too (`src/detthree.ts`). A unit test keeps `Math`'s out of the code, another pins the bits of each function and of a minute and a half of every arena's field, its course and probes and a flown ship, and the workflow CI runs the unit tests on x64 and ARM Linux and on macOS.
- **The lead resets the field for everyone.** Every browser computes the field alike, but a message can come late, out of order or not at all, and a piece that drifted apart would stay apart for the round. So the lead browser sends its field again and again (`src/world/fieldsync.ts`), in 20 slices, one every 0.5 s of field time: the whole field every 10 s, never more than about 30 KB a message even in a Rings round broken twice over with every piece knocked, a few kilobytes as a round goes. The field is cut into families, each a piece of the layout and every piece it breaks into, and a slice carries of each of its families only what can drift: which pieces broke and when, every piece's remaining hits, and the motion of each piece something moved (a knock, a ram, a comet's shove); with it, when each of the round's comets' heads broke up. Pieces that move as the layout or their break gave them follow from the field time and are not sent. Every other browser takes a slice only from the lead it follows, checked like every message (a family's pieces its own, hits and times in range) and each motion no faster than 500 m/s nor turning faster than 2 rad/s, so a lead cannot fling rock at a Host; and sets its field to it: a family whose breaks differ is built again from the layout's piece, broken as the lead has it, a piece whose motion differs takes the lead's. A correction of no more than 50 m and 20° glides in over a second (drawn and flown into, but what every browser decides alike, where a probe shows up and where a comet strikes, takes the piece where its motion puts it); a larger one is set at once, and a ship it lands on is pushed out of it as out of the pieces of a rock breaking beside it; a cable lying across a piece set so is wrapped, never snapped by it. A family with a break within 2 s of the slice, on either side, is left to the messages still on their way and set right a cycle later, and so is the count of a piece hit within 2 s of it on either side (the slice names when each piece was last hit). A newcomer keeps every break the lead lists, those of pieces whose own pieces all crumbled since included. A head the lead has whole flies on where a browser broke it more than 2 s before; one the lead broke ends there. A newcomer does not cut the pieces of a rock in the snapshot that the background worker has not cut yet (a moment each on the page, seconds for a heavily broken field): that rock stays whole until the lead's sync builds its family once they are cut, so joining never holds the page.
- **The lead browser** is the one that has been in the room longest and is still sending. It runs the bots and the round clock (in Capture the Flag with the sides and the cores, in the President's Fleet with his battleship) and brings newcomers' asteroid fields up to date. Any browser can take over this job at any moment: when the lead leaves, or falls silent for 2.5 seconds (a hidden tab runs no frames, a link drops), the next one takes over and adopts its bots with everything they had: name, ship, score, kills, deaths, hull, drive, thrusters, the torpedoes left to their clone (up to a fresh clone's load of 3 online, all the other browsers allow a ship they never saw), position, motion and whether their clone lives (a dead one boots its next clone 9 seconds later, a bot's usual wait: the respawn delay and the sponsor break's ad). Every browser works out the join times from the same reports, so all of them elect the same lead.
- **The lead browser's Host is the game master.** The HUD's room line says so on that Host's screen (and a message when the job comes to them), and a star marks them in everyone's standings. From the pause menu the game master can cut a round short and start a new one, in any of the seven arenas and any mode, every mode in turn included. The round ends for everyone by the lead's clock, as a round decided early does, and its points are paid out; its scoreboard shows for 3 seconds instead of 12, naming why. The arena chosen is that one round's, then the room's order goes on where the round number stands; the mode stays the room's rule until the game master changes it. Alone, the same choice in the pause menu pays out the round and starts the new one at once; the flight school has no such choice. The game master is the round's DJ too (see Radio 2584), named "game master (and DJ)" wherever the game names them.
- **The standings show each Host's ping and the lead.** Every browser pings each of its peers directly, once a second (a tiny message of its own, answered at once, checked and rate-limited like every other), and keeps the median of its last five round trips. The standings (the scoreboard, and the one Tab holds open) show it in milliseconds next to that Host's name, in the HUD's azure, orange from 150 ms and red from 300 ms. A bot's ping is to the lead browser, since only the lead runs its bots; a dash shows for the Host's own browser and, while it leads, its own bots, and before the first pong has come back. A small star before a Host's name marks whichever browser leads. Solo rounds show neither.
- **Who was gone starts over.** A browser that ran no frames for 2 seconds, or heard none of the others for 2 seconds and then hears one again, knows the others went on without it: it starts over as the newest in the room. It gives up the lead and its bots, joins the queue for the places behind everyone else, and takes round clock and field from the lead like a newcomer (its own score stays, unless the round changed meanwhile). It judges itself cut off half a second before the others judge it silent, so it is the one whose link failed that starts over. A browser that speaks again after 2.5 seconds of silence counts as sending only 2.5 seconds later, so it can report first that it started over. When two groups that lost each other meet again, the group whose lead is not the oldest takes the other group's world; with only two browsers, nobody can tell whose link it was, and the one that notices first starts over. Two browsers that lead at once (their join times too close to tell apart over the network) settle it: the one with the higher id starts over. Every message names how long its browser has been in the room, a time that only grows while it stays: a browser takes another for one that started over only when that time falls, never for a message that comes late after a stall.
- **Nothing from other browsers is trusted.** Every message is checked before the game uses it (finite numbers in plausible ranges, callsigns up to 16 characters, at most eight ships per browser, messages up to 64 KB). A message about a ship only counts from the browser that runs it. A shooter cannot hit the same ship, or the battleship (a laser one part a shot, a torpedo's blast and a nova core each part once, each blast and core going off counted once on each part however often its hits are told; only while the battleship is in the arena, never before it arrives or once it went down, and only on a part it has; a laser's hit counts one, a torpedo's 10, a nova core's at most the 188 of the reactor), more often than its laser fires, and every kind of report has a limit per browser and second: 45 ship states (three times the rate they are sent at), 96 beams, 96 hits, 20 asteroid hits, 8 destructions (a nova can take every ship a browser runs at once), 20 pieces of wreckage, 12 torpedo launches, hits, detonations, probes taken and novas (one shared limit), 2 cheat reports, 2 fields for a newcomer, 4 pings and 4 pongs each. Counts (score, kills, deaths, gates, items) must be whole numbers. A ship dies once per clone: a destruction only counts for a living ship and a death count that goes up.
- **Plausibility: cheaters are named.** Every browser checks, roughly, whether what another browser reports about its own ships could have happened; the bounds lie so far beyond what a ship can do that lag, lost or bunched messages never make an honest browser look like a cheater. Their windows run on real time, not on the game's time, which runs slower on a device below 20 frames a second. Every state carries the time it was sent on its browser's clock (the seconds its page has run), so states that waited in a jammed link and arrive at once count the time between them as it passed there; a state is never taken as sent later than 20 ms after the least delay its browser's states have shown so far, so a browser that dates its states ahead gains no time to fly. A ship must not move farther between two states it is seen alive in than 1 km/s (four times the speed limit; an honest ship reaches about 700 m/s at the most, and only for a moment: the limit with the overdrive's shove and a dodge on top, or a winch reeling it towards a ship flying the other way; a fall into the black hole, which the limit does not hold back, ends at about 640 m/s) plus 500 m allow. Every state carries the ship's orientation and spin, and its nose turns no faster than a ship's: between two states it turns as the mean of their spins says, off by at most 0.1 rad plus the change of spin and 10 rad/s times the time between them (a shove sets a hull spinning at up to 6 rad/s, a hit's decompression about 0.5 rad/s, faster than either state may show), and the spin grows by at most 60 rad/s² times that time plus 10 rad/s (the thrusters turn a ship at up to 26 rad/s², escaping air spins a hull whose thrusters are shot to pieces at up to about 3 rad/s²). The flight computer turns a nose at 3 rad/s about each axis, 4.5 rad/s with the stick in a corner and the thruster upgrade, whatever steers it (the Host's hand, the autopilot, a flip): the check holds the nose to the flight model's limits, not to any way of steering; at 15 states a second a nose said to be still may swing some 44° between two states, never a quarter turn. Every beam is judged with its ship's next state: it starts within 100 m of where the ship was at the state before it and the one after, plus 1 km/s of flight between them, and it leaves along the turn of the nose between the two, off by no more than a nose may stray from its spin, plus the muzzles' offset (50 m, as seen from the beam's end). A beam that starts more than 1.5 km from where its ship flies here, as this browser predicts it, counts as no evidence of anything, and a torpedo launched more than 1.5 km from it is not launched. A new clone, a new round, a start over and a nova's reprint are not judged by the nose of the clone before. Only what every browser sees makes a new clone that may be printed anywhere, with a fresh load of torpedoes: a death its browser told everyone of (the load is fresh with its first state 2 seconds or more after the death; one that comes earlier, bunched with the death by lag, still counts as the last clone's), a new round, a nova's reprint, a start over of its browser (all free for 10 seconds; a start over keeps the nova core a clone held, as its browser may keep the clone); a state that only says the ship died and lives again changes nothing. A laser hit on an own ship counts only from a beam that starts near its shooter (within 1 km plus half a second at that speed) and passes within 1 km of the ship; a rock between the muzzle and the ship, as this browser sees them, takes the beam (lag may let one slip in, so that is no cheat). A torpedo hit only names a torpedo that flew here too and went off within 1 km of the ship (fired by the ship it names, or held on its hook); a ship's hook takes a torpedo or a probe only within the hook's reach plus 1 km of it, and only the browser of the ship that took it may then say where it went off; a torpedo another browser says went off more than 1.5 km from where it flies here flies on. Every browser is told of every beam, and that is the evidence for what a ship did elsewhere: a torpedo it shot down (any browser but the one that launched it) and a part of the battleship it hit count only with a beam of that ship that ended within 1 km of them in the last 5 seconds (a torpedo's hits on the battleship with that ship's torpedo going off there, whether its clone still lives or died while the torpedo flew, a torpedo that flew here too and went off within 1.5 km of where it flies here, its word of where once; a beam's only from a living ship; a nova core's with that ship's core gone off in the last 5 seconds, and no more hits on a part than a core 200 m closer to it would deal, the battleship lying a little elsewhere on each browser), and a kill counts for the killer only when a beam of its passed within 1 km of where the victim broke up, a torpedo of its may still fly, its nova core went off, or it was within 1.5 km (its drive plume burns, its nova reaches 800 m), at the death or at about the same moment in the 5 seconds before (a shove sends the victim into a rock up to 3 seconds later); otherwise the death counts without a killer. A browser that has only just come takes rocks and the battleship's hits on trust, the beams before it came being unknown to it. A nova's field time counts only within 5 seconds of the own field clock (else the own clock breaks its rock). A probe taken elsewhere must be one that could exist (shown this round, or dropped by a known ship's dead clone); one this browser will only have in a moment (the arena's next, the drops of a death not heard yet) counts as taken only if it turns up within 3 seconds, so nobody wipes out probes ahead of their time; and a probe this browser has is only taken by a ship within its reach plus 500 m and half a second of the ship's flight; one refused for that still counts for what the probe held here (lag may put an honest taker too far; a nova core it held, that ship may set off later). A probe is taken once: a ship's word that it took a probe counts once per probe, and a take this browser did not see (refused, or of a probe no longer here) counts only for the first ship that names the probe at all, one whose contents this browser did not see with its torpedoes and its window for a nova core, so a take said again and again, or of a probe another ship took here, gains nothing. A ship hooks a ship this browser runs only within the hook's reach plus 500 m, and the cable is at least as long as the way between them. A peer first heard after this browser has settled cannot claim to have joined more than 6 seconds (the time a link takes to form) before it was first heard, nor to have been in the room longer than the lead: it lines up 2 seconds behind the lead, and it never counts as joined at once with it, so it can never make the lead step back. Its own join time is believed (behind the lead all the same) once most of the live peers this browser heard while it settled, and knows to have come after that time, say it came before them: every browser's states name, once a second, the peers it heard come before it, so one whose link to this browser formed late takes the same place, seat and tint here as on the others; only a lead whose join time lies within a second of another's heard while it settled steps back for it. A newcomer believes the join times it hears while it settles; from then on every browser checks once a second whom the others follow (their states say, a peer's vote for itself not counting): when most of the live peers that name one follow another peer than the one it would elect, that peer's own join time is believed and the one it would have elected lines up behind it, and a browser that led alone (it reached the others only after settling) starts over behind them and takes their world. Browsers that each lead alone (two that reached each other only after settling) have nobody to vote between them and cannot tell an honest older peer from a liar: they settle it as two that joined at once, the one with the higher id steps back and takes the other's world, so they never play on in two worlds. A liar can take the lead of such a room of two this way, never of one where others vote. A browser that starts over gets the field again every time (at most once in 2 seconds); what the checks know of its clone is cleared at most once in 10 seconds. A new round number is taken only once the running round is (nearly) over, and a round clock no lower than −60 s; the lead's round clock names the room's modes, and every browser plays those, whatever the link or the lobby said. A nova only goes off from a living ship that took a nova core (its probe; or its state says it holds one when this browser first sees it, or within 5 seconds of taking a probe this browser did not have), within that reach of where the ship was; anything else never goes off. A clone launches no more torpedoes than it carried (with its tubes up to the online cap) and took from probes, plus 2; it drops no more decoys than its probes gave it (2 each, a probe of unknown contents counting as one of decoys), plus 2, and only where its ship is, as a nova must be; more are not launched. A ship's kills grow no faster than the deaths everyone saw it cause (two for a nova, minus one for a nova's victim), plus 2; more only count once they stay for 3 seconds, as a nova's victims are told of by their own browsers a moment after the killer's state. A ship's score, as the other browsers show it, rises by at most 200 points a second, in bursts of up to 10,000 (a nova core that takes seven ships is 7,000 points, a streak of seven kills with its bonus 6,250, the battleship's reactor and sinking 1,500, a race's win 1,000), and falls as it likes; it passes at most a gate a second, two at once for states that come bunched (the gates lie 1 to 1.9 km apart); and its finish counts once all 25 gates do, at a time on the race clock no later than 3 seconds after the state that tells of it was sent (a state that waited in a jammed link or a browser that stood still counts from when it was sent), no earlier than 3 seconds before the last state that showed the ship racing, and not before 24 seconds. A ship first seen in a round may have made what the time since the round started allows. A claim beyond that shows as far as it allows; a new round starts every ship over. A death counts only for a ship that is alive here and whose count of deaths goes up (a ship dies once per clone; any other report changes nothing and credits nobody), and for a killer that is here. It counts for the killer its browser names whenever that is another browser's ship (the victim's browser saw it die by it); a death of a ship its own browser runs (a lead's bot killed by its Host or by another of its bots) only with the evidence above. A browser that catches a ship this way rejects what it can, and reports the ship's Host only for what the ship does again and again: 3 failures of one kind (a jump, a nose, a beam, a hit, a hooked torpedo or probe, a nova, torpedoes or kills), each at least a second after the last, within 30 seconds of one round, so a lag spike, a burst of beams through a jammed link or messages that cross never name anyone. It tells everyone once a round (`ch`), and as soon as a majority of the Hosts with a vote besides the accused, at least two and the own one included, say a peer cheats, everyone sees CHEATER with its Host's callsign (two of three, three of five, four of eight). What was said in a round goes with it, so single false reports never add up over rounds. A vote is a place: only the eight browsers that came first by their join times (those whose clones fly) vote, and only they are counted, as long as they send; a browser's word that it flies buys none, so a cheater who comes in to watch, with as many tabs as it likes, cannot take the majority. A cheater with tabs among the first eight of a room does vote with each of them: a room's places are first come, first served. A peer's own word never counts against it, so a single liar cannot frame anyone, and two tabs of one Host cannot vote an honest one out of a round of five or more; with only two browsers in a tournament, nobody is named. A peer named a cheater is cast out: every honest browser comes to the same vote, sends it nothing more and takes nothing from it, and its ships leave the tournament as if it had gone (its bots are adopted, the lead is elected anew); the browser named sees its own callsign called, but never leaves on the others' word: votes carry no evidence (in a room of three or four, two tabs of one person make a majority), so it plays on with whoever still listens to it. What the checks keep of a browser's ships (their tracks, beams, blasts, launches and strikes) goes with it when it leaves, by its id, whatever ship ids it named; and they keep nothing for an id no ship here has but its beams, the evidence of what it hit (a bot a new lead adopted under a new id fires before its first state arrives), dropped once they are older than any evidence counts. Only a newcomer takes an asteroid field, once, from the lead it elects, a browser that was there first; one that has taken none from the lead it follows once its 6 seconds of settling are over (the field came while it elected another, or was lost) starts over, and the lead hands it the field again. Callsigns lose control and invisible format characters on every screen alike (the own menu included), so none is left empty.
- **Rounds** last five minutes for everyone; the scoreboard shows over the running game, and the next round starts after 12 seconds. Meanwhile nothing scores and no mode's rule moves on: no kill, gate, core or part of the battleship counts, so the scoreboard stays what was paid out. A round is not decided in its first 3 seconds, while the other browsers' ships may still carry the last round's deaths and finishes. The pause menu does not stop the tournament.
- **A relay for strict networks.** Some networks (e.g. strict company firewalls or carrier-grade NAT) block direct connections between browsers. For them the game uses Cloudflare's TURN relay: when joining a tournament or opening the list of public tournaments (the lobby connects the same way), the browser asks the game's own site (`/turn`, a Cloudflare Pages Function in `functions/turn.ts`) for credentials that hold one hour, and fetches fresh ones every 50 minutes for the connections it has and the ones to come; the TURN key itself stays on the server. Only the game's own domains answer (the raw Pages addresses and pages of other sites get nothing), and a rate-limiting rule of the Cloudflare zone (5 requests per 10 seconds and IP) keeps scripts from collecting credentials in bulk. The data stays end-to-end encrypted (WebRTC); the relay only passes it on. Cloudflare's first 1,000 GB a month are free, and Cloudflare sets no spending cap on TURN, so the function sets its own: it reads the relay's outbound traffic of the month from Cloudflare's analytics and hands out no credentials beyond 500 GB, or when that number cannot be read. Without a relay the HUD says why next to the tournament's name ("Relay off: monthly budget spent", "no answer", …), so a Host who cannot connect knows where it fails; the function names only such a fixed reason, and what Cloudflare's analytics or credentials service answered (a status, an error's text) stays in the function's log. Without an answer within three seconds (the dev server, a project without a TURN key) the game connects with STUN alone.

For development, `?local=1#t=code` connects tabs of the same browser without the internet.

## Sharing

A small button with the share glyph sits at the end of the radio box in the top right corner of every screen (menu, plates, rounds, the flight school), and alone in the top right corner of the radio page and the Pilot's Handbook (`src/share.ts`).

- **What it shares.** Everywhere the game itself, https://solartournament.games/, with a line that fits where the Host is ("Five minutes. Die for the President. Again and again." from the menu, another from the flight school, a solo round, the radio, the handbook). In a running online tournament it shares that tournament's own link, the same one the address bar holds, public or private, so a friend joins this match at once: "I'm flying a round of SolarTournament right now, join me!" with the tournament's name. For a private tournament the menu says quietly that anyone with the link can join.
- **How.** On a touch device with a share sheet of its own (the Web Share API), the button opens it. Elsewhere it opens a small menu: copy the link ("Copied" confirms it; where the browser keeps the clipboard closed, the link stands in the menu to be selected by hand), e-mail (a `mailto:` link), and the plain share links of X, Bluesky, WhatsApp, Telegram, Reddit, LinkedIn, Facebook and Threads, each in a new tab. No network's script, button or pixel is loaded; a network hears of the link only when the Host clicks its name.
- **In a round** the round runs on, online and solo; while the menu is open the mouse does not steer, so the Host can reach it, and a pointer lock, if any, is released. Escape or a click outside closes it.

## Flight school

The flight school has five courses, each played on its own from the school's plate: **Basic training** (flying), **Racer** (the race), **Gladiator** (the fight), **Arsenal** (torpedoes, overdrive, probes, the hook on a ship, the nova core) and **Tactician** (how to win a dogfight). The menu leads with the flight school until a Host has completed the basic training; after that the solo round is the main action (Enter opens its choices, Enter again launches it). On the school's plate Enter starts the first course not completed yet. Each course remembers the drill a Host has reached, by the drill's name, so drills added to a course later do not move it: after a reload it continues there ("Continue at drill n of m"), with the autopilot as the drills before left it, and "Start … over" below the course begins it again. A completed course says so for good ("✓ Completed."), also when the Host trains again, and the menu's button counts the completed courses; after a course the school's plate comes up again, with the next course a key away. Each course trains one maneuver per drill, in the order a Host needs them. Each drill comes with a short spoken line, the keys as big keycaps (the spoken words name no keys; the keycaps show those of the Host's keyboard, gamepad or touch screen), and markers in space (orange diamonds, a course of rings built as a race's coils and bracketed like its gates, red debris). A drill waits until the pilot has really done it, some are repeated two or three times, and a failed attempt or a dead clone starts the attempt over; a drill done counts only once the clone has lived through it 1.5 seconds more, so one flung into a rock right after (off the hook, say) does the drill again. Every attempt starts, and every drill ends, with the wreckage of the ships shot down cleared away, so a drone's plates flying on at its speed never strike the clone in the attempt or the drill after; the campaign's drills alike. Training clones are free: no sponsor break. After every drill the flight computer praises the pilot and explains the physics behind the maneuver. Every attempt runs in clear phases, one after the other: what is left to say of the last one (the praise, or why it failed) to its end, then the instruction to its end, and only then the drill's markers, drones or bots show up and the drill counts. Her lessons are never cut off: a new one waits until she has finished the last, and her other lines (hits, asteroids, warnings) stay silent while she teaches; the HUD shows them anyway. Every drill is flown in free drift, as every round is: each attempt begins with the autopilot off (the jink, the dodges and the dogfights leave it as the Host has it), and only basic training's last drill has the pilot switch it on and off again. The keycaps show every key a drill needs: the stick wherever the nose has to turn, the drive wherever the ship has to fly. A sparring bot counts only when the pilot downs it (its laser, a knockout); one that crashes on its own starts the attempt over. The target drone leaves the arena with the drill it belongs to.

**Basic training** (14 drills, all in free drift):

1. Briefing: Nicole introduces herself ("Hi, I am Nicole. Your personal flight computer."), then the world of the games in four sentences.
2. Turning: three markers 150 m off into the crosshair (arrows or mouse), at rest, where steering turns the nose and the ship stays where it is; Nicole says turning alone takes the ship nowhere, and the drive's keys show beside the stick's. A pilot who burns to a marker instead (within 40 m of it) has done it too.
3. Main drive, right after the turn that alone moves nothing: W up to 50 m/s.
4. Drifting: let go, nothing slows the ship down.
5. Looking back while drifting: steering only turns the nose and the ship slides on, the flight stays (the prograde marker shows it); the praise: where you look and where you fly are two different things.
6. Brake: press S once, the flight computer flips and burns; press it again once the speed suits, and the ship turns back to where it looked (at rest it does that by itself). It counts once the brake is ended below 30 m/s (the ship drifts at 60 m/s as the drill begins).
7. Flip and burn by hand, twice: hold X and W together; the drive fires once the nose points back; the S brake does not count here, and pressing it starts the attempt over at once ("By hand"). The drill counts once the drift along the course is gone; a little sideways drift may remain.
8. Fine aim: Shift steers slowly; center a marker 400 m off exactly. A pilot who burns to it instead (within 40 m of it) has done it too.
9. Cockpit view: C switches into the cockpit and back (not given on touch screens, which have no camera switch: Nicole goes straight on to the next drill).
10. The curve: the ship drifts at 100 m/s at four markers, each in a blue sphere 100 m in radius: 400 m ahead, then a quarter turn to the right on an arc of 1.3 km radius, and 600 m straight on after it. The pilot turns the nose to one marker after the other and burns, and the drive bends the flight towards it; the racing line runs through them, and once the drive is let go the ship flies straight on. A pilot more than 2.5 km from the next marker starts the attempt over.
11. Stopping at a marker, near and far: burn, then the brake in time. It counts once the ship rests (below 10 m/s) inside the blue sphere around the marker, 200 m in radius.
12. The impact warning: drifting at a rock with 100 m/s, braking or sliding past it; at rest, or past the rock and flying away from it, for a second without the warning on, it counts, before the warning ever came up too.
13. The autopilot: T on, and T off again; with it on the flight computer catches every drift and the ship flies where it points, an aid for a head busy with something else.
14. Basic training complete: racer or gladiator next.

**Racer** (8 drills):

1. What the racer learns: a clean start, the rings, their coils and the racing line, in the order a race runs.
2. The start: 3, 2, 1, BURN! in big neon while the clamps hold the ship; the drive has to light within 0.3 s before BURN! or 0.2 s after it, as in a race. Lit earlier, the drive stalls ("Too early"); not lit in time, the attempt starts over ("Too late").
3. The X: a course of four rings (each counts from either side), as wide as a race's gates and laid out like one: a gentle slalom, the rings about 830 m apart, with the racing line through them and the X where the flight crosses the next ring's plane, as in a race; steered into the ring, it glows azure. A ring missed (its plane crossed beside it) starts the attempt over ("Missed").
4. The coils: three rings in a long line 1.1 km apart, each through its inner third (as a race's gates, see Modes), so each throws the ship on by 40 m/s; a ring passed off-center or missed starts the attempt over ("Off center").
5. Brake zones: the ship drifts at the speed limit (250 m/s) at three rings, the first 1.5 km ahead, the second 240 m to its right and 210 m on, so the racing line bends with about 270 m radius between them, tighter than the autopilot turns at that speed (about 325 m), and glows orange before the bend; at about 230 m/s the ship takes it. The brake as a switch makes the ring: S pressed before the orange flips the ship and burns, S pressed again once the orange is gone turns it back to the attitude it had, onto the line; a ring missed starts the attempt over ("Too fast for that bend"), and so do the rings flown without the S brake ("With the brake").
6. The line at full speed: five rings weaving over 2.7 km, the line bending no tighter than about 1.2 km (never orange, even at a coil's 290 m/s), each ring a coil. The course has to be flown at 200 m/s on average along its racing line, plus 4 seconds for the burn up to speed (the pilot starts at rest, turned to the first ring; the marker counts the seconds left): only a drive held all the way gets there ("Too slow"), the nose leading each bend so the drive bends the flight through it. A ring missed starts the attempt over.
7. The hook: 500 m off a rock, facing it and drifting past it at 50 m/s (a drift the autopilot, if the Host switched it on, leaves alone until the hook bites); hook it with one press, swing around it while the winch reels in, and let go with a second press faster than 65 m/s (a few seconds of reeling). The rock's mark says each step (press the hook, the swing's speed against the 65 m/s, press it again), and Nicole calls the second press the moment the swing is fast enough. Letting go slower starts the attempt over.
8. A racer now.

The ring courses and the curve lie ahead of the pilot, or, where they would reach out beyond 85 % of the arena's edge, run towards the arena's center instead (the pilot turns to the first ring; the curve, the brake and the full-speed drills turn it there). Where their way would run through rock (within 180 m of it), they tilt up or down, by 15°, 30° or 45°, the first way clear (and start 800 m farther on where the arena has room, so the pilot can climb or dive into them): above or below the Rings of Saturn's sheet of ice, say. A ring or marker still in rock moves straight out of it. A pilot more than 2.5 km from the next ring has lost the course and starts the attempt over. Every other drill with a marker more than 100 m off shows the racing line to it too: from where the pilot was when the marker came up (or last moved 10 m, so it keeps pointing at a sparring partner, drone or torpedo on the move) to the marker, ending there, bowing around rock like a race's. The flight school loses no pilot to deep space: beyond the arena's edge the HUD says "Return to the field" without a count; only 9.4 km out, where the Belt's belt of rock begins, is a ship lost. A drill done counts even in the moment it also ends what a failure would be: the hit with the last torpedo is a hit, a kill with the shot that overheats the laser a kill.

**Gladiator** (12 drills):

1. What the gladiator learns: the laser, the heat, and the dogfight.
2. Shooting a resting drone: armor shrugs the laser off, five hits into the drive blow it up. A target drone keeps its drive turned to the pilot, wherever the pilot flies.
3. Shooting a drifting drone, again into the drive (the laser hits instantly, no lead needed).
4. Firing until the laser overheats.
5. Cooling down in the shadow of an asteroid: the marker stays behind the rock nearest as the drill begins.
6. Jinking: a gunner on the tail fires red shots at where the ship was a moment ago; pushing left and right with the thrusters (A/D) makes them go past. It counts after four changes of side; turning and drift do not matter, a hit only sparks.
7. Dodging debris that drifts straight at the ship, twice, without turning (more than 45°). The debris starts where its way to the ship is free of rock; one that bursts on rock anyway starts the attempt over.
8. Chasing a bot that flies but does not shoot: it runs, staying on its tail opens its drive.
9. Fighting a weak bot that shoots back: keep the armored nose on it, slide, and work onto its tail.
10. Bursts: an armed bot (skill 0.15) 400 m ahead that fights back. It counts once the bot is gone with its drive hit at least once on the way; gone with its drive whole, the attempt starts over and Nicole says why. A laser that overheats only locks for a while. Nicole says why it comes to that: armor just heats the laser.
11. Final exam: a real bot that knows the trick too. Win the dogfight.
12. Cleared for the tournament.

**Arsenal** (8 drills):

1. What the arsenal holds: torpedoes, the overdrive, the probes and the nova core.
2. Torpedo: two in the tubes (a drill's load; a ship of the tournament carries four), a target drone 1.2 km ahead; keep it near the crosshair until the lock holds, launch, and hit it with a torpedo (the laser's hits do not count). Both launched without a hit starts the attempt over.
3. Shooting one down: the drone, 1.5 km off, launches a torpedo at the pilot; the HUD marks it ("Torpedo"); the laser has to hit it before it arrives. Hit by it ("It got you"), or it gone past, burnt out or broken on rock ("It got past you"), starts the attempt over.
4. The overdrive: from rest, one press and the ship is past 140 m/s (the shove adds 200 m/s). The overdrive is ready at every attempt, however recently it was fired, and the pilot turns to open space 1.5 km deep first.
5. The hook on a ship: an unarmed bot 600 m ahead runs; hook it and reel it in within 300 m. A runner 1.5 km off and not hooked (half again the hook's reach), or crashed, starts the attempt over.
6. A probe 400 m ahead carries two torpedoes and waits there: fly through it (the praise names the probes' colors, white the nova core, which the next drill hands over).
7. The nova core: the clone holds one, a drone 1.2 km ahead, beyond the blast's 800 m; fly close, fire, and both go up (the next clone is on the sponsors). A nova set off out of reach leaves the drone alive and starts the drill over.
8. The arsenal is ready.

**Tactician** (12 drills; every sparring bot of the flight school fights from a cool laser):

1. What the tactician learns: most clones die flying a straight line.
2. On the tail: an armed bot (skill 0.15) starts 300 m behind the ship, its nose on the drive; turn the armored nose to it and shoot it down.
3. The dodge, twice: debris drifts straight at the ship at 150 m/s from 600 m, too fast to slide out of its way; a thruster tapped twice kicks the ship aside. It counts once the debris is past and a dodge was fired, without turning (more than 45°). Debris that bursts on rock on its way starts the attempt over.
4. Turn, shoot, back on course: the ship drifts at 150 m/s towards a buoy 2 km ahead, a target drone drifting along 1 km behind it (far enough that the plates of a drone shot down, flying on along the ship's line, come only after the turn back to the buoy). S flips the nose onto the drone (braking, the ship slows and the drone comes on), the laser takes it down, and S pressed again turns the ship back to the attitude it had, the nose on the buoy; it counts once the drone is down, the brake ended and the nose within 10° of the buoy for half a second. The drone shot down without the S brake starts the attempt over ("With the brake"), and so does a drone that passes the ship.
5. Shooting in free drift: the ship drifts at 100 m/s past an armed bot (skill 0.15) that starts 250 m to its right and 600 m ahead. Turn the nose onto the bot while the ship slides on, and hit it three times with the nose more than 60° off the flight. A bot shot down with fewer such hits starts the attempt over ("Sideways"). The same tip comes in a deathmatch (see Nicole coaches).
6. The shove ("dirty is the point"): the ship and a drone drift side by side at 20 m/s towards a rock 200 m ahead, the drone 45 m off the ship's left, its way passing 20 m off the stone. A dodge into the drone as the rock comes up throws it at the stone at about 39 m/s; the drone starts with 15 hull points, a wreck already, so the stone finishes it, a knockout. It counts once the shoved drone touches rock or goes down; the rock left behind along the drift (however the nose is turned) starts the attempt over.
7. Cover from a hunter: the ship 150 m off a rock, facing it, an armed bot (skill 0.5) 1.2 km off to its right, with nothing between them at the start (the drill takes the nearest rock where that holds); three seconds in a row with the rock between them (the HUD marks the spot in the rock's lee, ahead and to the left).
8. Cover from a torpedo: the same, with a drone 2 km off to the right that launches a torpedo at the pilot (it would arrive in about 6 s; a middling bot's dash into the lee breaks its lock within 3 to 4 s); the rock has to break its lock, or the torpedo breaks on it. Hit by it, or shooting it down instead, starts the attempt over.
9. The orbit, at the Event Horizon: the Host's link goes to a clone printed there for the drill (and back to the Rings of Saturn after it). The ship starts 1.2 km straight above the black hole, out of the plane its rocks circle in and inside their ring, its nose across the line to the hole and drifting that way at 100 m/s (a circular orbit there flies 181 m/s). Burn along the nose until the readout says STABLE ORBIT (from about 157 m/s; see Orbit readout), let go, and fly half a turn about the hole in a stable orbit; whatever breaks the orbit (a burn too many, the autopilot on) starts the count again, and leaving the arena starts the attempt over. A middling bot flying the circle at its orbit speed gets ORBIT READY within about a second and the half turn in about 12 s more (`courses.test.ts`). The praise: in orbit you fall all the time and keep missing, which is why the alarms stay quiet up there.
10. The rescue, at the Event Horizon too: the drive shot down to 30 %, the ship at rest straight above the hole just inside where that drive outpulls it (about 1 km), its nose away. Burning away flat out, it still falls in; the alarm calls the overdrive at once (OVERDRIVE · AWAY FROM IT, "Overdrive, now!"), and fired within about ten seconds it carries the ship out; it counts once the ship is twice that far from the hole, the overdrive fired. A Host who burns away and fires the overdrive a second after the call gets out, one who only burns falls in (`courses.test.ts`). The praise: the overdrive is one big kick, and the drive cools while it carries the ship up; fired while the drive alone still gets out, the cold drive drops the ship right back in. The drive is whole again for the exam.
11. Final exam: a bot as good as the field's middling one (skill 0.5). Win the dogfight.
12. A tactician now.

## The Pilot's Handbook

The flight school teaches one maneuver per drill; what does not fit into a drill stands in the Pilot's Handbook of the Ministry of War (`handbook.html`), a page of the site in the game's four languages: a first look at the world (the Host and the clone, the Orange President and his Games, the Rockborn and the "aliens", the depot ships and the golden carrier, the sponsors, the radio and Nicole), each paragraph in the Ministry's words and under it the plain truth, which no brochure would print: Nicole's comment on the paragraph ("Note from Nicole"), shown beside it the way a reviewer's comment shows in a document's margin, joined to its passage by a dashed line and lighting it up when pointed at (on a screen too narrow for the margin, right under it); the controls on keyboard and mouse, gamepad and touch screen, flying with the autopilot and in free drift, racing, the dogfight, the dirty tricks the rules allow, every mode, the arenas, hazards and alarms, points and the hangar, and a word on honor among Hosts. Its headings speak the Ministry's bombast, its text is practical, and its numbers are the game's (see `src/handbook/`). The menu links it, the flight school's plate too, and the pause menu opens it at the section of the mode being flown; every section and the main maneuvers have an anchor of their own (`handbook.html#torpedoes`).

## Radio 2584

The tournament broadcaster pumps techno into every cockpit, composed live by the game with [Tone.js](https://tonejs.github.io/). There are no fixed tracks: every round grows its own track from one of 1,168 titles, and a title comes again only by chance (within 20 rounds about once in seven times), unless someone remembers it. **The title is the seed**: "Orbit Terror" always grows the same track, note for note.

**The game master is the DJ** (`src/music/dj.ts`). A room plays a programme: song after song from the room's code, one a round, every browser alike. The game master steps it back to a song worth hearing again or forward to set what comes, with the two buttons on its radio box (shown on its screen alone, in a round), the keys , and . (in fullscreen too), the d-pad's left and right on a paused gamepad, or on the pause menu's plate "Game master (and DJ)", and can type a title's words there (letters of any script, digits, spaces, hyphens and apostrophes, at most 40 characters and six words): the same words always make the same song, so any title heard once plays again. The song starts from its beginning the moment it is chosen; the next round goes on with the programme from where the DJ stepped it, and a typed title ends with its round. Online the lead browser's round state carries the DJ's word (its step, a typed title, and when the song began), so every browser plays the same song at the same beat, a Host joining late hears it where it is, and no other Host's browser can put a song on air. Alone the Host is the DJ of its round; a solo round's pause plays the menu's song, a new one each time the page opens, and the DJ's song once the round runs again. The flight school and the campaign have no DJ (the campaign plays its music by situation).

**Musical direction.** The maintainer is a big fan of Kraftwerk and Daft Punk, and of everything early techno had to offer: hard beats and that certain underground feeling. It was a great time that he was maybe a little too young for, so we live it out here. Radio 2584 therefore sounds like the underground: driving, raw and distorted, with machine-like sequences, and now and then the German *Autobahn* feeling of Kraftwerk, robot voices with a rolling R included. Tracks must never become predictable: every round takes its own path.

Decisions so far:

- Warm and driving most of the time, hard now and then: a radio that is hard all the time gets switched off. The main sound is *drive*, an analog night drive of the eighties (a bassline shaped live on a synthesizer, its filter and envelope the song); French is the second. Chiptune and lounge music do not fit.
- Hard flavors stay, as guests; schranz and generic rave are not the maintainer's taste and only play rarely.
- German words in the robot voice, but only now and then.
- Cheerful only where something is cheerful: the tournament's rounds and the flight school keep the station's dark sound; the campaign uses French house in a bright key (mixolydian) as a means of style for a scene in a good mood, the station's own sound rather than a pop flavor of its own.
