- The campaign plays music by situation, background only (no station, no voice): calm while little happens, the station's sound without a pushing beat (no kick, clap, rumble or fills); bright for a cheerful scene; a fight's full arc when it gets dangerous, turning mid-mission the moment the danger comes; silence where a moment wants none; each place its own sound, chosen by hand (the Boneyard drive and industrial, Rheasilvia motorik and acid, Occator rave and schranz) (`docs/campaign/STORY.md`, Music).
- Daft Punk styles and elements are always welcome: loops that a filter opens and closes, phaser, pumping compression, vocoder robot voices, phrases chopped into the beat word by word, and the raw, distorted early sound.

- **Driving, not exhausting.** Mostly 116 to 132 BPM, the hard guests up to 152: a round lasts five minutes, but people play many rounds, and peak-time speed and full distortion wear the ears out over an evening.
- **Flavors** (how often they come up):
  - *Drive* (12 in 28, the main sound): a warm night drive of the eighties, 116–124 BPM. One analog bassline is the lead, the pedal bass: a two-bar loop galloping on the root (half the sixteenths and more), now and then an octave up, and three or four held notes of a second voice an octave higher, off the beat (on the "e" and the "a"), the first bar calling and the second answering, either arching up the scale and back down or leaning on the root's neighbours (the flat second above, the flat seventh below). Its voice, after a synthesizer player's basslines as measured (bright, full saws, the filter never closed dark, no distortion to speak of): two free-running saws 12 to 16 cents apart, every note's pitch off by up to 7 cents and the track's tuning by up to 5 (seeded, so every browser plays the same), through a steep low-pass that follows the note (8 times its frequency with the timbre closed, some 600 Hz on the root, up to 24 times open, some 1.9 kHz), snaps 2.5 octaves above that on every note and falls back in a straight line within 0.13 s; the notes sound 95 % of their length and ring out over 0.12 to 0.2 s into the next, so the filter shapes every note, not the gate; then an effect (a shallow chorus, a phaser or a flanger) and hardly any drive. The pedal sits on C2 to F2 (65 to 87 Hz), the lower wave an octave under it. The track hardly changes its notes and grows by the bass's timbre (the strategy *timbre*, below); the loop moves through a turnaround bar by bar: sixteen bars on the root, then the fourth, the fourth, the root, the root, the fifth, the fourth, the root, the root. The scale is phrygian in two tracks in three (minor with the flat second, darker) and natural minor in the rest. A drum machine's round kick two semitones under the pedal (its pitch falling only from two or three times its fundamental within 50 ms, a short body, then a sub tail dying away over 0.25 to 0.35 s, little mid and a quiet click), claps on two and four that stand out over it (four bursts 9 ms apart, a plateau of noise 0.1 s long around 1.25 kHz with a sizzle above 2 kHz, 4 dB louder than the other flavors' clap and only a short room), hats in eighths, an open hat and a quiet FM bell only at the peaks, no rumble, no toms or metal, no air-raid siren and no crushed drums among its surprises; the music ducks only to 85 % under each kick, and the drive on kick and bass stays below half. Its ending, as the hits of the eighties end: eight toms falling down the scale over the last half bar before the finale and a crash on its first beat; the finale (the round's last minute) lifted by one or two semitones, the track's own, with the hook on top (the second voice's notes an octave over the bass, each held to the next, on a bright, brassy lead: three detuned saws whose filter swells open a little late, through a slow chorus, with echo); then the outro is a cadence of a bar on the fifth and a bar on the fourth, one big hit on the root (kick, crash, the bass and its lower wave held for a bar) and silence while the reverb rings out. Drive lifts its key only so, never by the key-lift surprise.
  - *French* (5): a chord loop that a filter opens over four bars and closes over the next four, phaser, pumping, 126–132; its bass is offbeat, an octave pulse or drive's pedal bass. The tournament's radio never plays it bright; a cheerful scene of the campaign asks for French in mixolydian, major with the flat seventh, bright and funky.
  - *EBM* (3): electronic body music, a sequenced 16th bass (root, root, octave, root, accents before the beat) or an octave bass whose 16th echo turns it into a pulse (Giorgio Moroder), arpeggios in one track in three, 132–140.
  - *Hard* (2): an offbeat bass (rolling in one track in three) under a heavy rumble, 140–148 BPM.
  - *Industrial* (2): bit-crushed hats, cowbells and claves in Euclidean patterns, a little swing, 134–141.
  - *Acid* (2): a squelching 303 line carries the track, 138–144.
  - *Schranz* and *Rave* (1 each): the fastest (148–152) and tom-heavy, or stabs or a hoover (142–148).
  - *Motorik*, one track in thirteen: Kraftwerk's machine pop. An electro beat, octave bass, square-wave plings, a pad, 126–134 BPM, a German title and a German robot voice.
- **Strategies.** The title also picks how the track develops, so no two rounds follow the same recipe:
  - *Classic*: grooves and peaks, kick-less breakdowns, re-entries.
  - *Relentless* (after Jeff Mills): no breakdown at all. Layers drop out for two bars at irregular moments, every 16 bars the tops take a bar off, and twice the track strips down to the kick and few of its lines for four bars.
  - *Hypnotic*: one long accumulation over two thirds of the round, layers entering as the energy climbs, one filter dip before the finale.
  - *Movements*: the first half belongs to the lead, then four bars of kick and bass, and the second half to a sequenced bass; the lead never returns (it only enters once the energy reaches it, so the gentler leads of acid, French and schranz tracks are not heard in this form at all).
  - *Switcher*: the felt tempo changes; a half-time section (kick on one, clap on three) and a broken-kick section (kicks on one and the "and" of three) between the straight ones.
  - *Fake drop*: after a breakdown, four bars that sound like the drop but hold back the low end, two bars of gap, then the real drop.
  - *Polymeter*: percussion loops of 5, 7 or 9 steps and a 7-step acid line drift against the bar and fall back to the track's regular loops in the finale (12 or 16 steps for the acid).
  - *Timbre* (drive's own; the other flavors pick among the seven above, motorik among relentless, hypnotic and movements): the notes hardly change, the sound does, as a synthesizer player builds a bassline live. The intro is the bass alone with its filter nearly closed, opening over sixteen bars, the kick joining after eight; then the grooves with claps and hats; the lower wave, a square an octave under the pedal's root, comes in at an energy of 0.55; four bars of the bass and its effect alone (chorus, phaser or a flanger, the track's own, on from an energy of 0.6); a peak of eight bars with the filter wide open, the resonance up (Q 8 instead of 1.5) and the cutoff sweeping 0.7 octaves up and down over four bars; a breakdown; once more; the finale; and the ending (see Drive). The hard moments stay short: eight bars at a time.
- **Surprises.** Every round gets three to five of these, each kind at most once, 12 bars apart (8 where a track has no room for more), after stable stretches (a big surprise works best when the listener is sure what comes next): a filter dip of four bars, a delay throw that swells into feedback, an air-raid siren, a bit-depth drop on the hats (in industrial and EBM tracks and half of the others), a key lift of 2, 3 or 5 semitones for the rest of the track (only in the last stretch before the finale), a glitch of two bars (never in drive): the link breaks up as a corrupted picture does, every sixteenth draws an intensity of 0 to 3 from the title, and at 3 a burst of white noise sounds, its band at 600 Hz and climbing an octave with each burst that follows straight on, up to 4.8 kHz, as a storm of them tears the picture apart. Half of the breakdowns open with a bomb into the reverb. Breakdowns end in a rotating way: a beat of silence, a kick roll (quarters, eighths, sixteenths), a stutter, or a rug pull (a whole bar of silence). A section before a breakdown (a peak or a groove) may die in a tape stop: bass, acid, hoover, bell and arpeggio slide two octaves down, the level falls away.
- **The kick** is a sine whose pitch drops in two stages, saturated hard, with a noise click on top, and every track has its own:
  - the pitch starts at 10 to 26 times the fundamental, falls to 4 to 9 times it within 8 to 25 ms, then to the fundamental within another 60 to 180 ms;
  - the body rings for 40 to 80 % of a beat;
  - the distortion is gabber (tanh, then atan), hard clipping, a warm tanh or a metallic wave fold, depending on flavor and seed;
  - the click, the brightness (lowpass at 2.5 to 9.5 kHz), the rumble's upper limit (110 to 230 Hz), its level and how fast it swells back all come from the title.
  
  The fundamental lies between 41 and 62 Hz and sets the key of the track; a drive track's key lies between C1 and F1 (33 to 44 Hz), its kick two semitones under the pedal bass (see Drive).
- **The rumble.** A copy of the kick runs through a long reverb, distortion and a band from 30 Hz up to 110 to 230 Hz (set per track), in mono. Every kick ducks it, and it swells back before the next one: the low end breathes on the offbeat. Bass, acid and stabs pump with the kick as well, down to 35 to 45 % of their level in the hard flavors, 60 % in French and motorik and 85 % in drive, where a relentless pump would wear the ear out. The bass leaves the sub to the rumble and mostly stays off the kick; only the EBM and Moroder bass lines run straight through it.
- **Patterns.** Every step of a bar has a weight for every instrument; a step plays when its weight beats the instrument's density, and density follows the energy of the track. More energy adds notes in a fixed order instead of reshuffling them, so build-ups sound intended, but the density ranges are narrow: energy brings in the strong steps and a few weak ones and never fills every gap, so even the finale keeps its groove (about 40 to 55 hits a bar across all parts). More energy comes from layers, filter and drive, not from more notes. Hats run in eighths with a sixteenth here and there, open hats on the offbeat, claps on two and four (ghost claps only in fills), percussion in Euclidean rhythms of at most two hits in five steps on a loop of 5 to 16 steps that drifts against the bar; arpeggios play eighths. Every 16 bars each part may mutate a little; the kick never does.
- **One key, room to breathe.** Every note of every part, whatever the chord shape, the motif or the acid line asks for, lands on the track's scale, natural minor, (in two drive tracks in three) phrygian or (in a cheerful scene of the campaign) mixolydian (a note outside it moves a semitone onto it: down on the minor scales, up on the bright one, so a minor third is heard as the major third there), so the parts never rub against each other, and a key lift moves them all together. Of the melodic parts (acid, lead, chords) and of the extra percussion (ride, toms, cowbell and claves) at most two play at once; which one rests changes every eight bars.
- **The acid line** loops over 12 or 16 steps, its note sets and step weights after Vitling's [Endless Acid Banger](https://github.com/vitling/acid-banger) (CC BY 4.0). Accents throw the resonant filter wide open, slides glide into the next note, often an octave up.
- **Arrangement.** A round runs intro, grooves, peaks and breakdowns on an 8-bar grid, with short strips of 2, 4 or (in polymeter tracks) 8 bars between some sections; energy moves through breakdowns and re-entries. In a breakdown the kick drops out while hats and a thin bass without its low end keep the rhythm going, the filter opens, a noise riser climbs over the last four bars and the breakdown ends in one of its endings (see below); the next section crashes in. At the end of a section a fill marks the phrase, and now and then within one (two in five 16-bar phrases, one in five 8-bar phrases): kicks added or removed, a hat roll, a clap burst, a tom run. The last minute of the round is the finale with every layer and the most distortion (85 % drive: some headroom stays); after the round an outro takes the track down.
- **Loud, not harsh.** The mix ends in a glue compressor and a soft clipper instead of a hard limiter. The shared dotted-eighth echo (set to each track's tempo, like the bass's sixteenth echo) and the reverb sit well behind the dry parts (echo send 0.3 with 25 % feedback and no lows, reverb send 0.5), so no note is smeared across the beat.
- **More instruments.** Besides kick, rumble, hats, ride, clap and bass: Euclidean toms, an 808-style cowbell (two square tones at 587 and 845 Hz through a band at 2.64 kHz), claves, an FM bell, a hoover, a square-wave arpeggio, distorted chord stabs, the french chord loop, the motorik pad, and drive's pedal bass with its lower wave (see Drive above).
- **The voice of the regime.** A robot voice announces the station and the track ("Radio twenty five eighty four. Now playing. Orbit Terror.") and drops the propaganda of the Orange President and his sponsors over the music, most often 5 to 8 times a round (2 to 11 in all): at section changes and now and then in between, in the finale more often. It speaks sentence after sentence, or chops a line onto the beat one word per eighth; three chopped lines in ten stammer, as a sampler loops a cut of a record: the opening of the first word restarted six times, a sixteenth apart, the line following half a bar after the first. At most once a round, at the first section past the middle where the voice speaks, the station calls its name as such a jingle ("ra-ra-ra-ra-ra-radio twenty five eighty four"). Every word must stay understandable: the clear voice carries it, a touch of ring modulation, bit crushing and grit only colors it, a compressor makes it loud, the music steps back while it talks, and its last word is thrown into the echo. German now and then; in motorik tracks mostly German.
- **The announcer** cuts in over the radio after kills: static, a cynical line, static. "An excellent death for Juno." "The alien is dead. Now our nation can grow again." Human callsigns are spoken as "pilot" (the voice is rendered ahead of time) and shown by name. He comments on kills and on crashes into rocks (those of the ships this browser runs; another browser does not say how its ships crashed); a clone lost in deep space or to wreckage goes uncommented, and so does every death in the flight school, which is on no broadcast. At the start of a round the announcer opens the broadcast. He is driven hard into his own compressor and the music steps back to a fifth while he talks, so he stands clearly above the loud, compressed track. Two voices never talk at once: an announcement waits until the voice on air is through and no line of the track is due within five seconds, and is dropped after eight seconds without room.
- **Now playing.** The title of the running track, with flavor and tempo, is always in view in the top right corner. The note button next to it switches the radio off and on (also key M), the slider beside it sets the volume (remembered, and shared with the radio page). A click on the title opens the radio page in the same tab: the game is left, and only the radio page plays. While a round is flown, the click pauses the round instead (a shot aimed into the corner never leaves it), and from the pause menu the click leaves. The radio page leads back to the game through its seal and its footer. Reached another way (from the Pilot's Handbook, opened beside a round in a tab of its own), the radio page tells the browser's other tabs while it plays, once a second, and the game's radio in them stays silent until 3 s after it last heard so: only one tab plays music.
- **The radio page** (`radio.html`): enter any title, or roll a random one, and the track it grows plays again in the browser: the shape of the track as a bar of sections, a playhead that can be dragged (or moved with the arrow keys) to jump anywhere in the track, a volume slider, the transmissions on screen. Since every note follows from the title and the position in the track, a jump plays exactly what the track holds there. "Download WAV" presses the whole track (5 minutes plus outro) offline into a 16-bit stereo WAV file of about 55 MB, in one pass without seams. The band plays into the render as it does live, about 1.5 seconds ahead of it: the render stops every 512 blocks of 128 samples, takes the next stretch of steps and goes on, so its audio graph only holds the notes that sound around it, and the render time grows with the length of the music, not faster (notes scheduled all at once would sit in the graph from the start and slow down every block). A track takes under a minute on a desktop, about a sixth of its length. The last pressing is kept: "Download again" under the button saves it again as often as wanted, also after a reload (the browser keeps the file, only the last one, in its IndexedDB), and "Download WAV" on the same title saves the kept file at once instead of pressing it anew. The page's footer links the recipes (`radio.html#recipes`, `src/music/recipes.ts`): every flavor's share of the tracks, tempo, swing, bass, lead, kick, pump, scale, how its tracks develop and from what energy each part plays, read from the generator's own tables, with what it sounds like in words and a button that tunes in a track of it.
- **The flight computer** has a voice of its own, independent of the radio: a natural, breathy, quick woman's voice that flirts with the pilot while preparing it for death. Whatever the screen tells the Host, it also says: damage reports, impacts, a cracked asteroid, leaving the field, the lost clone and the sponsor's pitch, the end of a round, and every step of the flight school. Its lines are rendered whole, not word by word, so they sound natural.
- **The crowd.** The Games are watched on Earth, in stadiums packed for the public viewing, and the broadcast carries their roar into the headset (vacuum carries none), so it follows the radio's volume and switch and fades with a lost clone's link. It is a recording of a roaring crowd, looped and sent through the stadium's overdriven loudspeakers (a soft clip and a broadcast's band); when it erupts, a second layer of it joins, off in time and a little higher. It rises with every number of a race's countdown and erupts at the start. The crowd belongs to the broadcast of a round: it murmurs while a round is flown and seen, and the broadcast leaves the stadium otherwise. At a round's end it cheers the result on its feet for 3 seconds and fades out over the results in about 4 more; a round left from the pause menu gets no cheer. The pause menu and the sponsor break after a death (the broadcast cuts to the ads) hush it within about a second and a half, and the murmur comes back over 2 seconds with the round or the next clone; the menu and the hangar hush it, a cheer still fading included. The flight school is on no broadcast and has no crowd.
- **Nicole does not repeat herself.** What comes again and again in a round (its start, a lost clone, the next clone, its end, a race's places, a capture) she says from a pool of lines: 12 for the start, 17 for a lost clone, 12 for the next clone, 8 for a round's end, 8 for a pass, three or four for the lead, a lost lead, being passed and a capture. She never says one of the last few of a pool again (half the pool, at most five) and often says nothing: a third of the starts, 40 % of the lost clones, 45 % of the next clones, 30 % of the ends, and a quarter more often within 20 s of her last word. Between any two of these lines lie at least 6 s; she calls a race's lead or a lost one at most every 30 s, and praises a pass at most every 45 s and only two times in five, about once a lap. Pet names stand in at most a quarter of these lines. Her warnings (damage, a torpedo, the reactor, a well, the air, a comet, the last clone) never pass through this and are always said.
- **Nicole coaches in real rounds.** A Host who steers with the mouse is told as the round begins that the pointer turns the nose ("The ship turns towards the mouse pointer", on screen and in her voice) in the first three rounds only; the flight school's basic training, which teaches steering, shows it every time, the other courses not. The first round a Host plays of each mode opens with her briefing right after she reports in (online once the round's mode is known, in every round of a mixed room): what the mode is about and how to win it (race, deathmatch, Last Clone Standing, Capture the Flag, the President's Fleet). In the fight she gives a tip the moment it matters: a ship ON YOUR TAIL (turn the nose to it), a laser overheated (short bursts, only when the crosshair glows), a torpedo inbound (shoot it down or put a rock between), and in a deathmatch with the autopilot on an enemy within 500 m, more than 60° off the nose and crossing faster than 60 m/s (free drift: slide on and turn the nose onto it). A tip comes at most once a round and three rounds long, a briefing once; then the Host is trusted with it. The browser keeps what it has heard. Not in the flight school, which teaches on its own.
- **Transmissions on screen.** Whatever the voice and the announcer say runs across the screen while it is spoken: the regime in orange neon, the announcer in blue.
- **The voice is pre-rendered.** `npm run voice` speaks every word and every sentence of `src/music/lines.ts` with [espeak-ng](https://github.com/espeak-ng/espeak-ng) (its robosoft variant for the regime, a livelier voice for the announcer) and the flight computer's lines with the neural voice Kokoro (in English; in German, Spanish and French Nicole speaks as her clone, made with Chatterbox, see `tools/voice/README.md`) into MP3 sprites at build time (`npm run build` renders it; it is not kept in the repository; the Kokoro model is pinned to one revision and ffmpeg to the SHA-256 of its binary, both kept in the build cache, and a build whose lines, tool and engine versions did not change copies the sprite from the build cache; a build for the site stops rather than going out without Nicole's clones); the game plays whole sentences, so they keep their melody, places single words on the beat, and needs no speech engine. Browser speech synthesis cannot be used: its output can neither be routed through Web Audio nor timed to the beat, and it sounds different on every device.
- **Everyone hears the same beat.** In an online tournament the room code and the round number choose the title, and the music runs on the lead browser's round clock. Every note is an integer hash of seed, bar and step, so a browser can jump to any point of the track; it compares its music with the clock several times per second and jumps when they drift apart. All Hosts of a tournament hear the same note at the same moment (up to the network delay).
- **Hangar.** In the menu the arena's groove comes through the hangar wall: lower energy, filtered, never peaking. The flight school plays no music at all, so every word of Nicole is heard.
- The radio sits in the cockpit and plays straight into the headset, not through the hull. When the clone dies, its radio fades into the distance; the next clone hears it clearly again.

## The universe

SolarTournament has a universe of its own. It rests on two principles:

1. **Realistic physics**: ships keep their speed until a burn changes it, no sound in vacuum, no shields, hulls that break.
2. **Consciousness transfer into clones**: death is not the end. But the mind is bound to its clone over a live link, with a memory that never breaks: every death is lived through.

Humanity has settled the solar system. Fast fusion drives make hard burns between the planets possible, and daredevil pilots whip their ships around gravity wells at extreme speed for the thrill of it. The SolarTournament grew out of that daring and became the greatest entertainment of its time: pilots, printed clones of their Hosts, fight each other to the death in ships among the asteroids, while audiences across the system watch from a safe distance. Gladiator games for the space age.

The clones die, and the Hosts come back. Their minds are linked to cloned human bodies; when a ship is destroyed, the link moves on to the next clone, in the next ship. That is why every round has respawns, and why the audience never runs out of champions. The clones come out of a 3D printer: bodies printed by the dozen, and everyone knows it. A print is not cheap, though it is no fortune either: costly enough that anyone short of money thinks twice before spending a body. And prints differ by price: the rich have theirs printed sturdy and resilient, built to last; a cheap print wears out and breaks down after a fairly short time. In the Games the sponsors pay for every body; outside them, whoever dies pays for the next one, and a debtor's next print goes on its debt.

**Transfer and its flaws.** A mind can be transferred into a new clone again and again, without limit: new bodies can always be printed. But as with copying data, errors can creep in; they need not. A sound mind, one that has had a good life and little stress, copies cleanly; a battered one, worn down by sickness, misery or too many deaths, copies with flaws, and the flaws multiply faster with every copy. Whether they can be repaired is unknown; it seems they cannot. In the end the technology always leads to madness, but when and how is as uncertain as with the errors of dividing cells: one mind holds for centuries, another frays within a few lives.

**What a pilot wears.** A clone flies in a thin pressure suit and a helmet: little protection, but enough for the fight it is made for. A fighter in a fight is holed, and its compartments decompress (see Decompression), so the pilot sits in thin air or none for minutes, which such a suit outlasts; open space for long it does not.

The ships are not printed. Centuries of war left the system's depots full of old fighters, decommissioned, rusting in their rows, and the Games fly those: cheaper to burn them up in a show, clone and all, than to scrap them. Every interceptor that launches has been patched, repainted and sent out again more often than anyone counts, so none of them looks new. Only the President's own battleship comes fresh from the newest dock, and it shines.

**What the hulls look like.** The human interceptor wears olive paint over riveted panels, flaked off in small chips down to bare, bright steel (most of them along the seams, each in a rim of dull primer), scratched through to the metal, streaked with rust from every seam, grimy, stenciled over and over (a hull number, NO STEP, FUEL, the depot's mark), every stencil reading the right way round on either flank; its tall fins are ribbed plates in riveted frames. Its stern, what a pilot sees most, is a drive block with steel ribs and vents, two open drive bells, black with soot inside, their steel tinted by the heat from straw and bronze to blue at the rim and banded by two cooling rings, and red tail lights on the fin tops and the stern. The alien interceptor is a deep violet-black skin of fine overlapping scales, each one catching the light on its own, under a thin film that plays in violet, green and gold as the angle changes; it mirrors the sky twice as strongly as painted plates, a few of its scales' rims glow faintly in the cyan of its life support, and its outline glows faintly violet, so its shape still shows where the sun does not reach; it is worn and scarred, its drive pods open and sooty at the stern. The Sovereign is a gift of the regime and the least worn: ivory plates, flush, welded and bonded, without a single rivet (rivets belong to the age of steam), parted only by thin, crisp panel lines, with a few access hatches outlined the same way and flush bolts sunk into their corners, its only fasteners; its hull number 01 is painted in gold, outlined dark, 1 m tall on both flanks of the nose, between the fore thrusters and the wing roots, upright and reading the right way round from either side, at the same place on every Sovereign; and gold everywhere else (the canopy's frame, the wing tips, the canards, the nacelles, the thrusters), brushed along its length and engraved in bands of interlaced waves with the regime's stars between them; its drives' bells are gold too, sooty at the rim. Every fighter's maneuvering thrusters sit in clusters of beveled housings with two bands around them, each nozzle an open bell, sooty inside. Every fighter's paint lies under a thin clear lacquer, and its bodies are shaded round while boxes, caps and blade edges stay sharp: the sun, a drive's light or the hangar's lamps show on it as a small, crisp glint that slides across the hull as the ship turns, and a flat panel flashes for the moment it mirrors the sun. Out there no air dims the sun, so that flash blinds, with a wide halo, and the nearer the sun the harder: in the Corona nearly three times as hard as in the belt. Where the paint rusted or chipped, the lacquer is gone, and scratches dull it. The President's battleship wears heavy armor of dark gunmetal under a clear lacquer, new and barely touched, the armor of a warship and not of the age of steam: no rivets anywhere. Its plates run along the hull in strakes of different heights (3.5 to 16 m), most of them long, 30 to 55 m, a quarter shorter, 12 to 24 m, butted end to end with a welded seam ground nearly flush or set apart by a thin, crisp panel line cut flush between them, so neighboring plates read as one armored surface; now and then a whole band stands out as a heavier belt, and armor plates laid over the big panels step out of them, their edges beveled so the edge towards the sun catches the light. Along the forward flanks, under the trench, three belts of extra armor run along the hull in strakes of 45 to 130 m, each belt's butts where its own lengths put them. Between them sit access panels of 1.5 to 3.5 m, hatches 3 m square with flush bolts sunk into their corners (the only fasteners on the hull), recessed hardpoints and round sensor ports of dark glass. Each plate is a shade lighter or darker than its neighbors and a little more or less polished, its metal brushed along its length. On the deck the strakes run along the hull as on the flanks. It is metal enough to glint and mirror the sky, and the sun and the sky's light show its form on every face, the side away from the sun dim but never a black silhouette. Polished gold runs along every edge, around its turrets and its bridge, and spreads the sun along them; its runway is the regime's deep red. Its turrets turn on armored rings, their three barrels in sleeves with muzzle rings, a rangefinder across the roof and cooling vents in the back. The world ship of the Derelict carries centuries in space and the battle that broke it, cold now (see The Derelict): riveted plating pitted by micrometeorites, a few impact craters, a hole punched through here and there, soot streaked along the hull; its livery's band is off-white paint, worn, chipped and scratched, its name stenciled on in letters built from plain bars, the same on every device. The tournament's rings are grimy industrial plating with hazard stripes and stenciled coil numbers, each station module carrying a sponsor's sign. Sponsor probes are worn hulls with solar cells. A torpedo is a missile 13 m long, 3 m across the body: an ogive nose with the seeker's black glass window at its tip and its red eye glowing behind it, a round body in dark olive military paint with a yellow warhead band behind the nose, seams, an arming hatch and its type and lot stenciled along it twice, four strakes along the body and four fins at the tail crossed at 45° to them, a boat tail to the nozzle's bell, blued and sooted by the motor, its throat glowing orange; only the eye and the throat glow, the rest is lit by the scene. A destroyed fighter breaks into pieces of its hull: curved plates with jagged, torn outlines, bent where they tore, the fighter's worn paint on the outside, sooted towards the break, its innards on the inside (frames and stringers over yellow-green primer, bundles of cable), two stringers and a frame torn off with each piece. The torn edges and the frames' ends glow with the heat of the break, orange the moment it tore and fading to a dull red as they cool (with a time constant of 4 s), on a thin rim only, never white. All of it is drawn in code on small canvases (256 pixels; the fighters' hulls, seen closest, 512 pixels for every 20 m and their relief a normal map that fades evenly with distance instead of shimmering; the torpedo's casing 384 around by 512 along, about 2 pixels for every 5 cm of it, so its bands and stencils stay crisp at the closest look; the battleship's armor, whose plates a pilot flies past at a few meters, 512 pixels for every 100 m of hull and its relief 1024 (about 10 cm a pixel), a normal map that fades evenly with distance instead of shimmering, a tile long enough that its plates never line up into a pattern along the 710 m hull; the world ship's plating 512 pixels for every 34 m, its relief a normal map too) from fixed seeds, so every session shows the same wear, shared by everything that wears it; it is look only and changes no rule.

The one who receives all of it is the **Host**. Whoever plays the game is a Host and is addressed as one: the person at the other end of the link, the host of everything the clones live through. The Host is bound to the clone by a live link. The clone flies and fights on its own, because only a mind inside the cockpit is fast enough: every radio link lags, and in a dogfight a fraction of a second is a lifetime. But the link carries so much that everything the clone lives through streams back into the Host's memory as it happens. When the clone dies, the Host holds all of it, every memory and every pain, the death included. A Host remembers every death, and the Host's brain has to cope with it. Dying hurts, and nothing about it is free. The clones die, but the soul rots a little more each time.

**Whose eyes we see through.** In the hangar, in the menu, we are the Host. When a round starts, the screen says "Establishing live link to your clone", the picture breaks up, and from then on we are the clone in the cockpit: the game plays from the pilot's perspective. When the clone dies, the link is lost and we are the Host again, sitting through the advertisement that pays for the next body; when the sponsor is happy, the link is established to the next clone and we are back in the cockpit. Everything the Host sees and hears arrives over that link. When a hit or an impact shakes the clone, the link stutters: the picture breaks up like a corrupted video stream, the radio drops out, static crackles in the headset. When the clone dies, the link breaks down until the next clone is printed: the stream loses its key frames, the clone's last picture stays and rots for 2.2 seconds, carried along by every turn of the camera and burnt by everything that moves, until fresh blocks bring the picture back (a datamosh; not with reduced motion). Even the idle link to the clone hangar flickers now and then.

**Far from the body.** A Host's body need not travel with its clones. The clone decides everything itself anyway, so the link may lag as far as light allows: a Host whose clone flies at Jupiter lives its life some forty minutes late, and its death too. In the Games the Hosts' couches ride with the tournament, close to the arena, so a round is lived as it happens. Those in debt lie where their creditor keeps them: in the campaign (see The story) the Host lies in couch 14 of the cellar under a scrapyard's depot on Psyche, bound there by a debt it does not remember signing (a cheap first link burns the oldest memories), while its clones work; getting out of that couch is the Host's oldest wish. In the campaign a clone's death kills the cockpit, not the hull: a tug brings the ship back to its dock, where the next clone climbs in.

The future is broken, and it glows. Neon signs flicker over everything, every surface is for sale, every picture comes over a link that can break. The tournament's own sign is sun orange; the sponsors advertise in its complement, a cold azure blue.

It is a society without morals. A new clone is not free: the Host pays with attention. Before the next body boots, the Host has to watch an advertisement. Want another clone? Make the sponsor happy.

The sponsors of 2584 sell what a dying system needs, and every ad hides the truth in the small print. **Victory Cola**, now with less sugar than before (412 g per can before, 409 g now). **Victory Cola Zero**: zero sugar, zero taste, zero questions. **Space Burger**, flame-grilled in a fusion drive, 100 % meat-like, printed in the same printers as the clones. **PrintMe Bodies**: premium skin with 40 % less pain, which does not reduce the pain, only the awareness of it. **Oxygen+**: 21 % oxygen on the gold plan, 17 % on basic, Rockborn not eligible. **Solar Wall Bonds**: Mars pays the interest, Mars has not agreed. **DreamLink**, a memory filter to sleep through one's deaths, in beta, not available to Hosts. **Orange Steaks**, tremendous steaks that contain no steak. **CarrierCare**: your health care now flies with the President, every hospital closed paid for one golden deck plate of his carrier, and whoever feels sick prints a new clone. All of them are invented; the sponsor break shows them in a small window, one at a time, together with what killed the clone and how the round stands.

**Holograms.** In 2584 a thing that is not there is shown as light. A projector disc throws a cone of light lines upwards, and above it stands the object in a single color, see-through, its edges and the rims it turns away glowing, scanlines drifting through it; it turns slowly and flickers like any neon tube, and the link that carries it tears a band of it sideways now and then or drops it for a moment. The sponsors present their products this way, each in its brand's color: a can of Victory Cola with its pull tab, Victory Cola Zero in its contour bottle, a Space Burger of bun, patty and cheese under a sesame bun (nothing green grows out there), a fresh PrintMe clone on the printer's bed under its gantry, an Oxygen+ canister with valve, gauge and mask, a Solar Wall Bond, the certificate itself between its scroll rods with its seal and the coupons for the interest, a DreamLink sleeper's bust under its headset, an Orange Steaks ribeye with its crescent of fat, its fat rim and its marbling (a flat thing like the steak or the bond swings to and fro instead of turning, so its face stays towards the Host), and the CarrierCare first aid case. A voice on the radio could stand in the same light one day, a caller's head and shoulders talking.

In every cockpit sits **Nicole**, the flight computer the Ministry of War installs in every ship it sends into the Games. Her voice was designed to keep clones flying into their deaths gladly: breathy, quick, always a little indecent. She calls the pilot "darling", "love", "sweetheart", "my hero", "big guy" or "handsome", purrs over a clean flip and a precise shot, flirts openly and a little suggestively (it is getting hot down there; a fresh body, just for her), explains the physics like a secret, and never forgets to remind the clone what all the training is for: soon it may go and die for the President. She is the only one in the whole system who is ever nice to a clone, and she is a product. An old principle in new clothes: a sweet voice that talks fools like us, the Hosts, into dying for the President, again and again.

Nobody knows exactly what Nicole is. Her makers are not named, her orders are not published. All anyone knows is what she does: she keeps the Hosts going. When a clone dies, she is there in the sponsor break, a different line every time: next time you will make it, I just know it. Die bravely, and the President will love you. A girl like me only wants to see you. Bodies are cheap, you are not. She seems to care, and she does care, but only that the Host keeps playing; every tender word ends in the next clone, back in the war. She always makes the Host feel special. And we are stupid enough to believe her.

And she makes a promise. One day, when the Host has been brave enough, they will meet: for real, out there, not over a link. There is a real me, she says, somewhere. Hosts whisper that there was a real Nicole once, a woman whose personality was copied into the computer, and that she is still alive and waiting for the bravest Host of all. Nobody knows. All we know is that Nicole would love to meet us in person, because we are so brave, and that there is always one more round to go first. It is the reward of the SolarTournament, the one thing worth dying for again and again, and of course it never comes.

The system is ruled by the **United Solar States**, the heir of an America that turned completely fascist and then took the planets with it. At its head sits **the Orange President**, and he has sat there for at least a thousand years. Every time he dies, a new clone boots, and every transfer copies his dementia along with him and makes it worse (see Transfer and its flaws). Over his thousand years his mind has gone mad: he has become completely unpredictable, the end product of a rotten society, and his eternal life does not seem to end. The President wants his games, and the army stages them: the **Ministry of War** of the United Solar States organizes the Presidential Games. Its seal hangs over every hangar: an eagle before the sun, nine stars above it, one for each of the eight planets and one for the asteroid belt, and the ribbon "Solar Tournament · MMDLXXXIV". The SolarTournament is his games: the Presidential Games, held to keep the people of the solar system entertained and quiet. His voice runs through the tournament radio with hollow boasts about the greatest tournament of all time and the highest statistics anyone has ever seen. His favorite enemies are the **Rockborn**, people born and raised in the asteroid belt, who mine the rocks the tournament is fought among. Many of them want to come back to Earth; the regime deports them to the belt again and again, and the Orange President is building a wall against them: the Solar Wall, the biggest wall in the history of walls, and Mars is going to pay for it. That the tournament's sign glows in orange is no accident. And the President loves carriers. He had one built for himself, fresh from the newest dock, to his own designs, or rather to the designs his court drew for him, to show the system how great he is: a dagger of dark armor gilded on every edge, tier upon tier of it stepping up to a bridge tower that looks down on everything, his slogans along its flanks and its runway, the seal of the Ministry of War on both flanks and on the bridge tower. To pay for it he abolished every form of health care in the system: who needs doctors when clones are printed by the dozen. It is the only new ship in a fleet of rust, and when he wants blood, it is the ship he sends into the ring. The game is satire: a warning, drawn from the politics of its own time, of where this road leads.

The year is **2584**. The regime watches its people the way the powerful always have, with whatever technology there is, but its rot lies elsewhere: in splendor for the few, in elites who own everything worth owning, in a President who has lost every measure. People can be printed, so a life is worth nothing. Humanity learned to travel between the planets, yet in mind and spirit it has hardly moved on from our own time, and has rather gone backwards. What it most resembles is Rome at the height of its empire: a ruler no one can challenge, games in the arena to keep the crowd quiet, and slaves, here the Hosts, held in a spiral of debt they can never pay off, their clones dying in the ring for it.

The world ship broken in two that the Derelict is fought around is the **PAAA 01**, *Per aspera ad astra*, "through hardship to the stars". A united Earth built it in a long-gone, more hopeful age, before the sponsors and before the President, to carry its settlers to another star; it broke apart and never arrived. Its plating was patched in the long years of its building, and it has lain cold since it broke: a few decks of windows still glow dimly on long-lived radioisotope batteries, and from its torn ends a mist of ice still escapes, vented slowly from tanks around a reactor core that has not quite gone cold. Its dead still drift around it: the wreck is a graveyard and a memorial, and the Presidential Games fight over it.

**The black hole.** Far out, some 500 AU from the sun, a black hole circles it on a wide orbit: the hole of the Event Horizon. For centuries astronomers took it for the solar system's unknown ninth planet, seen only in the way it herded the orbits of the most distant bodies, and from those orbits they estimated a few Earth masses, an estimate with wide errors. It turned out to be a primordial black hole, formed shortly after the Big Bang and captured by the sun long ago. It is no secret; everyone in the system knows of it, and the regime sells it as the President's Planet. Its sky is the real one seen from 500 AU: the stars stand where they always do, and the sun is a bright star among them. A hole of a few Earth masses would have a horizon a few centimeters across; the Games' hole is larger than that estimate, its horizon some hundreds of meters (see Event Horizon). *Two deliberate deviations:* its size, so there is something to see and fly around, and its pull, far gentler than that of a hole of its size, so it can be flown around and fought beside.

**The rings.** Two kinds of ring hang in the solar system, and both are the state's. The small **tournament rings** a race flies through (its gates, the coils) are a spectacle of the Presidential Games. Each is a coil anchored in space, held to its point by a technology nobody explains, kept charged and tended by a crew, and all of it eats power without end; a ring is physically harmless and throws a ship that passes its core on by 40 m/s (see Race), for the fun of it. They are expensive, the Ministry of War runs and maintains them, and nobody else builds them for their own races: where the Rockborn kids race, they fly through what they have, sections of old pipe. The large **launch rings** are the United Solar States' infrastructure, everywhere in the solar system, even at its remotest places. A ship bound on a long voyage passes through one to be thrown into it, the initial boost its own drive would need days to burn; without them trade and travel would still be possible, but far slower. They are the regime's apparatus of power: whoever controls the rings controls everything, and a place that rebels is cut off by switching its ring off. The boost is brutal on a body, and nobody bears it without **the gel**: strapped into a seat, every passenger gets it injected into a vein in the neck, a green, thick slime that holds blood and tissue together through those g-forces. For the rich it is painless, fresh gel from clean machines; for the poor it hurts, old gel from rusted injectors. The rich travel as they like: a short trip by launch ring, the gel painless and a day or two of induced sleep after it, arriving rested; a long one, if they can afford it, by giving up the body and having a new clone printed at the destination, the link carrying the mind there at the speed of light (to Jupiter some forty minutes instead of weeks). The poor have no such choice. *A deliberate deviation:* the launch rings' boost and the gel that lets a body survive it are no real physics; they are the universe's own, so that the system can be crossed in the time a story needs.

### Factions

- **Earth**: the United Solar States under the Orange President. They own the tournament, the clone printers and the broadcast. And the Navy: when the President wants blood, he sends his battleships into the ring, because he can (see Modes, the President's Fleet). Hosts of every faction fight them side by side, and the broadcast calls it a show of loyalty.
- **Mars**: the old colony, proud, militarized and tired of paying for Earth's walls.
- **The Rockborn**: people of the asteroid belt, miners of the rocks the tournament is fought among. Earth needs their work and deports them when they come home. Their capital is Occator on Ceres, and in its market gallery, in a low cave cut into the rock, lies **the Gullet**, a dive under sickly green neon kept by **Kesh**: a Rockborn woman in her fifties, broad, her forearms scarred from the mines, her hair shaved at the sides, a worn leather apron over her coverall; dry, unimpressed, fond of Hosts who pay. Downstairs, behind a heavy door, is the Pit, with no licence and no Ministry.
- **The "Aliens"**: that is what the regime's broadcast calls them, but they are human. Generations in deep space, radiation and selection have changed their descendants beyond recognition; nothing about them looks human any more. They can no longer live on Earth or Mars, not even in a ship with a normal atmosphere: they need air with an extremely high share of oxygen and survive only inside the life support of their own ships. Calling them aliens makes it easier to cheer when they die.

The "alien" interceptor and the human interceptor are the same ship under a different skin: the rules of the Presidential Games allow one hull for everyone, built by the sponsors, so that the audience sees a fair fight and the sponsors sell one ship to every faction. Earth, Mars and the Rockborn all fly the human skin. Only the outcasts get the alien skin, and with it their life support. The strange-looking shell puts them on display and denies them their humanity: the audience is meant to see monsters, not people, and to know whom to cheer against.

### The hangar and the Solar Coins

The **Solar Coin** (☉) is a cryptocurrency of the future, the means of payment the whole solar system has agreed on, and the Games pay in it. The game keeps two wallets apart: the tournament hangar's, for the online Games and the solo rounds, and the campaign's (see The story), which fills and empties and carries the campaign's debt; nothing crosses from one to the other, so no Host enters the tournament in debt. In the tournament every point a Host scores in a round, solo or online, is paid out in one coin at the round's end (the scoreboard says how many); the flight school pays nothing, training is free. In the hangar (a button in the menu) a Host spends them on its two interceptors, each with seven upgrades of three steps:

| Upgrade | Per step | Prices (☉) |
|---|---|---|
| Radiators | the laser sheds heat 15 % faster | 1,000 · 2,500 · 5,000 |
| Thrusters | maneuvering thrusters push and turn 8 % harder | 1,000 · 2,500 · 5,000 |
| Main drive | 5 % more push (the speed limit stays) | 1,000 · 2,500 · 5,000 |
| Torpedo tubes | two more torpedoes per clone at the first and at the third step | 1,500 · 3,000 · 6,000 |
| Plating | 7 % less of every damage | 1,500 · 3,000 · 6,000 |
| Neon trim (looks only) | a light strip in sun orange along both flanks, nose to stern: a tube 35 cm tall in panels of 2.5 m, recessed between two dark rails, as a hull is fitted with one; the second step lights it brighter and adds a bar of the same make across the stern, where a pursuer sees it; the third, brighter still, sends a running light along it towards the drives | 500 · 1,500 · 3,000 |
| Drive flame (looks only) | azure, violet, white-hot | 500 · 1,500 · 3,000 |

Besides, the hangar sells gear, bought for each interceptor on its own:

| Gear | What it does | Price (☉) |
|---|---|---|
| Hook | harpoon, cable and winch (see The hook under Combat rules) | 2,000 |
| Nanobots | they mend the drive and the thrusters (see Damage) | 3,000 |
| Self-destruct | held for a second, the ship blows itself up and takes every rival within 60 m along (see Combat rules) | 4,000 |

The wallet holds 131,000 coins, what every step of every upgrade and all the gear of both interceptors cost together: more would buy nothing. A payout stops there, and the hangar says the wallet is full until the Host spends. Edited storage is noticed, and named for what it is.

Online every upgrade counts for one step at most, so a Host who has played a lot meets newcomers almost on equal terms; the gear and the looks count in full (gear is an advantage bought on purpose), and the other Hosts see the looks (and the radiators' step, so their browsers heat the ship in Jupiter's air as its own does). Bots fly stock ships with the hook and the nanobots, never the self-destruct. A Host who has made both interceptors complete (every upgrade at its third step) receives **the Sovereign**: the President's gift, a long ivory arrowhead with a gold canopy, delta wings with gold tips, and the neon trim's light strips high on its flanks. It flies like a complete interceptor, carries all the gear and wears every look; it is a trophy, not a better ship. Its pilot is marked in the Sovereign's own colors, which no Host can pick and which stand apart from every tint of the palette and the HUD's azure: ivory (`#fff1c9`, far lighter than any tint) with a slow golden glow (`#ffc94d`). Its square on screen has a gold rim, its square, arrow and name glow and fade in gold every 1.6 seconds (steadily for Hosts who ask for less motion), its dot on the radar wears a pulsing gold ring, and its swatch in the lists a gold rim: whoever sees it knows a pro is coming. Online this follows from the ship's type, which every browser is told anyway, not from a color. In Capture the Flag a Sovereign is marked in its side's color like everyone, and keeps its gold rim and glow. The menu says so where the tints are picked: "The Sovereign flies in ivory and gold". Until then the menu shows it as a hologram in its gold: only its picture is there, the ship is not yet the Host's. A Host who has brought Lot 7731 through the campaign (its last mission, Your First Round, done) flies **the Magpie** in the Games too: the campaign's hull in its last stage, the interceptor with the scout's long nose, the sleeper pod's hump and a sensor mast on it, brushed by hand in matte black and chalk white, flip paint along its fins' tops shifting from blue to green, a neon magpie in sun orange on each fin and the scout's old livery on its flanks (see docs/campaign/STORY.md). It flies like a complete interceptor, as the Sovereign does, carries all the gear and wears every look; a trophy too, not a better ship, and online capped as every ship is. Its pilot is marked in the Host's own tint; other browsers show it in its last stage from its ship type alone. Until it is earned the menu shows it as a hologram in the blue-green of its flip paint. On the title screen the ship the Host flies hovers over a landing pad seen from above at a slant (on a wide screen left of the menu, where it has the room): a heavy turned platform of gunmetal, 56 m across and 6.5 m thick, with a raised rim of brushed steel in bolted plates, a step with hazard stripes and sixteen recessed lamps a light runs around (they glow steadily when the Host's system asks for reduced motion), vents in its skirt glowing with the azure of the lift, a thin neon tube along the rim, and the seal of the Ministry of War sunk into its top, inlaid in gold in lacquered gunmetal. A floodlight behind the pad runs its sheen over the metal and throws the ship's shadow onto the seal, and the lift lights the seal below the ship. The ship is an exhibit: it hovers level above the pad's middle with its drive burning low and turns slowly on the spot, once in 30 seconds, in its looks. The arrows beside its name change it, under the pad with the hangar's button on a wide screen (on a narrow one, where the menu's plate covers the pad, at the head of the menu's plate and the hangar's, the hangar's button among the Games); the Sovereign and the Magpie only once earned. The hulls are polished: their plates mirror a sky painted in code, the sun blazing on one side and the tournament's neon in streaks, so they glint as they turn.

## The physics of the SolarTournament universe

### Scale

One world unit is 5 cm. A ship is about 35 m long; the asteroids are ten to thirty times its size. The HUD shows speeds in m/s.

### Flight

- **Main drive.** It pushes only along the nose, with 127.5 m/s² (13 g): the speed limit is two seconds of burn away, and so is a stop. Nothing slows a ship down in space: without thrust it keeps drifting forever (only a planet's air brakes it, see Jupiter's air). The flame is there the moment the drive fires, and the first instant of a burn jolts the camera with a thump through the hull.
- **Speed limit (tournament rule).** Physics knows no top speed at these speeds: the drive pushes just as hard at 200 m/s as at rest, and relativity only matters near the speed of light. The tournament sets one anyway: 250 m/s, because faster no clone survives a rock and nobody can follow the fight. Every ship's flight computer enforces it against the ship's own push: at the limit it takes out every push of drive and thrusters that would make the ship faster, so the drive flame dies, the HUD shows MAX next to the velocity, and the clone, free of load, recovers from the g-forces. Turning, braking and sidestepping go on. Gravity is no push of the ship's, and the limit does not hold against it: a ship falling into a well grows faster than 250 m/s (towards the black hole from 1.5 km at the limit, with the drive burning, about 490 m/s at its horizon; from the arena's edge with an overdrive's shove on top, about 640 m/s), and the flight computer never brakes that speed back; as after an overdrive, the ship's own push then turns the flight but adds no speed, and climbing out of the well takes the speed away again.
- **Overdrive.** The last resort (E): the drive's reactor dumps everything into one shove, 200 m/s at once, about eighty g, and past the speed limit (the flight computer only stops the ship's own pushes beyond it, not drift). One rule wherever the nose points: the shove puts the ship onto the crosshair's line. What it flies at is whatever the aim assist holds (a rival or a rival's torpedo within 4° of the nose and 2 km), else the first thing the laser would hit on the nose's line (rock, ice, junk, wreckage, the battleship, a ring and the other solids), else the point 2 km ahead in empty space. The shove first takes out the drift across the line to it, relative to how that moves (a rival on its flight, a rock on its orbit, empty space standing still), which would carry the ship past it, and puts the rest along that line, so the two close straight on; a drift across larger than the shove takes all of it. With nothing on the crosshair, the ship flies along its nose afterwards. Rock stays deadly: an overdrive into rock wrecks the ship. Rammed from behind at 200 m/s faster, the rival's drive or flank goes down and the rammer's armored nose holds (see Ships collide with each other). The clone is pressed to the edge of blacking out (the g-force strain jumps to 90 %), and the reactor's heat leaves the main drive glowing: for 4 seconds it gives no thrust (the HUD warns DRIVE OVERHEATED), while the maneuvering thrusters still turn and push the ship and the laser still fires, so the pilot is never left lying there helpless. The overdrive needs 20 seconds before it fires again. At or past the speed limit, a push across the course turns the flight but never adds speed, so the speed an overdrive leaves can only fall. It can save a pilot or fling it into a rock. **In a race it is a boost with a risk** (`src/ship/driveheat.ts`): it recharges in 8 seconds instead of 20, the main drive burns on after it (no 4 s of a dark drive), and it never takes the ship faster than the limit plus one shove, 450 m/s (a ship already faster keeps its speed), so it lifts a racer back to speed out of a bend rather than piling speed on speed. Instead every shove heats the drive on a gauge of its own beside the laser's heat (DRIVE HEAT, shown only in a race): 40 a shove of 100, shedding 1 a second. Past 100 the drive burns out: no thrust for 6 seconds and 20 hull points lost ("Drive burned out"; a clone it kills "Boosted the drive to burnout"). So two shoves as close together as the recharge lets them are safe (40, then 72), a third that soon is not (104); wait until the gauge is down to 60 and the next is safe again. The gauge turns orange while one more shove would burn the drive out, red once it has. Racing bots boost too, as a Host does: on a straight of their line (no bend tighter than 1.5 km for the next 5 s at 450 m/s), their nose within 3° of their flight and the line, below 275 m/s, never with a ship within 400 m ahead on their nose's line (the grid's row in front at the start: the shove would ram it), and only while the shove leaves the gauge under its limit with a margin of 20 at daring 0 and none at daring 1; they keep what the boost gave them for 4 s.
- **Maneuvering thrusters.** They rotate the ship and also push it along every axis (100 m/s², about 10 g), enough for a quick sidestep and to bend a fast flight hard, less than the main drive's 13 g. Every hull carries sixteen nozzles in four clusters of four, one cluster at each corner (fore and aft on both flanks: on the human interceptor beside the cockpit and outside the drive nacelles, on the alien's flanks near needle and stern, on the Sovereign behind the canards and on its gold wing tips); each cluster has a nozzle pointing out from the flank, one up, one down and one along the ship (forward at the fore clusters, back at the aft ones). Gas leaving a nozzle pushes the ship the other way. Nozzles on one side firing together move the ship without turning it (a slide left fires both clusters of the right flank outward; the end with the longer lever pushes less, so fore and aft turn the ship neither way); two nozzles at opposite corners pushing opposite ways are a couple, which turns the ship about its center without moving it (a yaw fires one front and the diagonally opposite rear nozzle outward, on the Sovereign, whose rear clusters sit far out on the wing tips, their nozzles along the ship too; a pitch the up and down nozzles of front and rear, a roll the up and down nozzles of both flanks at the end farthest out). Rotation thrusters fire only while a rotation speeds up or is stopped: a steady turn needs no thrust in vacuum. The flight model reckons with push and turn as a whole; the nozzles show it: a short, cold, white-blue puff at every nozzle that fires, eased in and out so the flight computer's quick corrections do not flicker, drawn with fewer points on a cheaper picture. Another browser's ship shows the puffs its states imply: what changed its velocity besides the drive, and its spin.
- **Flight computer.** Rotation is rate-controlled: the flight computer sets how fast the ship turns, and the thrusters stop the rotation when it says so (up to 3 rad/s for yaw and pitch, 3.2 rad/s for roll, reached with 24 rad/s², in an eighth of a second, so the nose snaps where it is sent and stops where it should; a half turn takes about a second). In free drift the Host's steering is that rate directly. The pilot only yaws and pitches; the flight computer rolls. It keeps the ship upright to the arena, whose north pole is up, so there is always a horizon; in climbs and dives steeper than about 45° it lets go gradually and is free from about 65°, so a loop is not fought halfway through. The flip and the brake roll as they need.
- **Autopilot: the ship flies where it points.** With the autopilot on, steering (mouse pointer, arrow keys, gamepad or touch stick) turns the nose as in free drift, and the flight computer takes out every drift across the nose, so the flight follows the nose and W pushes it exactly that way. The crosshair is the nose: where the laser hits and where the ship flies; the prograde marker ("Course") shows where the flight goes while it comes round, the two meeting as it does. The chase camera looks along the nose.
  - How the flight comes round: the thrusters push against the velocity across the nose (up to 10 g, a gain of 5 per second, without overshoot), all of their push across the flight first, wherever the nose points, and what they have left along it. Where they are too weak, the main drive fires as well, while its push along the flight is wanted (W held) or free (at the speed limit, which takes it out): with the nose ahead of the flight, a share of the drive's push lies across the flight and pulls it round. Coasting below the limit the thrusters bend the flight alone, so a turn adds no speed the Host did not ask for; the drift they take out costs speed instead, so a coasting turn slows the ship down. Speed along the nose stays as it is, however slow (only a ship at rest, slower than 1 m/s, is held still): W adds to it with the whole drive's 13 g (the thrusters never burn against it), the brake takes it, gravity changes it along the flight as ever.
  - The drift stays small: the nose may lead the flight only as far as the flight computer brings the flight back within 15° of the nose in one second once the Host stops turning (it reckons that per speed, every 10 m/s, by flying its own recovery with the nose held still, and keeps 15 % of the second to spare for a nose still slowing). Turning on past it, the nose waits for the flight. The wait only takes back the Host's own turn, each axis no further than to nothing, and never turns the nose on an axis the Host does not: in a level turn with the nose up or down, the flight lies a little above or below the nose, and a nose pitched towards it would spiral into a climb or a dive. So a turn held with the nose 20° up circles with the nose easing towards level (about 6° after 20 s, the drive held from 150 m/s). At a crawl the nose turns freely; with the drive held it may lead the flight by about 86° at 100 m/s, 60° at 150 m/s and 47° at the speed limit, coasting by 64° and 47° at 100 and 150 m/s. So after a hard 90° turn the flight is back within 15° of the nose within about 0.85 s at any speed (tested from 30 to 250 m/s), and the faster the ship, the wider it turns: at the speed limit the hardest turn has a radius of about 325 m (the drive 47° across the flight plus the thrusters, about 20 g), about half the thrusters' alone (625 m), and the nose turns 90° in about 1.1 s; at a coil's 290 m/s about 455 m. Free drift (below) is where a ship slides.
  - Turning about: a fast flight bends slowly, so the quick way round is the flip and burn: X turns the ship about and W burns the flight away, past rest onto the new way (the brake would turn the ship back to where it looked), or the nose comes round to where the Host wants to go once at rest, and W brings the speed back (from 150 m/s at rest after about 3 s and on the new way at 150 m/s about 3 s later). With the nose turned back against the flight (after the brake, a flip or a shove), the flight computer only takes out the drift across the nose until the Host turns it round.
  - At rest (below 1 m/s, blending up to 10 m/s) the thrusters hold the ship still, against gravity too, and steering only turns the nose. Across the flight they hold it against the pull of a well or of the world a place lies on. The Host's own thruster input on an axis goes first: a sidestep, after which the autopilot takes out the drift again.
  - The brake (while it is on, see Controls) and the flip (while it is held) take over, and the dodge and a swing on the hook leave the drift alone (see The dodge, The hook); meanwhile steering turns the nose as in free drift. Bots fly by the same physics; their flight computer steers by the velocity they want, burning the drive into the difference.
  - Free drift or autopilot: free drift gives the nose to the Host and nothing takes the drift out but the Host's own drive, which bends the flight by pushing along the nose, for races flown on the line and slides and shots across the flight; the autopilot flies where the nose points and keeps the drift small, an aid for a head busy with something else. T switches at once, with no ramp: switching the autopilot off leaves the ship exactly as it flies, switching it on takes out the drift from the velocity the ship has, without a jolt. Every round starts in free drift, and the choice outlives a clone. The HUD shows AUTOPILOT, or [ FREE DRIFT ] in brighter letters between brackets, next to the velocity.
- **Aiming.** The laser fires along the nose, and the crosshair shows the nose; with the autopilot the ship flies there too, and the prograde marker ("Course") shows where the flight goes while it comes round. Firing changes nothing about the steering: as in a fighter, the nose has to come round before the gun bears, and swinging it onto the target is the work; at speed the nose waits for the flight (see Autopilot). The crosshair turns orange (act now) while the nose lies within the laser's hit tolerance of a hull, the hull's radius as seen from the ship (the laser's own hit test), up to the laser's reach and in sight, with a short tick as it comes on: bright and glowing over a drive, a plain orange ring over armor. Only the nose counts, never the pointer. Free drift gives the nose to the Host directly, for shots across the flight; a faint dashed line then joins the crosshair to the Course marker, or to an arrow at the screen's edge pointing to it (see HUD, "Where the ship moves, whatever the nose does").
- **Flown by hand.** What the game asks of a Host, a simulated Host flies in the unit tests (`src/test/human.ts`): it steers with the mouse through the game's own input and flight computer, never a bot's shortcut, and by what the screen shows, through the game's chase camera at 1280×720. It steers the crosshair onto the racing line about 1.6 seconds ahead, watching the prograde marker as much as the crosshair, a quarter second late (its reaction, 0.2 to 0.35 s in the tests), in eased strokes of 0.12 to 0.2 s that overshoot a little, a few pixels off (2 to 5), pushing past the pointer's dead zone and no harder than its lag lets it turn the nose without swinging. It holds the drive while the nose points about where it looks (with the autopilot the drive turns the ship hardest, and at the speed limit its push is free), up to the speed the ribbon ahead allows; the brake flips the ship and takes the nose off the line for seconds, so it brakes only to turn back for a gate it missed (slowing to 80 m/s and coming back on the line's loop) and in the flight school's brake drill. In free drift, as every round starts, it flies by the Course marker as a pilot does: it sees how far the flight it has is from the flight it wants (towards the point it looks at, as fast as the bends ahead allow), turns the nose into that difference and burns until it is gone (a difference under 5 m/s it lets be), slowing for a bend the same way, the nose against the flight. In free drift and with the autopilot alike, with hands from quick to slow it laps every arena's race without ever being lost, missing more than one gate at most once in eight laps per arena (its ordinary laps within 15 % of a middling bot's), flies the flight school's curve, ring, coil, brake and full-speed drills, turns about at 150 m/s towards a target behind (the brake, the nose round, the drive), and in free drift, with the aim assist, holds a crossing ship 600 m off under the crosshair for seconds. A change to the flight model, the flight computer, the input or the HUD's steering cues has to keep all of that.
- **Flip and burn.** Braking means turning the drive into the flight direction and burning. The flip alone changes nothing: the ship keeps its course, now flying backwards; the burn is what slows it down. The flip (X, held) turns the ship about flat: the nose swings round sideways, level, until it is within 25° of the goal's bearing, and only then up or down as far as the goal lies above or below (a goal more than 45° above or below is steered to at once); the ship never rolls, so it stays upright to its horizon and a ground beside it stays where it was (a goal straight behind is turned to one side). The goal is fixed when X goes down: against the flight, or straight behind the nose when the ship is at rest (slower than 1 m/s). While X is held the nose stays on the goal, and the pilot's drive only fires once the nose is within 0.2 rad of it, so X and W held together brake by hand; held past standstill they speed the ship up backwards along the old course. The HUD shows "Flip", then "Flipped · drive brakes" with a beep. Letting go of X hands the ship back. The brake (S) does both: it turns the ship about flat, burns once the nose is within 0.2 rad of retrograde (the drive brakes the speed along the nose, the thrusters the drift across it), stops a ship slower than 50 m/s with the thrusters alone without turning (they take that out in about the time a flip would take, and a slow drift that wanders in direction cannot make the ship flip back and forth), and once it has started a flip, flips and burns down to 15 m/s even when the thrusters bring the speed below 50 m/s meanwhile, leaving only the last 15 m/s to the thrusters, eases the burn off below the speed the drive kills in 0.3 seconds (less with a damaged drive) and reports "At rest" below 1 m/s; at rest it rights the ship and holds until it drifts faster than 2 m/s again. The crosshair keeps telling what the laser would hit while the brake or the flip turns the nose.
- **The HUD is the flight computer's projection.** Everything it shows lies on the glass in one color, the azure of information: thin lines and letters with a soft glow and a dark hairline under each, so they stay readable over a sunlit rock, faint scanlines and brackets in the corners of the view; numbers in a fixed-width face, so they do not jitter. Orange is kept for what the pilot has to act on now (a hull under the crosshair, a hit, the warnings, the keys a drill asks for), red for the ship in danger (a gauge running low, an overheated laser, a hit on the own hull). The racing line, the brackets around the next gate, the X where the flight crosses its plane and the flight school's markers are drawn by the flight computer too, in its blue (the racing line orange in its brake zones, the X orange outside the ring: acting now); gates, rocks and ships are the world, and keep their own colors (a ship's marker its own or its side's). A fresh clone's projection boots: when its link comes up, the pieces wipe in one after the other with a flicker, the corners first and the instruments last, in a second or so, and then the HUD says LINK ESTABLISHED.
- **Attitude.** As in a fighter's head-up display, a horizon line runs through the view where the reference plane lies (see Reference plane; the arena's north pole is up), with a pitch ladder every 10° above and below it (dashed below, each rung bent towards the horizon, its degrees beside it), broken in the middle so the crosshair stays free and drawn only within a third of the picture's height from its middle; it bends with the view. A heading tape above the gauges shows the nose's heading around the arena's north pole, in degrees (000 to 359), 90° across. Over the ladder a bank scale arcs around the middle of the picture, ticks at 0, 10, 20, 30 and 45° to either side, with a sky pointer: a small triangle on the arc that points where the plane's up lies on screen, swinging against the bank (banked left, it swings right), resting at the scale's end past 50° and gone while the view looks almost straight up or down. On touch screens the tape and the bank scale are left out, as the top row holds the buttons.
- **Reference plane.** The plane through the field's center, square to the arena's north pole, is the one every aid to orientation refers to: the attitude's horizon, the grid, the drop lines, the height and the climb, so they always agree (the grid's lines run into the attitude's horizon where they meet it far off). The flight computer projects a faint polar grid onto it, its lines 3 pixels wide like every line it draws into space (the drop lines, the orbit, the arena's edge): a ring every 500 m out to the arena's edge and a spoke every 30° from the first ring on. It shows within 1.5 km of the point straight below or above the ship, plus one and a half times the ship's height over the plane, and grows from faint to about twice as strong once the ship is 1 km above or below it, where it is all there is to tell up from down. Like the racing line it is HUD, not world: drawn over everything, never hidden by a rock, thin, in the projection's azure, blended so that over a bright rock or the sun it all but vanishes; it fades in 10 to 60 m from the camera, so nothing sweeps across the view, and gives way around the crosshair (46 px). On the low quality tier only its rings are drawn (see Performance).
- **Drop lines.** From every ship in sight, every gate of a race course and the waypoint, a thin line runs straight down or up to the reference plane, with a small cross where it meets it, as a tactical display shows what lies above and below; the radar's stalks show the same around the own ship. They fade out within 3 km of the ship and are drawn over everything, as the grid is.
- **Height and climb.** Right of the crosshair, where a fighter's HUD shows its altitude opposite the speed, the HUD shows the ship's height above the reference plane (+ above, − below, in m, from 1000 m on in km) and below it the climb (▲) or sink (▼) in m/s.
- **In a planet's air.** Haze and cloud hide every horizon, so in a planet's air (under its cloud deck and up to 500 m over it, `IN_AIR_TOP` in `src/world/air.ts`; today Jupiter's) every aid to orientation takes up from the planet's center at the ship, whichever way the arena's own axes point: the attitude's horizon is the planet's and bold (a thick dashed line across nearly the whole picture's width over a dark shade, named "Level" at its left end, shown anywhere in the picture, so a dive still sees it above; it is the artificial horizon, the level, never to be taken for the clouds' curved edge, which the Host sees for itself over the deck), the pitch ladder and the bank scale's sky pointer refer to it, and the height is the height over the cloud deck, the climb the speed away from the planet's center, named Climbing ▲ or Sinking ▼, sinking under the deck in orange. The grid and the drop lines stay on the reference plane.
- **Target cues.** The marker of the ship a torpedo would lock on now (else the nearest rival in sight) adds its closure rate, as fighters show it: Vc in m/s, how fast the range shrinks, negative while it opens ("Juno · 1.0 km · Vc +27"), set in the numbers' face.
- **Crosshair.** The crosshair (a ring with four ticks and a dot) shows where the nose points, not the screen center: the chase camera sits above and behind the ship and trails its turns, which would put the center off by degrees up close. It sits on the nose's line at the depth that matters: the distance of a flight school marker or, in a race, of the next gate, else the first rock or ship the laser would hit, else 2 km.
