Outside the flight school and the race, what lies under the crosshair is what the laser hits. It turns orange over a rival's torpedo the laser would hit, bright as over a drive, since a shot sets it off; over a torpedo of the own ship or side it stays azure, crossed out, with FRIEND beneath, as over a ship of the own side.
- **Aim assist.** A ship in sight within about 4° of the nose and 2 km, never one of the own side, pulls the nose onto it, and so does a rival's torpedo, which the laser shoots down (the closest of them to the nose): the closer it already is, the firmer (up to 85 %), and the less the flight computer turns the nose elsewhere (a turn command past a third of full rate weakens the pull, past four fifths it is gone). Its cone is wider than the laser's hit tolerance (a hull of 17 m covers 4° only within 240 m), so it pulls a nose that is close onto the hull; the orange crosshair (see Aiming) only says the laser would hit. The bots aim with a computer too; the pilot still pulls the trigger.
- **Feedback.** A hit of the own laser floats a word up from where it struck: DRIVE, or REACTOR for the fatal one, with a jolt of the camera, or a small grey ARMOR for a hit the armor shrugged off; a kill of the own shows "+500" in neon, a bigger explosion and a stronger jolt; a cracked asteroid shows its points. The jolt is left out for Hosts who ask for less motion.
- **Velocity markers.** The prograde marker (a circle with three fins) shows where the ship is actually drifting, the flight the autopilot brings onto the nose, the retrograde marker (a dashed circle with a cross) where the drive has to point to brake. Both show from 1 m/s on.
- **Where the ship moves, whatever the nose does.** In free drift the nose turns freely and the ship slides on, so the crosshair (where the laser fires) and the Course marker (where the ship flies) part. Four aids answer at a glance where the ship really moves, all in the flight computer's azure:
  - **Motion streaks**, always on while the ship moves (with the autopilot too), whether or not dust or rock is in sight: a handful of short, faint dashes flowing over the screen the way the world would stream past at one fixed depth. They stream out of the Course marker's point while the ship flies ahead of the camera, across the screen from the side the Course marker lies on while it slides sideways, and into the Brake marker's point while it flies backwards. They fade in from 1 to 10 m/s and flow faster and longer the faster the ship goes (the flow's rate 1.2 × speed / (speed + 150 m/s) per second: 0.6/s at 150 m/s, 0.75/s at 250 m/s; a dash as long as it flows in 0.1 s, at most 90 px), faint near the focus, where they barely move, and at the screen's edges. Twenty at most, fewer on a cheaper picture (the share of particles drawn, at least three quarters); with reduced motion fewer, shorter and standing still, turning with the flow. They come on top of the space dust and are drawn on a canvas of their own, cleared only where the last frame drew.
  - **The drift line**: in free drift, from 5 m/s on, a faint dashed line from the crosshair to the Course marker, so the gap between where the laser fires and where the ship flies shows at once; left out while the two all but touch.
  - **The Course arrow**: in free drift, from 5 m/s on, while the Course marker lies off screen, an arrow at the screen's edge points to it, named "Course" and placed like a ship's arrow (its name clear of the ships' names and of the HUD's pieces), and the drift line runs to it. With the autopilot neither line nor arrow shows: the flight follows the nose within about a second, so crosshair and Course marker sit together. Flying backwards (the Brake marker in the picture and the Course marker not) both stay out too: the Course marker lies behind, an arrow to it would flip sides with every twitch of the nose, and the Brake marker under the streaks streaming into it says it.
  - **The flash**: switching to free drift (T, the touch screen's AUTO button, the gamepad's right stick), the Course marker, or its arrow, flashes once for 0.6 s, swelling and glowing, so the eye finds where the ship really flies; with reduced motion it only glows.
- **Orbit readout.** Near the black hole of the Event Horizon the flight computer keeps asking: if the ship went into free drift now, with nothing but the hole's pull on it, where would it fly? Without thrust a ship in the pull of one body flies a conic about its center (an ellipse around it, a fall into it, or a path away for good), worked out every frame from the ship's distance and velocity (its energy and angular momentum; the rocks' pull, a rounding error next to the hole's, is left out). The path is a **stable orbit** when it comes back (bound), when its nearest point to the center (periapsis) stays where the hole's pull alone takes less than the CRITICAL share of the drive (60 % of the thrust, see Alarms: 715 m from the center with a whole drive, where the gauge reads CRITICAL at rest, 365 m over the horizon, 1.29 times the point of no return at 555 m, farther with a damaged drive), so that from any point of it a full burn away still gets the ship out, and when its farthest point (apoapsis) stays inside the arena's edge (3.4 km). Entering one takes 2 % more room on both bounds than keeping it, so a path right on a bound does not flicker in and out. Under the escape gauge the readout shows, wherever the gauge shows or the path is an orbit: **ORBIT READY** with the autopilot on, **STABLE ORBIT** in free drift, both with Pe and Ap, the path's nearest and farthest point over the horizon (as the hole's mark measures), and the whole ellipse drawn into space as a thin, faint line of the HUD's azure over everything (`src/view/orbitpath.ts`), so the Host sees where the ship will go; or what is wrong with it, **NO ORBIT · FALLS IN** (its periapsis too low: too slow across the line to the hole, or falling at it) or **NO ORBIT · LEAVES ARENA** (too fast: its apoapsis beyond the edge, or no apoapsis at all), whichever it meets first, both with the speed of a circular orbit where the ship is ("Orbit speed"). The fastest circular orbit, 234 m/s at the safe periapsis, stays under the speed limit; a circle 1.2 km out takes 181 m/s, and flying across the line to the hole there an orbit is ready from 157 m/s up to 219 m/s (once ready, it holds from 156 m/s to 220 m/s, the 2 % entry room). Nicole says "Orbit ready, darling." when the path becomes one with the autopilot on, and "Entering stable orbit." when free drift flies it, each at most once in 20 s, and "Orbit ready" not when the autopilot comes back on in the orbit flown. In a stable orbit the well's alarms are quiet (see Alarms). The readout is computed again every frame, so whatever changes the path (a burn, a ram, a hit) shows at once. Only the Event Horizon has it: Jupiter's center lies 40 km below its arena, so no orbit around it fits inside (the arena reaches 3.4 km from the field's center), and its air would brake any orbit anyway. The bots could read the same readout (it shows only what a Host's computer knows), but they need not: within 750 m of the horizon, or once its hold reaches 30 % of their drive, they burn away from the hole, in an orbit too, and never fall in.
- **Radar sphere.** In the bottom right corner (top left on touch screens) a sphere shows everything within 2.5 km around the own ship, which sits in its middle, nose up: ahead at the top, behind at the bottom, each mark on a stalk from the ship's horizontal plane, so above and below show too. Rocks and the world ship's hull show by their size, the arena's massive body as a large disk, ships with their names, each in its tint (see Ship markers; only ships the sensors see past the rocks), the field's center, and in a race the next gate. A name that would run into one already written is left out (the marks first, then the ships); its dot stays. The same goes for the names beside the ships' markers in the picture. Distances are compressed (by the power 0.6), so the near space gets the room. A solar storm fills it with static.
- **Fixed references.** The big things that never move are marked by small dim glyphs, each with its label: where they lie on screen, or at the screen's edge (on a frame a little inside the ships' arrows, clear of the heading tape) with the label beside the glyph, towards the middle. In every arena the field's center (a ring with a dot and its distance: the asteroids are densest there, and the edge of the arena is farthest away) and the sun (a dot in a broken ring); then each arena's own (see Arenas), the bases of Capture the Flag (a square with its core, and their distance) and the President's battleship while it is in the arena (a hull with its tower, and its distance). They are dimmer than the ships' markers and give way first: one that would sit on a ship's mark, the waypoint, the crosshair, a reference before it, a ship's name at the screen's edge or a panel of the HUD is left out, and one whose label would run into a ship's name keeps only its glyph.
- **Ship markers.** Every other ship the sensors see is marked in the picture: a square on it in its tint, its name behind the mark of who flies it, and its distance; a name that would run into another, under an arrow at the edge or under a piece of the HUD keeps only its square. A rival's torpedo in sight within 3 km is marked too, by a hollow triangle in its shooter's tint (a torpedo is a dot of a few pixels at any range worth shooting it at), orange while the aim assist holds it or the laser would hit it; one off screen goes unmarked, as TORPEDO INBOUND points to the one homing on the own ship. A ship off screen gets an arrow at the screen's edge pointing where it is, and beside the arrow, inward, its name with its mark, in its tint on a dark plate (12 px and at most 150 px wide; on a phone 10 px and at most 84 px, a longer name cut short with an ellipsis), without the distance. The nearest ship's name is written first. Where its place is taken by another name, an arrow, a name in the picture or a piece of the HUD (the pieces that keep apart, see the touch controls above), it tries a line up or down along a side edge, or a step left or right along the top and the bottom, up to three each way; where none is free, the arrow goes without its name, so names never cover each other or the HUD.
- **Tints.** Every ship is marked in a tint: its square, its arrow, its name, its dot and name on the radar, and its laser beam. A Host picks one in the menu, below the callsign, out of eight neon tints: Neon Yellow, Acid Green, Laser Green, Mint, Electric Cyan, Ultraviolet, Hot Magenta and Neon Pink (until one is picked); there is no other color to choose. All are bright and saturated, so they read on black space, and keep clear of the HUD's azure, of the sides' orange and blue in Capture the Flag and of the red of danger: their hues lie at least 25° from the azure and the orange, 20° from red and 25° from each other, and the palette runs round the color wheel in that order. Each bot has its own: Juno Neon Yellow, Ceres Acid Green, Kessler Laser Green, Vega Mint, Orion Electric Cyan, Sputnik Ultraviolet, Halley Hot Magenta, Laika Neon Pink; the solo round's choice of rivals, the intro's list of pilots and the scoreboard show every pilot's tint as a swatch beside the name. Online the tint a Host chose travels with its ship's slow state as an entry of the palette (its number), and a browser takes nothing else. Two Hosts who chose the same tint are told apart by when they joined the tournament: the one who came first keeps it, a later one gets the nearest free tint on the color wheel (the next one in the list first, then the one before it, then two away, and so on); with all eight taken, a Host keeps its choice. Every browser settles this twice a second from the join times it knows (those that elect the lead browser), so all come to the same. Bots keep their own tints, even where a Host flies in the same one: the mark before the name tells them apart. A beam is a neon tube: a core leaning mostly to the tint and a wide halo glowing in it, so the tint shows on every quality rung. In Capture the Flag every ship is marked in its side's orange or blue instead; its laser keeps its own tint. The two trophies wear their own colors, which no Host can pick (see the hangar): the Sovereign ivory with a glow of gold, the Magpie chalk white with the blue-green shimmer of its flip paint; their markers, swatches and radar dots ring in that glow, and the menu names the colors in place of the palette.
- **Labels.** The readouts at the bottom (autopilot, overdrive, hook, torpedo) carry their key as a small keycap, for the keyboard (in the Host's layout) or the gamepad, whichever the Host flies with, so nothing has to be remembered in a panic; touch screens name their buttons themselves. Every mark in the HUD says what it is: "Course" and "Brake" under the velocity markers, the name and distance above a fixed reference, and under a flight school marker what to do with it ("Target", "Stop here", "Ring 2/4", "Drone").
- **Who flies.** Every name the HUD shows (markers, kill feed, lock warning, sponsor break, scoreboard) stands behind a mark: a robot head for a bot, a person for a real Host. The game reads it from the ship's id, which no Host chooses, and draws it as a shape (a CSS mask), not as a character: a callsign may carry anything, emoji included, and still cannot pose as the other kind.
- **Space dust.** Fine dust stays fixed in space, so drift is visible even far from any rock. From 20 m/s on it draws streaks, the way the ship has covered in 0.05 s (12.5 m at the speed limit), growing to full strength at 200 m/s.
- **Feeling the speed.** The chase camera hangs back by 60 % of a ship radius at a full burn, the view widens by 7° while the drive burns, by another 10° at the speed limit and by 9° more for a moment after a gate's coil boost, and the camera trembles with the drive. Speed is felt most against something near: low over a world's ground (fully within 5 m, fading out by 100 m) or within 2.2 ship radii of anything of the field, the view widens by up to 6° more at the speed limit and the motion streaks double and stream up to 1.8 times as fast, eased in and out over a quarter of a second. As near the ground, the chase camera looks 5° down past the nose, so the ground and its horizon fill the picture rather than the sky over the nose, and leans with a ground that banks across the ship (by a third of the bank, at most 10°), both eased in and out over half a second; in free space it looks along the nose. Riding the hover field (within 20 m of the ground) the camera trembles in its buffeting, by 1.2 % of a ship radius at the speed limit, by 3 % more while the hull glides on the ground. The hover field lifts the ground's dust under a hull riding it faster than 20 m/s: up to 60 puffs a second from 50 m/s on at the ride height, fewer higher up, rising at 10 to 40 m/s and left behind the ship, seen within 4 km (looks only). Hosts who ask for less motion get no trembling and no jolts, and standing streaks.
- **Close call.** Passing a rock faster than 60 m/s within 2.2 ship radii of it, without touching it, earns 25 points and a "CLOSE CALL" in the HUD; the same rock pays again only once the ship has left it 5 ship radii behind. Not in the flight school.

### Arenas

Every round is fought somewhere else. A solo round plays in the Rings of Saturn, the default arena, unless the Host picks another or "By chance"; the flight school trains there (only its orbit drill flies at the Event Horizon), and the menu's backdrop is that arena too. An online tournament walks through all seven in an order of its own: the room's code shuffles them, and round after round follows that order, so no arena comes twice before all others have come, and every browser works out the arena from code and round alike, unless the game master chooses another for a round (see "The lead browser's Host is the game master"). When a round starts, the arena's name and what makes it special show in the HUD, and the announcer opens the broadcast from there. A new arena brings a fresh clone for everyone, printed on the edge of its field, since where a ship flew may be rock now.

- **The Belt.** The home of the tournament: 40 rocks of 110 to 350 m mean radius, ten ships long and more, in a field of 2.25 km radius. Cover everywhere. The Rockborn mine these rocks while the Games are fought among them (`src/world/mining.ts`). Ten rocks carry a claim. Six of them carry a rig: a platform on four legs, its rim in yellow and black hazard stripes, a lattice derrick 46 m tall of chamfered beams with its drill string going down into the rock, a crew hut with lit windows and the glowing screens of its control panels, a cab at the platform's corner with a lit window, sodium lamps under hoods on posts at the platform's corners, white floodlights in housings on the derrick, a red light on its top, and a warm pool of the lamps' light on the rock around it. The rigs and the buoys are built of second-hand steel, as everything out here: riveted plates patched in over the years, rusting from their seams, grimy, dented, chipped and stenciled with the claims' marks, the boom of a cutting laser striped as a moving part, and the Belt's dust has settled on all of it, darkening the steel into the rock's own tone towards the ground (fully at the ground, none 12 m above it). Dust rises from the drill hole beside each rig, 3 m/s up to some 50 m, spreading and thinning over 16 s, lit by the sun. On four rigs a boom reaches out over the rock with a cutting laser: it saws back and forth along a cut 36 m long, there and back in 7 to 11 s, sparks fly where it bites and cool within a second, and the cut glows behind it where the beam just passed. In vacuum the beam itself would be invisible; only the dust of its own cut shows it, faint along its way and bright where it bites. Up to three other rocks are cut half away: the Rockborn sawed a slab off and left a smooth face with saw marks, as wide as the rock allows (some 50 to 130 m), two or three tunnels leading in from it, lit warm inside. The face lies where the rock's measured surface reaches its plane, never more than 3 % of the rock's mean radius above it, so a ship touching the face touches the rock within that margin (the measured surface itself is within 2 %). Two or three beacon buoys on masts mark every claim, their amber lights blinking slowly (every 3 to 4.5 s, fading in and out), and on six rocks the Rockborn sprayed their slogans in neon paint, glowing faintly, in English as painted: ROCKBORN, MARS PAYS NOTHING, WE DIG, YOU DIE, THE BELT REMEMBERS, NO WALL IN SPACE, OUR ORE, NOT OUR VOTE. Between the rocks hang 60 thin clouds of dust, 250 to 700 m across, barely seen in the sun's side light and glowing when a pilot looks towards the sun, as dust scatters light forward (Henyey and Greenstein's phase, g = 0.55); they give the field its depth. What stands on a rock turns with it and goes when it breaks. Rigs and buoys are solid steel: the deck, its legs and lamp posts, the derrick (a box tapering in two steps, its lattice too tight for a ship), the drill string under the deck, the hut, the cab, a cutter's two booms and its head, a buoy's mast and can collide as boxes standing in their rock's own frame. Ships bounce off them and crash on them as on rock; beams, the hook, the line of sight, torpedoes, wreckage, probes and the field's other pieces meet them as they meet the rock, and a beam on a rig counts as a hit on its rock. The dust is scenery: a ship flies through it. Flying through the dust of a drill hole, the pilot hears it patter on the hull, the more the thicker it is and the faster the ship flies (in the heart of a plume at 50 m/s and more about 18 grains a second); the thin clouds between the rocks are not felt. Which rocks are claimed and where everything stands follows from the arena's seed, the same on every browser.
- **Rings of Saturn.** Inside Saturn's ring, the planet 13 km away, its ring running through the arena as a line across the sky. Saturn is drawn from a real map of it in its real colors, muted gold and tan with faint bands lying in the ring's plane, as its equator does; the ring from a real profile of Saturn's rings: the dim, translucent inner ring (the planet shows through it), the bright, dense middle ring the arena lies in, the dark gap beyond it and the fainter outer ring, a warm grey. The ring is not the same all around: its density swells and thins by up to 60 % in clumps drawn out along the orbit, some 3 km long and 400 m across, a little brighter where denser, and in patches faint wakes run through it, ridges 75 m apart slanting some 20° off the orbit, as a ring's own gravity gathers its particles; the dense middle ring stays opaque through them, so they show most in the thin rings, and the wakes fade out where they would lie closer on screen than about a dozen pixels, so they never shimmer. Planet and ring are scaled down alike, about a 13,000th of the real ones (4.5 km in radius), and the ring is drawn wider than the real one around a planet of that size (from 1.7 to 3.7 of its radii instead of 1.2 to 2.3), so the arena, 2.9 radii out, lies in its bright middle ring. The sun stands about 8° above the ring's plane: the planet throws its shadow across the ring, and the ring throws its own across the planet's southern clouds, as dark as the ring is dense where the sunlight passes it. The ring's sunlit face shines; seen from its other face, only its thin parts let light through. Here and there a crystal of ice in the ring glints in the sun, as do the specks of ice around the pilot, each catching the sun only from a few directions, so the ring sparkles as the pilot moves. Around the pilot the ring is a layer 250 m thick through the arena's middle (125 m above and below its plane), open space above and below it, and its sheet is drawn as the faces of a slab 200 m above and below the plane, beyond the reach of every chunk of the layer (up to some 185 m) and short of the large chunks above and below it (from some 255 m), so it never cuts through the ice. Each face shows the part of the slab beyond it as seen from the pilot: from outside the slab all of the ring on the far face and nothing on the near one, the open space on the pilot's side open; inside it both faces, the more on the side the plane lies, half each at the plane, so crossing the layer never jumps. How much of the ring's depth the sheet shows follows the pilot's height over the plane alone, never a point's distance from the pilot, so no hole ever opens in the ring around the pilot: all of it from 3 km above or below the plane and farther, a disk, growing smoothly more see-through the nearer the pilot comes, down to 8 % of it within the layer, a faint veil (straight down from the plane about 7 % opaque over the dense ring around the arena), where the chunks, specks and grains are the ring. Seen along a flatter line the sheet shows more, up to all of the ring, so towards the horizon it always closes into a band (from the plane, along lines 2.3° flat and flatter). Even the densest part of the ring holds back at most 88 % of the light behind it, so stars and the planet show faintly through it from anywhere. Up close the sheet's ringlets are softened (the ring's strip averaged over some 280 m in the layer, about 30 m at 1 km above it, hardly at all from 2 km), or they would run as stripes to the vanishing point of every view along the orbit. A shadow on the sheet keeps half the sunlight seen from afar (the slab's ice between the shadow's caster and the face scatters light into it) and fades out towards the layer, where a shadow on the faint veil would read as a hole and the specks carry the shadows. The menu's camera turns at the field's center, in the plane, the ring a band at the horizon. 16,000 specks of ice (3 m each, within 1.6 km of the pilot, moving along with the pilot) show the layer from afar: from above a glittering floor, from below a ceiling. Inside it, 32,000 grains of ice (20 cm each, fixed in space in a cube 120 m across around the camera, faded out within 6 to 15 m of it and from 30 m out, thinning out over the layer's outer fifth) rush past a ship flying through, drawn besides as streaks as long as the way the ship travels in 0.025 s (from 20 m/s on, in full from 200 m/s), and patter on the hull, heard through it (about 12 bursts a second at 100 m/s and faster, fewer the slower the ship and the nearer the layer's faces). The grains do nothing to ships, beams or torpedoes: they are the ring's look and feel. A real ring is thinner and harder: Saturn's main rings are mostly about 10 m thick, of pieces from centimeters to a few meters, which at a ship's speed would sandblast its hull; the Games' layer is 250 m thick so there is room to fly in it, and its grains are harmless. Wherever the sun's shadows reach (3.5 km around the field's center), the sheet, the specks and the grains lie in the shadows of the ice, the ships, the rings of a race, the battleship and a comet, so a chunk of ice casts a dark lane through the specks behind it and the own ship's shadow falls across the grains around it. Out at Saturn the sun gives about a hundredth of the light it gives Earth (some 15 W/m², about 1,400 lux in place of about 128,000 above Earth's air); the flight computer's sensors expose for it, so the picture is as bright as nearer the sun. 120 small chunks of ice (20 to 73 m) lie within the layer, reaching 2.9 km from the center: obstacles and cover among the grains; 16 large ones (90 to 127 m) float in the open space 400 m to 1.2 km above or below it, within 2.25 km of the center. The chunks are water ice, as Saturn's ring particles are, and not pure white. Each has the shape of one of the 16 rocks and collides exactly as that rock does; only its surface differs, and only in its maps (`src/world/ice.ts`): rounded masses under a porous frost crust, a few soft impacts with clean ice in them, sublimation pits whose floors are dark with the dust the vanished ice left behind, two to four flat, glossy fracture planes where the chunk split (cut 3.5 to 8.5 % of its radius below its highest point, so they never stray far from its outline), crack networks over it, finer in the fracture planes, specks of dark silicate dust and, on most chunks, a dirty band of dust frozen into the ice. The ice is a cool white, glossy where it broke and rough where frost covers it; its cracks are deep blue. Sunlight passes through it: the way the light travels through the chunk (taken on its ellipsoid towards the sun) dims it, red first, so a thin rim glows blue behind the line between day and night, and a chunk with the sun behind it shines blue along its edges and cracks. Its frost glitters: one grain may sit in each cell 2 m across on the template (1 to 5 m on a chunk), and lights up as a bright dot of a pixel or two where its crystal face mirrors the sun to the eye, so the glints come and go as the chunk turns or the pilot flies past, never by time alone; a cell smaller on screen than about six pixels shows none, so a far chunk never crawls with them. Up close a shared tile adds frost grain, small fractures and tiny pits. The billboards far away show the same colors. The layer is the arena's tactical plane: a pilot dives through it to shake off a chaser and hides among its chunks, and the race course dives through it four times a lap (see Race). Ice breaks easier (4 hits plus one per 25 m of mean radius) and turns faster. Out here the sun is weaker: radiators shed 24 % a second in sunlight instead of 20 %.
- **The Satellite Junkyard.** In orbit 5,000 km above Earth, in the junk of the Orbital Freedom Network ("Internet for everyone. Junk forever."): a mega-constellation of the 26th century, owned by a billionaire friend of the President, left to rot where it was launched. The field is 90 pieces of its junk (60 to 320 m long): flat satellites with their single long solar wing, some shredded, their wings broken off in pieces; spent upper stages with their cold engine bells; halves of payload fairings; stacks of satellites that were never released, one of them still lighting the network's name in neon. A satellite's bus is wrapped in crinkled gold or silver foil and carries its antennas: a flat phased array facing Earth, two dishes and a star tracker on top, a white radiator on its flank, thrusters at its front; its wing shows deep blue cells on the front and a white sheet behind, cells that read up close and fade to their mean blue afar, never into a moiré. The stages and fairings wear the network's white livery, laid once around each piece: its name stenciled once or twice, real panels and welds, the cable raceway along the tank, a few large scuffs down to bare metal, soot thickening towards the engine, the fairing's magenta band and its nose scorched by the ascent, quilted blankets lining it inside. A stage closes in domes at both ends, its payload adapter ring at the front, at the back its thrust frame, pressure bottles and the engine, its bell darkening from the copper of the throat through the heat's tint to soot. The stacks show their satellites one by one, each a bus in foil with its wing folded on top, latched to the posts. Each circles on an orbit of its own about the field's center, 600 m to 2.7 km out, all at 25 m/s, so their paths cross. The network's dead relay hub hangs in the middle: an engine block and two spars, a cross 800 m wide and one body of 19.5 million tonnes, which no laser breaks. A piece of junk collides as far as it is seen: as an ellipsoid hugging its bulk, and as the box around each part of it drawn in foil, solar cells or paint, out to a wing's tip and a stage's end. A ship punches through its thin sheets (a wing, a fairing, a stage's tank) unharmed and strikes its dense cores (a satellite's bus, a stage's engine, a stack) by their mass (see Collisions). Junk breaks easier (4 hits plus one per 25 m). The solar cells are glossy: as a piece turns, its panels flash in the sun. *Two deliberate deviations:* real junk at that height circles Earth at some 5.9 km/s (7.5 km/s in low orbit), and pieces on crossing orbits meet at several km/s, faster than any laser could follow; here they cross at 25 m/s, slow enough to fight among. And real mega-constellations orbit low, at some 550 km, where Earth is a floor filling half the sky, its horizon 23° below the level all around; seen from there by a chase camera looking down at a slant, that horizon reaches behind the camera and the picture bends it into a pointed dome. The Games' junk field hangs at 5,000 km instead, so Earth shows as the ball it is.
  - **Beyond the field** about 5,000 more pieces of the network drift on circles about Earth's center, from 12 km below the arena's orbit to 15 km above it, passing the field's center no nearer than 9.4 km (beyond the reach of any ship, so they need no collisions) and no farther than 19 km: 70 strings of up to 24 satellites still in their launch formation, 600 m apart, their wings across their orbit and their panels all tilted alike, so a flash runs along a string as it passes; 1,300 dead satellites and 300 shredded ones tumbling, flashing in the sun whenever a panel mirrors it; 120 spent stages turning end over end; 45 clouds of 40 fragments each, glittering, where two pieces collided. They wear the colors of the field's junk: a satellite's gold foil (silver on one in four) at the root of its blue wing, white on the wing's back, the stages white with soot towards one end, the fragments white paint or gold foil. They drift at 25 m/s, too, and show as long as they are within 24 km either side of their nearest pass. 11 km out, away from the sun, a billboard satellite of the network still flickers ORBITAL FREEDOM in neon, its power failing.
  - **Earth** hangs below as a ball, at its real size (6,371 km in radius): 68° across, its rim 56° beneath the level all around, so the whole ball fits the view looking straight down, and wherever the camera looks, Earth's disk lies in front of it or only its rim reaches into the picture, never bent into a dome. The arena does not hang straight above the point beneath it but 11.5° towards the sun from there, so the point beneath lies at dusk (the sun 3.2° beneath the horizon there) and the terminator runs through the middle of the ball, while the arena itself, high above, is in sunlight (the sun 53° above Earth's rim as seen from it), as a satellite catches the sun after sunset on the ground. Beneath lies the Ionian Sea; the ball reaches from the Arctic to central Africa and from Greenland to India. Towards the sun lies the day side, drawn from NASA's Blue Marble (a July without clouds, see License): the Atlantic, Spain, France and Britain, the Maghreb, the Sahara and West Africa, the sun low near the terminator and up to 53° high at the far rim, the seas darker with the sun's glint on them, clouds drifting over them, the air's blue haze thickening towards the rim. Italy lies in the dusk. Away from it lies the night: Greece and eastern Europe, Russia, Turkey, Egypt, the Middle East and India at the rim, drawn from NASA's map of Earth's night lights (Black Marble 2016, see License) and grown into the 26th century: today's lights brighter and spreading into megacities, white and neon at the hearts of the biggest, small towns glowing out into the dark, new towns sprouting around today's lights, the land between them dark; clouds drift over them and dim them. Between them the terminator is as wide and soft as on Earth: the sunlight crosses ever more air towards it (by Kasten and Young's air mass, through an optical depth of 0.15 for red, 0.21 for green and 0.33 for blue straight up), so the land dims and reddens; the sun's disk sets 0.85° below the horizon (its upper limb lifted by the air's refraction); the clouds, some 6 km up, keep its last red light 2.5° longer; after it the twilight's blue sky lights the ground, falling by a factor e every 3° and gone 9° below. The cities' lights come on between the sun 1.5° and 8° below the horizon, as the sky darkens. The day side is shown as an eye adapted to the low sun sees it, 2.8 times brighter than the sun overhead would light it, so land and cities read side by side. The air along the horizon: blue where the sun stands high over it, yellow, orange and red where it sets, the colors reaching a few degrees into the night (the haze some 25 km up sees the sun 5° longer than the ground), then on the night side the thin green line of the airglow 90 to 105 km up, apart from the planet's dark rim. Now and then a satellite of the network burns up in the air far below: a short fiery streak with pieces breaking off beside it, one starting about every 13 s somewhere within 2,300 km of the point beneath the arena, each running 12 s across some 160 km (a real one is slower, about 7.5 km/s; this one shows within a fight). Earth is scenery: it neither pulls nor blocks, and it is drawn behind everything else, since at its real size it lies far beyond the camera's far plane.
- **Jupiter.** Half the arena is space, half is Jupiter's air: its cloud deck, the top of its clouds, runs through the field's center, in the reference plane (what the air does: see Jupiter's air). The planet is 40 km in radius to the deck, the game's scale: the real Jupiter is about 1,750 times as large and pulls with 2.5 g, and only so small a planet shows its curved horizon from a few kilometers up. It pulls with 0.83 g at the deck, falling off with the square of the distance from its center: 0.71 g at the arena's upper edge 3.4 km above, 0.99 g at its lower edge 3.4 km below, nearly the same everywhere a ship flies, as near a real planet: whatever drifts sinks into the clouds. Its clouds are drawn from a real map of Jupiter in its real colors (NASA's Cassini map, see License): the arena hangs over its southern belts at 13° south, the white zones and rust belts running across the view, the great red storm, 9 km wide, turning some 6 km off to the south and west and drifting past as the planet turns, white ovals strung along a belt. The map gives the bands' colors and the great storms; on top of it the cloud tops are as turbulent as a probe skimming them sees them, generated in code: eddies some 750 m across shove the clouds and the map's colors up to 550 m aside, smaller ones some 175 m across stir them within, billows sharp-edged where they rise above the rest, grained down to a few meters, bright threads drawn out east-west along the shear between the bands. About half the cells of two grids, 2 km and 600 m wide, hold a vortex, an oval 1.6 times as wide as tall (240 to 580 m tall in the coarse grid, 70 to 175 m in the fine), its clouds wound into a spiral; one in five of them, an anticyclone, carries a white oval at its heart, ringed darker. The great red storm turns as one great spiral. Pop-up clouds, 35 to 95 m across, have risen above the tops in about a third of the cells of a grid 150 m wide, white, each casting a soft shadow away from the low sun, and the low sun lights the billows from the side. The colors are pushed a little richer than the map's: its light zones whiter and a cool blue-white in their brightest clouds, its belts browner, the white clouds over the belts cream. The light zones drift east against the dark belts at 2 m/s each way, the vortices' spirals turn slowly, and all of it turns with the planet. The sun stands low over the clouds (about 8° above the horizon): their tops scatter its light from within their haze (Lommel-Seeliger's law), so they keep more of it than a solid surface would at so low a sun. From above, the deck is a sea of cloud that thins in gaps through which the depths show and closes when seen at a slant; from below, a dim ceiling with the light coming through. Under it hang two thinner sheets of cloud, 400 m and 1.2 km down, the map's colors blurred, in the light the haze lets down to them, and below them only haze, wherever a view goes down. The haze under the deck is full of cloud, never one flat color: banks some 300 m across and 70 m tall right under the deck, lighter zones and darker belts in the map's colors, and dark towers 200 m across and 1.5 km tall, all of them smaller deeper (a tenth of that size 3 km down), as the eye's reach shrinks, and shoved about in swirls up to 300 m right under the deck, as the tops above them are; the haze is brighter looking up towards the deck, darker and redder looking down and the deeper the ship flies. Wisps of cloud, 50 to 180 m long right under the deck and shorter deeper, drawn out along the wind, stream past a ship within some 200 m from 50 m under the deck on. The wind carries all of it east at 30 m/s. The clouds only change the haze's light, never how far anything is seen (see Jupiter's air). Lightning strikes in the clouds around a ship and below it, every 5 s on average: three strokes within half a second, within 3 km across and 300 m to 2.5 km down, lighting the haze within some 400 m (its dense clouds brightest, the dark towers standing out against them), the wisps and the sheets around it. Over the southern horizon glow the auroras of its south pole, 77° away across the planet. Jupiter's auroras shine in its upper air, a few hundred kilometers above its clouds: a thin shell on a planet of 71,500 km, which at the game's scale would be some 150 to 300 m. The game draws them from 100 to 400 m above the deck, lifted a little so they show, and keeps that shell's proportion: they lie as a thin glowing band on the planet's rim towards the pole, 35° to either side of the pole's direction, as high over the limb as that shell rises over it seen from the ship (about 1.5° from the arena's top edge, more lower down, seen from no lower than 800 m). A deliberate deviation: the pole's oval itself lies far beyond the horizon of any ship in the arena, so its light is drawn on the limb. It looks as an aurora does on a planet's limb seen from orbit: one smooth, continuous layer of light hugging the rim, starting right at the clouds' edge, pale blue-white and brightest at its foot 100 m up, violet and magenta above, fading out smoothly upwards (Jupiter's auroras shine mostly in ultraviolet; the game shows them in these colors). Along the rim it varies only in broad stretches some 12° long, the oval's folds seen edge-on a little brighter and taller, drifting over most of a minute; a fainter, taller veil lies behind it, and a faint, broad striation changes its brightness by a tenth at most. No rays, spikes or flames, nothing sparkles. The band breathes gently, brightening and dimming some 8 s from bright to dim and back, as real auroras pulse slowly. With reduced motion the breathing holds still and only the folds drift; two of its moons hang in the sky, sulphur-yellow Io with dark volcanic spots (2.2° wide) and white Europa with rust-red cracks (1.4°), larger than from the real cloud tops, as the planet is smaller. 5 km below the deck, beyond the arena, its depths crush whatever reaches them, which the air keeps any ship from. 22 rocks.
- **Corona.** Close to the sun, which fills a quarter of the sky (3.1 km in radius, 12 km off, about 30° across), its face boiling with cells, darkening and reddening towards the limb. Four groups of sunspots, a leader and a smaller follower each, dark umbrae in penumbrae of radial filaments with bright faculae around them, lie in two belts north and south of its equator; the sun turns, so they drift across its face over a round, about 26° in five minutes, some turning in over one limb while others near the far one. The real sun takes about 25 days for a turn; the Games' turns fast enough for its spots to be seen moving, never within a second. Along the limb its chromosphere is a thin red rim and its inner corona a glow fading out over a radius in slow streamers. Over the limb stand prominences in six active regions, sixteen in all, arcades of plasma arcs along magnetic loops, the tallest half the sun's radius high: each fills with plasma over a few minutes and rises as it does, then fades while its plasma rains back down along the loop in bright knots, falling faster towards the feet (coronal rain), and comes again. All of it runs on the field time, so every Host sees the same sun. So close to a star the sky is never black: the outer corona and the dust around the sun scatter its light across the whole sky, white and brightest along the limb, falling off about as the square of the angle from the sun, orange farther out, and a dim warm brown even straight away from it. Looking near the sun, its glare lays a warm veil over the whole picture, rocks and ships included, the brighter the nearer the sun, and the picture's edge on the sun's side flares up; it shows as well when the sun stands just outside the picture, and fades once it is behind the pilot. In its glare the radiators shed only 60 % of their heat (12 % a second instead of 20 %), so the shadows of the 30 rocks are where lasers cool down. Every 50 seconds of field time a solar storm blinds the sensors for 8 seconds: no markers, no lock warnings, the link crackles, and the bots only see what lies within 750 m. The field time is shared, so a storm hits every Host at once.
- **Event Horizon.** A black hole in the middle, its horizon 350 m in radius, pulls everything in: 1 g at 2 km, four times that at 1 km, falling off with the square of the distance. Within about 555 m, 1.6 times its horizon, no drive gets out (its pull beats the 13 g of the main drive), and a ship that touches the horizon is gone; the speed limit does not hold against its pull, so a ship falling in passes 250 m/s (see Speed limit); the escape gauge and the flight computer's alarms warn long before (see Alarms). A ship can also fall around it without falling in: in free drift it flies an orbit about the hole, a circle 1 km from its center at 198 m/s, once around in 32 s, 2 km out at 140 m/s in 90 s, and at the arena's edge (3.4 km) at 108 m/s in 197 s; the flight computer's orbit readout says when letting go flies a stable one (see Orbit readout), and its alarms are quiet while it is flown. Hot gas spirals into it in a thin, glowing disk from 600 m to 2.8 km: smooth, luminous gas, white-hot at its soft inner edge, peach further out, orange and dark at the outer edge. Its brightest gas is held a little above the brightness at which the picture's bloom spills a glow, so only the hottest spots of the band and the halo glow out, the disk and its halo keep these colors instead of washing the picture out to white, and the stars and the black shadow keep their contrast. On the full picture the gas streams in fine streaks along its flow, about 80 m apart across the disk. A deliberate deviation for the look: the gas within 400 m of the pilot is left out, so flying inside the disk's thin outer gas does not lay an orange wash over the picture. It thins out above and below its middle plane over about 19 m at the inner edge and 32 m at the outer (a bell curve's width), so seen edge-on, where a line of sight runs through much more of it, it is a thin bright streak. Its brightness varies in large, soft clumps, a few around the disk and a few across it, redder where the gas is thin; they turn the way the arena's rocks circle (counterclockwise seen from above), the inner ones faster: once a second at the inner edge, once in about 8 s at the outer, and drift slowly. Where the gas streams towards the pilot it shines up to almost four times brighter and whiter, where it streams away much dimmer and redder (Doppler beaming), so one side of the disk outshines the other. The disk is scenery: nothing in it pushes, burns or blocks a ship. Three deliberate deviations, for the look: a real disk ends at three times the horizon's radius (1,050 m), where orbits stop being stable; a real one would look redder overall, its gas's time slowed by its speed and its inner edge's light by the hole's gravity; and the inner disk's far side, behind the hole as the pilot sees it, swells to about 200 m above and below the more edge-on the disk is seen, out to 1.1 km from the hole, because the lens bends only the picture it sees (see below) and a real hole bends the light of its far side's whole face up over its shadow. The hole bends light: the picture around it is drawn through a gravitational lens (the picture of space only: the flight computer's lines in space, the reference plane's grid, its drop lines and the orbit, go into the display after it, unbent), so the disk and the stars behind it wrap around its round black shadow, the disk's far side bent into a broad bright halo over the shadow and under it, the disk's near side crossing in front of the shadow as a bright band, and the light grazing the shadow piles up into a thin, warm-white ring. Three deliberate deviations, for the look: the disk's near side in front of the shadow is drawn as from a camera brought nearly into the disk's plane (a twentieth of its height above it), so it crosses the shadow as a thin, bright, cream-white streak just under the hole's middle instead of a broad dim band (a pilot closer than about 1 km sees none: what gas lies between them and the hole lies within the 400 m around them, which are left out); a warm glow hugs the photon ring outside it, standing in for the thinner rings of the disk's light bent round the hole again and again, which the lens's single bend leaves dark; and where the edge-on disk meets the halo's left and right sides it flares in two hot spots, the side whose gas streams towards the pilot the brighter, the band running on faintly from them, while the disk is seen within about 18° of edge-on (fading out by 41°). Shadow and ring are cones around the line to the hole, measured along each pixel's own ray, so they keep a round thing's crisp outline wherever the hole stands, stretched by the perspective towards the picture's edge, and a hole beside the camera still shows the rim of its shadow; the bend fades out as the hole comes abeam (from 73° to 90° off the camera's axis), where the light it would bend comes from outside the picture. Ships, rocks, gates and the racing line in front of the hole are seen as they are and never show up a second time in the bent picture (the lens reads the scene's depth: light it would bend that comes from something in front of the hole, nearer along its ray than the ray's closest point to the hole, is looked for a little farther out on the same ray, or the pixel stays unbent). 20 rocks circle it in a flat ring 1.4 to 2.9 km out, in the disk's plane among its faint outer gas, faintly veiled by the gas between them and the pilot (the lens adds it in front of whatever lies inside the disk's slab, from the scene's depth, held below a share of the rock's own light, so a rock keeps its dark side, its shading and its silhouette as cover should), at 25 m/s; a deliberate deviation: the hole's gravity would sling them around at 115 to 165 m/s, too fast to fight among. The hole is a primordial black hole, left over from the Big Bang, circling the sun some 500 AU out (see The universe, "The black hole"); the astronomers' estimate of a few Earth masses would give it a horizon a few centimeters across. Two deliberate deviations: its size, larger than that estimate (a horizon of 350 m would take about 0.12 suns, 2.95 km of horizon for every sun of mass), and its pull, softened so the hole can be flown around and fought beside: a real hole with a horizon of 350 m pulls with some 400 billion g at 2 km and would tear a ship apart long before it got there.
- **The Derelict.** A world ship, broken in two, 4.5 km from bow to stern in sections of 700 m, and nothing else: no rocks, only its hull to hide behind. The bow with its bridge tower and the stern with its engines drifted apart (a gap of about 800 m, 1.4 km between the centers of the torn sections), and the stern tilted; each half is one body, its sections welded (the bow 49.8 million tonnes, the stern 45.1), moving as one when something strikes it and meeting the other half as a body; the ship died long ago, so its plating, its torn ends and its drive are cold and dark, and only some decks of windows still burn on emergency power, fed by batteries of long-lived isotopes that last a thousand years: most runs steady, some dark, and about one in seven on a failing circuit that stutters in bursts of a second or so every half minute. Red beacons on its spine, its flanks and the bridge's mast blink slowly, once every 2.4 s. No sponsor ever got onto this hull: it carries no ads and no neon, only the paint of the age that built it, lit by nothing but the sun. Its plating is that of an old industrial hull worn down by centuries of micrometeorites: plates in courses of 2 and 4 m with grooved seams, rows of rivets 40 cm apart, access hatches and vents, and the pits of the micrometeorites everywhere; impact craters of a few sizes, the large ones (up to about 4 m across) rare, each a dark, pitted bowl in a rim scoured to bright metal with faint rays around it; here and there a hole punched through to the frames and decks behind it, its edge torn into petals; soot of the battle streaked along the hull, its edge faintly tempered to straw and blue by a heat long gone cold; dust in every recess. The plating's relief is a normal map baked from its heights, one tile of 512 × 512 texels for about 34 m of hull (6.6 cm a texel), with its mipmaps, so it calms from afar instead of shimmering; every part of the hull takes it at a place and a mirroring of its own, and its tone changes along the hull in patches about a hundred meters across (stretches replaced in the long years of its building, soot), so no two neighbors look alike. Along each flank runs a band of off-white livery with a dark blue stripe above and below, and on it in large stenciled letters its motto, split where the ship broke: PER ASPERA on the bow, AD ASTRA on the stern; its registry PAAA 01 by the bridge, beside the emblem of the united Earth (a globe with an orbit around it and five stars above, in white on a blue disc), and again on the stern. The paint is faded, chipped down to the plating, scratched and pitted by micrometeorites; up close its outline stays sharp and smooth. On the sections' backs sit flat biodomes 75 m across, their glass shattered (a third of the panes blown out), their gardens frozen when the air went: dead trees and shrubs rimed with frost. Frost grows on every face turned away from the sun, in patches, and in the plating's recesses on both sides, the sun keeping the rest of its own side bare. From both torn ends a mist of ice crystals still escapes into the gap, from tanks the reactor's still warm core slowly vents, drifting out at 4 m/s, 450 m far, widening from the hull's 140 m to 400 m across, each jet staying on its torn end when a comet shoves its half; it does no harm, but flying through it the crystals patter on the hull, the more the thicker the mist and the faster the ship (about 15 times a second where it is thickest, at 100 m/s and faster). Its dead drift in the mist and between the halves, 360 of them at their true size (a person 1.75 m, a twentieth of a fighter), half of them settlers and crew as the break caught them, real people made with MakeHuman (eight figures: men and women, young and old, in everyday clothes and coveralls, one under an old helmet with a cracked visor, and a pair; about 3,500 triangles each within 120 m of the eye, 600 beyond), their skin gone grey-white and waxy, blue-grey and faintly violet at the lips, nose, ears and fingertips, mottled, rimed with frost: curled up against the cold, limp with limbs spread, reaching for a handhold, two holding on to each other. The other half are the crew who fought the leak: when the hull began to leak they pulled on the ship's light emergency suits (a thin suit, a helmet, a small air pack, made for a decompressed deck and not for open vacuum for long) and tried to patch it from inside, until the ship broke apart and blew them out with the air (six figures from Quaternius, three of his astronaut, white plates over orange and teal under a helmet with a dark visor, and three of his SWAT figure, grey under a dark helmet with breathing hoses, as he made them, packs and all; about 5,600 triangles near, 960 far): clinging to where the plating was, tumbling, curled up, reaching back for the hull, one still holding the cable of its patch. The frost lies over all of them in the shader. They were blown out of their decks together and drift in groups of a few to a dozen within some 15 m, most of the groups in the jets of mist, the rest across the gap where the ships fly; the suited crew mostly where the breach was, a third of them still caught on the torn plating at the ends, most of the rest in the mist streaming out of them. Frost covers them, ice crystals in their hair, glinting in the sun; they turn slowly (up to 0.12 rad/s), a group drifting to and fro by up to 40 m over a few minutes, never far from the break. A ship that meets one knocks it away as the far heavier body (90 kg against 60 t): the body leaves at nearly twice the closing speed along the line between them and tumbles off, the ship keeps its speed but for some 0.3 % of the closing speed, takes no damage, and its pilot hears a soft knock through the hull. Each browser reckons this for every ship it sees by itself; nothing of it is sent or scored, and a new round finds the dead where they drifted before. On a cheaper picture fewer of them are drawn (down to two in five). The hull is solid: lasers only scorch it. It is industrial and angular: boxes of plating on frames, a spine, containers along the flanks, decks of windows, a stepped bridge tower, trusses, engine blocks with three nozzles. Each section collides as its box, exactly where its frames end.

**Races over a world** (`src/world/crack.ts`, `src/world/throat.ts`, `RACE_WORLDS` in `src/world/arenas.ts`). Besides five of the arenas of free space (every one but the Corona and the Event Horizon, which are fought in, never raced), a race may be flown over a world: a tournament arena of its own, played only by races. Its race is a track from a start to a finish, flown once (see Modes, Race over a world). Online, a race round walks through those five arenas and the race worlds in an order of its own, as every other mode walks through the seven; solo, the race worlds are offered while a race is chosen, first on the plate and marked RACE WORLD in the tournament's neon orange, and so on the game master's plate of a new round.

- **The Crack.** A rift in Europa's ice shell (see `src/world/crack.ts`), a site of its own off the campaign's Lineae on the edge of the face Europa keeps turned to Jupiter: 23 km of the ice along the rift and 6 km across it, under Europa's pull of 1.31 m/s² (0.13 g). The rift is a valley wider than it is deep, the way the long canyons of the shell are: its floor some 520 m wide (swaying by 40 m), its walls climbing 300 m to the rim in three terraces of 100 m, each a steep riser and a bench of 45 m, jagged by 25 m, so its rims stand some 940 m apart and the sky opens wider the higher the eye looks; rubble up to 15 m lies on its floor and blocks up to 35 m along the foot of its walls. It runs in two segments, each ending in a valley head where its floor climbs the 300 m to the plain over 1.6 km. Between them lies a field of **chaos terrain**, the shell broken into rafts of ice that drifted, tilted and froze in again (Conamara Chaos is the real one): rafts 60 to 110 m across standing 20 to 55 m over the frozen matrix between them, tilted up to 12°, one cell of 140 m in five left open, and a lane 300 m wide kept open for the track. In the second segment the **narrows** close its floor to 190 m for a kilometer. Three **slabs of ice** that fell across the rift and froze in span it, 600 m long, 60 m thick and 200 m wide, their undersides 90 m over the floor (in the gorge 120 m), their ends resting deep in the walls: ice 60 m thick over the 400 m it spans holds its own weight under Europa's eighth of a g (a bending stress of about 1 MPa, about what cold ice bears). The science station stands on the second segment's floor beside the track, four outposts with their masts and dishes on its eastern rim. Jupiter, 12° across, hangs 12° over the horizon straight ahead of the second segment's long straight, and the sun stands low behind it (15° up), so Jupiter shows its lit face and the rift's floor lies in the light along its length. The ice is Europa's (see the Lineae): white and a little blue, cleanest and bluest in the rift's walls, the double ridges' crests and the chaos's matrix stained reddish brown. The track starts on the first segment's floor, runs down it and under a slab, rides up its head onto the plain and across the chaos field in its lane, drops into the second segment past the station, runs its 8 km (the narrows and two more slabs on the way), climbs out of its head, swings round over the plain in a bend of 900 m and runs 3.5 km back over the double ridges to the finish, every point of it 45 m over the highest ground around: 25.5 km and 18 gates, one every 1.3 km (none within 700 m of the station), 10 km of it low over the rift's floor and 43 % over the open ice. The double ridges across the run back stand 10 m high on crests 400 m apart, gentle enough (at most 4.5°) that a ship riding its hover field over one at speed hops for a few seconds and comes down. It splits twice into two ways that join again: **in the second segment** (from 1.6 km to -4.6 km along it) the rift swings 1.2 km out to the east in a wide bow, the track's own way, 6.7 km, while the other runs straight on through a **gorge** 220 m wide with sheer walls, cut through a block of ice that stands 400 m over the plain between the two, 700 m deep, 6.2 km and the shorter and riskier one; **at the bend** the track's own way swings round wide in its 900 m, 5.9 km, while the other turns 500 m sooner, still in the last head, in a bend of 500 m low over the plain, tighter than a ship in free drift turns at the speed limit, and runs out onto the track's own way over 2.6 km of the plain, 3.9 km. Four gates stand on each way of either branch, none where the bow and the gorge still lie within 400 m of each other, every gate of either way at least 600 m from the next. A middling bot flies it in about two minutes (120 s on the track's own ways, 116 s through the gorge, 111 s through the tight bend, 107 s through both), a simulated Host with ordinary hands in 88 s with the autopilot and 98 to 135 s in free drift. The world has no edge: past its ground the shell goes on to the horizon, the first segment running on beyond the ground's end, drawn behind everything.

- **The Throat.** The winding strait that joins Kraken Mare's two basins on Titan (`src/world/throat.ts`, its landmarks' looks in `src/world/strait.ts`), under Titan's pull of 1.35 m/s² (0.14 g) and in Titan's air (as the campaign's Kraken Mare has them, see Jupiter's air): its still methane only 300 m under the deck of the orange haze, not 2 km as at the campaign's Circuit, so the air over the water is about 1.4 times as dense as at the deck. The Throat's air is drawn clearer than the haze's model elsewhere, its haze under the deck a third as thick (`THROAT_AIR`, `clear`): the eye, radar, lock and laser reach about 5.6 km over the water, three times as far as the haze's model would let them there, so the next bend always shows; this is the game's choice for a race, its density, drag, heat and lift Titan's. Titan's air clogs no radiators (nitrogen and methane, none of Jupiter's ammonia and sulfur), so only the air's own heat counts: at the limit it adds 33 heat a second against the 50 the radiators shed, and racing never burns a hull; an overdrive shove takes a ship to 450 m/s, where the air heats far faster, and two shoves 8 s apart burn about 9 hull points, a risk the pilot chooses. There the fins lift up to 20 g at the limit, the most the flight computer takes, at a drag of 2 g. The strait is laid as a course of bends, some 21 km of it from basin to basin: out of the northern basin (3 km of open water) through the Gate, a bend of 600 m between two bluffs some 120 m high, flown flat out on the thrusters alone; the Esses, three bends of 255, 260 and 250 m, 220 m apart, between banks 320 m apart; a sweeper of 900 m; the Narrows, the water 250 m wide for 1.5 km between bluffs of 170 to 215 m, two arches of rock across it 110 m over the water (part of the ground: they glide a hull that touches them, as a bluff does); the Kraken's Elbow, a bend of 255 m turning 125°; a long sweeper of 850 m; the Loop round the Mesa, bends of 650, 750 and 650 m turning 70°, 140° and 70°, and a last sweeper of 1 km out into the southern basin. The water is 320 to 600 m wide between banks of dark sand that rise over 90 m (over 30 m at the Narrows and in the canyon) to the land's top, 45 to 90 m over the sea under dunes of 12 m, low by the basins; on the outside of every bend tighter than 800 m sand is banked up 40 m high over 40 m from the water, fully from 300 m. The Mesa, a raised block of the land some 375 to 420 m high, stands inside the Loop, and the river canyon cuts straight through it: 220 m wide between walls of rock, 3.2 km long against the Loop's 4 km, swaying 200 m to either side in four bends no tighter than 287 m. Landmarks stand by the strait, solid as hulls are: a gas company's drilling rig on four stilts in the water on the Gate's outside, 170 m tall with its derrick and a burning flare; a Victory Cola sign on its pylon outside the Esses' middle bend; a beacon tower on the Mesa's tip where the canyon splits off; the wreck of an ice tug beached on the long sweeper after the Elbow. Beyond the ground the land runs on to Titan's horizon, its basins opening into the sea, and Saturn shows through the haze. The track runs from the northern basin to the southern one, 45 m over the water all the way: 20.3 km and 16 gates, one every 1.3 km, and at least 1.05 km apart in a straight line however the strait winds; it splits at the Loop into the strait's own way and the canyon's (see Branches), the canyon some 800 m shorter and the riskier. The Esses, the Elbow and the canyon's bends are tighter than the autopilot turns at the speed limit in a vacuum (324 m): only the air's lift carves a ship round them at full speed (the autopilot down to 161 m in the air over the water, in free drift to 196 m), and the racing line's orange reckons with it. A middling bot flies the strait's own way in about 109 s and the canyon's in 106 s, a simulated Host with ordinary hands in 77 to 79 s with the autopilot and 110 to 132 s in free drift.

**The sun in the sky.** Seen from space the sun is white, never the orange of a sunset on Earth: a blinding disk in a pale glare, tinted only faintly by each arena's sun color.

**The stars in the sky.** Every arena shows the real sky (`src/world/starmap.ts`): seen from Saturn, from Jupiter or from beside the sun the stars stand where they stand seen from Earth (the nearest star is 270 000 times as far as Earth from the sun; only the sun and the planets wander among them), and out there no air dims them. Its stars are the 9,096 of the Yale Bright Star Catalogue (see License), every star the eye sees under a dark sky, down to about the seventh magnitude, as points sharp on any screen: each where it stands, as bright as its magnitude says (each magnitude brighter shows about 1.7 times as bright instead of 2.5, so Sirius and the faintest fit on a screen: a star of the sixth magnitude a pixel or two, the brightest up to seven), in the color of a black body as hot as its color index B−V says (Betelgeuse orange-red, Rigel and Vega blue-white), a quarter paler than a camera sees it, as the eye takes the colors of stars. Below the catalogue's limit, where the eye makes out no single star, 24,000 fainter stars are generated in code, the more of them the nearer the Milky Way: they fill the band with its grain and change no constellation. Behind them the Milky Way glows, generated in code along the real galactic plane (its north pole at right ascension 192.86°, declination +27.13°, its center at 266.40°, −28.94°, J2000): a band about 6° to 15° wide, widest and brightest towards the center in Sagittarius with its bulge and its great star clouds (Sagittarius, Scutum, Cygnus, Carina), faint towards the anticenter in Auriga, clumped by noise, a thin dark lane of dust along its middle, the Great Rift north of it from Sagittarius through Aquila to Cygnus, the dark clouds of Ophiuchus, the Coalsack beside the Southern Cross, warmer towards the center and reddened behind dust, and the two Magellanic Clouds far south of it. Their places are real; their shapes are made up. The sky is turned into the arenas so that Saturn's north pole stands straight up (the Rings of Saturn's ring lies in its equator, and the ring is the arenas' reference plane) and the sun stands on the ecliptic where every arena sees it, 8.3° above the plane: the sun as it stands over Saturn's ring at the ecliptic longitude 151.7°, the ring open to it by as much as in 2023. Up is then 6.5° from the celestial north pole, close to Polaris; Orion stands 3.5° above the plane, 70° from the sun, the center of the galaxy in Sagittarius 33° below it. These two directions fix the sky, and the other arenas take the same: they have no pole of their own to turn it by (Jupiter's cloud deck is the top of its clouds, Earth below the Satellite Junkyard is seen from the side). The Event Horizon lies in the solar system too, some 500 AU out, where the stars stand as they do seen from Earth (see The universe, "The black hole"), its two faint dusky veils of nebula over the real sky. Until the real sky has loaded, about 6,000 stars generated in code stand in for it. Each arena's mood dims the real sky as it dims its stars (the Corona's glare leaves 15 % of it).

**The light and color of each arena.** Every arena has a mood of its own around its landmark, so it is known at first glance (`ArenaProfile.mood` in `src/world/arenas.ts`, drawn by `src/world/space.ts`): the color of space where nothing is drawn, the faint light of the sky from all around (one color from above, one from below, its strength; in the Corona from the sun's side and the far side instead), its nebulae (two to four clouds of glowing gas drawn from three cloud shapes made in code, each tinted, placed and turned for the arena, far larger than the sun's disk and softer than any star; under the real sky (see The stars in the sky) they are veils of the arena's own light, dust, the ring's glow or the sun's streamers, kept faint where they would lie over the Milky Way as a second, made-up one), how bright its stars show, the tint of the dust that streaks past, and a gentle color grade of the whole picture (a tint, a color in the shadows, and how saturated). The sun's light does the rest.

- **The Belt:** dusty and warm. Faint veils of amber and rust dust across the sky, the sky's light a warm sand, the dust ochre, the sun a little more golden than elsewhere, the shadows a warm brown; the stars a little dimmer through the dust.
- **Rings of Saturn:** cool silver and gold. A wide, faint silver-blue veil along the ring and a fainter golden one, the sky's light silver from above and the gold of the lit ring from below, the picture a touch cooler and less saturated, so the real colors of planet and ring stand out.
- **The Satellite Junkyard:** Earth's night. Blue-black above with two faint deep blue veils, the cities' warm glow lighting everything from below, the shadows a cold blue.
- **Jupiter:** warm ochre-brown. A dusty amber glow over the clouds, the sky's light ochre, the shadows brown; under the deck the haze's light takes over (see Jupiter's air).
- **Corona:** orange-white and blinding. The sky is the sun's scattered light, white around the sun, orange across most of the sky and a dim warm brown facing away, never black; the sun's outer streamers glow orange-white across a third of the sky around it. The sun shines nearly three times as strong as in the Belt, so every rock's sunlit side is near-blinding; the sky's light comes from the sun's side, warm white, and only faintly orange from the far side, so the shadows stay hard and nearly black, as under a single strong light. It drowns most stars (they show at 15 % of their brightness).
- **Event Horizon:** warm and dark. A dark starfield, its stars a little brighter than elsewhere, with only two faint dusky veils, so nothing but the disk's own cream, peach and orange gas glows around the hole; a dim warm sky, warm dust, the picture a touch warmer.
- **The Derelict:** cold, washed out and solemn, a graveyard. The sun dimmer (2.7 instead of 3.2) and pale, a cold grey sky, one faint pale veil over the wreck like a shroud, the colors drained by a fifth.

The grade never takes the flight computer's colors: in every arena the HUD's azure and the orange of what to act on keep their hue within 8° and at least four fifths of their saturation, so the racing line and the reference plane's grid read alike everywhere (`src/world/mood.test.ts`), and a ship's tint stays its own.

**The reference plane in each arena** (see Reference plane, Fixed references). Every arena lies in the plane through the field's center: the Belt's and the Corona's balls of rock around it; the Rings of Saturn in it (the ice's layer and Saturn's ring are the plane, Saturn marked beside the arena); the Satellite Junkyard's junk around it, the way down to the point of Earth beneath the arena marked as Earth; Jupiter's cloud deck in it, the planet marked straight below as Jupiter with the height over its deck (under it the depth, with a minus); the Event Horizon's disk and its ring of rocks in it, the black hole in its middle marked as the field's center (Black hole, with the distance to its horizon) instead of the center's ring; the Derelict's bow along it and its stern tilted below it, Bow and Stern each marked in the middle of its half, with its distance.

The arenas' shapes are generated in code like the rocks: ice is the rock shapes with a surface of ice (see Rings of Saturn), the network's junk is boxes and cylinders (buses, wings, tanks, bells and shells), the world ship's hull sections are boxes. Rocks and ice collide with their measured surface (see Asteroids); the junk and the hull carry an invisible collider box whose size is computed, so the collision shape is the same on every browser (an ellipsoid of 90 % of the box around the bulk of a piece of junk, the box itself for the hull).

### Jupiter's air

In the Jupiter arena the cloud deck is the top of an air that grows denser downwards, as a real atmosphere does, only squeezed (a deviation for the game): its density grows tenfold every 2 km, a scale height of 870 m where the real Jupiter's is some 27 km, so the arena's lower half goes from a thin haze to a thick soup within 3.4 km. Over the deck the air is a tenth as dense and thins by e every 100 m, a faint haze over the clouds. Everything the air does follows from a ship's altitude over the deck and its speed (`src/world/air.ts`), the same on every browser.

- **Haze.** The haze swallows light in proportion to the air's density. Just under the deck the eye sees 2.5 km (where the contrast of a dark hull against the haze falls to 5 %, an optical depth of 3), a kilometer down 790 m, two kilometers down 250 m, at the arena's bottom about 50 m; a level look just under the deck soon rises into the deck itself, as the planet curves away. The game sums the haze along a line in closed form from the altitudes of its ends, and the picture draws the same sum (see Performance); the clouds in the haze (see The arenas, Jupiter) change only the color of its light, never the sum, so a ship fades out at the same distance in a bank of cloud as in a gap. The deck is a veil on top of that: crossing it adds an optical depth of 1.5 straight on, more at a slant (its sheet in the picture holds back about 78 % straight on), so a ship that dives through it vanishes from a chaser above, unless the chaser looks almost straight down. In the haze a ship turns into a shadow before it vanishes: the haze scatters the clouds' light, while what is in it gets only the light that comes down through them (under the deck no direct sunlight at all, and of the sky's light ever less, down to 6 %); its neon and its drive still glow.
- **Fins.** Every ship of the Games carries two fins on its struts aft: a quarter of its radiators each, and in air what keeps it on its course, as a weathervane does. A hull short of one (the campaign's scrap, Lot 7731) sheds a quarter less heat, and in air is dragged round towards the side that keeps its fin, harder by the dynamic pressure (`finSpin` in `src/world/air.ts`): right under the deck at the tournament's limit some 100 rad/s² of yaw and 40 of roll, at 100 m/s some 16 rad/s², more than its thrusters turn against, so it cannot be steered there; at 40 m/s it drifts off a few degrees, which a hand on the stick takes back; over the deck, in its thin top, hardly anything. A ship for space only.
- **Senses.** Every sense reaches as far as the haze allows it: the eye and the laser to an optical depth of 3 (the beam scatters in the haze: a shot ends where the eye stops seeing), the radar to 6 (twice as far), the lock of a torpedo and the lock warning to 2 (two thirds of the eye's reach). The HUD marks a ship only within the eye's reach, the radar shows it within its own, and a torpedo loses a target it no longer sees. Bots sense the same. Rock is charted: the radar shows it at any depth.
- **Drag.** The air brakes a ship by its speed squared and its density: 1.5 g at the deck at the speed limit, a quarter of that at half the speed, ten times as much 2 km down. A step of the simulation takes off exactly what that drag takes over the step, so a slow frame never brakes a ship past standstill. The main drive (13 g) still holds the speed limit down to 1.9 km below the deck, giving back only what the drag takes: the HUD shows MAX, the flame burns low, and the clone feels the drag's push alone (1.5 g at the deck); deeper, drive and drag meet at less: 200 m/s at 2.25 km, 105 m/s at the arena's bottom. In the clouds a hard turn costs speed instead of a long drift; above them the drive brings it back without any drag, so a fight changes level: diving to shake a chaser, climbing to gain speed against the pull.
- **Lift.** In air a ship's fins and hull lift it (`aero` in `src/ship/flight.ts`): met by the air at an angle, they push it across its flight towards where its nose points, as a fighter aircraft's wings do, by the air's density and the speed squared: at most 15 g at the deck at the speed limit, a quarter of that at half the speed, three times as much a kilometer down. By the slip (the nose off the flight) it grows as a flat plate's lift does: all of it from 15° to 75°, none with the nose on the flight or square to it. The flight computer trims the fins to no more than 20 g, twice the thrusters, which deeper down the air would outdo. The lift costs drag on top of the air's own, by the lift taken squared: 3 g of braking for the 15 g at the deck, less for the same lift deeper, where the air is denser, so a carve brakes the ship and the drive pays for it. The clone feels it as it feels the drive. The autopilot counts the lift as a pull from outside and leaves drive and thrusters only what it leaves; in free drift the nose turned across the flight carves by itself. So in air a ship turns far tighter the faster it flies: at the limit with the autopilot on a circle of 184 m at the deck and 161 m a kilometer down, where in vacuum it takes 324 m (about 35 and 40 g of grip instead of 20 g); in free drift 231 m and 196 m instead of 490 m; at 100 m/s the air adds little. The racing line's orange stretches reckon with the lift where the ship flies. Every ship has it alike, bots too, and Titan's and Venus's air in the campaign give it as Jupiter's does: the place to carve is down in the clouds, the place to run is over them.
- **Heat.** The air heats a hull by the cube of its speed and the air's density: at the deck at the speed limit by 25 % of the heat capacity a second, which the radiators shed with ease (50 % a second under the deck, as in a rock's shadow: the clouds hide the sun). From 600 m below the deck on the speed limit is too fast; a kilometer down 215 m/s are still safe, two kilometers down 146 m/s, at the arena's bottom 86 m/s: the safe speed, where the air heats the hull as fast as the radiators shed (radiators upgraded in the hangar shed more). The air's heat goes into the same radiators as the laser's: the laser locks first, and once they are full, every unit of heat the air puts in beyond what they shed burns 0.075 hull points instead (at the limit two kilometers down, about 15 points a second). A clone that burns up was "Burned up in the clouds", a knockout for whoever shoved it last within 3 seconds.
- **Alarm.** HULL HEAT names the safe speed where the ship is: a CAUTION while the air heats the hull faster than its radiators shed, a WARNING from 70 % of the heat capacity, CRITICAL while the hull burns; its arrow points up, out of the air, and the screen's edges glow with the heat. Nicole says once that the hull is heating up and once that it is burning, again only after the hull has cooled below 40 %.
- **Clogged radiators.** The clouds are a place to shake a chaser off, not to camp in: under the deck their ammonia and sulfur crust over the radiators (`clogging` in `src/world/air.ts`).
