Titan's air, nitrogen and methane with none of the ammonia and sulfur (the campaign's Kraken Mare and the Throat), clogs nothing (`Air.clogs`). Just under Jupiter's deck the radiators are clogged through in 40 s, faster the deeper, twice as fast from 300 m down (20 s), and no faster below, so a ship deep down still has the time to climb out; over the deck the crust boils off in 4 s, quickly, so a ship that made it out is not burnt over the clouds. Clogged through, the radiators shed 90 % less, and the reactor's waste heat, 20 heat units a second at a full clog, which clean radiators shed without a trace, builds up in them: at rest 200 m down the laser locks within half a minute, and what the radiators cannot hold burns the hull as the air's heat does. HULL HEAT's safe speed counts the waste heat in. RADIATORS CLOGGED shows how far: a CAUTION from 25 %, a WARNING from 60 %, its arrow up; Nicole warns once when the warning comes, again only after the radiators were nearly clean (10 %). A fresh clone's radiators are clean.
- **The glow.** A hot hull shows it, to everyone (`src/view/reentry.ts`): each ship's heating is computed from its altitude and speed by the same air model, against the radiators' 50 % a second in the clouds (a hangar's better radiators too: every browser is told their step along with the looks), so the own ship, bots and other Hosts' ships look alike. From 79 % of the safe speed on (where the air heats the hull at half what the radiators shed) a shock layer of plasma glows on the side the ship flies towards and streams out behind it along its flight: a quarter of its strength at the safe speed, half at twice the radiators' rate, full at eight times (twice the safe speed); deep orange where it is thin, yellow-hot where it is pressed against the hull, and a trail of glowing plasma left behind in the air. Once the hull burns (its radiators full, see Heat) its plates glow dull red to orange, brightest on the side towards the flight, over a second and a half, cooling off over four; sparks and glowing flakes of it peel off and fall behind. A ship that dies burning (not one whose browser only went quiet) streaks on as a fireball, braked by the air and pulled down, and after 1.4 seconds breaks into seven burning pieces that sink into the clouds for 2.5 to 5 seconds. From the chase camera the trail of the own ship is not drawn (the camera would fly through it); above 45 % strength, plasma licks past the edges of the view instead. All of it is looks only, and it follows the game's heating, not a real atmosphere's: a real shock layer glows only at several kilometers a second, ten times the tournament's speed limit; the game heats a hull at 250 m/s by its squeezed air (see the scale height above), and the glow shows that heat.
- **Sound.** The air carries sound (see Sound): the wind roars past the hull, louder by the air's push on it (its density times the speed squared) and higher the faster the ship; a hot hull roars with its plasma, louder and brighter with the heat, and a burning hull crackles as its plates peel off; thunder follows lightning after the time its sound takes, at 900 m/s, the speed of sound in cold hydrogen and helium: a crack close by, a long roll far off.
- **The race.** Its course keeps to the deck: it dives up to 200 m into the clouds and climbs out of them twice a lap, never deep enough for the air to heat a hull at the speed limit.

### Asteroids

- **Huge and few.** 40 rocks with mean radii of roughly 110 to 350 m in the belt (smaller ones in other arenas; up to about 600 m along their longest axis) in a field of 2.25 km radius. A rock collides where it is seen: its surface is measured from its own mesh, for every direction from its center (the cells of a cube's faces, 32 × 32 each, each keeping the farthest point of the rock in it, blended between neighbouring cells), and ships, lasers, the hook and the line of sight all meet that surface. A ship gets at most 2 % of a rock's radius into the visible rock and touches it a little early only above a crater, whose rim its cell keeps (about 1 % of the radius in the median). The rock shapes are the same on every browser, and so is the measured surface.
- **Sixteen shapes, four kinds.** The rocks are generated: squashed or stretched lumps with crags, ridges and craters (many small, few large; the floors show fresher, brighter material). They come as dark carbon-rich rock, stony rock, reddish iron-bearing rock and shiny metal, like the real asteroid belt. Their surface is battered as a real asteroid's: 150 to 250 small craters on each, with raised rims, the young ones with bright floors and bright ejecta, the old ones weathered dark; on most of them one or two sets of parallel grooves running along the long axis, as on Phobos and Eros; networks of thin dark cracks; boulders and a fine grain of regolith; tone patches of dust. Space weathering darkens and reddens a surface over time, so fresh material shows paler and bluer than the rest, by kind. Up close a rock draws about 2,000 triangles, all this a baked relief map, and up close also a shared detail map of the smallest craters and pebbles, so the surface holds its detail when a ship flies along it; beyond twelve of its own radii, where its silhouette is a few pixels, the same shape is drawn with about 500, in its shadow as well. Collisions always use the full shape of about 22,000 triangles: the craters, grooves and cracks of the maps are only seen, never felt.
- **Gravity.** The field's pieces pull as real ones do: a rock of 100 m with some hundred-thousandth of a g at its surface, nothing a ship would ever feel, so the flight does not count it. What pulls is an arena's massive body (the black hole of the Event Horizon, Jupiter; see Arenas), and the world a place lies on: in the Boneyard, Psyche, with 0.144 m/s² (about 0.015 g), straight down, the same all over the yard, so a ship that does nothing sinks slowly towards the crater floor. A torpedo feels gravity, a well's pull and a geyser's column as a ship does, and an air's drag a third as hard per tonne: a slender body nose first loses about a third of what a fighter's blunt hull, wings, fins and drive bells lose to the same air at the same speed; in air its fins lift it as a ship's do, a third as much per tonne (10 g at the deck at its top speed), trimmed as a ship's to 20 g, on top of its thrusters, so it too turns tighter the faster it flies there; its computer counters them at once with its drive and thrusters, as a ship's autopilot holds gravity off, so it flies straight lines (one fired at the horizon without a lock holds its line and never sinks into the ground) wherever those are strong enough, and only a pull beyond them (a strong well up close) bends it; once its motor is out it falls. It has no hover field: a world's ground sets it off as rock does.
- **The hover field.** Every ship carries one. It pushes against the gravity of a large body (a planet, a moon, a planetoid such as Psyche) and so holds a ship off that body's ground: the crater floor of the Boneyard, Vesta's flank, Occator's terrace and steps, every moon's ground under a place. Where no such body pulls, in free space and against a rock of the field, it has nothing to push against and does nothing, and what stands on the ground (a rack, a module, a hull, a rock) is no ground to it. It pushes along the ground's normal with the world's own gravity times (5 m / h)², h the height of the hull's underside over the ground: at the ride height of 5 m it carries the ship's weight exactly, lower it pushes harder by the square (up to 64 times the weight), higher it fades, and beyond 20 m it is gone, fading out over the last 5 m. A damping of the speed along the normal takes out the bounce (critical at the ride height). So a ship left alone settles at 5 m over the ground, quickly on a world of strong gravity (Europa: within a few seconds) and slowly on a weak one (Psyche: within half a minute or so, Phobos: minutes). On a slope the field pushes along the slope's normal, so a ship in free drift slides down it as on ice, and settles where it carries the weight's share square to the slope (5 m over the cosine's square root). **Touching a world's ground never hurts**: where the field is too weak to hold a ship (a dive at speed, a soft world), the hull touches the ground and glides over it, never bounced and never damaged, its speed into the ground taken out and its speed along it bleeding off at a tenth a second while it touches, sparks under the hull from 20 m/s on and a grinding through it. No clone dies of a world's ground, in any mode and in the campaign. **The autopilot rides the ground**: within 100 m of a world's ground it flies the nose's way along it and holds neither the world's pull nor the field's push off along the ground's normal. Within the field's reach (20 m) it brings the hull to the ride height at 2 m/s per metre off it (up to 50 m/s), with thrusters and drive as it takes out any drift, so a ship hugs the ground's shape over crests and hollows; higher it leaves the speed along the normal alone, so a ship a ridge threw up (one ridden up at 100 m/s on Europa throws it some 60 m high) comes down again in an arc, and a geyser's column throws it up all the same. A nose pointing away from the ground steeper than about 10° climbs, and the autopilot flies it as anywhere. The field acts alike on every ship, bots and every browser's, and is the same on every browser. A wall of the ground that rises beside the hull over its middle (a canyon's, a cliff's) meets the hull where it crosses the height of the hull's middle, so the hull glides along the wall, pushed off it, never lifted up it.
- **What the HUD shows of a world.** Under the height the flight computer shows what decides how hard a ship can turn, only where it acts and nothing of it in free space: GRAVITY, the pull the ship is in (while a world, a massive body or a geyser's column pulls), in g; AIR, the air's density as a share of its density under the cloud deck (in a planet's air); HOVER, the height of the hull's underside over the ground while the hover field carries (within 20 m), orange while the hull glides on the ground; and GRIP, with any of them, the hardest turn the ship flies now at its speed, in g: thrusters, drive and lift as they are, with the autopilot or in free drift (see Autopilot). HOVER is called the same in every language, as the hover field is.
- **The laser only scorches rock.** A beam on rock or ice throws sparks and a spray of grit where it burns in, and nothing breaks: a few megajoules a shot crack nothing of a pile of rubble a hundred meters across, and the Games' rock is the sponsors' scenery, worth no points. Rock and ice break only where something of the energy to do it meets them: a comet's head (see Comets), a big blast (see Blasts) and, in the campaign's mining jobs, the Rockborn's charges (see the campaign). After two breaks the pieces are gone for good.
- **Mining charges.** The campaign's mining jobs (First Cut, Cut and Haul, the mining runs) hand the clone the Rockborn's charges: a few hundred kilograms of blasting gel in a borehole. Held (K, the D-pad left, CHARGE on touch) for 0.6 s within 60 m of a rock's surface, a charge is set where the hull is nearest it and rides with the rock, a blinking orange lamp; 6 s later it goes off. A rock up to 80 m across its mean radius breaks into its pieces at once; a bigger one takes more charges by its volume, (mean radius / 80 m)³ of them, each wearing its strength down by that share (`src/game/charges.ts`, `AsteroidField.charge`). The blast hurts a hull too close: up to 60 hull points at the charge, falling off with the square of the closeness to nothing at 150 m, and shoves it away by up to 15 m/s; the HUD calls CHARGE · GET CLEAR while the clone is inside it. Charges are never carried in the Games. The campaign is flown alone, so nothing of them goes to another browser.
- **It breaks into pieces of itself.** A rock breaks into 3 pieces, and each of those into 2 or 3: the rock itself split apart, every piece a part of the rock with the rock's own surface where it lay (its craters, grooves and crust) and fresh fracture faces where it broke off. Each shape has one fixed tree of pieces, cut from its own mesh around seeded points (a Voronoi split: the cut between two neighbouring pieces lies halfway between their points), so every piece holds at least a ninth of what it broke from, mostly a sixth to a half. A fracture face is never mirror-flat: the cut is lifted off its plane by up to a tenth of the rock's mean radius (12 to 33 m on a rock of the belt), in broad waves about a third of the rock across with sharper ridges on them, and the faces of two neighbouring pieces are the same rough surface, so the pieces fit together. A fresh face shows unweathered rock: paler and in the rock's fresh tint, with sharp mineral grain, conchoidal ripples and hackle ridges; in ice clear, glassy blue-white ice whose crystal faces glint in the sun. The pieces start exactly where they lay in the rock, turned as it was, with its drift and the speed its spin gave them there, plus a push straight away from its center and a tumble of their own: a break puts about the same energy into a rock whatever its size, so its pieces part the slower the heavier it was, by the inverse square root of its mass (`fragmentSpeed`): 7.5 m/s for a rock up to 20 m across its mean radius, 60 % to 140 % of it, 4 m/s for a 30 m one, never slower than 3 m/s, which every rock from about 38 m up gets (the fragments of a real hundred-meter pile would part at a few centimeters a second; the floor is the game's, since pieces that slow would lie in each other while their orbits carry them about, where 3 m/s takes them clear within seconds); so a ship outside the rock is outside every piece (a piece's measured surface is cut back to the rock's wherever it would bulge past it, to the 2 % of a radius a measured surface keeps to its mesh), and the break looks continuous. Each piece collides where it is seen: its surface is measured from its own mesh, as a whole rock's, around its own center. Its mass, and with it its gravity, is its share of the rock's volume: together the pieces weigh what the rock did. The junk of the Satellite Junkyard breaks differently: into 2 or 3 smaller pieces of junk with 55 % of its size, starting within its reach.
- **The smallest pieces crumble.** A piece of a piece (the second generation: of rock, ice or junk) is gone when it breaks, and it does not last: 90 s of field time after it broke off (`CRUMBLE_AFTER`) it falls apart into dust, shrinking away over 2 s (`CRUMBLE_SECONDS`) in a puff of grey dust. From that moment it is gone for everything (ships, beams, the hook, a cable lying on it, torpedoes, the line of sight, the other pieces, bots, the racing line, where a probe shows up); a hook bitten into it and a cable wrapped on it let go, never snapping for it. Nobody scores for it, and the dust harms nobody. Its moment follows from the field time alone, so every browser removes the same piece at the same field time without a word between them, a newcomer's snapshot and the lead's sync of the field included. The fragments of a rock (the first generation) stay for the round: they are the cover the field keeps. So the field never fills up with rubble: the pieces it keeps track of stay few, and the contacts between them stay cheap.
- **The field meets itself.** No piece passes through another (`src/world/contacts.ts`): rock on rock, a fragment on its neighbour, junk on crossing junk, a half of the world ship on the other, and any of them on a race's gate or a base of Capture the Flag. Two pieces that touch while they close trade an impulse as two rigid bodies, by their masses and their turn (each about its center as a ball of its mass): rock, ice and junk crush rather than bounce, so where they touch they part with 20 % of the speed they met at (and their centers with at least a fifth of theirs), sliding along each other loses at most 0.3 times the blow, and each takes the tumble the blow gives it where it was struck, the blow the softer for it; so no contact leaves them with more energy than they had. A fragment of 7.5 m/s meeting a rock a hundred times heavier head-on bounces off at about 1.5 m/s and barely moves it. Two pieces found more than 2 m deep in each other while they part (a tip that slipped between the points of an outline) are moved apart at 15 m/s, without a blow. A ring is held in place by its station: a piece bounces off it alone. They meet where they are seen: each piece's outline (256 points of its measured surface or its ellipsoid, a grid over each face of a box, of a rig, of a wing; on a world's ground the grid lies on its hills and hollows, and a point is in it as deep as it lies under the ground over it) is held against the other's shape and the other's against it, the deepest point gives the blow's place and direction, and the pair is pushed apart by its shares as far as it closed in a step and 5 cm. A world's ground and what stands on it where its place set it (the Boneyard's racks and the depot's modules) never move: a piece striking them bounces off as off a ring's housing. A piece struck keeps circling with its orbit, as one the battleship rams or a comet shoves; the pieces of one break fly apart through each other until they are clear of each other, as they lay in the rock a moment before, and the sections of a welded body (a half of the world ship, the junkyard's hub) never meet each other. Where a piece is follows from the field time, so contacts are looked for on a grid of 0.1 s of field time, never by the frame: whatever the frames, the same contacts happen at the same field times with the same outcome. The lead browser (or a solo round) decides them, and sends each piece's motion since (where it was pushed to, its drift and its tumble, at the field time of the blow); the others take it if the blow's field time lies within 3 s of their own field clock, the piece's bounding sphere then lay within 50 m of another piece's or a ring's (something it could have struck), it was pushed no farther than 50 m from where they have it then, and it drifts on slower than 250 m/s, and a Host who joins later finds it where it drifts, with the pieces it breaks into. How often they meet, in a round with a rock broken every 15 s and the round's comets: the Belt some 200 times (fragments grinding on their neighbours step after step), the Rings of Saturn some 30 (its ice lies still, only fragments move), the Satellite Junkyard some 80 (crossing junk and fragments), Jupiter some 25, the Event Horizon some 10, the Corona some 50, the Derelict only when a comet sends a half into the other. The pieces are sorted along one axis by where they begin, so only those whose bounding spheres meet are looked at closely: on a desktop core 0.01 to 0.02 ms a frame on average in most arenas, 0.07 ms in the Belt with its rigs, spent on the frames a step of the grid falls in.
- **Deterministic.** Layout and pieces come from seeded randomness and integer-hashed noise, so every player sees the same field and the same pieces; which piece of which rock a piece is stands in its id (`12.2.0`: piece 0 of piece 2 of rock 12), so a player who joins rebuilds every piece from the field's snapshot.

### Masses, ropes and bumps

Everything a rope pulls or a bump shoves is a body with a mass, and one rule for each serves all of them (`src/world/bodies.ts`):

| Body | Mass | Why |
|---|---|---|
| Interceptor (35 m) | 60 t | About three fighter jets of today: reactor, drive, armor. |
| Sponsor core (14 m canister) | 15 t | A quarter of a ship: a clear tug in tow, a fifth less acceleration. |
| Torpedo | 1 t | Its 20 g motor makes about 196 kN, a tug of about 0.3 g on a ship. |
| Sponsor probe | 60 kg | Solar wings, a dish and a beacon on a light frame: on a hook, the winch has it in at 32 g, and its thrusters (about 20 kN) take out its swing. |
| Wreckage, a piece | 5 t | A share of a broken hull. |
| Rock, ice, world-ship hull | from the volume of its mean radius: rock 2,000 kg/m³, ice 900, hull 500 | A rock of 100 m is some 10 million tonnes, ice about half. |
| The Satellite Junkyard's junk | what it is built of: thin sheets by their area, dense cores by their volume (see Collisions, junk) | 48 t (a small fairing half) to 76,000 t (a large stack of satellites). |
| The President's battleship (1,150 m) | 4,500,000 t | A carrier of today, 330 m long, displaces some 100,000 t; a mass goes with the cube of the length, (1,150 / 330)³ × 100,000 t ≈ 4.2 million t, rounded up for its heavy armor. |
| A comet's head (70 m) | 720,000 t | Dust and ice as loosely packed as real comets' nuclei, 500 kg/m³. |
| A welded body (a half of the world ship, the junkyard's hub) | the sum of its sections | Its sections move as one. |
| Race gates, the rings of the bases and the flight school | immovable | Held in place by their stations. |

- **A rope** (the hook's cable, a core's tow cable) only pulls, never pushes, and holds its length; the winch shortens it. Taut, it takes the speed apart from both ends and draws them back to its length, each by its share of the inverse masses, so momentum stays: two interceptors take half each, a core four fifths against its ship's fifth. A rock is millions of times a ship's mass: hooked to one, the ship takes all of it and the rock does not move (its motion, the same on every browser, stays as it is; this is the physics of the masses, not an exception). Each browser moves only the ends it runs, by their own shares of the same rope; the other end's browser does the same from its side, from the state it was last told of. Between two browsers the rope pulls as a stiff, critically damped spring (rate 10 per second: two interceptors burning their drives against each other stretch it about 2.5 m), so each end takes its share whatever the two frame rates; a rigid rope there would let the browser with more frames give in. A rope wrapped around the field's pieces (see The hook) is the same rope bent over its wrap points: frictionless there, it pulls both ends with one tension, each along its own stretch, splitting a jerk between them by their shares as before, and the pieces it lies on take the rest without moving.
- **A bump** between two bodies turns their closing speed around with the restitution of the pair and bleeds off sliding with friction, each body taking its share by mass; against an immovable body (a ring, a rock) the moving body takes all of it. Whether a bump harms is a rule of each pair: a ship striking rock as below, a sponsor core never.

### Collisions

- A ship bounces off a rock and keeps 30 % of its impact speed (a world's ground is no rock: the hull glides over it, see The hover field); scraping along the surface bleeds off sideways speed in proportion to how hard the hull was pushed off it (friction 0.15: a strike at 50 m/s takes about 10 m/s of sliding speed), so a ship resting on a rock slides to a stop at the same rate at any frame rate. The impact counts against the rock's surface, which moves with its drift and its spin: the tip of a long, turning rock hits harder than its middle.
- A ship that moves farther than its own radius in one frame is checked along its whole path, so even at extreme speed it cannot pass through a thin rock.
- Impacts below 12.5 m/s only scratch the paint. Above that the hull loses 1 point per 0.6 m/s; at about 72 m/s a ship is destroyed.
- **Junk is flown through, or struck by its mass** (`src/world/junk.ts`, `strikeJunk` in `src/ship/impact.ts`). The Satellite Junkyard's pieces are thin sheets around dense cores, and a 60 t armored interceptor meets each as what it is. Where it meets a **sheet** (a solar wing's panels, a fairing's shell, an upper stage's tank), the hull punches through: the plug it tears out (its cross-section of armor, some 25 m², times the sheet's mass per square meter) takes the hull's speed, the ship gives up the plug's share of its closing speed and flies on, and the piece takes the plug's momentum and a slow tumble from where it was struck (at most 0.6 rad/s), once as the hull enters it. The plug dents the armor of the nose by its kinetic energy in the ship's frame, at the speed the two close: a hull point for every 0.75 MJ, nothing slower than 30 m/s (a touch scratches the paint), and never more than 60 points from one sheet (it tears and shatters ahead of the nose before it passes more), so a sheet scrapes, dents or hits hard, and never wrecks a whole hull; the hangar's plating takes its share as of every blow to the hull. Where it meets a **core** (a satellite's bus, a stage's thrust frame and engine chamber, a stack of satellites), the two bump as bodies of their masses (see Masses, ropes and bumps): both keep their momentum, the piece flies off with its share, and the ship takes the impact its own share of the closing speed deals, as against rock (so a ship striking a body as heavy as itself feels half the blow, one twenty times as heavy nearly all of it). The numbers, from the shapes as they are built (each piece 1 to 3 times its template's size):

  | Piece | Size | Built of | Mass | Against an interceptor | A strike |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | A satellite's solar wing (whole or shredded) | 11 to 45 m wide, 55 to 230 m long | rigid panels, 3 kg/m² (satellites today: 2 to 3) | the wing 1.6 to 26 t | a plug of 75 kg | Flown through. The ship loses 0.1 % of its closing speed (0.3 m/s at 250 m/s); a scrape: the plug's 2.3 MJ at 250 m/s cost 3 hull points, at 100 m/s half a point. |
  | A satellite's bus | 20 to 85 m long, in foil | a bus of 100 kg/m³ (satellites today: 100 to 260) | the satellite 51 t to 3,400 t | 0.85 to 56 times its mass | A real hit. The smallest satellite struck at 50 m/s deals 23 m/s (17 hull points) and flies off at about 35 m/s; at 150 m/s it wrecks the ship. A large one hurts as rock does. |
  | An upper stage's tank, its adapter ring and bell | 77 to 309 m long | tank walls of 20 kg/m² (a stage today, 12 m by 3.7 m: near 4 t dry), two walls through the tank | the stage 115 t to 1,860 t | a plug of 1 t | Flown through. The ship loses 1.6 % of its closing speed (4 m/s at 250 m/s); a solid hit: the plug's 31 MJ at 250 m/s cost 42 hull points, at 100 m/s 7. |
  | An upper stage's thrust frame and engine chamber (the back tenth of it) | 7 to 26 m of its length | the engine and frame, 15 % of the tanks' mass | the stage, as above | 1.9 to 31 times its mass | A real hit, nearly as rock: the whole stage stands behind it. |
  | A payload fairing's half | 101 to 303 m long | a composite shell, 10 kg/m² (a fairing half today, 13 m by 5 m: near a tonne) | 48 t to 430 t | a plug of 250 kg | Flown through. The ship loses 0.4 % of its closing speed (1 m/s at 250 m/s); a dent: the plug's 7.8 MJ at 250 m/s cost 10 hull points. |
  | A stack of satellites never released (one with the network's neon) | 59 to 179 m | nine buses packed, 150 kg/m³ | 2,800 t to 76,000 t | 47 to 1,270 times its mass | As rock: deadly at speed. |

  So the light junk is what a pilot flies through, a stage's tank at speed worth a turn round it, and the cores are dangers. Bots know it as Hosts do: a wing's panels and a fairing's shell in their way they fly through, a tank whose walls would cost them 15 hull points or more at their speed they fly round while it lies 1.5 s or more ahead, and closer they punch through it; a core they steer off (see Bots). A torpedo goes off on any piece of junk, sheet or core (its fuse does not ask what it touched), and a beam stops at a sheet as at a core. The hook bites junk as rock and holds on it as an anchor; it never tows a piece. Online, the browser that runs a ship decides what the ship strikes and tells the others the piece's motion since (message `fj`); a browser takes it only for a piece within reach of that ship as it has it, pushed no farther than 50 m, its velocity changed by no more than a ship's blow gives a piece of its mass (500 m/s closing, the speed limit, an overdrive's shove and the junk's own speed with room, over again by the restitution, times the piece's share), dated no more than 3 s off; the lead's sync of the field keeps every browser's junk alike.
- **Ships collide with each other.** No hull passes through another: two ships whose hit spheres (17 m each) touch are pushed apart and bounce off each other as two bodies of equal mass (a bump, see Masses, ropes and bumps), keeping 30 % of their closing speed apart. Meeting slower than 15 m/s (their closing speed along the line between them) is a bump: a clunk through the hull, scratches and dents, no damage, so a pack flying together or two ships jostling at a gate never hurt each other. Faster, it is a ram. Its blow grows with the square of the speed above those 15 m/s: the square of that excess over 11 m/s, in hull points (5 at 40 m/s, 17 at 60 m/s, 60 at 100 m/s, 152 at 150 m/s), so the jostling of a pack costs a few points and a ram at full speed wrecks. Each ship takes it where it was struck, as with the laser: on the armored nose 30 % of the blow, on a flank 50 % and a shake of its thrusters (1 % of their health per hull point), from behind into the open drive all of it, and the drive loses a hundredth of its thrust per point of the blow, so from about 125 m/s into a drive its reactor goes critical. A ram that hurts at all costs at least what a laser's hit does (2 hull points, or a fifth of the drive's thrust from behind). So two noses meeting faster than 215 m/s (two ships at full speed head on) both go down, an overdrive's shove into a drive or a flank (about 200 m/s closing) kills the ship ahead and costs the rammer's nose about 86 hull points, a nose into a nose at 100 m/s costs each 18, and a racer bumped from behind at 60 m/s loses 17 and a fifth of its thrust. A ship a ram destroys counts as killed by the other, a kamikaze too ("Rammed by …"), and the HUD says "Rammed by … at … m/s". Spawn protection keeps a fresh clone from harm, not from the bounce. Online, every browser decides the collisions of the ships it runs from the closing speed both see alike: each changes the velocity of its own ship by its half of the same impulse and takes its own ship's damage, so no ship is hurt twice; a kill by a ram goes to the rammer with the destruction, as a shot's does.
- **Shoves and knockouts.** A ship struck by another at 5 m/s or more is shoved: its hull spins by where along it the blow lands (the hull a rod 35 m long, its moment of inertia m·L²/12, the blow's impulse its share of the ram, its lever how far towards the nose or the drive it lands: a blow square on the flank's middle or straight on the nose only shoves, one on the corner of the nose or the drive spins hardest, about 2.2 rad/s at 40 m/s closing, never more than 6; `src/ship/blow.ts`), which the flight computer catches within a fraction of a second, the Host feels a jolt, and if the ship crashes within 3 seconds (into rock, wreckage, the housing of a gate, the battleship's hull, a massive body, the solar flare, a comet's head, burning up in a planet's air or deep space) the shover gets the kill: a knockout ("Knocked out by …"; a Host's own knockout brings up the "KNOCKOUT" banner and the announcer). Shoved at 40 m/s a ship flies some 120 m aside in those 3 seconds. The dodge is the shove's tool: kicked into a ship alongside, the dodge closes at its 60 m/s, the struck ship flies off at about 39 m/s, and each takes a flank's blow, about 8 hull points; so a shove alone kills nobody, the rock beside the line does. At a race's speed a ship shoved off its line meets the rock or the gate's housing ahead at full speed. Online, the browser of the shoved ship decides the knockout, from the shover it saw, and credits it with the destruction as with any kill.
- **Everything else collides too.** Wreckage grinds to dust on rock and hull, on a ring's housing, a sponsor core, a comet's head and a hauler a mission of the campaign sets out (Patience, an ore barge), and two torn plates meeting clang off each other as two bodies of the same mass, keeping 30 % of the speed they met at (the pieces of one ship pass each other for the first 2 s, flying apart from where it broke). A torpedo goes off at rock, hull and the massive body, and at a ring's housing, wreckage, a sponsor core, a probe, another ship's torpedo, a comet's head or a mission's hauler (the torpedoes of one ship fly together and never set each other off; its warhead arms 0.5 s after the launch, some 45 m clear of the tube, so the wreckage of its own ship dying as it fires does not set it off, nor anything loose before; rock and hull stop it all the same). A probe bounces off rock, ice, junk and hull (their surface's own motion included), the battleship, a ring's housing, wreckage, a sponsor core, a comet's head (thrown off with the head's own speed) and a mission's hauler, keeping 40 % of the speed it met it at. The slot it drifts on, the place every browser has it, bounces only off what every browser has alike (the field's pieces, a ring's housing and the battleship): one knocked off its slot by wreckage, a core in tow or a comet's head, each as its own browser has them, waits drifting until the way to its slot is clear and then flies back to it. Where a probe of the arena shows up is laid out against the field as it stands at the probe's due time taken as a field time, never at a browser's own clock, so every browser picks the same place. A sponsor core adrift bounces off the other core, both taking their shares; one that falls into the arena's massive body, or that a comet's head runs down, is lost, and its sponsor's spare waits at home. The laser stops on a ring's housing (felt for along the beam in steps of 2 m, thinner than a strut), a sponsor core, a probe (as far as its wings reach), a comet's head and a mission's hauler, sparks flying, besides rock, hull, wreckage, torpedoes and ships. The flight school's debris bursts on rock it meets on its way, and the rock took it: the drill counts it as dodged.
- **Bots keep clear of each other**: each bot looks 1 second ahead, and another ship in sight whose way would come closer than 5 ship radii (about 85 m) in that time pushes the bot's wanted velocity off the point where they would meet, up to 40 m/s for a dead-center meeting; gentle enough that a racer stays near its line, clear of the rock beside it. A bot only rams by mistake, and shoves on purpose: a rival within about 50 m beside it, drifting against it slower than 15 m/s, with rock or a hull within 120 m beyond it, gets the dodge into its flank, by a bot as often as its daring says (Kessler five times in ten), at most once every 8 seconds; its thrusters leave the drift alone for 0.6 s so the kick lands.
- **Impact warning.** The flight computer warns when the current course hits a rock (or an arena's massive body, or the battleship's hull) sooner than the ship could flip and brake (plus 2 seconds of reaction time) and beeps faster the closer the impact gets. The flip time it assumes grows with the angle to turn (1.4 s for a half turn), adds 0.7 s for the roll that starts a head-over flip and stretches with damaged thrusters.
- **Lock warning.** The sensors see where every other ship points. A nose within four times the own ship's angular radius sets off TRACKED with the shooter's name and a slow beep; a nose on the own ship, where its laser would hit now, sets off LOCKED with a fast beep. A ship that does so from behind, where it reaches the drive, sets off ON YOUR TAIL with the fastest beep: the cue to turn the armored nose to it. Ships farther than 3 km and ships behind rocks do not count; the target drone and the unarmed sparring bot never shoot.
- **Who is shooting.** Every ship that hit the own one (a beam, a torpedo) in the last 5 seconds carries a red warning sign with an exclamation mark on its mark and on its arrow at the screen's edge, the latest attacker's bold and pulsing, so a Host who turns round knows at once whom to take on. Nicole names an attacker that hit from more than about 35° off the nose ("Vega is shooting at you!"; another Host by its number in the round, Host 1, Host 2, …, up to eight), instead of the hit's own line; the same ship at most every 10 seconds, any at most every 3.

### Damage

There are no shields. Every hit to the hull stays until the ship respawns; the drive and the thrusters the nanobots mend.

- **Nanobots.** Gear a Host buys in the hangar (every bot carries them too): 10 seconds after the last blow to the drive or the thrusters they begin to mend both, back to whole in 20 seconds from nothing, so nobody flies on with a broken drive for the rest of a life; a new blow stops them and the wait begins again. The hull they leave alone: it is the clone's life. The HUD says "Nanobots repairing" while they work. They run on the browser that flies the ship, and the others see the mended parts in its state.

- **The laser stops at the first hull.** A shot is a hitscan beam along the nose. It ends at the first thing in its path: a ship, a torpedo, the battleship, a wreck, an asteroid or a piece of debris, which takes the hit.
- **Friendly fire.** Nothing tells friend from foe. A ship of the own side (Capture the Flag's, or everyone against the President's battleship) takes a beam, a torpedo and a nova as any other: the beam ends on its hull, a torpedo goes off at the first hull it strikes (only its own shooter's never sets it off, unless it hangs on a hook's rope), and a nova core destroys every ship within its reach. Physics holds for everyone. A kill of the own side costs the killer what it would have earned against the other side (see Points: 500, twice that for a nova), whatever killed the ally, the killer's kills unchanged; a hit that does not kill costs nothing, as a hit earns nothing. The victim reads "Friendly fire · shot down by …", the killer "Friendly fire · … down" and the points it lost where it happened ("FRIENDLY FIRE -500"), and the announcer scolds the killer on air (the regime loves the waste of a clone). The HUD tells friend from foe unmistakably: a ship of the own side has a ring for a mark where a rival has a square (its color stays its side's), and while the nose is on one the crosshair stays azure, crossed out, with FRIEND beneath it (no orange and no tick: there is nothing to act on, and a shot would hit it all the same). Nicole warns when the trigger is pulled with the nose on one ("Darling, that is a friend! Hold your fire.") and when the own beam or torpedo hits one ("You hit one of ours, darling. Watch your aim."), at most once every 15 seconds, never in the flight school. Each browser takes the points from the killers it runs, as it scores their kills; the score checks of the other browsers let a score fall as it likes. What stays aware of sides is only what a pilot decides: torpedoes lock only on the other side, the aim assist pulls only towards it, bots hunt only it and hold their fire while a ship of their own side is in their line of fire (see Bots).
- **Armor and the drive.** The ships are heavily armored, and where the beam comes from decides what breaks:
  - **Armor** (front and sides): the beam only scorches it, 2 of 100 hull points, no hole; fifty such hits wreck a ship. A beam into the flanks also costs the maneuvering thrusters 10 % of their health. Their push follows 25 % + 75 % of the health and the turn rate 30 % + 70 % of that push.
  - **Main drive** (from behind, within 50° of the ship's axis): each hit costs a fifth of the thrust, makes the drive sputter and leaves holes; the fifth sends the drive's reactor critical, and the ship explodes. Five hits from behind, a good burst, win the fight.
  - So firing at armor mostly heats the own laser. A fight is won by position: get behind the other ship, and keep the own armored nose towards anyone who gets behind oneself. The crosshair glows orange only while a shot would hit a drive and dims over armor; a ship on the own tail with its nose on it sets off ON YOUR TAIL.
- **Decompression.** Each beam into the drive leaves an entry and a larger exit hole. The escaping air jolts the ship into a slow spin (about 0.25 rad/s at the moment of the hit) and keeps pushing it for a few seconds (at first 1 m/s², fading with a time constant of 2.5 s) until the compartment is empty: a drive compartment's few hundred kilograms of air, leaving at up to about 770 m/s, give a 60 t hull no more. A healthy flight computer catches the tumble quickly; damaged thrusters take longer.
- **Blasts.** In vacuum there is no blast wave: a blast kills by its radiation and the debris it throws, and what it does falls with the square of the distance (the energy per area, E / 4πd²). A hull dies where that reaches about 1e7 J/m² (the game's own figure, an order of magnitude), so a blast's energy follows from how far it kills, E = 4πr² · 1e7 J/m², the same rule for each (`src/game/blasts.ts`): a nova core, killing out to 800 m, about 8e13 J, some 19 kilotons of TNT, less than a gram of antimatter meeting its matter in a sponsor's core; the President's battleship going down, out to 1 km, about 1.3e14 J, its reactor scrammed and the blast what was left in its loops; a self-destruct, out to 60 m, about 4.5e11 J, an interceptor's reactor overloaded on purpose. A rock between a blast and a hull shields it. Rock and ice break by the energy their face takes in: its cross-section πR² of the energy per area, 40 % of it going into breaking (the rest re-radiates or boils off its skin), against what disrupts a pile of rubble held together by its own weak gravity, about 90 J/kg of rock at 2,000 kg/m³ and 40 J/kg of porous ice at 900 kg/m³. So a blast breaks a piece out to d = √(3 · 0.4 · E / (16π · Q · ρ · R)): a nova a 110 m rock within about 300 m, a 20 m piece within about 700 m, a 350 m rock only within about 170 m, ice farther; the battleship's sinking somewhat farther, a self-destruct only the smallest pieces close by. A whole piece breaks into its fragments, a fragment to dust, as a comet's head breaks them, at the field time of the blast on the comet's grid of 0.1 s, so every browser breaks the same pieces (a nova's field time travels with it; a newcomer's snapshot and the lead's sync of the field carry the breaks). Each break is named in the dev log (`game.log.filter('blast')`). A ship whose reactor went critical (its drive shot to nothing) takes a little of the space round it along: within 60 m it shoves a hull away by up to 15 m/s and costs it up to 10 hull points, both falling off with the distance.
- **Wreckage.** A destroyed ship breaks into five pieces of torn hull, their edges glowing from the break (see What the hulls look like), that keep its momentum, drift on (towards a well, where there is one) and grind to dust when they hit rock. Flying into a piece costs hull points depending on the impact speed; lasers shatter them. Every browser breaks a ship into the same pieces and flies them in fixed steps of 1/30 s of their age, so their paths do not depend on the frame rate; a piece shot or rammed bursts on every screen. Where they fly still differs a little between browsers (each saw the ship break up a little elsewhere, and reads the rocks' pull at its own field time), and each takes a piece away on its own where it grinds into rock: the debris is about the same everywhere, not exactly.
- **Where the game bends the numbers on purpose.** The President's battleship rams pieces of up to a quarter of its mass aside at its cruising speed without a scratch, though such a ram frees some 3.5e11 J: its armor is the Ministry's best, and the Games keep it whole until the Hosts break it. The harpoon flies at 2 km/s and bites a hull harmlessly, as a sheet of junk passes a hull with only a dent: its head is a light grapnel that spreads its blow, the game's choice. The black hole's horizon is the hole's edge by the game's rule: its gentle pull would let a ship escape there at about 473 m/s (see Event Horizon), but whatever touches it is gone all the same. The Rings' chunks of 20 to 73 m and the Belt's forty rocks of 110 to 350 m within 2.25 km lie far denser and larger than any real ring or belt, whose particles are centimeters to meters and whose rocks lie a million kilometers apart: an arena needs things to fly round. A sponsor's core weighs 15 t in a canister 14 m long: a hollow shell of light composite round a small heart, so a ship tows it on its cable.
- **The arena.** Space has no walls. A ship that stays more than 3.4 km from the center for 20 seconds is lost in deep space (enough to flip, burn off an outward drift and fly back in). The edge is a sphere with a thin grid (lines every 600 m): inside it is a faint blue that grows stronger near the pilot; while the own ship is outside (the ship, not the camera hanging behind it), the whole edge glows orange, so the way back is always in sight.
- **The belt.** The field is one dense patch of an asteroid belt that goes on all around, flat in the arena's horizon plane (its north pole is up), most of its rocks within 1.5 km above or below the plane (up to 4.5 km), from 9.4 km to 17.5 km from the center: 480 rocks of the same 16 shapes in low detail. It starts beyond the reach of any ship (one that leaves the arena at top speed is lost before it gets there, and one faster than that after an overdrive is lost the moment it reaches the belt), so its rocks are scenery: they neither collide, pull nor break.

- **Drive plumes.** A main drive burning at more than 30 % throws hot plasma out behind it, 150 m long and spreading by about 7°. A ship inside it gets grilled: at the nozzle of a full burn its radiators take 60 % of the heat capacity and its hull 8 points a second, less with the burner's throttle and fading to nothing at the plume's end, so a laser overheats in under two seconds right behind a burning drive. A clone that burns up counts as a kill for the ship that burned ("Grilled in the drive plume of …"). Vacuum knows no slipstream: whoever tails a burning ship closely has to get out of its line to overtake. The flight school has no plumes, because its drills chase drives on purpose.

### Heat

The laser draws no ammunition but produces heat. Each shot adds 20 % of the heat capacity; the radiators keep working between shots (0.18 s apart), so a cool laser fires a burst of six in sunlight and nine in shadow; at 100 % the laser locks until the ship has cooled down to 30 %. Radiators shed 20 % per second in sunlight and 50 % per second in the shadow of an asteroid. They glow with the heat they shed, for every ship to see: from a quarter of the heat capacity on dull red, deepening to orange-red at the limit, following the heat over about 0.4 s as hot metal does (the human interceptor's cooling ribs and the vents over its drive block, the alien's inner side blades, the Sovereign's delta wings). A rival glowing bright is close to locking its laser; online a ship's glow is estimated from the shots seen, the heat itself being its own browser's. The asteroids cast real shadows; a ship in shadow goes dark and the HUD shows ☾ next to the heat gauge. Ships, the rings of a race, the battleship and a comet's head throw shadows as well, but only on the picture: the radiators count only the shade of the field's pieces, and under Jupiter's cloud deck the clouds' (see Jupiter's air, where the air heats the hull as well).

### The pilot

- **G-forces.** Above 3 g the pilot starts to grey out. A full burn (13 g) brings on the full strain within 5 seconds, the hardest turn of the autopilot at the speed limit (about 20 g) within about 3, with the pilot's heartbeat getting louder; 3 seconds without hard acceleration clear it again. The tunnel vision is only hinted, so the picture stays readable: the edges darken to at most 55 % and close in by a fifth of the screen; on a phone only the very edges darken, to at most 30 %. Every clone takes the strain, a bot's too, from its own burns, coils and kicks: at full strain a bot's aim scatters half as wide again and it pulls the trigger 0.15 seconds later.
- **Line of sight.** Sensors need a clear line: a ship hidden behind an asteroid, or deep in Jupiter's haze (see Jupiter's air), disappears from the HUD, and bots cannot see it either: not as a target, not as a ship to keep clear of, and a probe behind rock does not lure them.

### Alarms

The flight computer ranks every danger to the clone in three tiers, like the master caution of a fighter. Below the crosshair a lit plate names the worst tier, and under it one line per danger, the worst first (at most four), in the tier's color, with an arrow towards where the danger comes from (up is ahead and above) and a countdown where it has one.

| Tier | Color | Tone | What else |
|---|---|---|---|
| CAUTION | bright azure | a calm falling chime, again every 5 s | Nicole names it where it matters (a gravity well) |
| WARNING | orange | a double beep every second | Nicole speaks |
| CRITICAL | red | a two-tone siren | the plate blinks, the screen's edge pulses red, the link frays for a moment now and then (about every 2 s, under 0.2 s) |

Only the worst alarm sounds; a danger with a beep of its own beeps that instead of its tier's tone. Without motion (`prefers-reduced-motion`) nothing blinks or pulses.

| Danger | Tier | Line |
|---|---|---|
| Impact ahead (see Collisions) | WARNING, CRITICAL within 1.5 s | IMPACT with the seconds, its beep faster the closer it is |
| Laser overheated | CAUTION | LASER OVERHEATED |
| Drive overheated | CAUTION | DRIVE OVERHEATED |
| A nose on the own ship (see Collisions) | TRACKED a CAUTION, LOCKED and ON YOUR TAIL a WARNING | its own line with the shooter, below the list; its beep |
| Torpedo homing on the own ship | WARNING within 2 km, CRITICAL within 500 m | TORPEDO INBOUND with the seconds to its hit at the closing speed, its beep every sixth of them (0.6 s at most, 0.07 s at least); Nicole counts it down (see Torpedoes) |
| Nova core close by | CRITICAL within 1.2 km | NOVA CORE ACTIVATED with the distance, its siren |
| Outside the field | WARNING, CRITICAL in the last 3 s | OUT OF FIELD with the seconds left (no countdown in the flight school) |
| Gravity well | by its hold, see below | BLACK HOLE · BURN AWAY (GRAVITY WELL over Jupiter), OVERDRIVE · AWAY FROM IT, NO RETURN |
| Hull heat in Jupiter's air (see Jupiter's air) | CAUTION while the air heats the hull faster than the radiators shed, WARNING from 70 %, CRITICAL while the hull burns | HULL HEAT · SAFE with the safe speed where the ship is, its arrow pointing up out of the air; Nicole warns that the hull heats up, and that it burns |
| Hull failing (30 of 100 points or less) | WARNING | HULL FAILING, a steady loud heartbeat, and Nicole: one more hit and you are dead |

- **Gravity wells.** The hold of a well is what the drive would need to get out, as a share of its thrust: the pull where the ship is, plus the braking it would take to stop a fall towards the body before it reaches it (v² / 2s for the speed towards it and the distance to the surface), over the thrust of the main drive (less with a damaged drive). From 8 % the HUD shows the escape gauge PULL with the percentage; CAUTION from 15 %, WARNING from 35 %, CRITICAL from 60 % (with a loud heartbeat), and at 100 % the main drive's normal thrust no longer holds a ship at rest. What still gets a ship out, the flight computer flies ahead from WARNING on, five times a second, with the game's own flight model and the well's pull (`wellEscape` in `src/game/alarms.ts`): half a second for the Host to act, the nose turned at the full rate straight away from the well (a Host already burning away within 30° of it burns on from the start), then the full drive away, the tournament's speed limit on the ship's own push included; out is where the drive outpulls the well, climbing, with the drive burning. Its answer is the drive alone, the overdrive, or nothing. The overdrive fires a charge it has stored (it charges in 20 seconds), the same shove of 200 m/s however hurt the drive is, but leaves the drive cold for 4 seconds after it; where the drive alone still gets out, those 4 seconds cost more than the shove gives (a whole drive gives some 510 m/s in them), and from CRITICAL on the kick and the cold drive after it drop a ship right back in that a burn would have got out. So the overdrive is the answer only where the drive alone no longer gets out and the shove still carries the ship far enough while the drive is cold: with a whole drive hardly ever, with a hurt one far more often, since its point of no return lies farther out, where the pull is weak (a drive at 30 % has none closer than about 1 km, and a ship at rest there, burning flat out and still falling in, is out with the overdrive for some ten seconds). Then, with the overdrive ready, the alarm says OVERDRIVE · AWAY FROM IT and Nicole calls it ("Overdrive, now!"); where nothing gets the ship out any more, the alarm says NO RETURN. Nicole warns once for each tier the hold reaches ("The black hole is near", "It is pulling us, burn away from it", "Now it gets really tight, full burn, now"), calls the overdrive the moment only it gets the ship out, and only once nothing does says that she is sorry; once the hold has fallen below 10 % she warns again from the start. At rest near the black hole the gauge comes up at 1.96 km from its center, CAUTION at 1.43 km, WARNING at 940 m, CRITICAL at 715 m and the 100 % at 555 m, where a whole drive no longer holds it; a ship falling towards it gets them much earlier. A ship flying a stable orbit about the black hole (see Orbit readout) hears none of this: the well's alarm, its line, Nicole's warnings, the heartbeat and the silence before death stay off, and the gauge stays in plain azure, since the ship falls around the hole and never into it; whatever puts it on a path that is no stable orbit (a burn, a ram, a hit, the autopilot back on) brings them back in the same frame, by the hold.
- **Silence before death.** When death is a breath away (an impact within 0.8 s, or a well past return), the radio drops out for 1.2 s, at most once every 8 s: only the heart and the alarm are left.
- Any part of the game raises its own alarm (`Game.alarms.raise` with an id, a tier, its line, and where it has them a direction and a countdown), meant for timed dangers such as a comet or a solar flare's front.
- In the flight school the alarms work the same, but never cut off a lesson: an alarm's line is not said over one of Nicole's lessons.

### Sound

There is no sound in vacuum. The one place with sound from outside is a planet's air: under Jupiter's cloud deck the wind roars past the hull, a hot hull roars with its plasma and thunder rolls after lightning (see Jupiter's air), heard through the hull like everything else. Everything the Host hears travels through the clone's hull and sounds muffled: the rumble of the drive, the hiss of the thrusters, the own laser, hits on the own ship, and debris of nearby explosions rattling against the hull. Low over a world's ground the hover field pushes on the hull against every bump of the ground, and the plates ring with it: a rush through the hull, a deep drone slow and a hiss at the speed limit, while the field carries (within 20 m); a hull gliding on the ground grinds over it, louder the faster. In air a close pass by anything (within 2.2 ship radii) rushes too, the air squeezed between them; in vacuum a rock passed close is not heard. Clean beeps come from the ship's computer: hits, kills, the end of a flip, the alarms. The radio and the static of a disturbed link play in the headset and are not muffled. While the tab is hidden the drive, the thrusters, the air's wind and plasma and the stadium's crowd fall silent (an online round runs on without its picture), until the tab is back.

**Deathmatches over a world** (`FIGHT_WORLDS` in `src/world/arenas.ts`). A deathmatch is fought half the time over a world: a tournament arena of its own on the ground of a place of the campaign, played only by deathmatches, under its gravity and over its ground, which the hover field keeps every ship off and which never hurts (see The hover field); what stands on the ground (a station, a listening post) is as hard as rock. Its edge is its own where the world has one (Valhalla: 1.4 km from the middle across the ground, under a ceiling 550 m over the plain, a wall and a lid whose grid shows within 300 m), else the arena's sphere around the field's middle, 3.4 km out, the middle some 100 m over the ground; the ground is the floor either way, 20 s to come back as anywhere. A clone is printed on the ground, resting on the hover field at its ride height with its nose along the ground towards the middle: on one of the world's pads where it has them (Valhalla's eight, round its edge 1.05 km out), else on level ground (at most some 14° of slope) on the field's edge 2.25 km from the middle; never within 80 m of another ship, the one farthest from the nearest enemy and out of its sight taken first (where no ground is clear, 100 to 400 m over it as a last resort). A world fought over sees no comet, nor does a world raced over. Online, the deathmatches of a room alternate between the seven arenas of free space and the worlds fought over, each walked through in an order of its own from the room's code (a mixed room counts its deathmatches, every fifth round); solo, the worlds fought over are offered while a deathmatch is chosen, and "By chance" picks one of them half the time. Bots fight over a world by the same rules: they take its ground for no rock in their way and follow a target or a probe down to it, the hover field carrying them over its ridges and along the walls of its canyons; under an edge of its own they keep inside it across the ground, turn down within 80 m of its ceiling and roam 520 m under it, low over the world (over Valhalla 30 m over the plain); a torpedo, which has no hover field, goes off on the ground as on rock.

- **The Lineae.** Europa's ice plain as the campaign's place has it (see `src/world/europa.ts`), the Crack running through the arena's middle (200 m wide, 800 m deep, its lips raised 80 m), the older double ridges across the plain, the science station on the Crack's floor, under Europa's pull of 1.31 m/s² (0.13 g), Jupiter 12° across in its sky; and east of the Crack, from 800 m off the field's middle, a field of chaos terrain on the plain (as on the Crack, see Races over a world: rafts of ice 60 to 110 m across, 20 to 55 m high, tilted up to 12°), which the campaign's place leaves open. The canyon is the cover and the duel: down in it, its walls glided along, a ship is seen only from above it or along it; among the rafts a ship rides their slopes and breaks a lock behind them. The ice looks as Europa's does, one of the brightest surfaces in the system (it throws back some two thirds of the light): white with a touch of blue, glossier than dust so the sun glints off it, the Crack's walls the bluest, the double ridges' crests, the Crack's lips and the chaos's matrix stained the reddish brown of Europa's cracks, and the shadows lit by the ice around and warmed a little by Jupiter's glow, never black.
- **Valhalla.** Callisto's ring zone packed tight for a fight (see `src/world/battlefield.ts`), under Callisto's pull of 1.24 m/s² (0.13 g), Jupiter standing still in its sky; low and tight: 1.4 km round its middle, under a ceiling 550 m over the plain. A scarp every 650 m across it, 100 m to 250 m high, each rising gently and dropping off in a cliff of bare ice into a gorge 70 m deep at its foot; ice bridges span four of the gorges, 20 m thick with some 60 m of gorge open under them to fly through, ice of the world as its ground is: the hover field carries a ship over them, and one that meets them glides. Through all the scarps runs the furrow the Navy's battlecruiser **Steadfast** ploughed when she came down in the old wars, 340 m wide and 40 m deep, a berm of thrown-up ice along its lips; she lies in it broken in two, each half 600 m long, 150 m wide and 120 m high, sunk 10 m into the ice, rolled and turned askew, 300 m of open gorge between their torn ends. Through each half runs its hall from end to end (80 m wide, 60 m high, its floor a ground the hover field carries a ship over) and a tunnel across it from flank to flank at its middle (70 m wide); the rest of her is solid hull, as hard as any, her bridge tower on the stern half's back and two turrets on the bow half's. She is the arena's cover and its prize: most of the round's sponsor probes show up in her halls, between her halves and beside her in the furrow, the rest low over the ground. Her plating is the Navy's grey-olive, sooted at her torn ends, frost on her back, her name on her flanks and in blue neon on the bridge tower, two tubes of it dead and the rest failing now and then; amber emergency lamps still light her halls. The clones are printed on eight pads round the edge, 1.05 km out.

### Modes

A round is a **race**, a **deathmatch**, **Last Clone Standing**, **Capture the Flag** or **the President's Fleet**. The race is the game's heart; the deathmatch is for the hard pilots; Last Clone Standing is for those who want it to end; Capture the Flag for those who would rather fight side by side; the President's Fleet puts everyone on one side. A solo round is chosen on its own plate: race (the default), deathmatch, Last Clone Standing, Capture the Flag (the rivals split into the Host's side and the other) or the President's Fleet (the rivals fly with the Host), all in one row, an arena or "By chance" (the Rings of Saturn chosen until the Host picks something else; each arena named with a line on what it is like; the arena chosen shows live behind the plate, beside it, the seal stepping back: the camera drifts slowly around its landmark, a turn in three and a half minutes, while its rocks turn as in a round; once the plate closes or shows "By chance", the title screen is back in the Rings of Saturn, with no planet's haze, sky or color left from the arena shown), and the rivals, any of the eight bots, up to seven (none: alone against the clock; the default is a full grid of seven, all but Halley); the browser keeps the choice. The rivals chosen set how hard a round is, a race too: every bot races from gate to gate at a speed of its own, by its skills on average, from 140 m/s (all skills 0) to 220 m/s (all 1, just below the speed limit, where they still make the turns): Laika about 148 m/s, Kessler 180 m/s, Halley about 208 m/s; the same online. Every bot has three skills of its own, each 0 to 1, which the plate shows as scales of five pips: aim (how close its aim comes), reflexes (how fast it reacts, how quick its hand is, how well it starts a race) and daring (how hot it runs its laser, how often it takes the hook). Laika (aim 0.1, reflexes 0.2, daring 0), Sputnik (0.3, 0.1, 0.2), Juno (0.2, 0.4, 0.3), Ceres (0.4, 0.4, 0.4), Kessler (0.5, 0.5, 0.5), Orion (0.7, 0.5, 0.6), Vega (0.6, 0.9, 0.6) and Halley (0.9, 0.8, 0.85): on average from 0.1 to 0.85, Kessler the middling one at 0.5 (the first three, who fill a tournament first, average 0.4). A bot flies like a Host with a hand on the controls: what it wants reaches stick, drive and thrusters eased in over 0.35 seconds (reflexes 0) to 0.12 seconds (reflexes 1), so it gathers speed into a turn, sweeps and settles, and overshoots a little; only its trigger is instant. A bot fighting a ship circles it at 60 m/s about an axis of its own, its nose on it, drifting in a wide arc on its way behind it, where the drive is open; once there it settles and fires. A racing bot fires single shots only, even at an open drive, and only now and then (in three of ten thinking spells, with a laser below 10 % heat), unless it looks back at a ship on its tail: it flies first, and the pack is a race, not a shooting gallery. A tournament room is created as race (the default), deathmatch, Last Clone Standing, Capture the Flag, the President's Fleet, or all in turn (a mixed room plays deathmatch, race, Last Clone Standing, Capture the Flag and the President's Fleet round by round, starting with a deathmatch), and the list of tournaments shows it. The room's mode lives in its link (`&m=race`, `&m=survival`, `&m=flag`, `&m=fleet` or `&m=mixed`; without it, a deathmatch).

- **The opening of a round.** Every round, in every mode (not the flight school), opens as a show, on the round clock, so every browser of a tournament sees it at the same moment:
  - *Intro* (the first 5 seconds): the camera opens on the arena's landmark, drifting around it, and sweeps down onto the own ship, ending where the chase camera sits. A card presents the round: its mode, the arena and what makes it special, and the field, one pilot after another sliding in (the bot or Host mark, a bot's skills as pips, the own pilot in orange); the rest of the HUD steps aside meanwhile. The stadium murmurs and the announcer opens the broadcast from the arena. Enter (A on the gamepad, a tap on "Skip") skips it: in a solo round the countdown starts at once, online only the camera comes home, the round clock being everyone's.
  - *Countdown* (the next 3 seconds): 3, 2, 1 in big neon with a beep each, while five start lights come on one by one above it; the stadium grows louder with every number, and on the first the announcer calls the round ("three laps. no brakes. the president wants a show", "eight clones. one winner. the president is watching", one pair of lines for each mode).
  - *Start* (8 seconds into the round): the lights go out, BURN! in a race, FIGHT! in every other mode, huge, punching in with a tremor, a flash over the picture, a shake of the camera, a glitch of the link, and the stadium erupts.
  - Until the start the clamps hold every ship (no drive, no thrusters, no laser, no torpedo, no overdrive, no hook); online they hold until the lead browser's clock is known. A browser that comes in after the start sees none of it. What runs on the round's or the field's time counts from the round's start as before, the opening included: the battleship comes in 4 seconds after FIGHT!, the first probe 12 seconds after the start, the solar flare 52 seconds after it, and the round still lasts five minutes in all. With less motion (`prefers-reduced-motion`) the words only fade and the card does not slide.
- **Deathmatch.** Five minutes, kills and rocks score, the best score wins. Half of a tournament's deathmatches are fought over a world (see Arenas, Deathmatches over a world).
- **Race.** Eight gates sit on a circuit of the arena's own, rising and falling by up to 200 m; three laps. A circuit is laid as a race track is, straights and bends of set radii one after the other, two of its straights taking whatever length closes the lap. *Grand* (the Satellite Junkyard, the Belt, Jupiter): a main straight of 2.4 km into a hairpin of two quarter bends 1.2 km in radius, 100 m apart, a fast S of two bends 1.4 km in radius, a back straight and a long last bend. *Sweep* (the Rings of Saturn, the Derelict): wide bends of 1.3 and 1.45 km, a chicane of two bends of 1.2 km, straights of 1.9 and 0.9 km. No bend of a circuit is tighter than 1.2 km, so the racing line, bowed around rock where it meets one, stays wider than the autopilot turns at the speed limit. The Corona and the Event Horizon are fought in, never raced: the Corona's storms blind the sensors, and the Event Horizon's hole is the fight's own danger. A course is turned about the field's center in steps of 45° and drawn at 90 to 110 % of its size, as large as fits within 85 % of the arena's edge, at the field's height or 600 m above or below it (over a ball or disk of rock, say); over a thin disk with open space above and below it (the Rings of Saturn's layer of ice) it runs at the disk's height and dives up and down by 300 m instead, so a lap crosses the layer four times, about a quarter of it within the layer, at no more than about 18°, its dives no tighter than 2.7 km. Of these the course ranks first those whose gates lie least deep in rock, in the racing line's bows around it or in the circuit's tight bends (see below), then those that run through least rock, and takes of the best 32 the first whose racing line bends nowhere tighter than 730 m. A lap is 13 to 15 km, the gates at least 1.2 km apart along it, on the straights and where the bends open out, and the course itself bends no tighter than about 1.1 km; the racing line bows around rock no tighter than about 730 m, more than twice the autopilot's hardest turn at the speed limit (325 m, see Autopilot): a ship takes every bend of it at the speed limit, the drive held all the way, with grip to spare. Only rock that moves (the junk of the Satellite Junkyard, and what circles a well) can bend it tighter for a while, and the ribbon glows orange there (see below). A middling bot at 180 m/s flies a lap in one to one and a half minutes, three laps in three to four and a half. Each gate is an electromagnetic coil 120 m in radius, a structure of the tournament built like everything out there from flat armored blocks: ten coil housings around the opening (each 10 m thick and 18 m deep, with gaps between them bridged by cable conduits), plated in riveted steel patched over the years, each housing in a paint of its own (the depots' gray-blue, gunmetal, a faded ochre or azure primer), grimy, streaked with rust and soot, worn hazard paint along the opening on both faces; the windings, copper wire wound in turns of 50 cm and clamped by a steel strap every 15 m, glow on their inner faces and along the rim of both faces, so from ahead the ring reads as a dashed circle of light, and up close the turns show through the glow (an unlit ring shows its copper); four struts reach out to red position lights, domed lenses under a guard of two crossed bars, that blink once every 1.2 s, and on top sits a small station module with a sponsor's plate and a lamp of its own. Its windings tell the pilot which gate is which: the next one glows orange, the one after it blue, the start and finish pink, the rest faintly. The whole structure lies outside the opening, and its housings are solid: flying through the opening is the point, flying into the coil is a crash like one into rock (a ship bounces off and an impact above 12.5 m/s costs hull, a fast one wrecks it), so a ship's center keeps within about 104 m of the center to pass clear (the housings start 121 m out, a ship's hit radius is 17 m); the struts, the station module and its sponsor's plates are just as solid, only the lights and the windings' glow are not. The same holds for the bases of Capture the Flag and the rings of the flight school still standing. The flight computer brackets the next gate on screen in its azure, four corners around the opening sized to it and a small reticle on the core, and the gate after it with fainter brackets, from when the opening spans 18 pixels until it fills the view. An X marks where the own flight, held as it is, passes the next gate's plane (when it gets there within 12 seconds): azure while that lies inside the ring, orange while it lies outside, so the pilot steers the X into the ring. It counts when a ship flies through it, whichever way: a ring is a ring. The rings are coils: a ship that flies through the inner third of one (within 40 m of its center) is pulled through by its magnetic field and leaves it 40 m/s faster, the way it went through, though never faster than the speed limit plus those 40 m/s (290 m/s; a ship already faster keeps its speed). The ring's windings flash white, the hull sings with the coil, the clone is pressed into the seat and the view widens. Off-center the field would drag the hull into the coil, so the ring only fires for a ship in its core: the clean line is the fast one. Racing bots aim for the center too, and keep what the coil gave them. The grid lies 300 m before the pink gate; flying through it starts the race, and three laps later the race ends there (25 gates in all). A race opens like every round (see The opening of a round below), its start word BURN!; the race time, and with it every finish time, runs from BURN!. A drive lit within 0.3 seconds before BURN!, or within 0.2 seconds after it, makes a perfect start: a shove of 40 m/s along the nose, with the coil's sound and a wider view; the stadium cheers and the announcer says so ("what a start. the crowd goes wild"). A drive lit earlier and still burning at BURN! stalls for 1.5 seconds ("Too early"); one let go again before BURN! costs nothing. A bot lights its drive by its own timing and is judged by the same rule: aiming at BURN!, it misses by up to 0.9 seconds either way at reflexes 0 and 0.3 seconds at reflexes 1 (Kessler: a perfect start four times in ten, a false start one time in four). Every lap of the own racer is timed, from one crossing of the pink gate to the next: the race panel runs the lap's clock next to the arena's best lap, every gate shows the split (time since the pink gate) against the best lap's, green when ahead, red when behind, and every lap its time. A course depends only on its arena, so each arena keeps one best lap in the browser, its time and splits; a lap faster than the best becomes the new best ("new best"). The race is called as it runs: the announcer of Radio 2584 names a new leader ("and Vega takes the lead"), the leader's last lap and the winner; Nicole tells the own pilot "Final lap" at the pink gate, and a gain or loss of a place ("Nice pass", "Someone got past you", "You are in the lead", "You lost the lead"), at most once in four seconds. On the own last lap the cockpit's radio opens up: the lows 5 dB and the highs 4 dB louder, faded in over two seconds; the notes and the beat stay those every pilot hears. Every gate scores 20 points; the finish scores by place, 1000, 800, 650, 500, 400, 300, 200, 100, among those who finished before. Lasers stay on: a kill scores 200 in a race. A new clone takes up the race where the last one died, facing the next gate (120 m beyond the last gate the last one passed, when that spot lies in rock, in a well or in its grip, or outside the arena, so the eight places side by side, up to 111 m from the gate's axis, keep clear of its housings); the race begins on a grid 300 m before the first gate. The course follows from the arena's field as generated and its seed, so every browser lays out the same one; every gate keeps 200 m clear of the farthest reach of every rock as the field starts out (however a rock turns, the tip of a long one sweeps around it) and 800 m of an arena's massive body, never where its pull alone would sound the escape gauge's CAUTION (see Alarms), and off the stretches where the racing line bows around rock (the bow, as wide as a circle of 730 m makes it, the S-bends of that radius into it and out, and 300 m more): a gate there would pin the line onto the course in the middle of a bend. Nor does it sit in a bend of the course tighter than 1.5 km, or within 360 m of one: there the line runs at its tightest, and a gate in it leaves a pilot no room to swing wide. It lies never nearer than 1.2 km along the course to the gate before it, so a racer has room to line up for each. It finds such a place back and forth along the course, up to about 720 m away, and where there is none, the place least deep in a bow or a bend; but rocks that circle may cross the course later. The HUD shows lap, gate and place, and marks the next gate; a racing line shows the ideal line as a dashed ribbon in the HUD's blue, in a dark rim (readable on a bright disk or a sunlit rock): a smooth curve along the loop through the center of every gate (where its coil fires), bowing out around rock as it lies right now (laid anew every second of field time), 100 m clear of a rock's farthest reach (room for a Host's hand) and 200 m clear of an arena's massive body, and running straight through each gate, 100 m to either side of it. The bows are an elastic band: every rock whose reach comes near the line keeps it out of a sphere that holds that reach plus the clearance and is 730 m wide (the same near side, the rest of it behind the rock), so where the line wraps a rock it bends no tighter than that; pass after pass, 2,000 of them on points 100 m apart, every point's bow eases its bending against its neighbours' and is pushed back out of every sphere it lies in. Its dashes sit on the line every 20 m (12 m lit, 8 m dark), fixed in space like the marks of a track, 1.3 km ahead, so they rush past the faster the ship flies; the ribbon is 4 m wide and never thinner than 4 pixels on screen. Where the ship is off the line, the ribbon leaves it along its flight and joins the line within a second of flight (at least 150 m), so it shows where to turn; a ship more than 50 m off sees it grow brighter and wider, up to 80 % at 250 m, so a pilot under stress finds it again. Pulses of white light run along it towards the gate at 600 m/s, 400 m apart, through the dashes and the gaps alike. Brake zones: where the ribbon ahead bends tighter than the own ship can turn at its present speed with the autopilot and the drive held, or in free drift with the drive alone, the nose turned across the flight (see Autopilot; the bend measured over 60 m to either side, with its drive and thrusters as they are), the ribbon glows orange, fading in from 95 % to 105 % of the speed that bend allows: ease off or brake before it. One gate a lap may be left out: while a racer has not spent that lap's skip, the gate after its next one counts as well (the HUD shows both, the next one orange, the one after it blue), and flying through it skips the next one, which scores nothing ("Gate 3 skipped · your one skip this lap"); a racer more than 120 m past its next gate's plane and still moving away is shown on to the one after it ("Missed gate 3: fly on through gate 4, your one skip this lap"), the racing line and the brackets with it. The pink gate, which starts, counts and ends the laps, is never left out. Who misses a second gate in a lap has to turn back and fly through it, from either side, and the HUD says so ("Missed · turn back") while the ship is more than 120 m past its plane and still moving away. A missed gate costs its points, not the race, so a racer can take a bolder line. Racing bots play by the same rule. Racing bots never give up the race: they fly the ideal line at their own speed (see the solo round's rivals above), for a point on it 0.8 seconds of flight ahead (at least 100 m, but before its next gate no more than 50 m past it, so a sharp turn after a gate does not pull it off the gate's center; a bot past a gate's plane without going through it comes back for a point at least two turn radii at its speed before the gate, so the way back is a wide loop that comes at the gate square; one within 150 m of its next gate's plane but beside its opening first flies for a point 200 m before the gate on its axis, so it never runs into the coil from the side, and one in front of the opening whose way would cross the gate's plane within 34 m of the rim, or that is that near it already, flies for the gate's center), and shoot whoever flies within 30° ahead of their nose. Every bot steers clear of the housings and struts of the rings (the race's gates, the bases of Capture the Flag): where its way would cross a ring's plane within 2 seconds on them or within 1.5 ship radii of them, its wanted velocity is pushed sideways, by up to 125 m/s the nearer the crossing: into the opening of the gate a racer flies through next, and of any other ring when the way meets the inner quarter of the housings, else out past them.
