A ship on their tail they answer with a flip: their drift carries them on along the course while the nose turns back for 2.5 seconds of fire, at most once in 8 seconds, then forward again. A race ends after five minutes, or 5 seconds after the last Host has finished, whichever comes first; bots do not hold it up (online the lead browser shortens the round clock for everyone). The scoreboard ranks by the finish: who finished, by time, then who is racing, by gates passed.
- **Race over a world.** Over a race world (see Arenas, Races over a world) the race is a track from a start to a finish, flown once: its gates stand on the world's ground at points laid along the track, each 45 m over the highest ground about it (where the track rises out of a rift a little higher), so a gate's ring of 120 m stands with its lower housing sunk into the ground and a ship riding its hover field 5 m over the ground flies through the opening, and through the coil's core when it keeps to the gate's middle. In a narrow gorge the ring's sides are sunk into its walls. The racing line runs through every point of the track, not round a loop, and bows round what stands on the ground (the science station, the outposts) as round rock; the ground itself it never bows round, and it runs under the slabs of ice fallen across a rift. The first gate is the start and pink, the last the finish and pink too; the grid stands 300 m before the start, two abreast 40 m apart in rows 40 m apart along the track. The race ends at the finish, and its time from the start's crossing is the arena's best run kept in the browser, its splits gate by gate. One gate a race may be left out ("Gate 3 skipped · your one skip this race"), never the start nor the finish. **A crash costs seconds, never the race**: a clone destroyed on a track is printed again after 1 s at the last gate it passed, 120 m beyond it on the track, without a sponsor break and without the kill cam, already flying along the track at 150 m/s; the bots alike. A world's ground never hurts (see The hover field), so what kills on a track is what stands on the ground, a gate's housings, other ships and their lasers. The last stretch, where the cockpit's radio opens up as on a loop's last lap, is the last quarter of the gates; the announcer calls no last lap. Racing bots fly the track by its line as round a loop, keep no edge to turn back at (the world has none), and never take a world's ground for rock in their way. **Branches**: where a track splits into two ways that join again, every gate between stands on both, and either counts; both glow alike as the next gate, and the HUD says "Gate 2 of 21 · two ways, either counts" before the split. The racing line, the gate's mark and the crosshair's waypoint follow the way the pilot is on: coming up to the split, the way whose first gate the flight heads for more nearly (switching only once the other lies about 8° nearer the flight, so the line does not flicker), and from a gate on, the way that gate was passed on. A clone destroyed on a branch is printed again on the way it was on. A bot takes the shorter, riskier way with a daring of 0.5 and more, the longer, safer one with less. **The boost**: in a race the overdrive is a boost with a risk (see Overdrive).
- **Last Clone Standing.** Every Host, and every bot, has three clones for the round; a ship whose third clone dies is out, and no clone of it is printed again until the next round. The tournament schedules the round for a solar flare: the sun throws a wall of plasma at the arena and drives everyone into a corner. The plasma is a sphere 13.5 km in radius (four times the arena's), its middle out towards the sun, so its front bulges only gently into the field. Half a minute before the first minute of field time is out it shows up 6.75 km beyond the arena's edge on the sun's side and comes at it; from the first minute its front eats into the arena from the sun's side, evenly at 30 m/s, until at four minutes it stands 2.03 km past the center on the far side and holds. What is left is the arena's sphere minus the plasma's: a pocket on the side away from the sun, 1.35 km deep at its middle and about 6 km across where the front meets the arena's edge, thinning out towards that rim; its inner side cuts through the field's outermost rocks (they reach out to 2.25 km), so there is some cover, but no room to keep out of each other's way. The arena's edge holds as in every round. A ship in the plasma burns: 40 heat and 6 hull per second (about 17 s from a whole hull to nothing, and the laser locks from the heat long before); with a rock between it and the sun only a quarter of that, since the rock takes the brunt of the radiation and the ship sits in the thinner plasma of its shadow, so a clone caught by the front can hide behind a rock for a while, but the front keeps coming. A real coronal mass ejection is far too thin to burn a hull and has no sharp front; the Games' flare is denser and sharper on purpose, for the fun of it. On screen the plasma is a roiling wall of fire, dark red where it is thin, orange where it boils, with white-hot filaments twisting through it and its front glowing brightest; embers blow off the front towards the far side, the Host's screen glows orange from 300 m before the front to deep inside it, and the sun swells to two and a half times its size and burns more than twice as bright and white-hot, so every rock throws a long hard shadow across the field. The radar marks the front's nearest point in orange and the pocket in blue; from the first minute the HUD marks the pocket as the safe zone. The flight computer raises a WARNING "FLARE FRONT" with the distance and the seconds until it arrives once the front is within 600 m, and a CRITICAL "SOLAR FLARE · AWAY FROM THE SUN" (with "SHADED" behind a rock) in the plasma, both pointing away from the sun. The announcer and Nicole call the flare when its front reaches the arena. New clones are printed in the pocket, around its middle and clear of the plasma, and bots fly away from the sun once the front is within 400 m and keep to the pocket. The panel shows the clones left, how many still stand and how far the front is (or how deep in it the clone is); the sponsor break names the clones left, and Nicole warns of the last one. Whoever is out watches the rest, as a watching Host does (Tab: next pilot), and the announcer names everyone who drops out. Once no more than one ship has a clone left, the round ends 5 seconds later (online the lead browser shortens the round clock for everyone), and the announcer names the last clone standing. The scoreboard ranks by the clones left, those out by how long they held out, and then by score; kills and rocks score as in a deathmatch. The flare runs on the field time, which every browser shares, and every browser knows every ship's deaths, so all agree on who is out.
- **Capture the Flag.** Two sides, Orange and Blue. Each has a base about 1.8 km from the field's center, opposite each other (laid out from the arena's field and seed like a race course, clear of rock and of a massive body, and out of its grip: never where its pull alone would sound the escape gauge's CAUTION): a coil of 80 m radius (the structure of a race's gate, a little smaller, its windings in its side's color) facing the center, and in it the side's sponsor core, the flag: a sponsor's power cell (Space Burger's for Orange, PrintMe Bodies' for Blue), an octagonal canister 14 m long in dark, oily armor whose heart, a coil of copper wound tight, glows in its side's color through four windings and between armor staves, every turn of their wire showing through the glow, with the sponsor's lit sign on two plates and a cage of two crossed bails on top where a tow cable grips. A ship takes the other side's core with its hook (100 points), in two ways: by touch, when its hull comes within 40 m of the core and the hook on the hull grabs it; or by a hook shot, when the harpoon (see The hook) bites the core from up to 1 km away (it bites a core whose center passes within 20 m of the nose's line). The core is then in tow on the hook's cable, made fast at the carrier's stern: a core taken by touch on a cable of about 75 m (4.5 ship radii) at once, one bitten from afar on the harpoon's cable, which the winch reels in (32 g on a body a quarter of a ship's mass, braking so the core comes to rest at the end) to the same 75 m. From then on the cable holds that length. The core is a trailer on a rope: a body of its own with a quarter of a ship's mass and its own momentum. The cable only pulls, never pushes: slack, the core flies on by itself; taut, it jerks, and the speed apart is taken from both by their masses (four fifths from the core, one fifth from the ship), so a carrier tows with a fifth less acceleration and feels every jerk as a tug. In a turn the core swings out and whips around; when the carrier brakes hard it overtakes on its momentum and bumps into the hull or comes taut ahead. It is a lively thing: off a rock, a ring's housing or a hull (its own carrier's or any other ship's) it bounces with 60 % of its speed into the surface (a ship's hull keeps 30 %), both sides of a bump taking their shares by mass; a bump only shoves, it harms neither the ship nor the core, and it scores nothing. It flies 75 m behind the ship, beyond the chase camera (2.6 ship radii behind), so a carrier flying ahead keeps a clear view; a core in tow that comes between the camera and the ship it follows, or the line from that ship to its crosshair, fades to a faint outline in its side's color, and from the cockpit the own core in tow is not seen at all. A core is shown at its size in tow, 1.4 times it adrift and twice it at home. When the core itself passes through the opening of the carrier's base ring (either way) while the carrier's own core is at home, it captures (1,000 points to the carrier, a capture for its side) and both cores are home again; the carrier passing through alone is not enough. A carrier that dies, leaves or changes sides, or whose tow cable snaps (the tow cable wraps around rock, ice, junk and hull sections that come across it as the hook's cable does, and snaps where wrapping is no answer; the lead browser decides it from where it has carrier and core, as every rule of the mode; see The hook; wrapped, the winch holds the tow cable as it is, since reeling it in over a rock would drag the carrier back to it), lets the core go where it hangs, with the velocity it has on the rope; its station-keeping thrusters then brake it with 2 g (a core dropped at 220 m/s comes to rest after about 11 seconds and 1.2 km), and adrift it bounces off rock, rings and ships as in tow, and off the other core, both taking their shares while both are adrift (a core at home or in tow is held by its own rules: one adrift bounces off it as off a wall moving with it, with 60 % of its speed). The other side takes it up again with its hook (touch or shot), the own side sends it home the same ways (150 points), and after 30 seconds adrift, beyond the arena's edge, in the arena's massive body or run down by a comet's head, it goes home by itself (its sponsor's spare). A core is solid: a beam stops on it, so a core in tow covers its carrier from behind, and a torpedo goes off at it. The HUD's mark says when the core to fly for is in hook range ("Their core · in hook range", "Your core · in hook range"). The first side with three captures wins at once (the round ends 5 seconds later); otherwise the side with more captures when the time is up. Kills (500) and rocks score as in a deathmatch; the own side is hit as any other ship (see Friendly fire), and a kill of it costs the killer 500 points; torpedoes never lock on it, the aim assist never pulls the nose onto it, and bots never hunt it. Sides: a Host joins the side with fewer Hosts, a bot the smaller side, a tie goes to Orange; whenever one side has two ships more, one of its bots (never one with a core in tow) moves over. A ship that gets its first side is printed at its base (its hook's cable and any cable hooked on its hull let go, on every browser), and every new clone is printed between 160 and 320 m from its own base. The HUD names the score, the own side and both cores (at home, adrift, in tow and by whom), and marks where to fly: the other side's core, the own core when it is away (a thief or adrift), the own base with a core in tow; the radar shows both bases and every core away from home in its side's color, and every ship's marker takes its side's color. The announcer calls every capture; Nicole tells the own pilot that it has their core, that they took ours, and who scored. The scoreboard names each pilot's side, with the captures above it. Bots: one in three of each side guards its base and hunts a thief of its core (wherever the core is, as a Host's HUD marks it, but it fires only once the thief is in sight); the others raid. A raider never flies straight at a core at home, where it would have to turn around under the guards' fire: it swings out 200 m beyond the base and 200 m to one side, then dives through the core at 120 m/s, already on its way home. A bot flying for a core it may take fires its harpoon at it once the core lies within 3° of its nose, between 150 and 900 m off, with nothing in between (and its hook is ready again 10 seconds later). With a core in tow it flies home at 220 m/s, easing off to 120 m/s while its flight is more than 35° off the way home, so the core does not whip around: lined up with the opening (or from afar) it flies through the middle of the ring and on to 180 m beyond it, twice the cable, so the core passes through too; beside the ring it first flies for a point 180 m in front of it on its own side. An own core adrift, every bot of its side fetches; a raider whose side already has the other core escorts it, flying for the core on its rope. Online the lead browser decides: who is on which side, who takes, sends home and captures a core, from where each core is on its own screen. A hook shot that bites a core is told to the lead, which takes it if the ship is still within the hook's reach of the core (plus 500 m for the lag) and sees it past rock: a harpoon flies straight. The lead sends the sides, the captures, where each core is (in tow: the carrier and the core's position and velocity) and the last six of these events along with the round clock, 15 times a second. Every browser swings a core in tow on its own rope from the carrier as it sees it, and only the browser that runs the carrier lets the rope tug it; a browser that does not run the carrier takes the lead's core when its own has drifted more than 100 m from it. Each browser scores the events of the ships it runs, and a Host who joins hears only what happens from then on.
- **The President's Fleet.** The President wants blood and sends his battleships into the ring. Because he can. Every Host and every bot are on one side (whose beams, torpedoes and novas hurt each other as in Capture the Flag, a kill of one costing its killer 500 points, see Friendly fire), against one battleship in every round: a carrier refitted for the President, 1,150 m from its bow to its drive bells, built in code (its hull, superstructure and parts are laid out once and grown by one factor, 1.5, into the world, while the hangar deck keeps the size a fighter needs; see the hangar deck below). Its hull is a dagger of dark armor: 83 m wide at the bow, its keel rising there, 285 m wide 350 m aft and 345 m at its widest, 180 m from the flight deck to the keel; an armored belt drops straight from the deck's edges, and below it the hull slopes in to the keel. Gold runs along every edge. Under the deck's edges runs a trench with a row of lit windows; aft of it the mouths of the hangar deck open in both flanks, gilded around, his slogans along the slopes beneath them (PAID FOR WITH YOUR SACRIFICE, THE PRESIDENT IS WATCHING YOU); the seal of the Ministry of War stands large on both flanks. Forward, the runway in the regime's deep red, with catapult rails and parked fighters and his slogan painted along it in letters thirty meters high (THE GREATEST SHIP EVER BUILT), runs to the bow's hangar, too low for a fighter (20 m) and shut by an armored blast door with a lit seam. Aft, the superstructure steps up in three tiers of armor, each with rows of windows, to the bridge tower at the stern: four rows of windows down both its flanks, the seal on its front, a command deck 195 m wide with a band of windows all around it, its roof 250 m above the deck, two sensor masts above it. Two great drive bells and four small ones close the stern, and the regime's orange neon runs in a procession along the deck's edges. Six laser batteries, armored turrets with three barrels each on a turret ring, stand on sponsons forward, on the deck's edges beside the superstructure and under the keel, two drives at the stern, and a reactor under an armored hatch on the deck. The Hosts win the round when it goes down; when the five minutes run out first, it got away and the President wins (the announcer says so, and the scoreboard names the winner). It comes in 12 seconds into the round from near the arena's edge (2.7 km out, its whole hull inside it), on a side that follows from the arena, and circles the center 2.5 km out, along the outer rocks, at 30 m/s, at a height of its own, its lane: of the heights up to 600 m above or below the field's plane, in steps of 25 m, the one nearest the plane whose band around the center (where its level hull reaches on its orbit, from where it comes in to the orbit) the fewest pieces of the field cross on their way, with 60 m to spare (in the Rings of Saturn its hull also 60 m clear of the ring's drawn sheet, 200 m above and below the plane; over Jupiter no lower than keeps a ship attacking any of its parts, 600 m off it on the side the part faces, 50 m over the cloud deck, so the batteries under its keel show themselves in clear air: 700 m over the plane). The lane follows from the field as laid out, the same on every browser: 400 m over the Rings of Saturn's sheet of ice, 500 m over the Belt's plane and the Event Horizon's disk, 525 m over the Derelict's plane, 600 m over the Satellite Junkyard's, 575 m under the Corona's, and in Jupiter's plane itself. It comes in at its lane's height and holds it.

  Everything collides with it, as everything does in the field, and nothing ever lies in its hull. It keeps clear of the pieces of the field of at least a quarter of its own mass, a piece welded into a structure (a half of the world ship, the junkyard's hub) weighed, and if ever rammed moved, as its whole structure (a rock of the Belt is 2.3 to 76 times as heavy as it, 10 to 340 million tonnes; every rock of the Belt, the Corona, the Giant, the Event Horizon and the Derelict, 23 of the Rings' 136 chunks of ice and 38 of the Satellite Junkyard's 93 pieces of junk; it rams the other 113 chunks and 55 pieces aside): such a piece would slow it hard or throw it about. Its sensors follow each of them 20 s ahead, where the field time will put it, against its own course straight on, and one that would come within 60 m of the hull pushes it aside, square to their closing while there is time to pass and, once it is close, straight away from it where the hull comes nearest it, at up to 50 m/s on top of its course, the harder the sooner the contact; its thrusters do this, so it keeps clear with its drives shot out too, and it turns its hull (to its course, or its stern on a pilot) only where the turn stays 30 m clear of such a piece and, nearer already, never on towards it. A lighter piece it does not dodge: it rams it aside. The hull and the piece trade momentum by their masses, the battleship's turn sweeping its hull along included (its gyros hold the turn, so its share goes into its drift): rock, ice and junk meeting armor crush rather than bounce, the two part with 20 % of the speed they met at, sliding along the hull loses 0.3 times the blow, and the piece takes a tumble from where it was struck, up to 0.3 rad/s more. So a chunk of a tenth of its mass flies off ahead of it while it loses a tenth of its speed into the chunk, one of a quarter at most a quarter, which its drives win back within seconds; meeting a heavier one after all, it is slowed hard or thrown back by the same rule. A lighter piece leaves the hull at 1 m/s at least, however gently it was touched, so a hull pressing on it does not shove it again frame after frame; against a heavier one it meets too slowly to change that one's speed by 1 m/s, the hull alone bounces off. The piece is pushed out of the hull at once, by the shortest way out of every part of it the piece reaches into (its outline, 256 points of its measured surface, against the hull's boxes), each by its share. A rammed piece does not break: rock and ice break where comets, big blasts and the campaign's charges break them. Online the lead browser decides every ram and tells every browser the piece's motion since (where it was pushed to, its drift and its tumble, at the field time of the blow); the others take it if the blow's field time lies within 3 s of their own field clock, the piece lay at the battleship's hull as they see them (within its reach and 1 km for the glide), was pushed no farther than 100 m and leaves no faster than 500 m/s, and a Host who joins later finds it where it drifts, with the pieces it breaks into. Wreckage that hits the hull, 900,000 times its own mass, grinds to dust as on rock; a probe bounces off it as far out as its wings reach, keeping 40 % of the speed it met it at, and so does the slot it drifts on and flies back to. It gets out of a comet's way (see Comets); a comet's head that strikes it all the same breaks up on it and sinks it, and its shower bursts on the armor.

  Its parts are as solid as its hull: a battery's turret (its ring, collar, housing and rangefinder) and its barrels, reaching 90 m forward of its mount, the two great drive bells and the four small ones astern, the six fighters parked on its deck, and once the dome is open the reactor's glowing core where it bulges out of the deck. Ships bounce off them, and wreckage, probes, the comet and the pieces of the field meet them as they meet the hull; a round bell collides as two boxes crossed, their outline at most 7 m outside the bell's and at most 11 m inside it where the two meet. A beam striking a battery's turret or barrels, a drive's bell or the bared core hits that part, and a torpedo counts on every part within 20 m of where it goes off, its barrels and bell included; a broken battery is gone, and a burnt-out drive's bell is bare armor. Its hull takes no harm: a laser stops on its armor, and a ship that rams it bounces off as off a rock. Like a rock, the hull blocks the line of sight: a pilot behind it is hidden from the others, off the HUD and out of a torpedo's lock, and a battery does not fire through it, so the side away from a battery is cover. Its parts break under laser hits: a battery after 64, a drive after 94, the reactor after 188. A torpedo locks on a part as on a ship (the reactor only once it is open), in sight of the part: the line from it to the part meets that part before any armor, so a battery's turret shows over the deck's edge and the reactor only from above the deck; one whose part slips behind the hull loses its lock. It counts 10 hits, about a sixth of a battery, on every part within 20 m of where it goes off; one that strikes bare armor counts nothing. A nova core (see Probes) is the heaviest blow the battleship takes, for whatever lies within its 800 m is gone: every part it can hit whose surface lies within 200 m of the core breaks outright, and one farther out takes a share of all the hits it takes, falling evenly from the whole at 200 m to a third at 800 m (a battery at 500 m 43 hits, four torpedoes' worth; at the edge 22, a drive 32 and the open reactor 63, each more than two torpedoes). Set off over the deck it can break several batteries at once; the sealed reactor takes nothing, as against every weapon. Its sparks and fire burst from every part it reaches, on every browser, and the hits count for the holder (10 points each and the parts it breaks). Every 1.6 seconds each battery fires at the nearest ship it sees within 1.5 km, a heavy beam in the regime's red: what a laser does where it strikes, and 10 hull on top. It hits with a chance of 70 %, halved at 250 m/s and again at the edge of its range, so moving is what saves a pilot; whether a shot hits follows from its number and the target, the same on every browser. Each browser picks the nearest ship by its own view of the field: where two browsers see a different ship nearest, as the lag between them may have it, a shot can strike on both or on neither. With half its batteries gone it turns its stern on the nearest pilot within 1.8 km (7° a second) and, once its stern points there within 14°, lights its drives: a plume of 1.5 km that burns 150 heat and 40 hull a second at the nozzles. Without drives it drifts and burns nothing. With every battery gone the dome opens, and only then does the reactor take hits. When the reactor breaks, it goes critical ("get clear"): its hull stays solid for ships, beams and torpedoes alike (a beam stops on it, but no part takes a hit any more) and still hides what lies behind it, and 4 seconds later the battleship goes up and destroys every ship within 1 km, and 5 seconds after that the round ends (online the lead browser shortens the round clock for everyone). A hit on a part scores 10, breaking a battery 150, a drive 200, the reactor 1,000, and every ship of the side scores 500 when the battleship goes down. The HUD names its batteries, drives and reactor (or when it comes) and marks the part to go for, the nearest battery while one stands; the radar shows the battleship in red. The announcer calls its arrival, its drive turned on the pilots, the open reactor and its end; Nicole tells the pilot to take out the batteries first, to get out from behind it, that the reactor is open, and to get clear. Bots go for the nearest battery first, then a drive or the reactor, and hold 600 m off it on the side it faces, where it shows itself: a battery on deck above the deck and out over its edge, one under the keel below it, a drive astern and out to its side (clear of the plume), the reactor above the deck. Each comes at that side along its own way, turned towards it (straight in from that side, at 27° from it coming from abeam, round the hull from the other side), so they spread over it; behind the hull they burn round, and they fire only with the part in sight and the nose on it, lasers and, now and then, a torpedo with a lock on a part; they range out to the battleship's orbit, flee a critical reactor within 1.5 km, and while its stern hunts a pilot they keep 100 m clear of its plume, flying out to the side. Online the lead browser flies the battleship and sends whether it went down, the hits on each part, where it is, its motion and turn, whom its stern hunts and whether its drives burn along with the round clock; the others glide after it. A Host who joins after it went down finds it gone: no blast, no points. Every hit on a part goes to every browser with its round and its count (one for a laser, 10 for a torpedo; checked, and limited like laser hits), so all count the same; the lead's count fills in hits a browser missed. Each browser fires the batteries at the ships it runs and burns them in the plume.

  **The hangar deck** is flown through. Three tunnels run straight across the hull under the flight deck, from the belt of one flank to the other's (310 to 340 m, the hull widening aft), their middles 615, 750 and 885 m aft of the bow, under the superstructure. Each is 75 m wide between the machinery on its walls and 53 m high from its floor to its ceiling, the flight deck 49 m thick above it; the walls between them are 60 m thick. The tunnels keep this size whatever the battleship's: a narrow slot in a flank 180 m high. A fighter's hit sphere is 34 m across, so it has 20 m to spare to either side and 9.5 m above and below: room enough at fighting speed, little to wander. Inside, the ceiling is dark steel, four girders along each tunnel and ribs across it, with two rows of warm lamps in the bays between them, the deck is dark with lit markings (a dashed line down the middle, its edges, a bar at each mouth), and the walls are machinery from floor to ceiling, 6 m deep, all of it within the walls' colliders, so nothing in a tunnel is in the way (`src/game/machinery.ts`): deep bulkhead frames at uneven spacing, one or two catwalks with yellow kick plates, rails and ladders, banks of pipes in rows and one behind another (dark steel, gunmetal, copper and brass, flanged, held at every frame, a valve now and then with its yellow-and-black collar), risers from floor to ceiling, thick black cable bundles sagging from frame to frame, cable trays, hoses hanging from the plant to the pipes, and the plant itself in the bays: tanks, pumps with their yellow coupling guards, heat exchangers, cabinets, transformers, fans, machine blocks and control desks, with a few small lights on their panels, azure for information and, rarer, orange where a hand is wanted. Each tunnel has its emphasis, so the three read apart: the forward one is the coolant plant (pipes, tanks, pumps, heat exchangers), the middle one the power plant (cable bundles and trays, cabinets, transformers), the aft one the air plant (ducts along the ceiling's edge, fans, machine blocks). The battleship's seed lays it all out, the same on every browser: 13,600 to 22,700 triangles a tunnel, 54,000 to 63,000 for all three, depending on the arena, so each tunnel's is a mesh of its own, drawn only while the camera is in the hangar deck or within 100 m of it (above or below the deck's height only out beyond a flank, where a mouth shows: over the flight deck or under the hull the hull hides it), and only the one of the tunnel nearest the camera along the hull: from inside one tunnel the others are out of sight (a unit test keeps a tunnel's under 50,000). From anywhere else each wall is a picture of its machinery, drawn flat as seen straight on (a texel about 33 by 37 cm on the widest wall; the six pictures share one texture, each with its edge rows repeated around it, so from afar no neighbouring picture bleeds into its edges) on a plate just behind the machinery's back, part of the hull's meshes: the hangar deck seen from outside costs some 600 triangles and no draw call of its own. The hull keeps the sun and the sky out, so the deck and the machinery carry the lamps' warm light baked into their corners: brightest on what faces the ceiling and towards the top of the walls, dimmer towards the deck, darker the deeper it lies in the wall, the plates at the back darkest; the ceiling between its girders gets only what the deck throws up. The space dust and the Rings' ice specks and grains stay out of the hangar deck (they are looks only, and against its walls they would read as stars), so only the mouths show space. Only floor, ceiling and walls collide: a ship that touches them bounces and scrapes as off any hull, and a beam, a line of sight or a torpedo passes along a tunnel as through open space, while the hull around it hides a ship inside from everywhere else. So a tunnel is cover from the batteries and a way to shake a chaser, who has to follow through it. The battleship moves on at 30 m/s while a ship crosses: a Host who aims at the far mouth without leading it meets the wall coming after it, so a Host leads the far mouth as it leads a crossing ship. The simulated Host flies through every tunnel at 150 m/s and at the speed limit with every hand of the race tests, coming in from 1 km abeam and leading the mouth by the battleship's motion, without touching (`src/game/fleet.test.ts`). Bots treat a tunnel's mouth as hull and fly around it (the way ahead they check reaches 51 m out to every side of their line of flight, more than a tunnel leaves); one caught inside looks ahead only along what the tunnel leaves open and flies out without touching. Online every browser places the tunnels where its own view of the battleship lies, which may trail the lead's by a little: a ship flying through is judged by its own browser, and a beam fired along a tunnel strikes on another browser only where that browser sees the line open too.

### Bots

Bots fly by the Hosts' rules. Their ships are the same interceptors with the same flight model: speed limit, drive, thrusters and turn rates, damage to drive and thrusters, gravity, collisions, laser heat and cadence, torpedoes with the same lock, the hook with the same reach and winch, and their shove is the dodge's kick. A bot only moves its ship through the controls a Host has (stick, drive, thrusters, trigger, eased in by its hand), never by setting where it is or flies. Its clone takes the same g-force strain, and it knows only what a Host's sensors show: ships in sight, a nose on its tail as the lock warning shows it, a ship that hit its drive as far as the eye sees its beam (it turns its armor to either and fires back), blind in a solar storm, a thief where the HUD marks the core, in Jupiter's haze a ship within the radar's reach, firing only at one within the eye's. Friendly fire hurts a bot's side as a Host's, so it holds its laser while a ship of its own side in sight passes within 17 m of its line of fire before the first rival hull on that line, and launches no torpedo while one lies within 50 m of the line to its lock; one behind it never counts, however close (`allyInLine` in `src/ship/bots.ts`). The air drags and heats its hull alike, and it reads the HUD's HULL HEAT as a Host does: it keeps its wanted speed, dodging rock included, under 85 % of the speed at which the air heats its hull as fast as the radiators shed (over the deck far above the speed limit, so there it flies at the limit and keeps a coil's 290 m/s; under the deck 268 m/s, down to 180 m still the limit), climbs straight out of the air once its heat passes 60 % while the air still heats it (until it has cooled below 30 %), and fights over the clouds, where a Host sees the fight: unless it races (a course runs along the deck), it keeps 200 m over the deck, rising when it is under that by up to 80 m/s (in full from 500 m under it), and over it sinking no faster than it could stop there braking at 1.5 g against the planet's pull. The clouds are a place to shake a chaser off, not to camp in: a bot under fire slips down into them at 40 m/s, to 300 m under the deck, and stays as long as it is hit and 8 s after the last blow, then rises again; a daring bot (daring 0.5 and more: Kessler, Orion, Vega, Halley) follows its target down into the clouds, the others wait above for it to come up; a bot heading back into the arena from its edge flies home first. None of them stays once the clouds have clogged its radiators past 30 % and 40 % more of a full clog for every point of daring (Laika at 30 %, Halley at 64 %): then it neither hides nor chases, and climbs out at once, as fast as the air's own heat allows (80 m/s at least): the waste heat comes whatever its speed, so the quicker out the better. Its start is judged by the same rule, and its next clone waits as long as a Host's at the soonest. What remains different by design: a bot decides by its AI and its skills (aim, reflexes, daring) instead of a Host's hand, it never takes the overdrive, it flies a stock ship (see the hangar), and in a solo round it goes easy on a Host who keeps dying. Online the lead browser runs the bots, and every other browser checks their ships with the same plausibility checks as a Host's.
In the Satellite Junkyard a bot knows the junk as a Host learns it (see Collisions): a solar wing or a fairing in its way it flies on through, a stage's tank whose walls would cost it 15 hull points or more at its speed it flies round while it lies 1.5 s or more ahead (closer, it punches through), a core (a satellite's bus, a stage's engine, a stack) it steers off as it does rock.

### Combat rules

- A kill scores 500 points (200 in a race); rock scores nothing (the laser only scorches it). A ship of the own side killed (shot, torpedoed, vaporized by a nova, shoved into a crash, burned in a plume, rammed) costs its killer as many points as a kill earns, twice that for a nova (see Friendly fire): the victim's browser tells the others who killed its ship but not how, so every way costs alike.
- **Streaks pay.** Kills close together are the show the crowd tunes in for, so the sponsors pay for it (`src/game/streaks.ts`): a kill of the other side within 4 seconds of the same ship's last one makes a streak, and the second kill of a streak earns 250 points more (half a kill; 100 in a race), the third and every one after it 500 more (a whole kill; 200 in a race). A double kill pays 1,250 for two, a triple kill 2,250 for three. A streak's kill so pays at most twice a kill, as a nova's does, never more. A nova's kills already count double and earn no bonus, but they count in the streak (a beam's kill right after a blast of two is the streak's third). A kill of the own side counts in no streak, the killer's death ends its streak (the nova's reprint does not), a new round starts every streak over, and nothing counts after the round's end. Every ship alike, bots and other browsers' Hosts too: each browser counts the streaks of the ships it runs, on its own clock, a remote victim's kill when its browser's report arrives. The own pilot sees the kill's points with the bonus where the victim broke up, and the bonus beneath the streak's banner (see Big moments).
- **Torpedoes.** Every clone of the tournament carries four, a salvo larger than the decoys a ship can hold; in a drill of the flight school and a campaign's job every ship but the Host's carries two, and the Host's clone what its drill hands it or its hull holds (G, D-pad up on the gamepad, the TORPEDO button on touch screens; the HUD counts them). A torpedo locks on the ship (or part of the President's battleship) in sight closest to the nose within 17° and 3 km, leaves the tube 40 m/s faster than its ship, and flies nose first: its main drive pushes with 20 g along its flight only, its thrusters with 10 g across it and against it to brake. Its top speed is 360 m/s, a notch above a ship's 250 m/s (only an overdrive's 400 m/s is faster, for a while), and as for a ship a push beyond it adds no speed. It wants to fly at its top speed towards where its target will be when it gets there (the first moment t at which a flight at top speed, the target's velocity held, meets it: |aim + v·t| = 360 m/s · t) and steers for that velocity (a gap of 100 m/s asks for 10 g); its seeker reads the target's motion 0.4 s late, and its drive and thrusters follow the steering 0.25 s later still. Without a lock it flies straight on. A world's gravity and a well's pull act on it as on a ship and an air's drag a third as hard per tonne (a slender body), and its computer counters them with its drive and thrusters (see Gravity); in air its fins' lift adds to its thrusters' 10 g across its flight (see Jupiter's air, Lift). So it is quick on a straight line but less agile than a ship (about 20 g in a turn): at top speed it turns on a circle of about 1.3 km. A ship fleeing straight at full speed is caught from 1 km behind in about 9 s; a hard turn across its path started 0.6 to 2 s before the hit makes it overshoot by 20 to 180 m (held on, the turn lets it come round again), jinks one way and the other every 1.5 s, started 0.6 to 1.5 s before the hit, shake it until it burns out, and a dodge 0.6 to 1.2 s before the hit makes it miss (`src/game/torpedoes.test.ts` flies all of it). Its seeker needs a clear line: a ship that slips behind a rock or a hull loses it for good, and it flies straight on from there. It goes off at a ship's hull, as soon as its flight passes within a ship's hit radius (a pass between two frames counts too), whatever side it hits (armor does not stop it): 60 hull points and 30 % of the thrusters. Two torpedoes kill. A rock, an arena's massive body or the battleship's armor sets it off harmlessly, and it burns out after 15 seconds. The laser shoots it down (a 6 m target; 50 points, none for the own torpedo, whose end the HUD jeers as OWN GOAL) where nothing solid lies before it on the beam (rock, wreckage, a ship, the battleship's hull, a ring's housing, a core, a probe, a comet's head), so a pilot with a torpedo on its tail flips and fires. A torpedo homing on the own ship within 2 km sets off TORPEDO INBOUND with the seconds to its hit and a beep that quickens as they run out, and the radar shows torpedoes in red. Nicole counts the closest one down: "Torpedo!" as it comes within 2 km (not over one of her tips), then "One thousand", "Five hundred", "Three hundred", "Two hundred", "One hundred" as it passes those meters, each only while at least 2.2 s are left at the closing speed and nothing else is being said, and "Break!" (cutting in) 1.5 s before the hit. A torpedo that went past starts the count over when it comes round. Bots with a lock on their target (or on the part of the battleship they attack) fire one now and then (about once in 12 seconds). Online, a torpedo flies on every browser from its launch, but only its shooter's browser decides where it goes off and tells the browser of the ship it hit, which takes the damage; a beam that shoots one down is told to every browser. Launches, hits and torpedoes gone are checked like every message, at most 12 a second per browser. **A torpedo on the hook** is a deliberate dirty trick, a bomb on a string. The harpoon bites a torpedo (within 20 m of the nose's line, up to 1 km) and it hangs on the rope: a tonne against the ship's sixty (see Masses, ropes and bumps), the winch reels it in to 70 m. It keeps its lock and its motor keeps pushing for its target, and the rope decides who pulls harder: its 20 g on a tonne is about 196 kN, so a drifting ship is tugged towards the torpedo's target at about 0.3 g, and a ship that burns its drive (13 g on sixty tonnes) drags it off its course. A torpedo homing on the ship that holds it pulls towards that ship; that is the danger of the trick. When its motor has burnt for its 15 seconds it is a plain mass on the rope, towed without resistance, and stays as long as it is held. Its fuse stays live for every ship, its shooter and its holder included: swing it into an enemy, or let go to fling it. While it is held, and for 3 seconds after it was let go (then a torpedo whose motor is out goes off where it is), a ship it goes off on is the holder's kill. HUD: TORPEDO ON THE LINE. Online, the hooker's browser tells the others which torpedo its hook took, and from then on that browser decides where it goes off (the shooter's browser lets go of it); every browser checks that the torpedo lay within the hook's reach of the ship (plus 1 km for the lag), and a claim beyond it counts as a cheat. Every browser flies the torpedo on the rope; only the hooker's browser moves the hooker. Bots do not hook torpedoes.
- **The hook.** A harpoon on a cable, gear a Host buys in the hangar (see The hangar and the Solar Coins; the flight school lends it for its drills, and without it the HUD shows no hook): V, the right mouse button, D-pad down, HOOK on touch screens. Pressed, it flies along the nose at 2 km/s and bites the first rock, ship, sponsor probe or torpedo within 1 km (a torpedo then hangs on the rope, see Torpedoes; in Capture the Flag also a core the ship may take, which it then tows; see Capture the Flag; a core, probe or torpedo whose center passes within 20 m of the nose's line is bitten). A probe bitten hangs on the rope (the HUD says PROBE ON THE LINE) and, while the hook holds it, the winch reels it in all the way to the ship (to one ship radius, about 17 m) at 32 g, the flight computer braking it to arrive gently: from 400 m it arrives in about two and a half seconds, ahead, behind or beside a ship racing on at the limit. The winch pulls a probe (and a torpedo) as long as it comes in slower than the speed limit plus 40 m/s along the cable, whatever the ship's speed across it; and the probe helps: a sponsor wants its probe taken, so on the line its thrusters (about 20 kN, some 33 g on its 60 kg) burn in full against its swing across the cable (reeled in on its swing alone, a probe hooked beside a ship racing past would circle it on the shortening rope ever faster, its angular momentum kept, and come in after many seconds or never). A torpedo on the line keeps its swing. It counts only when it arrives: the ship takes it as if it had flown through it. Until then it is anyone's: a ship that touches it on its way first takes it, and the cable comes back empty. A probe another hook holds cannot be bitten. If the hooker lets go before it arrives, or dies, or the cable snaps, the probe drifts on with its speed, then its thrusters (4 g, never faster than 100 m/s towards it, and slow enough to stop there) fly it back to the place it would have had without the hook, its slot, where it is again within 5 m: a sponsor wants its probe where the pilots look for it, and on its slot every browser has it alike again; a rock whose surface passes within 3° of the nose's line counts as ahead, so it need not be aimed to the meter, unless a ship, core, probe or torpedo lies on the nose's line itself, which is bitten instead however near that rock; on nothing it flies out and is reeled back. An arena's massive body or the battleship's hull on the nose's line stops it: no ship beyond is bitten. Hooked to a rock, the cable is made fast where the harpoon bit, and the winch reels the ship in no closer than where it still keeps 70 m (four ship radii) off the rock's farthest reach R on a cable that leaves the rock's surface flat: √((R + 70 m)² − R²) from where the cable leaves the rock (about 110 m for a rock reaching 50 m, 220 m at 300 m, 290 m at 550 m); hooked to a ship, 70 m from that ship. While the hook holds, the winch yanks the cable in with 8 g, as hard as a coil throws (up to the speed limit plus 40 m/s), and the flight computer burns against any speed towards where the cable leaves the rock it could no longer stop before that distance (with up to 12 g, as late as it can), so holding on never slams the ship into its rock; letting go before, the ship keeps all its speed: a jump. The cable never gets longer: a taut cable takes away every speed away from the anchor and holds the ship at its length. So a ship moving past its rock swings around it, and faster the more cable the winch takes in, since its angular momentum stays; when the pilot lets go, it flies off along its swing like a stone from a sling, and for 1.2 seconds the flight computer turns the nose onto the new course while the autopilot leaves the drift alone (unless the pilot steers). The cable is a rope between two bodies (see Masses, ropes and bumps): the winch's force gives a ship 8 g and a lighter body more (by the ship's mass over its own, at most four times, 32 g); one force pulls both ends, so where the other end is lighter than a quarter ship, the force is that much smaller for the ship: a probe comes in at 32 g while its ship feels hardly a thirtieth of a g of it, a torpedo at 32 g against half a g. Hooked to a ship, the winch pulls both alike, the two ships having the same mass, and each one's own drive pulls against it; every speed counts against the other ship's (or the rock's drift and orbit), so two ships flying side by side are pulled together as two at rest, and the flight computers of both share the braking, so they never slam into each other. **Tug of war:** two ships on one rope that burn away from each other pull through it, the rope splitting every jerk half and half: equal drives (13 g each) hold a stalemate, and the stronger drive along the rope wins, both drifting its way at half the difference (a drive at four fifths, damaged or not upgraded, loses at 1.3 g); the hangar's drive upgrades and a damaged drive count as they count for flying. The overdrive's 200 m/s shove, through the taut rope, yanks the other ship along: both go on at 100 m/s, and then the overdriven drive is cold for 4 seconds. The rope does not break in a tug of war; only the hooker lets go (or the hooked ship's browser sees it snap far beyond its length, from lag). **A cable wraps.** It never passes through a solid body; it lies on it. Where rock, ice, junk or a hull section comes across a stretch of it (more than 15 m in from either end, so an end scraping along a surface counts for nothing), the cable lies on that piece from then on: a wrap point goes where it touches, 2 % of the piece's reach off its surface (at least 1 m), kept in the piece's own frame, so it turns and drifts with it. The cable is a chain of straight stretches from the anchor over its wrap points to the ship, and its length is their sum: wrapping and unwrapping never change it. Swinging around the rock it bit, the cable wraps onto that rock alike, and the ship swings around the last place the cable touches. A wrap point is let go once the cable would lift off it, its two stretches no longer bent around the piece: the ship swung back. The wrap is frictionless (with friction no winch could take in cable wound round a rock), so the tension is the same all along: the winch, the flight computer's brake and the rope act on the ship along its own stretch, towards the wrap point next to it, on a ship or probe at the other end along that end's stretch, and the pieces between, millions of times a ship's mass, take the rest and do not move. The winch keeps the ship's stretch at its floor (as above, with the reach of the piece the stretch leaves), paying out what the cable winds onto a rock: winding round its rock, the ship circles it 70 m off its reach, but the winch's brake takes what the payout gives, and the swing bleeds off (a ship circling a rock of 80 m at 180 m/s is down to some 40 m/s within a second): let go before the cable winds on. The cable snaps 1.5 km beyond where the winch stops, and where wrapping is no answer: on a planet's or a black hole's body; on the battleship's hull (a hull under its own drive, turning and ramming its way through the field, would drag whatever lies on it with millions of tonnes, and its collision boxes only roughly follow its plating, so a cable laid on them would hang in the air beside the hull); on a piece that reaches more than 30 m into a stretch within one step (it swept through faster than a cable slides round it; a ship swinging at 300 m/s moves its stretch some 5 m a frame), beyond however far the browser deciding it set its picture of another browser's ship at the cable's far end right in that step (a late word's jump is no sweep); and beyond 16 wrap points (a cable round a rock takes twelve to fifteen for a whole turn, so it winds once round its rock before it snaps). Wreckage does not cut it: its torn plates are light and are pushed aside. Every browser lays every cable over the pieces itself, from ship and anchor as it sees them (the wrap points are not sent): it draws the cable that way and pulls the ships it runs along it. The browser that runs the hooking ship decides whether it snaps and tells the others the hook let go; the own pilot hears CABLE SNAPPED and feels a crack through the hull. It also lets go when its rock breaks or its ship dies, and when the clone at either end is printed anew without dying (a nova core gone off, a first side in Capture the Flag): no cable comes along to the new clone, and every browser that learns of the reprint lets go of the cables on it, the browser that runs a hook telling the others. Hooked ships hear about it ("Hooked by …"). The hook is ready again 1.2 seconds after it was let go; the HUD shows "Hook ready", "Hooked · 300 m" or the seconds left. Not in the clamps of a round's opening. The cable is drawn in light blue from the nose over its wrap points. Bots carry the hook too (all but the flight school's), and use it sparingly: when the ship a bot hunts lies dead ahead between 150 and 900 m, with a chance of a quarter of its daring a second (Halley about once in five seconds of such a chance, Sputnik once in twenty, Laika never, less when a solo round goes easy on the Host), a bot hooks it, reels it in for 1.5 seconds and lets go, its hook ready again 10 seconds after that. Never in a race, and never the flight school's bots. Online, the browser of the ship that hooked tells the others what its hook holds (a rock with the point in the rock's own frame, a ship, a torpedo or a probe) and when it lets go; each browser pulls the ships it runs. A probe, which every browser has, every browser pulls on that cable itself, from the hooking ship as its states tell of it, so all see it come in alike; the others take the bite only from a living ship within the hook's reach plus 1 km of the probe (a cable said to be shorter than the way between them counts as that long), and of two hooks that bit the same probe or torpedo at once, the ship with the lower id keeps it on every browser (the other lets go), so exactly one browser decides where such a torpedo goes off. Who took the probe is decided as for any probe: by the browser of the ship that touched it.
- **Silent running.** A burning drive (its flame above 5 %) and a laser or torpedo fired glow on every sensor; a fresh clone's ship starts warm. A ship that has done neither for 2 seconds runs silent (`Ship.silent`, `detectable` in `src/ship/ship.ts`): the radar, the HUD's marks (its square, arrow and name, its drop line), a torpedo's lock and the bots find it only within 1 km (the radar reaches 2.5 km, a lock 3 km); the eye still sees it where nothing hides it, and the first burn or shot gives it away. The HUD says SILENT RUNNING while the own ship does. Every browser judges it alike from what it sees of a ship (its flame as its state tells, its beams and launches), so nothing more goes over the network. Bots obey it as Hosts do: a ship running silent is no target of theirs beyond 1 km.
- **The self-destruct.** Gear a Host buys in the hangar (B, D-pad left, SELF-DESTRUCT on touch screens, held for a second; the HUD counts it down). Then the ship's reactor goes off: every ship within 60 m (1,200 units, about two ship lengths) dies, each a kill of the holder's, and the holder's clone dies too, a death of its own as a crash is ("Self-destructed"), its sponsor break and all; the torpedoes and wreckage within the blast go with it. The last answer to a rival on the own tail or on the hook: hook it, reel it in, hold B. Not in a race and not in the flight school. Online, the holder's browser tells the others where it went off, and each kills the ships it runs within the blast; a self-destruct far from where the holder's ship was last seen is taken for a cheat. No bot carries one.
- **Probes (items).** Sponsor probes drift in the arena, each carrying one thing; whoever flies through one (within 15 m of it) takes it, or reels it in on the hook and takes it when it arrives (see The hook). A probe is a small satellite with wide solar wings, a dish and a neon ring and beacon in the color of what it carries: twin laser (pink), plating (blue), +2 torpedoes (orange), overdrive (yellow), repair (green), nova core (white), decoys (violet). Its hull is a sponsor's cheap, much-used canister, grimy, dented and stenciled; its wings carry solar cells under glass that a sheen of the sky runs over as it turns. Its neon glows in full from 100 m off, so it blooms from afar, and dims to about a third within 15 m, below the glow of the bloom, so up close it rings the probe instead of swamping it. From the 20th second of a round a probe shows up every 25 seconds at a place in the field (in a race on the track: between two gates, within 40 m of the straight line from one to the next; never in rock, nor where a massive body's pull alone would sound the escape gauge's WARNING, within 940 m of the black hole's center) and drifts there for 100 seconds unless taken, so at most four are out at once, the same on every browser whatever was taken when; which probe shows up when, where and with what follows from room and round (in a solo round from its own seed), so every browser has the same probes without a word, and taking one is told to the others (a Host who joins learns which ones are gone; in the rare case that two take the same probe at the same moment, both get it). In a race the arena's probes are sealed: their neon runs through every color, the radar calls them "Sealed probe", and what one holds is decided when it is taken, by its luck (from room and round, like everything about a probe) and the taker's place, so whoever lies behind catches up. The field is split into thirds by place (a ship alone counts as the middle). The leaders' third draws twin laser, plating, torpedoes, overdrive, repair, nova core and decoys in the ratio 1 : 4 : 2 : 1 : 4 : 0 : 1, so mostly plating and repairs and never a nova core; the middle draws the usual 2 : 3 : 3 : 2 : 3 : 1 : 2; the last third 3 : 1 : 4 : 4 : 2 : 2 : 3, mostly torpedoes, overdrives and decoys, and the nova cores. The taker's browser names its third along with the probe; another browser takes that third unless it sees the taker two thirds away (first against last), then its own view counts. The probes a dead clone drops show what they carry, in a race too.
  - *Twin laser*: the laser fires twice as fast (0.09 s between shots) at half the heat per shot (10 %). It stays with the clone.
  - *Plating*: two hits into the drive bounce off (blue sparks), or one torpedo takes all of it. It stays with the clone until it is used up.
  - *+4 torpedoes* (two in a drill of the flight school or a campaign's job, where every ship carries two: torpedoes are scarce off the tournament), *overdrive* (charged at once), *repair* (hull, drive and thrusters as new, the holes patched): used at once.
  - *Decoys* (violet): two hot lures, held up to three. Z (the D-pad right, DECOY on touch) drops one off the stern, 30 m/s slower than the ship, with a clonk through the hull as the clamp lets go and a small jolt of the view; it burns for 6 s, and while it burns it draws one torpedo homing on the ship, the nearest within 1 km of the lure (one launched after the drop too), which turns to it and goes off on it as on its target. One decoy, one torpedo: once the torpedo it drew is gone, the decoy is spent, so a salvo larger than the decoys dropped gets through; one whose torpedo lost sight of it behind a rock draws the next. Bots drop theirs when a torpedo after them comes within 600 m. Every browser drops the decoys the others tell of, so every torpedo turns alike; the shooter's browser still decides where its torpedo goes off. Decoys stay with the clone and are lost with it.
  - *Nova core* (white, about one probe in eight, two in seventeen by weight): an overcharged reactor core. The ship that holds it pulses in shifting neon for everyone to see, and its next press of fire is no laser: the core goes off (fire held since before the pickup does not count; it has to be let go and pressed again). Every other ship within 800 m in its open sight is destroyed (rock between the core and a hull shields it, from the radiation as from the debris; see Blasts), of the own side too (spawn protection does not help, plating does not help), and each of the other side counts as a double kill: the holder gets two kills and their points (1,000, 400 in a race), and the victim loses one kill and its points, so a Host who lets novas catch it again and again goes below zero, torpedoes in the blast go off, wreckage shatters, the President's battleship takes it on every part it reaches (see The President's Fleet: a part within 200 m breaks outright), rock and ice close by break (see Blasts), and a white shell runs out to 800 m in 0.8 seconds; whoever sees it within 2.4 km is blinded white for a moment. The holder's clone burns up in its own core and leaves nothing behind, whatever else it held; its hook's cable and any cable hooked on its hull go with it. It is not counted as a death or a lost clone and costs no points: the next clone is printed at once at the edge of the field (with the usual spawn protection; fire still held from the blast does not shoot until it is let go, so it does not end the protection), and the sponsors are so thrilled by the show that they ask for no ad this time; Nicole tells the Host so. Its own world looks different while it holds the core (shifted, oversaturated colors and a breathing white rim), and Nicole tells it what it has and to fly into the crowd, so the pilot has to hold back the trigger until it is there. Everyone within 1.2 km of a holder gets the panic: a two-tone siren, a flashing red rim, NOVA CORE ACTIVATED with the distance, and Nicole telling it to get out. The radar shows the holder in white. Bots flee a holder within 1.2 km at twice their cruising speed, and a bot holding one never sets it off with a ship of its own side within 800 m; otherwise it does with at least two rivals within 640 m, or where it would take three torpedoes' worth of hits (30) from the battleship's parts, as it does from where a bot attacking a battery holds; after holding it 20 seconds a single rival within 800 m is enough (a double kill all the same, and rivals flee a holder, so a crowd may never come), and after 60 seconds it sets it off wherever it is, for its laser stays silent until the core is gone and the reprint costs nothing. It stays with the clone until it goes off, and flies out again when the clone dies. Online, the holder's browser tells every browser where the core went off, and each destroys the ships it runs; the hits on the battleship's parts the holder's browser counts and tells every browser, as with a torpedo's.
  - Whatever a clone takes is named with what it does, in a few words (*Twin laser · fires twice as fast*); the HUD lists what it holds next to its torpedoes the same way.
  - When a clone dies, its twin laser, plating and nova core fly out again as probes with its wreck's momentum, up to 20 m/s apart, for whoever gets there first; they drift for 45 seconds. The radar shows every probe in green with what it carries. A bot with nothing to fight goes for a probe within 1.5 km. No probes in the flight school.
- **Comets.** Now and then a comet crosses the arena: a dark nucleus of two lobes, its head, 70 m in radius, in a cold blue-white coma, two tails streaming away from the sun, and its shower, 800 small pieces of ice and rock (3 m) flying with it along its route, from 60 m to 3 km behind the head (5 s of its flight), the swarm widening from 60 m to 260 m around the route and densest near the head. A round sees one in each of two windows of field time, each with its chance: one reaching the arena's edge between 75 and 135 s with a chance of 3 in 4, and one between 175 and 235 s with a chance of 2 in 5 (about 1.15 a round, none in a quarter of them). The head flies at 600 m/s on a route within 35° of the arena's horizon plane, passing the field's center within 800 m, from 8.8 km before the center to 8.8 km beyond it; the shower's pieces follow the head's route, each as far behind as it trails. That is deliberately slow: a real comet crosses a planet's neighborhood at 5 to 25 km/s and would be through the arena in under a second; at 600 m/s it can be watched coming and dodged, and it does not keep the Host waiting. The head comes into view 5.4 km beyond the arena's edge, about 9 s before it reaches it, crosses the arena (6.75 km) in about 11 s, and its shower takes 5 s more to pass: about 25 s in view until the last piece has left the arena. Eight seconds before it reaches the arena's edge, just after it came into view, with the head 4.8 km beyond it and 5.6 s more from the edge to the center, the flight computer warns ("Comet inbound …", a beep, and a WARNING alarm "COMET INBOUND" pointing at the head and counting down the seconds to the arena's edge until the head has passed), the announcer calls it, and the radar shows the head. The head keeps its route, and nothing stops it:
  - A whole rock in its way breaks into its pieces. Whatever was a piece already (a fragment of rock or ice, broken junk) goes to dust at once, without pieces of its own, in a burst of dust and grit; so do the rock's fragments born in the head's way. A comet never leaves a piece of a piece behind. Nobody scores for what it breaks.
  - Solid pieces (the derelict's hull, the relay hub of the Satellite Junkyard) are wrecked already and do not break: the head breaks up on the first one whose own shape it touches (its box, as a ship does), as on the battleship's hull, and reaches nothing beyond it; on a world's ground (should one ever come over a world) it breaks up the same and moves nothing, as the ground stands for good. Its rubble stays with the hull, so the hull takes its momentum: the head, a ball of 70 m of dust and ice as loosely packed as real comets' nuclei (500 kg/m³, 0.72 million tonnes), and the struck body move on together at the speed that keeps it, 600 m/s times the head's share of both masses. A half of the world ship is one body (see Arenas): the bow drifts on at 8.5 m/s, the stern at 9.4 m/s, the relay hub (19.5 million tonnes) at 21 m/s, along the comet's course; a single piece on its own would also turn from the off-center blow (about its center as a ball of its mass, at most 0.12 rad/s more), a welded body only moves. A flash, sparks and debris mark where it struck, heard and felt like a blast nearby. A pilot's cover moves.
  - Every ship it meets dies, whatever protects it, "Run down by a comet"; nobody scores for it, unless a rival shoved it into the head within 3 seconds (a knockout, see Shoves and knockouts), and the announcer may comment on it.
  - The President's battleship always gets out of its way: the lead browser sees every comet of the round 40 s before its head passes nearest the battleship, and while the route runs within 975 m of the battleship's center (the shower's width, a piece, the battleship's own reach of 640 m, 75 m to spare) it leaves its orbit and burns straight away from the route at 50 m/s, turning onto that course twice as hard as onto its orbit; from right on the route it is clear in about 21 s, and it takes up its orbit again once the shower has gone by. One whose drives are shot out drifts and cannot get away. A battleship the head strikes all the same goes down as if its reactor broke ("The comet rams the battleship"): its reactor goes critical, it goes up 4 seconds later with its blast of 1 km, and every ship of the side scores the 500 points of a sinking; the Hosts win the round. The head itself breaks up on its hull: a body of ice and dust 140 m across meeting over a million tonnes of armor at 600 m/s bursts (each kilogram of it brings 180 kJ, far more than holds a loose body together), a flash and its shards where it struck, and it is gone; it flies on through nothing and breaks no rock beyond. A head that strikes a battleship already going down breaks up all the same. Each browser breaks it where its own battleship meets it, and at the field time the lead browser says it did (sent with the battleship), so a browser whose battleship, gliding after the lead's, only grazed it or missed it by a little breaks it there too. The shower flies on: each piece bursts on the armor where it meets the hull, sparks flying (checked along its way since the frame before, so none slips through a battery's barrels), doing the armor no harm; the battleship is cover from the swarm, and the pieces that miss it fly on.

  The arenas' massive bodies bend a comet's route with the same gravity they pull ships with, and they win against it. The route is flown in the body's pull in steps of 0.01 s and kept every 0.1 s of field time. In the Event Horizon the comet is aimed at the black hole (its route would pass it within 368 m), whose pull bends it in (at 600 m/s a route aimed up to 445 m off falls into it) and draws it in faster and faster: 0.7 to 1 s before it would have passed the center it reaches the black hole's body (350 m) and is swallowed, 250 to 285 m off the straight line it came on. Over its last 2 s the head is drawn out along its course to four times its length, its coma swelling; a flash of 1.5 s marks where it went; the shower's swarm draws together onto the route over each piece's last 3 s and follows the head in, piece by piece, its tail drawn in after it until the last piece is gone, 5 s later. Over Jupiter the comet dives towards the planet at 26° to 43° as it crosses the field; its route is flown from 23.6 km off and aimed higher against the planet's pull (five rounds, each by what the last one missed), so it still crosses the field within 800 m of the center (within 1 m, in fact), and timed so that it passes there at its appointed field time. The pull, almost a g all the way down from so far off, speeds it up to 735 to 760 m/s at the center: it starts out 8.8 km off only 11.3 to 13.3 s before and reaches the arena's edge 3.5 to 5.5 s before (the warning still comes 8 s before that, as the head comes into view). There it plunges through the cloud deck, and the pull draws it on down into the depths, which swallow it 7.7 to 12.2 s after it crossed the center, with the same flash, its shower after it. The derelict and the other arenas have no body; their comets fly straight.

  The head is the comet's nucleus (`src/world/nucleus.ts`), as the nuclei spacecraft have photographed are, and its shape is only its look: it strikes as a sphere of 70 m. It is an irregular, elongated body of two lobes joined by a neck, one lobe larger than the other and the two at an angle, about 185 m long and 120 m by 105 m across (reaching up to 1.5 times the head's radius from its center); it is generated like the rocks (integer hashing and polynomials only), the same on every browser. Its crust is dark dust reflecting about 4 % of the light (real nuclei reflect 2 to 6 %), darker than any rock of the field: smooth plains where the dust settled, in the neck above all; rough ground pitted with small hollows and broken into terraces about 2.5 m high, each ending in a cliff; six sinkholes 6 to 11 m in radius with flat floors and steep walls, where the ice beneath boiled away; and bright ice exposed (six times brighter than the crust, cool white) on some of the cliffs, on the sinkholes' walls and in a few ragged patches, about 4 % of the surface. Its fine relief and its shade lie in a relief map baked as a rock's is, with the rocks' shared detail tile on it up close; within 2 km it is drawn in about 8,800 triangles, farther off in 2,000; as the picture's quality draws the rocks' near meshes less far out, so it draws the nucleus's, and from the low tier on only the far one. It tumbles slowly, mostly about its shortest axis, as a body of its shape spins, a turn in a little over a minute. The sinkholes are its vents: from each one the sun shines into, a narrow jet of gas and dust streams out, 450 m to 1 km long, 3 m wide at the vent and fanning out to 70 m, leaving along the ground turned towards the sun and bent back by the sunlight's push, so it rises towards the sun, turns and streams back into the coma; a vent wakes as the sun climbs over it, lies dormant in the dark, and each breathes slowly. A jet is never drawn thinner than about 2 pixels on a phone (its light spread over the width, so it only grows fainter with distance), and it fades out within 250 m of the camera. The coma fades to 8 % of its glow from 6 km in to 1 km of the camera, so the dark nucleus shows against the sky up close: from inside, its gas is far too thin to see.

  The tails are the comet's light show and harm nobody: gas and dust the sun boils off the head, blown straight away from the sun whichever way the comet flies, as real comets' are, so a comet is seen crossing the sky from anywhere in the arena. The ion tail (blue) runs straight down the sun's line, 25 km long, its half width opening from 200 m at the head to 1.2 km at its end; the dust tail (white-yellow, sunlight on dust) is broader, 22 km long and from 400 m to 4 km in half width, and curves back along the route, its end lagging 30 % of its length behind (the heavier dust is pushed away more slowly than the gas). Both brighten out of the coma over their first 4 % and fade out towards their end; a well that swallows the comet draws them in with its shower. Flying through a tail does nothing to the ship, but the pilot feels it: dust patters on the hull (heard through it, about 25 bursts a second deep in the tail, fewer at its edge), the link crackles and the picture glitches slightly now and then (about 1.5 times a second deep in it), the dust streaks past, and the first time in a comet's tail the HUD says "Comet tail · only dust and gas, harmless". Seen from inside, a tail is too thin to see: it fades out within 1.5 km of the camera and where it is seen along its axis.

  The shower is the real danger, and it looks it: each piece glints in an orange halo (the color of what hits, at least 3 pixels wide however far off), so the swarm along the route reads apart from the blue and white tails. Every piece that comes within a ship's hit radius strikes it once, like a laser beam from where it comes (the comet's flight against the ship's) but half again as hard: the armor takes 3 points (into the flanks the thrusters 15 %), the drive 30 % of its thrust, and the fourth piece into the drive sends the reactor critical; plating (the item) takes a piece into the drive as it takes a beam. So a pilot in the shower turns the armored nose into it, or better, gets out of its way: rock within 300 m upstream of the ship takes every piece, and the lee of an asteroid is the safest place there is. Each piece bursts on whatever solid it meets on its way (checked along its way since the frame before): rock, ice, junk, hull, a rig, wreckage, a ring's housing, a sponsor core, a mission's hauler, as on the battleship's armor, sparks flying from a quarter of them; each browser sees it so for the ships it runs (a CRITICAL alarm "COMET SHOWER · NOSE INTO IT OR TAKE COVER", pointing at the head, while the own ship is in the way of the head or its shower: within 500 m beyond the swarm's width of the route, from 8 s ahead of the head to the swarm's end). Bots near the route as the head nears or the shower passes fly away from it, sideways. A clone the shower kills dies "Riddled by a comet's shower"; nobody scores for it. Everything about a comet follows from the room's seed, the round and the field time, so every browser sees the same comet, the same bent route and the same shower without a word sent; a rock the head breaks or a hull it breaks up on is struck at the first moment, on a grid of 0.1 s of field time, at which the head touched it, so every browser breaks it at the same field time with the same fragments, or shoves it the same way, however its frames fell. A newcomer takes the shoved pieces' motion from the field's snapshot; whether the battleship got away, the lead browser decides and sends as it sends the battleship. Comets come in every mode, in a race across the course too; the flight school has none, and nor has a world raced or fought over (a head's route would plough through its ground), and nothing there warns of one. The warning, the alarm and the announcer follow only the comet of the round that comes now: a round's end clears them, so a comet called in one round never sounds in the next or in the flight school.
- **Kill cam.** When the own clone is shot down, the last two seconds before the kill play again at two thirds of their speed (over three seconds, as long as the next clone takes to print, so watching it costs no time), seen from behind the shooter and looking at the clone, with the beams that were fired; the HUD steps aside. Enter skips it. The game keeps the last 240 frames of every ship and the last 64 beams for it, and the slow motion puts each ship between the two kept frames around the moment it shows, so it glides instead of stepping from frame to frame; the replay only moves the ships' pictures, so the round goes on underneath. The sponsor break follows, and behind the ad the replay runs again and again until the next clone boots (meanwhile nothing new is recorded, so the moments before the kill stay). Not in the flight school, and not for deaths without a shooter. In the sponsor break the death can be kept as a clip ("Save this death as a clip"): the replay starts over and one run of it, three seconds, is recorded in the browser from the 3D picture (without the HUD and the ad) and the cockpit's sound (radio, announcer, hull), as MP4 where the browser records it, else WebM, at 30 frames a second; a second press ("Share the clip") hands it to the device's share sheet with a line and the game's link, or, where the browser shares no files, saves it. Nothing leaves the device until the Host picks where it goes.
- **Big moments.** The own pilot's big moments are made for the broadcast: a banner in neon above the crosshair (never on it), the crowd of the stadium through the radio, and for the biggest the announcer ("{pilot} makes it three. somebody print more clones"). A kill within 4 seconds of the own last one makes a streak: two a DOUBLE KILL, three a TRIPLE KILL, more a MULTI KILL; a nova core that takes several ships at once is a SUPERNOVA; killing the ship that killed the own last clone is REVENGE (once; a new death names a new one); a ship shoved into a crash is a KNOCKOUT (see Shoves and knockouts); and there are TORPEDO KILL, INTERCEPTED (a torpedo shot down), OVERTAKEN and IN THE LEAD (a race's place won), CORE CAPTURED and BATTLESHIP DOWN. The crowd erupts for the biggest (multi kill, supernova, triple kill, battleship down, capture), nearly so for a knockout, and cheers for the rest, then settles back to its murmur; the announcer calls the triple and multi kill, the supernova, the revenge and the knockout (unless he spoke a moment ago). One banner stands 2.4 seconds; meanwhile only a moment as big or bigger replaces it (MULTI KILL and SUPERNOVA first, then TRIPLE KILL and BATTLESHIP DOWN, CORE CAPTURED, REVENGE and KNOCKOUT, DOUBLE KILL and IN THE LEAD, TORPEDO KILL, INTERCEPTED, OVERTAKEN). A streak starts over with a new round and with the own clone's death. The moments themselves score nothing; a streak's kills earn their bonus (see Streaks pay), shown beneath the banner ("Streak bonus +250"). Not in the flight school.
- Destroyed ships respawn at the edge of the field, at the best of twelve random places clear of rock and out of a massive body's grip (never where its pull alone would sound the escape gauge's CAUTION): the one farthest from the nearest enemy, preferably out of every enemy's sight behind the asteroids. Where none of them is clear (a dense field, a base among rocks), the first of four dozen more places on the edge clear of rock at all, and as a last resort open space just beyond the field's rocks. A fresh clone has 5 seconds of spawn protection (it blinks); bots leave it alone meanwhile, and firing ends it early (fire held from before the clone was printed does not shoot until it is let go). The Host sits through a sponsor break while the next clone is printed: after 3 seconds an ad appears, and once it has run (6 seconds), a button boots the next clone. A bot's next clone takes as long as a Host's at the soonest: 9 seconds. The round goes on meanwhile.
- Human and alien interceptor are identical apart from their looks.
