Both hulls are built in code from facets, shaded round where they meet at less than 60° (so the octagonal bodies read as curved) and hard at sharper edges, 344 triangles the human and 256 the alien (the drive flame and glows of the model files come on top): the human interceptor follows the original model (a fuselage with a canopy, two tall fins on struts with the tournament's orange stripe, a gun under the nose, two drives), the alien interceptor is a slim dark dart with a tall dorsal blade, a keel and swept side blades, veined with the turquoise glow of its life support. From the model files they keep only the collider box (so the shared hit radius is the same), the drive flame and the glows at the nozzles.
- **The dodge.** Tapping a thruster key twice within 0.3 seconds (A, D, R or F; two flicks of the thruster stick or the touch push stick work the same way), or LB on the gamepad (the way the thruster stick points, else to the right), fires every maneuvering thruster at overload: a kick of 60 m/s sideways, up or down (weaker with damaged thrusters), a jolt and 15 % g-force strain, enough to throw the ship back onto a racing line after a missed ring. The autopilot leaves that drift alone for 1.2 seconds, so the ship plainly leaves the line of fire, about 70 m, two ship lengths; the thrusters need 1.5 seconds before the next dodge. Kicked into a ship alongside, it is a shove (see Shoves and knockouts).
- **Dodging.** The laser hits the instant it fires, so nobody outruns a beam; a dodge beats the aim. Bots sense how hard their target changes its motion across their line of sight (smoothed over 0.6 s) and their aim scatters by it: at half the thrusters' push one ship radius, at most two and a half. Steady flight, however fast, is easy to lead; jinking left and right with the thrusters, autopilot on or off, is what makes them miss. Bots fight for position: each works its way to a point 200 m behind its target, keeps its nose on the target meanwhile and lets the thrusters carry it around; it fires into an open drive and only now and then takes a single shot at armor (in three of ten thinking spells, and only with a laser below 10 % heat), so it keeps the heat for the drive. A ship on a bot's tail that the lock warning would show (within 3 km, in sight, its nose tracking the bot, not in a solar storm) makes it turn its armored nose to that ship, jink left and right (with 4 g of its thrusters' 10 g, so a Host of middling skill still lands its hits) and fight back, and so does a hit into its drive from however far, its beam showing where it came from: for 3 seconds the bot defends against the shooter; the flight school's unarmed sparring bot runs instead. Bots look 5 seconds ahead along their flight for rock (or a massive body, or the battleship's hull), and not along a line but along the tube their hull sweeps, 3 ship radii (about 50 m) around it: a rock in it pushes the wanted velocity out past its side and slows it, and one less than 3 seconds ahead makes the bot turn and burn away from it. A line alone would let a rock the line just misses through, until the hull strikes it. Bots steer around the President's battleship as around rock. Near a massive body they read the escape gauge as a Host does: within 750 m of its surface, or once the gauge reads 30 % (its pull plus the braking their fall towards it would take, over what their drive has left), they burn away from it before anything else. What a shot drive has left counts only while they fall towards the body: over Jupiter, whose pull fills the arena, a bot with its drive shot would otherwise burn away for the rest of the round. Skill decides a fight visibly, so the rivals a Host picks set how hard a round is. A bot's aim misses its target by up to 1.8 ship radii at aim 0, 0.9 at aim 0.5 and 0.36 at aim 0.8, at any distance, and also wanders by an angle: about 2.3° at aim 0, 1.1° at aim 0.5 (one ship radius at 1 km, so a far target is hard to hit) and 0.46° at aim 0.8. Aim beyond 0.8 counts as 0.8, so no bot aims like a machine. It pulls the trigger once its target has been in its sights for 0.55 s at reflexes 0, 0.3 s at reflexes 0.5, and never sooner than 0.15 s, a quick human's reaction. In a solo deathmatch of the default rivals the best (Vega, Orion) score about twice what the worst (Laika) does, and the middling Kessler about 130 points over the median. Bots see ships within 3.5 km, across the whole field, and fire only within 1 km. Each bot hunts the nearest ship in sight, but a ship others already hunt counts as farther away, and one hunted by two is left to them; a bot reads who hunts whom from what it sees (ships in its own sight, within its sensors' reach or a storm's 750 m and not behind rock, within 1 km of a ship, their noses within 17° of it), never from another bot's mind. A bot keeps its target unless another is clearly nearer: the bots fight each other too, not only the Host. In a solo round they go easy on a Host who keeps dying: every death by an enemy raises their ease by 0.35 (up to 1), every kill of the Host lowers it by 0.1, and it fades over 90 seconds; at full ease they aim two and a half times worse and pull the trigger 0.4 seconds later.
- **Several against one.** In the campaign's fights a mission's raiders (an ambush's bandits, a bounty's face and escorts, a wave round a hull) never all press the Host at once (`pressOn` in `src/ship/bots.ts`): only two of them fight with their own skills, the two nearest the Host when a place falls free, kept while they live. The others fight on held back, at half their aim, reflexes and daring, and fire as a racer does, only now and then a single shot. Each raider shot down lets the next one press, so the fight is hardest at its end and every one of them down is earned. The raiders keep to the place they raid: they turn back beyond 2.5 km of it, as the Games' bots do from the field's middle, since a place of the campaign has no edge.
- A solo round lasts five minutes of real time, like the radio's track, so a slow frame rate does not stretch it. It pauses when the tab is hidden, and while paused. When game time runs at another speed (the time-lapse of the balance runs, a freeze for a screenshot: `game.timeScale` in development), the round's clock runs with the game's time instead, its opening, its probes and its end included, so a time-lapse plays the round as it is played. A long frame (a slow device, a time-lapse) is run in steps of at most 1/30 s, and a held trigger fires on the laser's exact cadence, so shots, collisions and bots come out the same at any frame rate.
- The personal best counts solo and tournament rounds; points from the flight school's drones and sparring bots do not count.
- The sponsor break shows the sponsor's product as a hologram (see Holograms under The universe; `src/view/hologram.ts`, the products in `src/view/products.ts`): one small WebGL view of its own, made at the first break and kept, drawn only while the break is shown, at most 30 frames a second and at the title screen's pad's pixel ratio and frame rate on the quality ladder, its object freed when the break ends; each product at most 3,000 triangles. With reduced motion it stands still at a three-quarter view, without flicker or glitch. Where WebGL cannot be had, the sponsor's small flat picture shows instead.
- The sponsor break can be paused. If an ad fails or runs too long, the button to boot the clone appears after 20 seconds at the latest. When an ad blocker hides even the satire ads (it hides the ad slot by its name), the break notices that the ad has no size and scolds the Host instead: "You even blocked an ad for products that do not exist. Shame on you. How is an indie developer supposed to make a living?" The clone boots all the same; the check looks only at the page itself and sends nothing anywhere.

## Privacy

The game collects as little as possible: no accounts, no cookies, no tracking, no ad network, nothing loaded from third-party servers. The German legal pages [Impressum](impressum.html) and [Datenschutz](datenschutz.html) are linked from the menu and the radio page. What happens to data:

- **Hosting:** Cloudflare Pages delivers the site and sees connection data (IP address, time, file, user agent); Cloudflare also asks browsers to report failed connections (Network Error Logging). Links to GitHub carry no referrer.
- **Browser storage:** fourteen localStorage entries (callsign, the tint picked, personal best, the drill reached in each course of the flight school, the courses completed, how often Nicole's briefings and tips were heard, radio on or off, radio volume, the last solo round's choices, the best lap of each arena's race course (its time and splits), the hangar: Solar Coins, the upgrades of the ships and the ship flown, the language chosen, the campaign Lot 7731: the missions flown, the drill reached in the running one, its coins, what the debt still asks, its ratings, the parts fitted to its ship and the modules of its flight computer, the picture's quality: a tier pinned in the menu, or the rung the governor last found smooth on this device), and in IndexedDB the last track pressed on the radio page (its title and WAV file, only the last one, to be saved again); they stay in the browser, only the callsign, the tint and the looks of the own ship (type, neon trim, drive flame) are shown to the other players of an online tournament.
- **Online tournament** (only when a Host starts or joins one): the browsers connect directly over WebRTC, so the other players see the Host's IP address, callsign, ship and score. To find each other, the browsers post short connection messages (with network addresses) on public Nostr relays run by independent third parties: a fixed list of 12, checked every week and shipped with the game (Trystero's built-in relays when none of them answers). Each browser asks Cloudflare's STUN server for its public address; Trystero's default STUN servers of Google are replaced by Cloudflare's alone. Joining fetches short-lived credentials from `/turn`, and where no direct connection is possible the game data runs through Cloudflare's TURN relay (end-to-end encrypted by WebRTC). Every browser checks roughly whether what the others report is possible and tells them when it is not; once a majority of the other Hosts with a place (at least two; watching Hosts have no vote) say so, everyone in the tournament sees the cheater's callsign and stops sending to it. Nothing of it is stored. Opening the list of tournaments connects to the lobby the same way, and the names, codes, modes, Host counts and round times of public tournaments are visible to everyone there; with the code anyone can join.
- **Sharing:** the share button sends nothing by itself. Only when the Host picks a target does the link with its text go there: to the device's share sheet, the clipboard, the own mail program, or the chosen network's share page (X, Bluesky, WhatsApp, Telegram, Reddit, LinkedIn, Facebook, Threads), which opens in a new tab without a referrer. A tournament's link carries its room code.
- **Sponsor break:** invented satire ads, no ad network. The privacy policy has to be extended before real ads are shown.

**Code:** Copyright © 2012–2026 Johannes Hoppe, licensed under the [GNU Affero General Public License v3.0 or later](LICENSE). Anyone may play, study, change and share the game. Whoever publishes a changed version, including as a website, has to publish its source code under the same license.

**Contributions:** Johannes Hoppe keeps the right to license the code under other terms as well, commercial ones included. Whoever contributes signs the Contributor License Agreement in [CONTRIBUTING.md](CONTRIBUTING.md) once, with a file `contributors/<GitHub username>.txt`; the contributor keeps the copyright, and the contribution is AGPL for everyone else. A check on every pull request (`.github/workflows/cla.yml`) labels it `cla: missing` or `cla: signed` and says so in a comment.

**The mathematical functions** (`src/detmath.ts`) follow the algorithms and constants of fdlibm (Copyright © 1993, 2004 Sun Microsystems, Inc.: use, copying, changing and distribution freely granted as long as its notice is kept) as the musl C library keeps them (Copyright © 2005–2020 Rich Felker et al., MIT License); both notices stand in the file's header. Both licenses allow relicensing.

**Name and brand:** The name *SolarTournament*, the logo and the domains solartournament.games and solar-tournament.games are not covered by the license. A changed version has to use its own name.

**Ships:** The two interceptor models and their drive sprites (`public/assets/models/`, `public/assets/textures/`) are by Johannes Hoppe and not covered by the license. Please ask for permission before using them in other software.

**Everything else is generated in code** and covered by the license: asteroids, the Milky Way, the faint stars below the catalogue's limit, the stars shown until the real sky has loaded, every arena's nebulae, sun, the surfaces of hulls, rings and the world ship, particles, sound effects, music and the icon, except the crowd, the stars of the real sky and the maps of Saturn, Jupiter and Earth's night and day.

**The ground of Psyche's crater and of Vesta's flank** (`public/assets/psyche/ground-2k.jpg`, `ground-1k.jpg`): a part of the orthophoto of the Apollo 17 site (`NAC_DTM_APOLLO17_MOSAIC_120CM`, 1.2 m per pixel) by the Lunar Reconnaissance Orbiter Camera's narrow angle cameras, credit NASA/GSFC/Arizona State University, from the LROC archive of NASA's Planetary Data System, whose data products are in the public domain per the LROC terms; flattened, made to tile and stretched (details in that folder's README).

**The campaign's people and the Derelict's dead** (`public/assets/people/`, the holograms' busts, the figures in the flesh, and the frozen dead `frozen-<n>.glb`): figures made with MakeHuman's Blender extension MPFB from MakeHuman's system assets, which are CC0, its project claiming nothing of what is made with them (its `LICENSE.md`); shaped, posed, cut to busts and thinned out by `tools/people`. The suits the Derelict's crew died in and the clones fly in (`suit-astronaut.glb`, `suit-swat.glb`, and the suited dead among `frozen-<n>.glb`) are the astronaut and the SWAT figure of **Quaternius**'s *Ultimate Modular Men Pack* (https://quaternius.com/packs/ultimatemodularcharacters.html), released under CC0 1.0 (https://creativecommons.org/publicdomain/zero/1.0/); its flat colors laid into small worn textures, posed and thinned out by `tools/people/frozen.py`.

**The stars of the real sky** (`public/assets/sky/stars.bin`, packed by `npm run sky`): the Yale Bright Star Catalogue, 5th revised edition (preliminary version), by Dorrit Hoffleit and Wayne H. Warren Jr. (Yale University Observatory and NASA's Astronomical Data Center, 1991), as NASA's High Energy Astrophysics Science Archive Research Center (HEASARC) distributes it as its table [BSC5P](https://heasarc.gsfc.nasa.gov/W3Browse/star-catalog/bsc5p.html). NASA's open data portal lists this dataset ([Bright Star Catalog](https://data.nasa.gov/dataset/bright-star-catalog), published by HEASARC) under the license of [U.S. government works](https://www.usa.gov/government-works), which carry no copyright in the United States; its use implies no endorsement by NASA. Of each star the file keeps its place (J2000), its visual magnitude as its brightness and its color index B−V (or, for the few without one, its spectral class) as its color (see `public/assets/sky/README.md`).

**Saturn** (`public/assets/planets/saturn-2k.jpg`, `saturn-1k.jpg`, `saturn-ring.png`): the Saturn map and the Saturn ring texture of [Solar System Scope](https://www.solarsystemscope.com/textures/), licensed under [CC BY 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/). The map is scaled down from 4096 × 2048 to 2048 × 1024 and 1024 × 512; the ring is averaged across its width into a strip of 2048 × 1, cropped to the ring, its color brightened by 1.6 and drawn a little towards a warm grey (see `public/assets/planets/README.md`).

**Jupiter** (`public/assets/planets/giant-2k.jpg`, `giant-1k.jpg`): ["Cassini's Best Maps of Jupiter (Cylindrical Map)"](https://science.nasa.gov/photojournal/cassinis-best-maps-of-jupiter-cylindrical-map), PIA07782, credit NASA/JPL/Space Science Institute, used under the [JPL Image Use Policy](https://www.jpl.nasa.gov/jpl-image-use-policy/); scaled down from 3601 × 1801 to 2048 × 1024 and 1024 × 512, its colors unchanged. Its use implies no endorsement by NASA, JPL or Caltech.

**Earth at night** (`public/assets/planets/earth-2k.jpg`, `earth-1k.jpg`): ["Earth at Night / Black Marble 2016 Color Maps"](https://science.nasa.gov/earth/earth-observatory/earth-at-night/maps/), the 3 km map (13500 × 6750), credit NASA Earth Observatory, used under [NASA's Images and Media Usage Guidelines](https://www.nasa.gov/nasa-brand-center/images-and-media/) (NASA content is generally not subject to copyright in the United States; NASA is acknowledged as the source; its use implies no endorsement by NASA). Its land and its lights packed into one grey value, redrawn as Earth seen from far above the point beneath the Satellite Junkyard (an orthographic projection about 35° north, 20° east, out to 62° from it), at 2048 × 2048 and 1024 × 1024 (see `public/assets/planets/README.md`); the 26th century's denser lights are added in the shader.

**Earth by day** (`public/assets/planets/earth-day-2k.jpg`, `earth-day-1k.jpg`): ["Blue Marble: Next Generation with Topography and Bathymetry"](https://science.nasa.gov/earth/earth-observatory/blue-marble-next-generation/base-topography-bathymetry/), July (`world.topo.bathy.200407.3x21600x10800.jpg`), credit NASA Earth Observatory, used under [NASA's Images and Media Usage Guidelines](https://www.nasa.gov/nasa-brand-center/images-and-media/) (NASA content is generally not subject to copyright in the United States; NASA is acknowledged as the source; its use implies no endorsement by NASA). Redrawn in the same projection as the night map, cut to the western 62 % of it that the sun or the twilight reaches, at 1270 × 2048 and 635 × 1024, its colors unchanged (see `public/assets/planets/README.md`); the clouds are added in the shader.

**Mars** over Phobos (`public/assets/planets/mars-2k.jpg`, `mars-1k.jpg`): ["Mars Viking Global Color Mosaic 925m"](https://astrogeology.usgs.gov/search/map/mars_viking_global_color_mosaic_925m) of the USGS Astrogeology Science Center, made from some 1,000 Viking Orbiter images (NASA), public domain, without use constraints; scaled down from 23059 × 11530 to 2048 × 1024 and 1024 × 512, colors unchanged.

**Crowd** (`public/assets/sounds/crowd-roar.mp3`): ["RoaringCrowd.wav"](https://freesound.org/people/benfree/sounds/130568/) by benfree on Freesound, CC0 1.0 (public domain), made into a seamless mono loop.

**The SolarTournament logo, the seal of the Ministry of War** (`public/crest.svg`, drawn by `npm run crest`; logo, favicon, menu, loading screen, radio page): the eagle is based on ["Zurich Eagle Silhouette"](https://openclipart.org/detail/282180/zurich-eagle-silhouette) by GDJ on Openclipart, CC0 1.0 (public domain), cut into straight facets by the generator.

**Font:** [Michroma](https://github.com/googlefonts/Michroma-font) for headings and signs, SIL Open Font License 1.1 (see `public/assets/fonts/OFL-michroma.txt`). Text is set in the system's sans-serif.

**Voices** (`public/assets/voice/`, rendered at build time by `npm run voice`, with ffmpeg 6.1.1 as the static builds of the release `b6.1.1` of [ffmpeg-static](https://github.com/eugeneware/ffmpeg-static), GPL-3.0, a build tool only, fetched by `tools/voice/ffmpeg.ts` and checked against the SHA-256 pinned there): the radio voices by [espeak-ng](https://github.com/espeak-ng/espeak-ng) (GPL-3.0; its output is not covered by the GPL), the flight computer by [Kokoro-82M](https://huggingface.co/hexgrad/Kokoro-82M) (Apache-2.0, voice `af_nicole`) through [kokoro-js](https://www.npmjs.com/package/kokoro-js) (Apache-2.0), and in German, Spanish and French her clone by [Chatterbox Multilingual](https://github.com/resemble-ai/chatterbox) (MIT, code and weights; its output carries Resemble AI's inaudible Perth watermark), checked by [Whisper](https://huggingface.co/openai/whisper-small) (Apache-2.0); the cloned lines are kept as assets of the release `voice-nicole`, not in the repository (see `tools/voice/README.md`). They all run only when the voices are made; none ships with the game.
